Result: On the sealed 22-case source test, all three discovery seeds show instance-set performance improvement with segmentation fidelity and safety preservation: instance F1 increased by 0.0504–0.0612 and positive-frame set-failure rate decreased by 0.0848–0.1060. Direct ROBUST-MIPS transfer reproduced the complete result in all three seeds. Endoscapes supported only one of three seeds, not establishing stable direct transfer.

Conclusion: The evaluated complete relational path improves coherent instance-set construction from fixed Mask2Former candidates within specified native-instance contracts; however, stable cross-domain transfer and component-specific effects remain unestablished.

Abstract: Accurate foreground masks can still form an incorrect surgical-instrument instance set: duplicate, fragmented, merged, missed, or empty-frame predictions may preserve favorable pixel overlap while violating object identity and count. Final query selection is therefore a relational, variable-cardinality problem rather than a collection of independent candidate decisions. We evaluate topology-aware query selection, which represents the nonempty candidates of a fixed Mask2Former as a complete graph, learns relational candidate and pair representations, predicts set cardinality, and solves an exact structured subset problem. The formal comparison is the complete relational path versus a node-feature-matched path; it evaluates the combined effect of pairwise geometry, message passing, and the additional relational-path capacity, not an isolated component. On the sealed 22-case source test, all three discovery seeds supported instance-set performance improvement with segmentation fidelity and predefined technical-safety preservation: instance F1 increased by 0.0504--0.0612 and positive-frame set-failure rate decreased by 0.0848--0.1060. Direct ROBUST-MIPS transfer reproduced the complete result in all three seeds. Endoscapes supported only one of three seeds and therefore did not establish stable direct transfer. Taken together, the results support a bounded conclusion: the evaluated complete path improved coherent instance-set construction from fixed Mask2Former candidates in specified native-instance contracts, while stable cross-domain transfer and component-specific effects remain unestablished.

</details>


### [27] [KANResDiff: Learning Local Residual Diffusion via Kolmogorov-Arnold Network for Ambiguous Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11617)
*Fanding Li,Chenglin Wang,Xiangyu Li,Xingyu Qiu,Xinghua Ma,Xiangming Yin,Haiyang Li,Suyu Dong,Wei Wang,Kuanquan Wang,Gongning Luo,Shuo Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: KANResDiff introduces a local residual diffusion framework with Kolmogorov-Arnold Network for ambiguous medical image segmentation, featuring Independent Time Encoding with spline-based time embeddings and a Residual Schrödinger Bridge for learnable deterministic priors, achieving state-of-the-art on GED and HM-IoU and competitive MDM results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ambiguous segmentation methods inject stochasticity in fixed, predefined ways, hindering a progressive semantic modeling of segmentation hypotheses. There is a need for diverse yet plausible segmentations tied to evolving semantic roles across inference stages.

Method: The approach uses local residual diffusion governed by a Kolmogorov-Arnold Network. Key components include Independent Time Encoding (spline-based time embeddings that enhance stage independence) and a Residual Schrödinger Bridge that infuses learnable, deterministic priors via a local Schrödinger Bridge to enable flexible deterministic-stochastic interactions and stage-aware ambiguity modeling.

Result: Empirical evaluation on two public datasets shows state-of-the-art performance on GED and HM-IoU (max gains of 16.8% and 7.7%), with competitive results on MDM.

Conclusion: KANResDiff effectively enables diverse but plausible segmentation hypotheses with progressive semantic roles across stages, and code is available at the linked repository.

Abstract: Ambiguous medical image segmentation aims to provide a series of diverse but plausible segmentation hypotheses. However, existing methods introduce stochasticity in a fixed and pre-defined manner, failing to form a progressive semantic modeling process. To address these challenges, we propose KANResDiff to learn local residual diffusion with Kolmogorov-Arnold Network, thereby assigning distinct roles across stages for ambiguity modeling. Specifically, we propose Independent Time Encoding that offers spline-based time embeddings instead of linear ones from MLPs, which enhances the independence across inference stages and assigns progressive semantic roles to different stages. We propose Residual Schrodinger Bridge that injects deterministic residual prior with learnable weights by constructing local Schrodinger Bridge instead of following manually settings, achieving a flexible deterministic-stochastic interaction and stage-aware ambiguity modeling thanks to local optimal diffusion path. Extensive experimental results on two public datasets demonstrate that KANResDiff achieves SOTA performance on GED and HM-IoU, with maximum improvements of 16.8% and 7.7%, respectively, while maintaining competitive performance on the MDM metric. Source code is available at https://github.com/PerceptionComputingLab/KANResDiff.

</details>


### [28] [Generative Video Compression Based on Hierarchical Referencing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11618)
*Daowen Li,Ding Ding,Zifu Zhang,Kai Li,Ying Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GVCHR introduces a hierarchical latent-reference framework for diffusion-based video compression, pairing hierarchical reference/quality structures with temporal context mining and a hierarchical attentive adapter in a video diffusion transformer to curb denoising-induced artifacts, achieving substantial BD-rate reductions and improved visual quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion-based video compression currently lacks a principled reference and quality structure for latent coding, and suffers from artifact propagation during denoising due to frame-level quality variation. A hierarchical scheme could improve coding efficiency and reconstruction quality by prioritizing influential references and constraining information flow during denoising.

Method: 1) Latent coding with a hierarchical reference structure and a hierarchical quality structure, allocating more bits to lower-layer frames that are reused as references. 2) Hierarchical Temporal Context Mining to exploit complementary short- and long-term temporal context for latent coding. 3) Generative reconstruction with a Hierarchical Attentive Adapter integrated into a video diffusion transformer, using hierarchical attention so each latent frame attends only to same- or lower-layer references, reducing artifact propagation.

Result: Evaluations on multiple benchmarks show GVCHR achieving substantial BD-rate reductions: approximately 50.5% on LPIPS and 54.0% on DISTS, compared with the previous state-of-the-art, along with visibly improved perceptual quality.

Conclusion: Hierarchical referencing and attention-guided denoising effectively improve coding efficiency and robustness for diffusion-based video compression, outperforming prior methods across multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: Diffusion-based generative video compression has emerged as a promising paradigm to improve perceptual quality, where latent frames are required to be encoded efficiently while serving as denoising conditions. However, existing methods neither carefully design reference and quality structures during latent coding nor account for the impact of frame-level quality variation on denoising procedure, which limits coding efficiency and aggravates artifact propagation during generative reconstruction. In this paper, we propose GVCHR, Generative Video Compression based on Hierarchical Referencing. The key idea is to organize latent frames hierarchically, where the selected high-quality references benefit both latent coding and generative reconstruction. In latent coding, GVCHR couples a hierarchical reference structure with a hierarchical quality structure, assigning more bits to lower-layer frames that are reused more frequently as references. Built on this design, we introduce Hierarchical Temporal Context Mining to exploits complementary short- and long-term temporal context for effective latent coding. In generative reconstruction, the coding-side hierarchy is incorporated into a Hierarchical Attentive Adapter which is attached to a video diffusion transformer. This adapter uses hierarchical attention to restrict each latent frame to attend only to the same- or lower-layer references, thereby reducing artifact propagation during denoising. Experiments validate GVCHR on multiple benchmarks. Compared with the previous state-of-the-art method, GVCHR achieves 50.5% and 54.0% BD-rate gains in terms of LPIPS and DISTS, respectively, while also delivering clearly improved visual quality.

</details>


### [29] [CAM-Guided Saliency Cutout and Image-Based Malware Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11634)
*Yasaman Ebrahimi,Martin Jurecek,Mark Stamp*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Saliency-guided cutouts via HiResCAM show domain-dependent benefits; malware images do not benefit from saliency-guided cutouts, while CIFAR-100 shows slight gains with low-saliency cutouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether HiResCAM-based saliency-guided cutout placement improves CNN regularization compared to random cutouts, and whether benefits transfer from natural to malware images.

Method: Four training conditions (no cutout, random cutout, low-saliency, high-saliency); datasets: RawMal-TF grayscale malware (~1k samples per of 17 families) and CIFAR-100; model: ResNet18; cutout sizes: 5%, 10%, 20%, 30%; M in {4,8} augmented copies per image; ~100 epochs.

Result: RawMal-TF: all cutout variants perform worse than no cutout. CIFAR-100: low-saliency cutout yields a small improvement; other cutout variants show little to no improvement.

Conclusion: The value of saliency-guided cutout is domain-dependent; malware images are not equivalent to natural images; therefore, saliency-guided data augmentation may not generalize across domains.

Abstract: Dropout regularization is commonly used to reduce overfitting by removing parts of a neural network during training. For Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN), cutouts serve a somewhat analogous purpose. Cutouts can be implemented as data augmentation: the original training image is retained, and additional copies are created with regions removed. In this chapter, we test whether cutout placement can be improved by using High-Resolution Class Activation Mapping (HiResCAM). We compare four controlled training conditions: no cutout, standard random cutout, low-saliency cutout, and high-saliency cutout. We experiment using grayscale malware images from the RawMal-TF dataset (17 families with~1,000 samples per family), and for comparison to natural images, we experiment with the well-known CIFAR-100 dataset. All experiments are based on ResNet18 with~100 training epochs. For the cutout experiments, we test cutout areas of~5\%, 10\%, 20\%, and~30\%, and we consider~$M\in\{4,8}$ augmented copies per original training image. The RawMal-TF results are slightly worse for all three cutout cases (random, high and low saliency) as compared to no cutouts. In contrast, our CIFAR-100 experimental results improve slightly under low-saliency cutout. These results suggest that the value of saliency-guided cutout is domain dependent, and that malware images should not be treated as equivalent to natural images.

</details>


### [30] [Robustness of AI-Art Detectors under Generator Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11643)
*Shivank Singh Thakur,Meien Li,Mark Stamp*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Cross-generator evaluation reveals a robustness gap in AI-art detectors under Stable Diffusion 3.5m; CLIP ViT-L/14 shows the strongest robustness among five U-Net LD detectors; in-distribution detection is strong, but cross-generator shifts cause many AI-art images to be misclassified as human, with low human false positives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess robustness of AI-art detectors to generator shifts across evolving diffusion architectures and to address concerns about copyright, misinformation, and authenticity in AI-generated content; evaluate cross-generator generalization using a diverse SD3.5m artwork dataset.

Method: Train five detectors on U-Net-based latent diffusion artwork. Evaluate zero-shot cross-generator performance on the SD3.5m dataset spanning ten art styles using reverse prompting of held-out human samples. Perform Grad-CAM analysis to study activation patterns.

Result: Detectors perform well in-distribution but degrade under generator shift, yielding many false negatives (AI-art labeled as human) while human false positives remain low. CLIP ViT-L/14 emerges as the best-performing model overall. Grad-CAM reveals weaker, more diffuse activations for false negatives.

Conclusion: There is a generalization gap in current AI-art detectors under cross-generator shifts. Detectors should be deployed as part of a layered defense to maintain reliability as generative architectures evolve.

Abstract: Text-to-image generative models have advanced rapidly, with modern Diffusion Transformer architectures producing images that are increasingly difficult to distinguish from human-created artwork. This development has raised significant concerns regarding copyright protection, misinformation, fraud, impersonation, and the authenticity of digital content. Most AI-art detectors are trained and evaluated on the same generator family, leaving robustness to newer architectures underexplored. In this chapter, we analyze generator shift based on a Stable Diffusion 3.5 Medium (SD3.5m) artwork dataset spanning ten art styles through reverse prompting of held-out human artwork samples. Five detectors are trained on U-Net-based latent diffusion artwork and evaluated in a zero-shot cross-generator setting on the SD3.5m dataset. Deep learning models perform strongly in-distribution but degrade under generator shift, misclassifying many SD3.5m images as human while human false positives remain low. The CLIP ViT-L/14 model performs best overall, while Grad-CAM analysis reveals weaker and more diffuse activation on false negatives. These findings highlight a generalization gap in current AI-art detectors and motivate the development of detectors as one component of a layered defense that remains reliable across rapidly evolving generative architectures.

</details>


### [31] [Cloak of Invisibility: Real-Time Privacy-Preserving Volumetric Video Streaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11645)
*Hossein Khalili,Philip Do,Alexander Vilesov,Kittipat Apicharttrisorn,Nader Sehatbakhsh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: InViStream provides a real-time privacy-from-source solution for multi-view RGB-D volumetric streaming by depth-aware masking, cross-view consistency, and sanitized fusion, achieving strong synthetic and real-world metrics (>30 FPS).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Volumetric video privacy is a multi-view 3D problem: naive per-frame redaction fails under RGB-D fusion; existing privacy methods are single-camera and do not address calibrated multi-view fusion. There is a need to remove private content at capture while preserving public scene information for real-time 3D reconstruction.

Method: Integrates object detection with depth-aware masking, propagates public/private decisions across calibrated views, and fuses only sanitized point clouds to produce privacy-preserving volumetric data.

Result: Evaluations on synthetic and real RGB-D scenes show Dice 0.799/0.891 and 0.792/0.908 (synthetic/real) with SSIM > 0.98 (synthetic) and real-time >30 FPS.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasible, real-time privacy-preserving volumetric streaming; effective cross-view privacy consistency; potential limitations include detector accuracy, domain shift, and handling of edge cases, suggesting directions for robustness and broader privacy guarantees.

Abstract: Volumetric video streaming turns privacy into a 3D, multi-view problem. Unlike ordinary video, where sensitive content can often be redacted frame by frame, RGB-D volumetric pipelines capture people, rooms, and personal objects from multiple cameras and fuse them into a shared 3D representation. A private object missed in one view, or only partially removed before fusion, can therefore reappear in the reconstructed scene. This creates a privacy challenge for 3D telepresence, education, entertainment, and immersive applications: private content should be removed before raw visual and geometric data leave the camera side, while the public part of the scene should remain useful for real-time reconstruction. Existing volumetric streaming systems mainly optimize reconstruction, data movement, and latency, while privacy-preserving vision methods are designed for single-camera, single-frame images and do not directly address calibrated multi-view RGB-D fusion. We present InViStream, a real-time "privacy-from-source" system designed for this setting. InViStream addresses three challenges in volumetric capture: private objects may appear differently across views, RGB masking alone can leave geometric privacy leakage in depth, and public/private instances of the same class must be separated consistently before cloud-side fusion. To address these challenges, InViStream combines object detection with depth-aware masking, propagates public/private decisions across calibrated views, and fuses only sanitized point clouds. We evaluate InViStream on synthetic and real RGB-D scenes, including offices, conference rooms, living rooms, and settings with multiple public and private people and objects. InViStream achieves synthetic Dice/Recall of 0.799/0.891 and real Dice/Recall of 0.792/0.908, with synthetic SSIM above 0.98 and real-time streaming above 30 FPS.

</details>


### [32] [Hybrid-LUT: Channel-Aware Hybrid Lookup Table and Filtering for Efficient Image Denoising](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11646)
*Zhilin Ai,Boyu Li,Sidi Yang,Wenqing Shi,Wenyong Zhou,Binxiao Huang,Chenchen Ding,Ngai Wong*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Hybrid-LUT reduces LUT storage by 66% by using a Y-channel multi-band LUT with pixel-level weight fusion and lightweight UV filtering, maintaining throughput and delivering state-of-the-art denoising performance on benchmarks with only 421 KB storage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RGB-LUT approaches require three identical LUTs for RGB channels, leading to high on-chip SRAM consumption; naive denoising in YUV by using only the luminance channel causes color distortion and residual artifacts.

Method: An asymmetric YUV-based framework, Hybrid-LUT: a multi-band LUT branch with pixel-level weight fusion processes the Y channel to recover fine textures, while lightweight filtering handles the UV channels to preserve color consistency; overall design reduces LUT storage and maintains runtime throughput.

Result: State-of-the-art performance across multiple benchmarks; storage limited to 421 KB; surpasses existing LUT-based denoising methods by at least 0.63 dB CPSNR on real-world datasets.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the effectiveness of Hybrid-LUT for image denoising on resource-constrained edge devices; open-source implementation available at the project link.

Abstract: Lookup table (LUT)-based image denoising methods have attracted increasing attention due to their high efficiency and hardware-friendly properties. However, existing RGB-LUT approaches require three identical LUTs to process RGB channels in parallel, resulting in large on-chip SRAM consumption. A simple alternative is to apply LUT processing only to the luminance (Y) channel in the YUV color space to reduce memory usage. However, this naive strategy leads to degraded restoration quality, since ignoring the chrominance (UV) channels introduces color distortion and residual artifacts. In this work, we propose Hybrid-LUT, a YUV-based asymmetric channel-processing framework that combines LUT and filtering in a unified design. Specifically, a multi-band LUT branch with pixel-level weight fusion is applied to the Y channel to recover fine textures, while lightweight filtering is used for the UV channels to maintain color consistency. This design reduces LUT storage by two-thirds compared with RGB-LUT methods while maintaining the same runtime throughput. Extensive experiments show that Hybrid-LUT achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance across multiple benchmarks with only 421 KB of storage. In particular, our method surpasses existing LUT-based denoising approaches by at least 0.63 dB CPSNR on real-world datasets, demonstrating its effectiveness for image denoising on resource-constrained edge devices. The project is available at https://github.com/Ai-ZL/Hybrid-LUT .

</details>


### [33] [Motion-as-Prompt: Enhancing Motion Reasoning in Multimodal Large Language Models via Motion-Guided Cross-Frame Visual Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11655)
*Xikai Sun,Kebin Liu,Haotian Wang,Li Liu,Xu Wang,Yunhao Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MaP is a track-guided cross-frame visual prompting method that recovers dense point trajectories and overlays motion cues on video inputs to enable frozen MLLMs to perform motion-centric reasoning. It improves motion reasoning on CLEVRER and Something-Something-v2 without any model training or architecture changes, achieving +4.2% and +8.9% respectively (GPT-5.5), and maintains non-motion understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparse uniform sampling in multimodal LLMs often omits critical frame-to-frame transitions, hindering motion understanding such as object motion, collisions, and causal interactions.

Method: Recover dense point trajectories; select motion-informative frames; accumulate and mark trajectories between sampled frames directly on visual inputs to reveal displacement and interactions. Apply as prompting to frozen MLLMs without training or architectural changes.

Result: Consistent improvements in average motion-reasoning accuracy: +4.2% on CLEVRER and +8.9% on Something-Something-v2 with GPT-5.5. Robust to non-motion tasks, with no degradation observed.

Conclusion: MaP offers a simple, effective, and model-free prompting solution for motion-centric video reasoning that does not require retraining or architectural modification; demonstrated on two benchmarks with a project page provided.

Abstract: Motion-centric video reasoning is fundamental to interactive applications such as robotic manipulation and autonomous navigation. However, multimodal large language models (MLLMs) typically process videos through sparse uniform sampling to control visual-token and attention costs. This strategy may discard critical transitions between sampled frames, limiting reasoning about object movement, collisions, and causal interactions. To mitigate this issue, we propose Motion-as-Prompt (MaP), a track-guided cross-frame visual prompting framework. MaP recovers dense point trajectories, selects motion-informative frames, and marks the trajectories accumulated between consecutive sampled frames directly onto the visual inputs, making otherwise hidden displacement, direction changes, and interactions observable to frozen MLLMs. Experiments on CLEVRER and Something-Something-v2 show that MaP consistently improves average motion-reasoning accuracy, yielding gains of 4.2% and 8.9% for GPT-5.5, respectively. Notably, these improvements are obtained without degrading non-motion understanding, highlighting the robustness of MaP. These results demonstrate that MaP provides a simple and effective solution for enhancing motion-centric video reasoning without model training or architectural modification. Project page:https://github.com/SunVictor23/MaP.

</details>


### [34] [Zero-OVCD: Bridging Training-Free Foundation Models and Pseudo-Label Learning for Open-Vocabulary Change Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11663)
*Daifeng Peng,Yuanke Peng,Haiyan Guan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Two-stage, training-free open-vocabulary change detection (Zero-OVCD) that generates pseudo-labels from foundation-model cues to train a detector, with noise-robust selection and refinement; yields strong gains across several datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-vocabulary change detection aims to identify user-specified land-cover changes without pixel-level annotations. Existing training-free pipelines struggle with noisy candidate masks, ambiguous semantic assignments, and accumulating errors, motivating a method that produces high-quality pseudo-labels and robust learning from them.

Method: Stage I generates high-quality change pseudo-labels via complementary candidate-mask refinement, multiscale semantic similarity fusion with margin-based reliability filtering, and response-guided mask correction/completion to suppress noise and recover missed changes. Stage II trains a change detector on these pseudo-labels, employing checkpoint voting and high-agreement sample selection to mitigate residual pseudo-label noise. Evaluation on LEVIR-CD, WHU-CD, S2Looking, and SECOND reports Stage I F1s of 86.25%, 85.82%, 50.48% and Stage II F1s of 88.65%, 88.85%, 57.96% respectively, with macro-average F1 on SECOND rising from 47.91% to 50.92%.

Result: Stage I produces high-quality pseudo-labels by refining candidate masks and fusing semantic similarities across scales with reliability filtering and mask correction; Stage II trains a detector with those pseudo-labels and uses checkpoint voting and high-agreement sampling to reduce label noise. Quantitative results show consistent gains across multiple datasets, including notable improvements on SECOND (macro-average F1 from 47.91% to 50.92%).

Conclusion: Bridging training-free foundation-model inference with noise-aware pseudo-label learning enables effective open-vocabulary change detection without target-domain pixel-level annotations.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary change detection (OVCD) enables the identification of user-specified land-cover changes in bitemporal remote sensing images, but existing training-free pipelines remain vulnerable to inaccurate candidate masks, ambiguous semantic assignments, and accumulated inference errors. To address these issues, we propose Zero-OVCD, a two-stage framework that requires no pixel-level annotations from the target domain. In the first stage, high-quality change pseudo-labels are generated through complementary candidate-mask refinement, multiscale semantic similarity fusion with margin-based reliability filtering, and response-guided mask correction and completion. These components jointly suppress noisy candidates, enhance mask-level semantic discrimination, and recover missed change regions. In the second stage, a change detector is trained using the generated pseudo-labels, while checkpoint voting and high-agreement sample selection are introduced to mitigate residual pseudo-label noise. On LEVIR-CD, WHU-CD, and S2Looking, Stage I achieves F1 scores of 86.25%, 85.82%, and 50.48%, while Stage II further improves them to 88.65%, 88.85%, and 57.96%, respectively. On SECOND, the macro-average F1 across six category-wise one-vs-rest tasks increases from 47.91% to 50.92%. These results demonstrate that bridging training-free foundation-model inference with noise-aware pseudo-label learning provides an effective solution for open-vocabulary change detection without target-domain pixel-level annotations. Code will be available at https://github.com/1321663019/Zero-OVCD.

</details>


### [35] [Learning from Multimodal Pseudo-Labels for Robust Open-Vocabulary Instance and Panoptic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11681)
*Duy Tran Thanh,Yeejin Lee,Byeongkeun Kang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A multimodal framework for open-vocabulary instance segmentation (OVIS) and open-set panoptic segmentation (OSPS) that uses Grounded SAM, LLaVA, and CLIP to generate pseudo-labels (masks, captions, synonyms) and GPT-based caption reconstruction; introduces three training losses to improve visual-textual alignment; shows state-of-the-art gains on COCO with improvements on OVIS/OSPS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-vocabulary segmentation suffers from noisy pseudo-labels, weak grounding, and OOV synonyms; need automatic, robust supervision using pretrained vision-language models to cover unseen categories without manual annotation.

Method: Target-vocabulary-assisted pseudo-labeling: generate pseudo masks, captions, and synonym sets via Grounded SAM, LLaVA, CLIP; filter synonyms with CLIP-guided method; reconstruct captions with GPT; train with extended grounding loss incorporating synonyms, semantic consistency loss, and generative caption reconstruction loss.

Result: Experiments on COCO show the method consistently surpasses prior SOTA under the proposed protocol, with substantial gains on OVIS and OSPS benchmarks.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that multimodal supervision and vocabulary-aware alignment can effectively address open-vocabulary segmentation without manual masks, enabling better grounding and captioning.

Abstract: This work addresses the challenge of open-vocabulary instance segmentation (OVIS) and open-set panoptic segmentation (OSPS), which aim to recognize both predefined and unseen object categories without exhaustive human annotations. Existing methods often suffer from noisy pseudo-masks, limited visual-textual grounding, and difficulty handling synonyms or out-of-vocabulary (OOV) words. To overcome these challenges, we propose a multimodal framework that leverages pre-trained vision-language models for automatic pseudo-label generation, CLIP-guided synonym filtering, and GPT-based caption reconstruction. In our target-vocabulary-assisted pseudo-labeling setting, the framework first constructs pseudo segmentation masks, descriptive captions, and semantically aligned synonym sets using Grounded SAM, LLaVA, and CLIP, providing multimodal supervision without manual annotation. We then enhance visual-textual alignment through three complementary training objectives: an extended grounding loss that incorporates visually grounded synonyms, a semantic consistency loss, and a generative caption reconstruction loss. Extensive experiments on the COCO dataset demonstrate that the proposed method consistently outperforms previous state-of-the-art approaches under this protocol, achieving substantial improvements on both OVIS and OSPS benchmarks.

</details>


### [36] [EGM-Det: Entropy-Guided Multimodal Adaptive Fusion for UAV RGB-IR Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11685)
*Cunzheng Fan,Dawei Yan,Guanlin Wang,Xingshuo Yang,Yupeng Jia,Jing Yang,Haokui Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Entropy-guided adaptive fusion (EGM-Det) for RGB-IR UAV object detection using a dual-stream with entropy-based gating and cross-modal distillation, achieving state-of-the-art results (notably >10pp on VEDAI).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address spatially varying modality reliability in multimodal fusion for UAV-object detection, where fixed fusion weights underutilize reliable cues in RGB/IR data.

Method: A dual-stream network with an Entropy Offset Gate Fusion module that derives shallow entropy priors from input intensity, local entropy, and cross-modal discrepancy to guide local offset alignment and spatial-channel gated fusion; includes cross-modal distillation with a teacher-student setup and entropy-adaptive supervision.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on DroneVehicle, LLVIP, and VEDAI; improves over prior methods by more than 10 percentage points on VEDAI.

Conclusion: Entropy-guided adaptive fusion improves reliable multimodal fusion for UAV detection; cross-modal distillation regularizes fusion gates to reduce degradation.

Abstract: Joint use of RGB and infrared (IR) imagery can improve UAV-view object detection, but most existing methods fuse multimodal features with static or fixed weights and therefore overlook spatially varying modality reliability. We propose EGM-Det, an entropy-guided multimodal adaptive fusion framework for RGB-IR object detection. EGM-Det employs a dual-stream architecture to preserve modality-specific representations and introduces an Entropy Offset Gate Fusion module for adaptive multi-scale fusion. The module derives shallow entropy priors from input intensity, local entropy, and cross-modal discrepancy, and uses them to guide local offset alignment and spatial-channel gated fusion. It therefore selectively aggregates reliable RGB and infrared cues instead of uniformly combining heterogeneous features. We further introduce cross-modal distillation to regularize the learned fusion gates and reduce fusion degradation. Each student branch extracts complementary knowledge from the cross-modality teacher branch matched to the main branch, while entropy-adaptive supervision emphasizes uncertain modality decisions. Experiments on DroneVehicle, LLVIP, and VEDAI demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across all three benchmarks; in particular, EGM-Det outperforms prior approaches by more than 10 percentage points on VEDAI.

</details>


### [37] [Boundary-Enhanced Segmentation of Pig Point Clouds in Commercial Housing Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11697)
*Zhankang Xu,Fei Shi,Xiangyu Qi,Zhaoyang Wang,Mengxin Guo,Yikai Fan,Simon X. Yang,Qifeng Li,Weihong Ma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A boundary-aware pig point cloud segmentation method using an Octree Transformer backbone with boundary supervision and dual boundary-semantic interaction improves segmentation accuracy and reduces boundary adhesion for downstream livestock tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real pigsty point clouds often blur boundaries due to proximity to background structures, causing mis-segmentation that harms downstream tasks like completion and body size measurement.

Method: Backbone is Octree Transformer; combines octree convolution, self-attention, and multi-scale fusion to fuse local geometry with global context. Introduces soft-distance boundary pseudo-labels for continuous boundary supervision and a bidirectional cross-boundary semantic module for interaction between boundary and semantic features.

Result: Experiments on a comprehensive pig dataset show superior performance over state-of-the-art models in segmentation accuracy, mean IoU, and boundary delineation, indicating reduced boundary adhesion and more reliable inputs for downstream tasks.

Conclusion: Boundary-feature-aware segmentation framework with boundary supervision is effective for pig point clouds in real environments and benefits precision livestock farming workflows.

Abstract: In real pigsty environments, pig point clouds often come into close contact with background structures, resulting in blurred target boundaries, local adhesion, and background mis-segmentation. This reduces the accuracy of subsequent point cloud completion and body size measurement. To address these challenges, this study proposes a pig point cloud segmentation method based on boundary feature analysis. The proposed method adopts Octree Transformer as the backbone network and integrates local geometric details with global semantic context through octree convolution, self-attention encoding, and multi-scale feature fusion. Furthermore, soft-distance boundary pseudo-labels are generated to provide continuous boundary supervision, and a bidirectional cross-boundary semantic module is designed to enable explicit interaction between boundary and semantic features. Experiments conducted on a comprehensive dataset demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms various state-of-the-art models in terms of segmentation accuracy, mean intersection over union, and boundary delineation. The results indicate that the method effectively alleviates boundary adhesion, providing reliable point cloud inputs for downstream precision livestock farming tasks.

</details>


### [38] [STAR: A Spatial-Topology Aware Routing Framework for Generalizable 3D Scene Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11699)
*Mingwei Xing,Xinliang Wang,Yifeng Shi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: STAR introduces Spatial-Topology Aware Routing for unified 3D scene understanding across modalities using a Domain-Spatial-Guided Routing (DSR) and Entropy-controlled Dynamic Allocation (EDA) within a Mixture-of-Experts framework, aided by a multi-attribute self-supervised pre-training branch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological inconsistencies across sensor modalities hinder unified 3D understanding; conventional feature-only MoE routers underrepresent local sampling topology under semantic supervision, making expert allocation difficult when semantics and geometry clash.

Method: A multi-attribute self-supervised pre-training branch (topology and texture variations) anchors cross-domain priors; a domain-aware expert branch with DSR to capture local topology from spatial context and EDA to adapt the number of active experts based on routing uncertainty.

Result: Extensive experiments across indoor/outdoor tasks; STAR achieves 80.1% mIoU on ScanNet val and 77.2% mIoU on S3DIS, consistently improving strong baselines; code released.

Conclusion: STAR stabilizes cross-domain representations and enables adaptive expert allocation, yielding consistent gains in multimodal 3D scene understanding.

Abstract: Constructing a unified 3D scene understanding model has long been hindered by the topological discrepancies across sensor modalities. While applying the Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture is a flexible approach for multi-domain 3D understanding, we observe that conventional feature-only MoE routers may underrepresent local sampling topology under semantic supervision, making expert allocation difficult when semantic consistency coexists with geometric heterogeneity. To overcome this challenge, we propose STAR (Spatial-Topology Aware Routing Framework). Specifically, we introduce a multi-attribute self-supervised pre-training branch, covering topological and textural variations, to anchor cross-domain structural priors. Building upon this, we design a domain-aware expert branch with two mechanisms: Domain-Spatial-Guided Routing (DSR), which captures local topological variations from spatial context, and Entropy-controlled Dynamic Allocation (EDA), which adjusts the number of activated experts according to routing uncertainty. Together, these branches combine stable cross-domain representation learning with adaptive expert allocation. Extensive experiments across various tasks, encompassing both indoor and outdoor scenes, demonstrate the effectiveness of STAR. It achieves 80.1% mIoU on the ScanNet validation set and 77.2% mIoU on S3DIS, consistently improving over strong baselines. Code is available at our project page (https://xmw666.github.io/STAR/).

</details>


### [39] [Advancing MLLM-based UAV Image Understanding and Reasoning: A Benchmark and a Training-Free Multi-Agent System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11738)
*Haoyu Zhang,Shuoxun Zhang,Peng Ye,Lin Zhang,Jiakang Yuan,Shenghong Yi,Yuening Wang,Tao Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes UAVQA-Bench and UAV-MAS for unified evaluation and improved reasoning in MLLM-based UAV understanding; shows multi-agent, tool-augmented approach beating baselines on UAVQA-Bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address fragmentation in UAV understanding benchmarks and the need for unified, domain-specific evaluation of MLLMs in complex UAV tasks involving extreme scale, orientation, and density variations.

Method: Construct UAVQA-Bench with 1,500 QA pairs from 13 UAV datasets across 6 capability dimensions and 16 tasks in multiple-choice and grounding formats; systematically evaluate open/closed MLLMs and agent-based systems; identify failure modes; propose UAV-MAS with DSPE (tool routing), CAIR (refinement validation), DAAS (adaptive search) as a training-free system.

Result: UAV-MAS with a 32B open-source MLLM achieves 77.0% overall accuracy on UAVQA-Bench, surpassing Gemini 3 Pro by 4.0%; the 8B variant improves 8.7% over its base model.

Conclusion: Unified UAV-specific benchmark reveals three key failure modes—domain-toolset mismatch, unchecked error propagation, and static reasoning—and demonstrates that a training-free, multi-agent system with domain tools and adaptive search can significantly improve UAV understanding performance.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM)-based UAV aerial image understanding and reasoning is essential for aerial intelligence yet poses distinct challenges arising from extreme scale variation, arbitrary camera orientations, and high object density. Despite growing interest, existing evaluations remain fragmented across individual datasets and narrow tasks, leaving a critical gap in unified assessment of UAV understanding and reasoning capabilities. To fill this gap, we construct UAVQA-Bench, a benchmark of 1,500 human-annotated QA pairs drawn from 13 public UAV datasets, covering 6 capability dimensions and 16 tasks in both multiple-choice and visual grounding formats. Systematic evaluation of a broad range of open-source and closed-source MLLMs as well as agent-based systems on UAVQA-Bench identifies three key failure modes: domain-toolset mismatch, unchecked error propagation, and static reasoning. Motivated by these findings, we propose UAV-MAS, a training-free multi-agent system for MLLM-based UAV aerial image understanding and reasoning, comprising a Domain-Specific Perception Engine (DSPE) that routes queries to task-appropriate visual tools, a Context-Aware Iterative Refinement module (CAIR) that validates intermediate reasoning to curb error accumulation, and a Difficulty-Aware Adaptive Search mechanism (DAAS) that adjusts search depth to question difficulty. UAV-MAS with a 32B open-source MLLM achieves 77.0% overall accuracy on UAVQA-Bench, surpassing Gemini 3 Pro by 4.0\%, while the 8B variant improves 8.7\% over its base model.

</details>


### [40] [JieZi: A Large-Scale Expert-Audited Dataset and Benchmark for Ancient Chinese Character Exegesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11741)
*Ran Li,Huiguo He,Jiahuan Cao,Junle Liu,Hiuyi Cheng,Lianwen Jin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ACCE introduces a vision-language QA framework for scholarly exegesis of ancient Chinese characters, with two expert-curated datasets (JieZi-Dataset and JieZi-Bench) across four progressive levels; initial results show gaps in glyph/semantic/diachronic reasoning, with fine-tuning on JieZi-Dataset improving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI approaches target basic recognition/retrieval and lack datasets/benchmarks for scholarly exegesis in historical linguistics, hindering nuanced analysis of ancient scripts.

Method: Propose ACCE with four levels (basic ID, glyph-form analysis, meaning exegesis, diachronic evolution). Build JieZi-Dataset (~500K expert-verified QA pairs) using template-driven generation and source-text references with human verification; construct JieZi-Bench aligned with the exegesis process; evaluate multimodal LLMs and fine-tune on JieZi-Dataset.

Result: Models excel at basic character identification but struggle with glyph-level analysis, semantic reasoning, and diachronic understanding; fine-tuning on JieZi-Dataset yields substantial improvements across all four levels.

Conclusion: The datasets enable structured scholarly exegesis in a vision-language setting and improve model performance through targeted fine-tuning; resources and code are released for community use.

Abstract: The scholarly exegesis of ancient Chinese characters demands integrating visual observation, linguistic analysis, and historical context. However, existing computational approaches focus narrowly on subtasks such as character recognition and retrieval, lacking the structured datasets and benchmarks required for comprehensive scholarly analysis. To address this limitation, we introduce Ancient Chinese Character Exegesis (ACCE), a vision-language question answering (VQA) task that models the scholarly exegesis process. ACCE is organized into four progressive levels: basic character identification, glyph-form analysis, meaning exegesis, and diachronic evolution analysis. To support this task, we construct two complementary resources. JieZi-Dataset is the first large-scale, expert-audited VQA training dataset for ACCE, comprising over 500K QA pairs. It is constructed via a pipeline that reduces factual errors by constraining generation with expert-designed templates and source-text references. Human verification is further applied at each key stage to ensure scholarly accuracy. JieZi-Bench is an evaluation benchmark aligned with the exegesis process, constructed and verified by human experts to ensure evaluation reliability. It consists of four levels with reference answers curated from authoritative lexicographic works held separate from the training data. Experiments on multimodal large language models show that current models perform well on basic identification but struggle with glyph analysis, semantic reasoning, and diachronic understanding. Fine-tuning on JieZi-Dataset substantially improves performance across all four levels. Code and dataset are available at https://github.com/Ran00w/JieZi.

</details>


### [41] [LiveAnimate: Stable Long-Form Streaming Human Animation in Real-Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11745)
*Yuxuan Zhang,Haozhong Xiong,Yubo Huang,Jiayi Song,Jinpeng Yu,Haofan Wang,Jiaming Liu,Ruihua Huang,Liwei Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LiveAnimate delivers real-time, long-form, high-quality pose-driven animation using a 14B video Diffusion Transformer with a novel PR-Sink cache and two-stage training, achieving ~19.6 FPS on H100s and stable ~3-minute identity preservation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable interactive applications (live streaming, telepresence, avatars) by bridging diffusion quality with streaming latency; ensure stable long-form generation at scale with bounded memory.

Method: Two-stage training: (1) Reference-Anchored Teacher-Forcing Adaptation converts a bidirectional DiT into a block-causal autoregressive generator; (2) Block-wise Self-Forcing Distillation reduces sampling to three steps. Pose-Retrieval Sink Attention (PR-Sink) uses a Static Sink anchoring the first block, a Dynamic Sink holding a pose-retrieved historical block, and a three-slot rolling window to maintain appearance context while keeping memory/latency constant. Ulysses sequence parallelism and operator fusion enable streaming inference on two NVIDIA H100 GPUs (≈19.63 FPS).

Result: On a three-minute benchmark, LiveAnimate maintains nearly constant perceptual quality and identity (IQA ~4.047 to 4.026). Compared with prior systems, it avoids substantial degradation or hours-long offline computation, delivering a new operational point in quality, latency, and duration for interactive full-body animation.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that a billion-parameter video diffusion model can support real-time, long-form, stable pose-driven animation with bounded memory and constant per-block latency, enabling interactive applications and setting a new standard for quality-latency-duration trade-offs.

Abstract: Pose-driven human animation synthesizes a video of a target person from a single reference image and a driving pose stream. Real-time generation is essential for interactive applications such as live streaming, telepresence, and virtual avatars, yet diffusion-based systems require minutes to hours per clip, precluding responsive interaction. We present LiveAnimate, to our knowledge the first animation system to combine real-time streaming with stable long-form generation at billion scale, built on a 14B-parameter video Diffusion Transformer (DiT). A two-stage training pipeline first adapts a pretrained bidirectional DiT into a block-causal autoregressive generator through Reference-Anchored Teacher-Forcing Adaptation, and then reduces the sampling budget to three steps through Block-wise Self-Forcing Distillation. To preserve appearance over extended streams, we introduce Pose-Retrieval Sink Attention (PR-Sink), a bounded KV-cache mechanism combining a Static Sink that permanently anchors the first generated block, a Dynamic Sink that holds a pose-retrieved historical block, and a three-slot Rolling Window. When a pose recurs, PR-Sink restores the relevant appearance context without retaining the entire sequence, so memory and per-block latency remain constant regardless of stream duration. Together with Ulysses sequence parallelism and operator fusion, these designs enable 19.63\,FPS streaming inference on two NVIDIA H100 GPUs. On a three-minute benchmark, LiveAnimate maintains nearly constant perceptual quality and identity from the first 30 seconds to the final minute (IQA 4.047 vs.\ 4.026), while prior systems degrade substantially or require hours of offline computation for the same rollout. These results establish a new operating point in quality, latency, and duration for interactive full-body animation.

</details>


### [42] [Making Every Step Count: Spatio-Temporal Information Allocation for Imaging Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11747)
*Yi Cao,Xiangyong Cao,Pei Liu,Yong-Jin Liu,Deyu Meng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces Spectrum-Adaptive Scheduling (SAS) and Measurement-Prioritized Attention (MPA) to improve training-free flow-based inverse solvers under a fixed number of function evaluations by balancing temporal exploration/refinement and spatial data-prior guidance, with plug-and-play applicability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow-based priors are strong for inverse problems but naive allocation of function evaluations over the flow steps and limited use of data priors in missing regions hinder performance. There is a need for training-free components that balance semantic exploration with detail refinement and propagate information into underconstrained regions.

Method: Two components: (1) Spectrum-Adaptive Scheduling (SAS) allocates available NFEs across flow time based on the degradation spectrum and logSNR geometry. (2) Measurement-Prioritized Attention (MPA) uses data-prior conflicts to steer information toward weakly constrained regions. Both are plug-and-play with existing solvers and do not require retraining.

Result: Experimental validation across standard image inverse problems (super-resolution, motion deblurring, inpainting) shows that SAS and MPA improve restoration quality when integrated into flow-based solvers, without extra retraining or additional flow evaluations, and can significantly enhance existing solvers.

Conclusion: SAS and MPA effectively address temporal-spatial allocation challenges in training-free flow-based inverse solvers, yielding improved semantic fidelity and structural detail under fixed NFEs and incomplete data.

Abstract: Flow-based generative models have emerged as powerful image priors for training-free inverse problem solving, capturing coherent semantics and fine-grained structure. Despite these strengths, existing flow-based inverse solvers primarily focus on the design of individual updates, largely overlooking spatio-temporal information allocation under a fixed number of function evaluations (NFEs). Temporally, insufficient early exploration can trap the flow trajectory in an incorrect semantic basin, whereas excessive allocation of NFEs to early stages leaves little budget for late-stage refinement. Spatially, data consistency provides direct constraints only within observed regions, whereas the recovery of missing regions relies mainly on the generative prior. To address these two issues, we introduce two complementary and training-free components, i.e., Spectrum-Adaptive Scheduling (SAS) and Measurement-Prioritized Attention (MPA). For temporal allocation, SAS distributes the available NFEs over flow time according to the degradation spectrum and logSNR geometry, thus better balancing semantic exploration and detail refinement. For spatial propagation, MPA exploits data-prior conflicts to guide information toward weakly constrained regions, thereby enhancing semantic and structural fidelity. Extensive experiments on standard image inverse problems, e.g., super-resolution, motion deblurring, and inpainting, demonstrate that the proposed components can be integrated into existing flow-based inverse solvers in a plug-and-play manner without retraining or additional flow-model evaluations, and can also significantly improve the restoration quality of existing solvers.

</details>


### [43] [Dual Modality Prompted Diffusion Priors for Zero Shot Hyperspectral Pansharpening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11748)
*Pengwei Xie,Fei Zhu,Jiajun Li,Xiangyuan Liu,Xiangyuan Liu,Kangqing Shen,Gemine Vivone*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A diffusion-based hyperspectral pansharpening method (DIDM) uses dual modality prompts to condition a frozen diffusion model and introduces a panchromatic-guided weighted TV regularizer, achieving state-of-the-art results on multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable stronger integration between observed PAN/HSI data and the diffusion prior in hyperspectral pansharpening, addressing the weak coupling when priors are only used via external objectives.

Method: Encode LRHS and PAN as spectral and spatial prompt tokens, respectively, and inject them into intermediate features of a frozen remote sensing diffusion model via cross-attention. Additionally, apply a panchromatic-guided weighted pixel-aware TV regularizer combining degradation fidelity with PAN fidelity and gradient-adaptive structural regularization.

Result: DIDM achieves best performance across all evaluated metrics on Pavia, Chikusei, and Houston under reduced-resolution protocols; full-resolution evaluation on FR1 yields the highest HQNR among comparators.

Conclusion: Internal dual-modality prompting and PAN-guided regularization effectively balance spatial detail enhancement with spectral preservation in hyperspectral pansharpening.

Abstract: Hyperspectral pansharpening aims to reconstruct a high resolution hyperspectral (HRHS) image from a panchromatic (PAN) image and a low resolution hyperspectral (LRHS) image while preserving both spatial details and spectral fidelity. Recent diffusion based methods exploit pretrained image priors by generating a low dimensional representation and subsequently mapping it to the hyperspectral domain. However, the observed panchromatic and hyperspectral images are typically imposed only through external reconstruction objectives, limiting their direct interaction with the diffusion prior. To address this issue, we propose dual-modality image-prompted diffusion model (DIDM) for zero shot hyperspectral pansharpening. DIDM encodes the low resolution hyperspectral and panchromatic observations into spectral and spatial prompt tokens, respectively, and injects them into intermediate features of a frozen remote sensing diffusion model through cross attention, allowing complementary spectral and spatial information to directly guide diffusion feature evolution. In addition, we introduce a panchromatic guided weighted pixel aware total variation regularizer that combines low resolution hyperspectral degradation fidelity and panchromatic response fidelity with gradient adaptive structural regularization, thereby preserving structural discontinuities while suppressing spurious variations in homogeneous regions. Extensive experiments on Pavia, Chikusei, and Houston under reduced resolution protocols show that DIDM achieves the best performance across all evaluated metrics, while full resolution evaluation on FR1 yields the highest HQNR among the compared methods. These results demonstrate that internal dual modality prompting and panchromatic guided structural regularization provide an effective balance between spatial detail enhancement and spectral preservation.

</details>


### [44] [UniSwap: Streaming Audio-Visual Identity Swapping for Talking Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11752)
*Yuxuan Zhang,Haozhong Xiong,Jiayi Song,Jinpeng Yu,Yang Shi,Jiaming Liu,Ruihua Huang,Liwei Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UniSwap is a streaming, unified audio-visual identity replacement framework for talking videos that jointly transfers appearance and vocal timbre while preserving source motion and timing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the mismatch and synchronization challenges when audio and visual modalities are optimized separately, UniSwap provides a single framework for joint appearance and voice replacement in streaming talking videos.

Method: A bidirectional audio-visual diffusion backbone adapted via In-context Pretraining for joint replacement, Conditional Streaming Adaptation with block-causal KV caching for streaming generation, Efficient Self-forcing DMD to reduce sampling steps from 30 to 3, and Efficient Multi-LoRA Switching to share a frozen backbone across DMD roles; a swap-and-reconstruct training pipeline removes identity from real clips and uses original clips as reconstruction targets; Feature-RoPE Decomposition maintains cached positional information for stable long-form inference.

Result: Experiments demonstrate strong audio-visual synchronization, competitive identity preservation, efficient streaming, and stable long-form generation.

Conclusion: UniSwap demonstrates efficient, unified joint audio-visual identity replacement in streaming settings, leveraging innovative training/inference strategies and a unified diffusion framework.

Abstract: Talking-video character replacement requires coordinated transfer of appearance and voice while preserving the source motion, scene, linguistic content, and audio-video timing. Existing methods use separately optimized models for the two modalities, making audio-visual consistency difficult to enforce. We present UniSwap, the first framework for streaming joint audio-visual identity replacement in talking videos. Given a source video, a reference image, and a reference voice clip, UniSwap transfers the reference appearance and vocal timbre within a single audio-visual diffusion transformer while preserving the source content and dynamics. To address the scarcity of aligned cross-identity training pairs, we introduce a swap-and-reconstruct pipeline that removes visual and vocal identity from real clips and uses the original clips as reconstruction targets. Starting from a bidirectional backbone, we progressively adapt the model through In-context Pretraining for joint replacement, Conditional Streaming Adaptation for block-causal KV-cached generation, and Efficient Self-forcing DMD for mitigating exposure bias and reducing sampling from 30 to 3 denoising steps per block. Efficient Multi-LoRA Switching enables the three DMD roles to share a single frozen backbone. Feature-RoPE Decomposition keeps cached positions within the training range, supporting stable long-form inference. Experiments demonstrate strong audio-visual synchronization, competitive identity preservation, efficient streaming, and stable long-form generation.

</details>


### [45] [Automated binary classification of hazelnut X-ray images: A deep-learning benchmark for quality assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11759)
*Giancarlo Sportelli,Nicola Belcari,Roberta Pace,Umberto Bernardo,Sharmin Sultana,Alessandra Toncelli,Matteo Giaccone*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Benchmark for binary hazelnut quality classification using 799 X-ray images; multi-model and ensemble evaluation; multi-split protocol; expert reassessment improves performance; best ensemble (BCE-trained CNN + frozen Swin Transformer) ~86.3% balanced accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-destructive X-ray imaging reveals internal hazelnut defects but interpretation is challenging due to subtle radiographic differences, class imbalance, and limited annotated data; a robust evaluation framework is needed for small agricultural imaging datasets.

Method: Dataset: 799 segmented single-kernel X-ray images (224x224, grayscale), grouped into 101 acquisition units. Evaluated seven single-model configurations and ten probability-aggregation ensembles using a group-wise split-rotation protocol across five data splits with different random seeds. Thresholds chosen on validation; performance assessed deterministically on validation and test sets. Expert reassessment of ambiguous samples conducted.

Result: Best average-probability ensemble: binary cross-entropy-trained CNN + frozen Swin Transformer, achieving 86.3% balanced accuracy (±1.8%, five seeds). Other ensembles comparable. Substantial split-to-split variability observed; multi-split evaluation is essential. Expert reassessment improved performance of all 17 methods by 2.8–8.1 percentage points, with limited effect on cross-split variance.

Conclusion: Deep learning shows potential for automated X-ray hazelnut quality assessment; emphasizes rigorous evaluation and label curation in small, imbalanced agricultural imaging datasets.

Abstract: Non-destructive X-ray imaging can reveal internal hazelnut defects that are difficult to detect by external inspection alone; however, automated interpretation remains challenging because of subtle radiographic differences among classes, marked class imbalance, and limited annotated data. Here, we present a benchmark for binary hazelnut quality classification (healthy versus defective) based on 799 segmented single-kernel X-ray images (224 x 224 pixels, grayscale), grouped into 101 acquisition units. Seven single-model configurations and ten probability-aggregation ensembles were evaluated using a group-wise split-rotation protocol across five data splits generated using different random seeds. Decision thresholds were selected on the validation set, and performance was assessed deterministically on validation and test sets. Under the expert-reassessed annotation condition, the average-probability ensemble of the binary cross-entropy-trained convolutional neural network and frozen Swin Transformer achieved the highest mean balanced accuracy (86.3% +/- 1.8%, five seeds), with several other ensembles providing comparable performance. Across methods, substantial split-to-split variability was observed, indicating that multi-split evaluation is essential for reliable model comparison at this dataset scale. Expert reassessment of ambiguous samples improved the performance of all 17 evaluated methods by 2.8-8.1 percentage points, while having only a limited effect on cross-split variance. The results highlight both the potential of deep learning for automated X-ray-based hazelnut quality assessment and the importance of rigorous evaluation and label curation in small, imbalanced agricultural imaging datasets.

</details>


### [46] [ProBAG: Prototype-Guided Boundary-Aware Graph Diffusion for Weakly Supervised Histopathology Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11765)
*Duy-Dong Nguyen,Le-Van Thai,Hoai Nhan Pham,Ngoc Lam Quang Bui,Tam Tran,Zhi Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ProBAG is a stage-1 pseudo-mask generator for weakly supervised histopathology segmentation that fuses dataset-specific visual prototypes with pathology-aligned CONCH text prototypes over multi-scale frozen UNI features. It introduces class-wise power recalibration and one-step graph diffusion to produce high-quality pseudo-masks without CRF, improving two-stage segmentation when combined with a Phikon-FPN; pathology-aligned text semantics yield the largest gains, with graph refinement providing smaller added benefit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce labeling costs and improve localization/boundaries in WSSS for histopathology, where CAMs typically cover only discriminative regions and struggle at tissue interfaces.

Method: ProBAG generates stage-1 pseudo-masks by integrating dataset-specific visual prototypes with pathology-aligned CONCH text prototypes over multi-scale frozen UNI features. It applies (1) class-wise power recalibration to reshape inter-class competition while preserving per-pixel foreground mass, and (2) a one-step graph diffusion where feature affinities are penalized by late-transformer attention-context discrepancy as a soft boundary cue. These pseudo-masks obviate the need for CRF or external segmentation models; the full pipeline optionally trains a downstream Phikon-FPN segmenter in a two-stage setup.

Result: On BCSS-WSSS and LUAD-HistoSeg, ProBAG achieves consistent gains over recent WSSS methods. Ablations show that pathology-aligned text semantics contribute the largest improvement, while graph refinement provides a smaller complementary gain.

Conclusion: ProBAG provides effective stage-1 pseudo-masks for histopathology WSSS, enabling competitive two-stage segmentation without extra post-processing and highlighting the importance of pathology-aligned text semantics and graph-based refinement.

Abstract: Weakly supervised semantic segmentation enables histopathology tissue segmentation from image-level annotations, avoiding costly pixel-level labeling by expert pathologists. However, CAM-based methods often localize only highly discriminative regions and remain unreliable near tissue interfaces. We propose ProBAG, a stage-1 pseudo-mask generator that combines dataset-specific visual prototypes with pathology-aligned CONCH text prototypes over multi-scale frozen UNI features. ProBAG introduces two complementary mechanisms: class-wise power recalibration that reshapes inter-class competition while preserving the total foreground activation mass at each pixel, and one-step graph diffusion in which feature affinities are penalized by a late-transformer attention-context discrepancy used as a soft structural boundary cue. The resulting stage-1 pseudo-masks require neither CRF nor an external segmentation model; for complete two-stage comparison, they additionally supervise a downstream Phikon-FPN segmenter. Experiments on BCSS-WSSS and LUAD-HistoSeg show consistent gains over recent WSSS approaches, while ablations indicate that pathology-aligned text semantics provide the largest improvement and graph refinement provides a smaller complementary gain. The code is available at: https://github.com/wterrr/WSSS

</details>


### [47] [Achieving Near-Zero-Overhead Multi-Model Hierarchical Classification in Real-Time Detection Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11770)
*Vaishnav Raju*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Five-step zero-GPU-fallback INT8 deployment method for DLA on Jetson platforms, enabling concurrent GPU+DLA inference in edge detection-classification pipelines with backbone-agnostic compatibility and practical engineering guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge vision pipelines need real-time throughput with hierarchical stages, but running all models on GPUs creates bottlenecks. DLAs/NPUs offer parallelism, but deploying custom models is hard due to operator constraints, quantization gaps, and opaque end-to-end pipelines. The work targets NVIDIA Jetson DLA cores to enable practical, low-overhead deployment of classification backbones alongside GPU detectors.

Method: Five-step methodology: (1) architecture adaptation for DLA compatibility, (2) manual dynamic range workaround to rescue TensorRT implicit quantization (improves accuracy from 75% to 94%), (3) quantization-aware training, (4) ONNX graph surgery for DLA compilation, and (5) a concurrent GPU-detection/DLA-classification inference pipeline. The study documents nine engineering constraints with root-cause analysis and generalizable solutions; validates on a dual-head attribute classifier running on DLA with a GPU detector on Jetson Orin NX.

Result: Demonstrates near-zero pipeline overhead: 12.5 FPS (DLA+GPU pipeline) vs 13.3 FPS detector-only at 1080p; dual-DLA scaling incurs no extra cost. Backend-agnostic and generalizable to other detection-classification edge pipelines; 94% accuracy recovered from 75% implicit quantization baseline.

Conclusion: The methodology is backbone-agnostic and broadly generalizable to edge detection-classification pipelines, providing a practical blueprint for zero-GPU-fallback deployment of custom backbones on DLAs with end-to-end concurrency and quantization-aware validation.

Abstract: Edge-deployed vision systems in target recognition, surveillance, autonomous vehicles, and drone domains require hierarchical inference pipelines where a detection model identifies objects of interest and downstream classifiers provide fine-grained attribute analysis. Running all models on the GPU creates a serial bottleneck that limits real-time throughput as pipeline stages grow. Modern edge SoCs pair GPUs with dedicated neural accelerators (NPUs, DLAs) capable of concurrent execution, yet deploying custom models on these accelerators remains impractical due to strict operator constraints, quantization incompatibilities, and an undocumented end-to-end pipeline. We target NVIDIA Jetson DLA cores as the representative platform. We present a five-step methodology for zero GPU fallback DLA INT8 deployment of classification backbones, comprising architecture adaptation, manual dynamic range workaround to rescue TensorRT's implicit quantization (recovering 94.0% accuracy from implicit quantization's 75%) for rapid pipeline validation before explicit quantization, quantization-aware training, ONNX graph surgery for DLA compilation, and a concurrent GPU-detection/DLA-classification inference pipeline. We document nine engineering constraints with root-cause analysis and generalizable solutions. Validation on a dual-head person attribute classifier running on DLA alongside a GPU object detector on a Jetson Orin NX demonstrates near-zero pipeline overhead (12.5 vs. 13.3~FPS detector-only at 1080p), with dual-DLA scaling at no additional cost. The methodology is backbone-agnostic and generalizes to any detection-classification edge pipeline.

</details>


### [48] [VOLA: Improving Open-World Driving by VLM-Based Semantic Attribute Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11777)
*Yuchen Zhang,Yuan Gao,Sebastian Schmidt,Johannes Betz*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes dense, per-pixel action-relevant attributes (drivability and vulnerability) for driving scenes, using Qwen3.5 image-token features with a boundary-aware decoder, trained in CARLA and transferred to real/open-world scenes; outperforms vision-only and VLM baselines on open-world anomaly transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Move beyond object recognition to actionable scene understanding for safe driving in open-world environments where unseen obstacles may appear; require region-level interpretation of how scenes should affect motion and collision risk.

Method: Use 7-rank drivability and 5-rank vulnerability as dense attributes. Read Qwen3.5 image-token hidden states as spatial semantic representation. Apply a lightweight boundary-aware decoder to convert token grid into sharp full-resolution attribute maps. Train on dense attribute labels from CARLA; test transfer to real scenes and novel obstacles; no autoregressive text generation or external masks (e.g., SAM). Compare with vision-only segmenters trained on the same attributes and prompted VLM segmenters.

Result: Achieves 69.4% mean vulnerability-rank recall, outperforming strong vision-only baselines (57.1%) and prompted VLM baselines (53.9%). Demonstrates that VLM image tokens provide useful semantic cues for transferring driving attributes to unseen objects, with strong transfer to real open-world anomalies while maintaining performance on familiar categories.

Conclusion: Dense, action-oriented, pixel-level attributes can generalize better to open-world driving scenarios than conventional category-based segmentation. VLM token-based semantic information aids transfer to unseen obstacles, enabling safer and more robust scene understanding for autonomous driving.

Abstract: Driving in the real world is open-world: a car may encounter a fallen mattress, a deer, or other objects outside its training data. Naming them is not enough. The system must know how to treat each region: can it drive over it, and how severe would a collision be? We therefore shift scene perception from category labels to dense action-relevant attributes, where each pixel is labeled by how it should affect motion rather than by object name. We instantiate this general formulation with two ordered attributes: 7-rank drivability and 5-rank vulnerability. We read Qwen3.5 image-token hidden states directly as a spatial semantic representation. A lightweight boundary-aware decoder then turns this coarse token grid into sharp full-resolution attribute maps. The whole process requires neither autoregressive text generation nor an external mask model such as SAM. We train on dense attribute labels built in CARLA and test transfer to real scenes and to novel obstacles never seen in training. We compare with vision-only segmenters trained on the same attributes and prompted VLM segmenters. Our model matches strong vision-only segmenters on familiar categories and improves transfer to real open-world anomalies, reaching 69.4% mean vulnerability-rank recall versus 57.1% for the best vision-only baseline and 53.9% for the best prompted VLM baseline. These results show that VLM image tokens provide useful semantic cues for transferring driving attributes to objects outside the training vocabulary.

</details>


### [49] [Anti-Shortcut Distillation via Temporal Negative Knowledge Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11789)
*Syed Muhammad Raza,Omer Tariq,Jeongbae Son*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ASD (Anti-Shortcut Distillation) uses the teacher’s optimization trajectory to identify and suppress shortcut directions, via a temporal contrastive loss and a shortcut suppression loss, outperforming standard KD across multiple datasets and showing robust subspace alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KD often ignores the teacher’s own trajectory. Early features that a converged teacher suppresses encode shortcut directions. Exploit this signal to push the student away from such shortcuts.

Method: ASD employs two losses: (1) a temporal contrastive loss (L_tc) that treats the early-teacher features as negatives against the final-teacher features in an InfoNCE setup; (2) a shortcut suppression loss (L_ss) that penalizes the student for projecting onto the top eigenvectors of the uncentered second-moment matrix of early-to-final feature displacements D_h D_h^T.

Result: Across 13 teacher–student pairs on CIFAR-100, ImageNet-100, and TinyImageNet, ASD yields the highest clean top-1 accuracy in more than 10 pairs and outperforms standard KD in 12. It also achieves the lowest mean corruption error (86.1 mCE) on CIFAR-100-C for a cross-architecture pair (WRN-40-2 → ShuffleNet-V2). Mechanistic diagnostics show the ASD student is anti-aligned with the shortcut direction and has larger projection onto the robust subspace (0.45 vs 0.12).

Conclusion: ASD enforces a geometry where the student avoids shortcut directions learned by earlier teacher stages, while remaining robust by leveraging the subspace of stable features; this yields better generalization and corruption robustness than standard KD.

Abstract: Knowledge distillation (KD) trains a compact student by attracting it towards a converged teacher. It is silent about which directions the teacher itself learned to suppress: repulsive and bias-aware objectives exist, but none exploits the teacher's own trajectory to identify what the student should avoid. We observe that the missing signal is already encoded in the teacher's optimization trajectory: features that an early-stage teacher emphasizes but that a converged teacher attenuates are precisely the shortcut directions worth pushing the student away from. We instantiate this observation as \textbf{A}nti-\textbf{S}hortcut \textbf{D}istillation (ASD), a push--pull KD framework that treats the converged teacher $\Tfinal$ as a positive semantic anchor and an early-checkpoint teacher $\Tearly$ as a temporal negative reference. ASD couples two losses: a temporal contrastive loss ($\Ltc$) that places the early-teacher feature as a same-sample negative against in-batch and memory-bank final-teacher features in an InfoNCE objective; and a shortcut suppression loss ($\Lss$) that penalizes student projection onto the top eigenvectors of $\E[\Dh\Dh^{\top}]$, the uncentered second-moment matrix of early-to-final feature displacements. Across 13 teacher--student pairs on CIFAR-100, ImageNet-100, and TinyImageNet, ASD attains the highest clean top-1 accuracy on more than 10 pairs and outperforms standard KD on 12. On CIFAR-100-C corruption robustness, ASD obtains the lowest mean Corruption Error ($86.1$\,mCE) on the most challenging cross-architecture pair (WRN-40-2$\to$ShuffleNet-V2). Mechanistic diagnostics confirm the intended geometry: the ASD student is systematically anti-aligned with the shortcut direction, while its projection onto the robust subspace is substantially larger ($0.45$ vs.\ $0.12$).

</details>


### [50] [PolarSym: Polar Geometry-aware Attention for CAD Floorplan Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11793)
*Kerui Chen,Yiqing Wang,Kangzhou Xin,Qinghan Zhang,Songyang Ding*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PolarSym introduces a polar-coordinate geometry-aware attention mechanism for CAD plan parsing, decoupling direction and distance into separate branches and fusing them with a gating mechanism, yielding improved geometric modeling with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer-based CAD parsing relies on self-attention to capture global semantics but does not explicitly model intrinsic geometric symmetry of building layouts, leading to mismatches in long-range and symmetric configurations.

Method: PolarSym decouples geometric relations into two independent components: direction and distance. Directional constraints enforce structural consistency, while distance constraints address long-range symmetric correspondences. A dynamic gating mechanism fuses the two branches within a vanilla Transformer architecture, providing enhanced geometric modeling with negligible extra computation.

Result: On a public CAD plan parsing dataset, PolarSym surpasses the reproduced SymPoint V2 baseline by 1.73% PQ, 1.54% RQ, and 4.31% mIoU under identical training settings. It also converges faster and yields more stable optimization. Ablation studies show the two branches are complementary.

Conclusion: PolarSym improves the geometric awareness of Transformers for CAD plan parsing at low computational cost, offering an effective geometric modeling paradigm for CAD plan parsing.

Abstract: CAD plan parsing is a fundamental task in Building Information Modeling (BIM), aiming to automatically extract architectural elements including walls, doors, windows, and furniture from 2D engineering drawings.
