Existing Transformer-based methods capture global semantic dependencies via self-attention, yet they infer spatial relationships merely from semantic features without explicitly characterizing the intrinsic geometric symmetry of building layouts. Such methods tend to produce mismatched correspondences in long-range matching and complex symmetric spatial layouts. To tackle this limitation, we propose PolarSym, a polar-coordinate geometry-aware attention framework for CAD plan parsing. The framework decouples geometric relationships of buildings into two complementary components, direction and distance, which are modeled independently. Structural consistency is strengthened by directional constraints, while long-range symmetric correspondences are built with distance constraints. A dynamic gating mechanism is adopted to synergistically fuse the two geometric information branches while maintaining the vanilla Transformer architecture. This design boosts geometric modeling capacity with negligible extra computation. Experiments on a public CAD plan parsing dataset show that PolarSym surpasses the reproduced SymPoint V2 baseline by 1.73% PQ, 1.54% RQ and 4.31% mIoU under identical training settings. PolarSym also converges faster and yields more stable optimization. Ablation experiments verify the complementary effects of direction and distance modeling. Our results reveal that PolarSym improves the geometric awareness of Transformers at low computational cost, offering an effective geometric modeling paradigm for CAD plan parsing.

</details>


### [51] [CoDiR: Confidence-Guided Diffusion Refinement for Semi-Supervised Histopathology Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11807)
*Hoai Nhan Pham,Dang-Nguyen Bui,Le-Van Thai,Thanh-Hiep Vo,Lan Anh Dinh Thi,Tien Dat Nguyen,Duy-Dong Nguyen,Ngoc Lam Quang Bui,Tam Tran,Zhi Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CoDiR: a semi-supervised histopathology segmentation framework using confidence-guided diffusion refinement with a Mean Teacher, achieving competitive mDice with limited labels on GlaS/CRAG; refinement module provides substantial gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scarcity of annotations and unreliable pseudo-labels in ambiguous gland regions; improve segmentation accuracy with minimal labeling.

Method: Mean Teacher segmentation; teacher outputs soft predictions; a conditional diffusion model refines low-confidence regions using labeled data; refined mask fused with reliable teacher predictions; student trained with confidence weighting and consistency regularization.

Result: On GlaS and CRAG with 10% labeled data: 88.09% and 89.83% mDice; with 20% labeled: 89.19% and 90.29% mDice; matches or exceeds strongest published on seven of eight metrics; ablation shows refinement module adds +6.36% mDice over Mean Teacher; code available at GitHub.

Conclusion: Diffusion-based refinement for uncertain regions significantly boosts semi-supervised segmentation; CoDiR is competitive with reduced labeling; the refinement module is the largest contributor to performance gains.

Abstract: Semi-supervised histopathology segmentation is challenging due to scarce annotations and unreliable pseudo-labels in ambiguous gland regions. To address this problem, we propose Confidence-Guided Diffusion Refinement (CoDiR), a semi-supervised framework that combines a Mean Teacher segmentation model with diffusion-based pseudo-label refinement. Given an unlabeled image, the teacher first produces a soft prediction, and only low-confidence regions are refined by a conditional diffusion model trained to capture plausible mask structures from labeled data. The refined mask is then fused with reliable teacher predictions and used to train the student with confidence weighting and consistency regularization. On the GlaS and CRAG datasets CoDiR reaches 88.09\% and 89.83\% mDice with 10\% labeled data, and 89.19\% and 90.29\% mDice with 20\%, matching or exceeding the strongest published method on seven of the eight benchmark metrics. Ablations attribute the largest single contribution to the refinement module, which adds +6.36\% mDice over the Mean Teacher baseline. The implementation code is publicly available at: https://github.com/vongla345/codir

</details>


### [52] [Can Vision Models Read the Radar Display? On the Feasibility of Radar Imagery for Air Traffic Complexity Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11810)
*Hyewook Kim,Byul Kang,Seokbin Yoon,Keumjin Lee*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Vision Transformer can model air-traffic complexity from sparse radar imagery by encoding traffic as a position image plus aircraft state channels; it achieves high fit (R^2 > 0.96) on complexity metrics and perturbation analysis shows sensitivity aligns with each aircraft's contribution, suggesting radar visuals are viable for complexity modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether radar-based imagery, which is sparse and self-similar, is a viable input for deep learning models to predict controller-perceived traffic complexity and to understand model interpretability with respect to individual aircraft.

Method: Represent each traffic scenario as a position image with five channels for aircraft state variables (heading, speed, altitude). Train a Vision Transformer (ViT) to regress four intrinsic complexity components derived from pairwise geometric relations among aircraft. Evaluate via R^2 on components and conduct a one-aircraft-removal perturbation study to assess attribution of complexity changes.

Result: The ViT achieves R^2 > 0.96 on all four complexity components. In the perturbation study, removing a single aircraft changes the output in proportion to that aircraft’s contribution to sector complexity, not uniformly.

Conclusion: Radar imagery, despite sparsity and self-similarity, is a viable input format for air traffic complexity modeling, and a ViT can capture operationally meaningful complexity variations from such data.

Abstract: Air traffic controllers perceive traffic complexity through the radar display, suggesting that a computer vision model operating on the same imagery may provide a natural architecture for modeling controller-perceived complexity; however, whether radar imagery is a viable input format for deep learning vision models remains unclear. Unlike natural images, radar images are extremely sparse and self-similar, consisting primarily of a black background and a few visually identical aircraft blobs, while small changes in aircraft positions can substantially alter sector-level complexity. To test whether a vision model can capture these operationally important differences, we encode each traffic situation as a position image supplemented by five channels representing aircraft state variables, including heading, speed, and altitude, and train a Vision Transformer (ViT) to regress four intrinsic complexity components derived from pairwise geometric relations among aircraft. The model achieves $R^2 > 0.96$ for all four components, and a one-aircraft-removal perturbation study shows that its response changes proportionally to how much the removed aircraft contributed to sector complexity rather than treating every removal as equivalent. These results demonstrate that, despite its atypical visual characteristics, radar imagery is a viable input format for air traffic complexity modeling.

</details>


### [53] [TD-VAD: Breaking Visual Dependence in Video Anomaly Detection with Text-Driven Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11820)
*Shuangqing Zhang,Lei-Lei Ma,Zhao Wang,Wen Dong,Xinyi Xu,Guo-Sen Xie,Caifeng Shan,Fang Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Text-Driven Video Anomaly Detection (TD-VAD): trains VAD models using video-like text descriptions of anomalies generated by LLM, reducing reliance on target-domain anomaly data; includes an event evolution causal attention module for temporal reasoning; uses frozen CLIP at inference to align text and video; achieves large-margin improvement on XD-Violence and UCF-Crime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome scarcity and variability of labeled anomaly videos by leveraging easily collected textual descriptions of abnormal events as supervision.

Method: Generate temporally-aware text descriptions of anomalies using LLM; train VAD without target anomaly data; integrate event evolution causal attention to model long/short-range temporal dependencies; at inference, apply frozen CLIP encoder to extract frame embeddings and align with text modality.

Result: Outperforms prior one-class and unsupervised VAD methods by a large margin on two large-scale datasets (XD-Violence, UCF-Crime).

Conclusion: Demonstrates that text-driven supervision with specialized temporal modeling and cross-modal alignment can effectively detect anomalies without target-domain anomaly examples, offering a scalable alternative to visual annotation.

Abstract: Visual data is typically a prerequisite for training existing video anomaly detection (VAD) methods. However, obtaining sufficient annotated anomaly data for training is challenging and not scalable due to the rarity of anomaly data and the wide variety of abnormal events. In this work, we advocate that the effectiveness of treating texts as video sequences for the VAD model and propose a novel Text-Driven Video Anomaly Detection (TD-VAD) approach to break visual dependence. In contrast to the anomaly video data, text descriptions of abnormal events are easy to collect, and their class labels can be directly derived. Specifically, our method utilizes video-like text descriptions with temporal characteristics generated by LLM to train a VAD model, without any reliance on target-domain anomaly data. To capture the long- and short-range temporal logic of events, we design the event evolution causal attention module to model contextual dependencies across time. During inference, considering the domain gap between the texts and video sequences, we use the frozen CLIP encoder to extract embeddings of video frames to align the text modality while retaining crucial visual information. Comprehensive experiments on two large-scale VAD datasets, XD-Violence and UCF-Crime, demonstrate that our method outperforms prior one-class and unsupervised VAD methods by a large margin.

</details>


### [54] [Distractor-Aware Video Object Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11835)
*Andreas Robinson,Abdelrahman Eldesokey,Michael Felsberg*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduce a distractor-aware one-versus-many framework for semi-supervised video object segmentation, improving robustness by modeling distractors as a separate class and achieving state-of-the-art results on DAVIS 2017.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address false positives from distractors that resemble the target in semi-supervised video object segmentation. A one-vs-one target-vs-background setup is insufficient in cluttered scenes with distractors; separating distractors helps focus learning on challenging regions.

Method: Extend the Learning-What-To-Learn (LWL) framework to be distractor-aware via a one-vs-many scheme that isolates distractors into their own class, enabling targeted attention to challenging regions.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on the DAVIS 2017 validation set and improves test-dev by 4.6 percentage points over the baseline.

Conclusion: Modeling distractors as a separate class within a one-vs-many framework yields robustness gains for semi-supervised video object segmentation and sets a new performance benchmark on DAVIS 2017.

Abstract: Semi-supervised video object segmentation is a challenging task that aims to segment a target throughout a video sequence given an initial mask at the first frame. Discriminative approaches have demonstrated competitive performance on this task at a sensible complexity. These approaches typically formulate the problem as a one-versus-one classification between the target and the background. However, in reality, a video sequence usually encompasses a target, background, and possibly other distracting objects. Those objects increase the risk of introducing false positives, especially if they share visual similarities with the target. Therefore, it is more effective to separate distractors from the background, and handle them independently.
  We propose a one-versus-many scheme to address this situation by separating distractors into their own class. This separation allows imposing special attention to challenging regions that are most likely to degrade the performance. We demonstrate the prominence of this formulation by modifying the learning-what-to-learn (LWL) method to be distractor-aware. Our proposed approach sets a new state-of-the-art on the DAVIS 2017 val dataset, and improves over the baseline on the DAVIS 2017 test-dev benchmark by 4.6 percentage points.

</details>


### [55] [GeoBridge: Decoupled Semantic Conditioning for Generative Image Geolocalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11838)
*Zhiyang Dou,Xumeng Han,Fengde Peng,Zipeng Wang,Moxuan Zhao,Zhipei Huang,Zhenjun Han*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GeoBridge decouples semantic guidance from the coordinate decoder in MLLMs, enabling continuous, geometry-aware geocoding on the sphere via a frozen Riemannian flow-matching head, improving geolocalization accuracy at multiple thresholds on IM2GPS3K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete place-name predictions for geocoding lose image evidence and ignore multi-granular semantics; a continuous, geometry-aware decoding interface is needed.

Method: Introduce GeoBridge: a role-decoupled conditioning mechanism that connects a frozen semantic MLLM with a frozen Riemannian flow-matching head; separate projection forms continuous condition; decouples semantic supervision from condition interface; no disturbance to spherical decoder; decode-side algorithmic contribution.

Result: Achieves 38.67/52.89/70.37 at 25/200/750 km on IM2GPS3K; outperforms place-name-to-API pipeline and reasoning-augmented direct prediction at these scales.

Conclusion: GeoBridge is a decode-side, orthogonal to and complementary with chain-of-thought reasoning; code will be publicly released; addresses the bottleneck by enabling a smooth, geometry-aware decoding while keeping semantic supervision decoupled.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have advanced image geolocalization mainly by improving how they reason about geographic cues. How that reasoning isdecoded into coordinates, however, has lagged behind. Predicting a place name for a geocoding API is discrete and lossy: it ignores image evidence and collapses multi-granular semantics into a coarse lookup. We argue that the bottleneck has shifted from what a model reasons to how that reasoning is represented for a continuous, geometry-aware decoder. We present GeoBridge, a role-decoupled conditioning mechanism that connects a frozen semantic MLLM to a frozen Riemannian flow-matching head that generates coordinates on the sphere. The central obstacle is arole conflict: supervising the condition with discrete semantic labels biases its representation toward class-discriminative geometry, at odds with the smooth manifold the generative head requires. GeoBridge keeps the semantic supervision decoupled from the condition interface: a separate projection forms the continuous condition the frozen head expects, injecting geographic priors without disturbing the spherical decoder. On IM2GPS3K, GeoBridge reaches 38.67/52.89/70.37 at the 25/200/750 km thresholds, improving over a place-name-to-API pipeline and reasoning-augmented direct prediction at these precision-relevant scales. GeoBridge is a decode-side algorithmic contribution, orthogonal and complementary to chain-of-thought reasoning. Code will be made publicly available.

</details>


### [56] [BoltNet: An Ultra-Lightweight Convolutional Network for On-Device Plant Species Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11844)
*Daniel Rossi,Guido Borghi,Roberto Vezzani*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: BoltNet is a ultra-lightweight fully convolutional network for high-cardinality plant species identification, achieving 0.682 F1 with 341K parameters and strong on-device efficiency across CPU/GPU/NPU via Spatial Redistribution Bottleneck and Logit PreSampling, evaluated using the AccuracyCompression Tradeoff and transfer evidence on AIDERv2/CLRS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-grained, open-world plant recognition with large label spaces, constrained by memory, latency, and hardware variability; need evaluation on target hardware beyond model-size metrics.

Method: Introduce BoltNet architecture combining a Spatial Redistribution Bottleneck and Logit PreSampling; define AccuracyCompression Tradeoff; provide on-device benchmarks across Raspberry Pi 5, Jetson Orin Nano, and Hailo-8; evaluate transfer on AIDERv2 and CLRS; release code.

Result: Achieves 0.682 F1 with 341K parameters (1.37 MB) on Pl@ntNet300K, the highest F1 among models under 2 MB; performance close to larger backbones; BoltNet shows best FPS/W on GPU and NPU, second-best on CPU; transfer corroborated on AIDERv2/CLRS.

Conclusion: BoltNet offers a favorable size-to-performance and hardware-efficient trade-off for high-cardinality, fine-grained plant recognition, with practical on-device applicability and transferability across related tasks.

Abstract: Automated plant species identification from citizen-science imagery is an established, demanding fine-grained recognition problem: large taxonomic label spaces, visually similar species, and long-tailed observations require real model capacity, while field use constrains memory, latency, and power. Model size is only part of the deployment cost: intermediate activations held in memory during inference and platformdependent execution behavior matter too, so compact recognition must be assessed on target hardware rather than through complexity metrics alone. We present BoltNet, an ultra-lightweight fully convolutional architecture combining a Spatial Redistribution Bottleneck and Logit PreSampling to improve the tradeoff between predictive performance and model size in high-cardinality classification, and report the AccuracyCompression Tradeoff as a complementary diagnostic. On Pl@ntNet300K, BoltNet reaches 0.682 F1-score with 341K parameters (1.37 MB), the highest F1-score among evaluated models below 2 MB and close to substantially larger convolutional backbones. Model-only measurements on a Raspberry Pi 5, Jetson Orin Nano, and Hailo-8 characterize execution across CPU, GPU, and NPU platforms, where BoltNet is the most consistently efficient model, with the best FPS/W on the GPU and NPU and second-best on the CPU. Results on AIDERv2 and CLRS provide secondary evidence of transfer across environmental image-classification tasks. Code available at: https://codeberg.org/danielrossi/BoltNet

</details>


### [57] [LookBack: Where and How to Score LVLM Responses via Visual Reference Usage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11847)
*Beomsik Cho,Jinhyeong Kim,Dongseok Lee,Jaehyung Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Training-free LVLM response scoring (LookBack) augments token likelihood with a visual lookback score to measure how much each token refers to image content, improving Best-of-N selection with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LVLMs can hallucinate with respect to both text and image content; existing confidence metrics from LLMs fail to align with image-grounded truth because removing the image input does not change confidence, indicating many tokens are grounded in textual priors rather than the image.

Method: Introduce LookBack, a training-free scoring method that augments token likelihood with a lightweight visual lookback score per token, quantifying how strongly each response token refers to image tokens.

Result: Across four benchmarks and three LVLMs, LookBack consistently improves Best-of-N selection over baselines, with negligible additional computational overhead.

Conclusion: LookBack fills a gap in LVLM evaluation by incorporating image-grounded token relevance, enabling more reliable and efficient response selection without additional training.

Abstract: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) integrate visual perception with language generation, enabling responses that span image understanding and complex reasoning. However, LVLMs do not just inherit the text-level hallucinations; they also hallucinate against the image, producing fluent responses ungrounded in what they see. This makes LVLM response scoring inherently harder, and our diagnostics show that existing confidence-based metrics adopted from LLMs are insufficient for LVLMs. Specifically, removing the input image barely changes confidence-based selection, suggesting that output-space confidence primarily captures textual plausibility rather than agreement with the image. To address this gap, we propose LookBack, a training-free LVLM response scoring method that augments token likelihood with visual lookback score, a lightweight measure of how strongly each response token refers to image tokens. Across four benchmarks and three models, LookBack consistently improves Best-of-$N$ selection over existing baselines with negligible additional overhead.

</details>


### [58] [Warping Earth Observations for better ice labeling in the Marginal Marginal Ice Zone](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11883)
*Tom Kelly,Martin S. J. Rogers*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Mutual information warping aligns multimodal satellite imagery to enable sea-ice segmentation under dynamic drift, using sparse expert labels for dense segmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resolve spatial and temporal misalignment across heterogeneous satellite sensors (radar, visible, thermal) in fast-changing Antarctic sea-ice regions where pixel-wise correspondence is violated.

Method: Introduce a mutual-information-based warping module to align multi-sensor sequences (Sentinel-1 radar, MODIS visible/thermal) before segmentation; train with a sparse expert-labeled dataset (2,088 pixel-wise cues, 7,046 point classifications across 43 scenes) to achieve dense sea-ice segmentation from sparse labels.

Result: Grounding and aligning modalities prior to segmentation improves classification accuracy and enables dense sea-ice segmentation from sparse supervision.

Conclusion: Spatially aligning heterogeneous modalities via mutual information warping is effective for accurate, dense sea-ice segmentation in rapidly drifting, multi-sensor environments, suggesting broader applicability to dynamic multimodal EO tasks.

Abstract: Multimodal satellite imagery provides complementary information for Earth Observation, but accurately combining heterogeneous sensors remains challenging in dynamic environments. Fast-changing regions, such as the Antarctic marginal ice zone, cannot fully exploit multimodal information from different satellite sensors because surface features move between image acquisitions. This spatial and temporal mismatch challenges effective perceptual grounding, violating the assumption of pixel-level correspondence that underpins most multimodal reasoning and downstream classification pipelines. Antarctic sea ice provides a challenging benchmark due to the rapid, heterogeneous drift of individual ice floes and the differing responses of sea ice to radar, visible and thermal sensing modalities. Accurate, dense supervision of sea ice remains scarce because generating pixel-wise labels requires time-consuming expert interpretation of noisy data, leading to historical reliance on coarse-resolution maritime ice charts for model training. This paper presents a novel architecture based on mutual information warping to align multi-satellite (Sentinel-1 and MODIS platforms) multimodal (visible, thermal, radar) satellite scenes. To demonstrate the approach, we introduce a sparse expert-labeled dataset of 2,088 pixel-wise annotations (7,046 expert point classifications) located at the ice-water margin interface across 43 scenes. Our results demonstrate that spatially grounding and aligning modalities prior to segmentation improves classification accuracy, and enables accurate, dense sea ice segmentation from sparse point-wise supervision.

</details>


### [59] [Do You See What You Draw? A Semantic Closed-Loop Framework for Holistic Evaluation of Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11907)
*Hao Zhang,Jiaxin Qi,Zhijiang Tang,Jianqiang Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SGU is an annotation-free, holistic evaluation framework for unified multimodal models (UMMs) that uses a closed-loop pipeline (perception → description → visual reconstruction → reasoning) to measure integrated generative and understanding capabilities, revealing that top UMMs still struggle with reasoning over their own generated contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for system-level evaluation of unified multimodal models that integrate generation and understanding in one parameter space. Current benchmarks treat generation and understanding separately and require annotations, leaving a gap in holistic assessment.

Method: Introduce Self-Generative-Understanding (SGU), an annotation-free evaluation framework. The model first perceives an image and generates a textual description, then reconstructs a visual context from that description, and finally performs reasoning over the self-generated output. This closed-loop process yields a zero-cost, integrated performance score for UMMs.

Result: Experiments show that even high-performing UMMs struggle to reason over their own generated contexts, exposing limitations not captured by separate evaluation of understanding or generation.

Conclusion: SGU provides a complementary holistic evaluation framework for unified multimodal models and lays the groundwork for benchmarking next-generation unified models.

Abstract: As Large Vision-Language Models increasingly aim to integrate visual generation and understanding within a single parameter space, evaluating such structural unification in a cohesive manner remains a critical challenge. Current evaluation protocols predominantly treat generative and discriminative capabilities as separate tasks, leaving a gap in system-level evaluation for unified multimodal models (UMMs). In this work, we propose Self-Generative-Understanding (SGU), a novel, annotation-free evaluation framework that probes the integrated capabilities of unified models through a semantic closed-loop challenge. Without requiring new annotations, SGU leverages the dual understanding-and-generation abilities of UMMs by asking them to first perceive an image and produce a textual description, subsequently reconstruct a visual context based on that description, and finally perform reasoning over the self-generated output. This pipeline provides a zero-cost testbed that yields an integrated performance score specifically tailored for evaluating UMMs as unified systems. Extensive experiments show that even high-performing UMMs often struggle to reason over their own generated contexts, revealing limitations that are not captured by separate evaluations of understanding or generation alone. Our work provides a complementary holistic evaluation framework and offers a foundation for benchmarking the development of next-generation unified multimodal models.

</details>


### [60] [HarmoniDPO: Video-guided Audio Generation via Preference-Optimized Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11913)
*Wenshuo Peng,Kaipeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HarmoniDPO introduces a diffusion-based V2A framework with (1) dual video representations for temporal and semantic fidelity, (2) online Direct Preference Optimization (online-DPO) inspired by RLHF to tune via human preferences, and (3) Dual-scale Diffusion Search (DDS) for adaptive test-time fidelity, yielding state-of-the-art audio-video synchronization and perceptual quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video-to-audio generation currently loses temporal dynamics and relies on reconstruction-based objectives that poorly align with human perceptual judgments. A representation that preserves temporal semantics and an optimization objective aligned with human preferences are needed.

Method: Three components: (1) dual video representation combining global context and frame-wise features, (2) online Direct Preference Optimization (online-DPO) to fine-tune a diffusion V2A model from human preferences, (3) Dual-scale Diffusion Search (DDS) for adaptive, test-time optimization of output fidelity during inference.

Result: Empirical results show HarmoniDPO outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both audio-video synchronization and subjective audio quality.

Conclusion: HarmoniDPO provides a robust solution for generating realistic, human-preferred audio from video by integrating richer visual representations, preference-based optimization, and adaptive inference; it is a promising direction for perceptual alignment in V2A tasks.

Abstract: Video-to-audio (V2A) generation faces significant challenges in achieving precise temporal synchronization and high perceptual quality due to the complex, ambiguous relationship between visual and auditory cues. Existing methods typically compress video inputs into single feature representations, leading to significant loss of temporal dynamics and fine-grained visual information. These approaches also rely on reconstruction-based training objectives that poorly correlate with human perceptual judgments of audio quality and appropriateness. We propose HarmoniDPO, a novel framework that integrates preference-based optimization into diffusion-based V2A generation to address these limitations. (1) Our approach leverages a dual video representation: combining global context with frame-wise features to preserve temporal dynamics and semantic detail. (2) Inspired by reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), HarmoniDPO employs online Direct Preference Optimization (online-DPO) to fine-tune a diffusion-based V2A model from preference judgments, enhancing perceptual quality and alignment. (3) Additionally, we introduce Dual-scale Diffusion Search (DDS), a test time scaling algorithm that adaptively optimizes output fidelity during inference. Experiments demonstrate that HarmoniDPO outperforms state-of-the-art methods in audio-video synchronization and subjective audio quality, offering a robust solution for generating realistic, human-preferred audio from video.

</details>


### [61] [Seed2GS: Camera-Free, Training-Free Object Extraction from 3D Gaussian Scenes via a Single Reference-View Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11928)
*Zongjian Ding,Yudong Gao,Jiale Liu,Xinglin Yu,Junxing Ren,Dong Wei,Yajing Chen,Shan Huang,Mingjun Cheng,Min Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Seed2GS extracts a designated target from pre-built 3D Gaussian Splatting scenes without reconstruction cameras or per-scene training, achieving state-of-the-art LERF-MASK/mIoU and fast latency by decoupling target identity from 3D coverage, using QD-SAM3 for a reference mask, seed-lift and virtual orbits for view exposure, and tracking propagation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable interactive 3D editing on pre-built assets without expensive training or camera access; address limitations of existing methods that demand long per-scene training, reduced accuracy, or unused original cameras.

Method: Select a reliable reference mask from open-vocabulary candidates (QD-SAM3), freeze identity; seed lift and visibility-adaptive virtual orbits; tracking propagates seed; scene remains frozen; masks supervise one temporary foreground logit per Gaussian; no per-scene re-training.

Result: On LERF-MASK, 92.1% mIoU with 9.3s latency; +3.7 pt vs strongest scene-trained baseline; +7.6 pt vs closest camera-free baseline. With one fixed test reference, 91.1% mIoU. Ground-truth seed improves by 0.72. On 3D-OVS, 95.7% mIoU.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that separating target identity from 3D coverage enables high-accuracy, camera-free object extraction in 3DGS, reducing reliance on original cameras and scene-specific training; efficient pipeline robust to reference mask choices.

Abstract: Extracting a target object from a pre-built 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) scene enables interactive 3D editing. Existing methods either train for tens of minutes per scene, sacrifice accuracy, or require original reconstruction cameras that pre-built assets may not include. We present Seed2GS, which achieves the highest reported LERF-MASK accuracy without original reconstruction cameras or scene-specific representation training. Its key insight is to separate target identity from 3D coverage. QD-SAM3 selects one reliable reference mask from several open-vocabulary candidates, fixing identity once. Seed lift and visibility-adaptive virtual orbits then expose the object from new viewpoints, while tracking propagates the seed without repeated detection. Because the scene remains frozen, these masks supervise only one temporary foreground logit per Gaussian. On LERF-MASK, Seed2GS reaches 92.1% mean intersection over union (mIoU) with a measured compute-only latency of 9.3 seconds, 3.7 points above the strongest scene-trained baseline and 7.6 points above the closest camera-free baseline. With one fixed test reference per scene, the complete pipeline retains 91.1% mIoU; replacing its predicted seed with a ground-truth mask improves mIoU by only 0.72 points. On 3D-OVS, Seed2GS reaches 95.7% mIoU.

</details>


### [62] [Dual Anchors, Do It Better: Hierarchical Group Merging for Zero-Shot Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11933)
*Jimin Roh,DongKyu Kim,Suk-Ju Kang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A Dual-Anchor framework for zero-shot anomaly detection that fuses text-based prompts with hierarchical image anchors derived from top-down grouping, using a Group-Gated Token Refiner to refine global representations and construct dynamic state prompts, yielding strong generalization across 8 industrial and 6 medical benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CLIP-based ZSAD methods rely heavily on text semantics, making them sensitive to prompt design and suffering from weak visual grounding under domain shifts. There is a need for robust cross-modal alignment and reduced prompt dependency.

Method: Introduce hierarchical image anchors by a top-down grouping mechanism that aggregates local-to-global image features into normal/abnormal group tokens. These anchors serve as gating signals in a Group-Gated Token Refiner to enhance global representations. Refined image anchors are fused with text prompts to create dynamic state prompts.

Result: The approach stabilizes image-text alignment, reduces prompt sensitivity, and achieves strong generalization across a broad set of industrial and medical benchmarks (8 industrial + 6 medical).

Conclusion: Jointly reinforcing visual and textual semantics via dual anchors improves robustness of ZSAD to domain shifts and prompts, offering better generalization and potential applicability beyond the tested domains.

Abstract: Zero-shot anomaly detection (ZSAD) aims to identify anomalies in unseen domains, a setting that is particularly critical for industrial and medical applications where domain shifts are prevalent. However, most CLIP-based ZSAD methods anchor semantics solely on the text modality, making performance highly sensitive to prompt design and leading to weak visual grounding. To mitigate these limitations, we propose a Dual-Anchor framework that complements conventional text anchors with hierarchical image anchors constructed via a top-down grouping mechanism. This mechanism progressively aggregates local-to-global image features to form normal and abnormal group tokens, which serve as image anchors and act as gating signals in a Group-Gated Token Refiner to enhance the global representation. The refined image anchors are then fused with text prompts to construct dynamic state prompts. By jointly reinforcing visual and textual semantics, our framework stabilizes image-text alignment, reduces prompt dependency, and achieves strong generalization across 8 industrial and 6 medical benchmarks.

</details>


### [63] [Surfsvr: 2D Surface Priors as 3D Geometric Regularizers for Sparse Voxel Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11938)
*Yan Di,Chengxi Li,Yaoxing Wang,Mengge Liu,Zhigang Li,Ruida Zhang,Mingyang Li,Pengyuan Wang,Shan Gao,Xiangyang Ji*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SurfSVR introduces 2D surface priors as explicit 3D geometric regularizers for sparse voxel reconstruction, organizing images into coherent surface regions and fitting adaptive planar/quadratic surface models to guide subdivision and optimization, achieving state-of-the-art results on three benchmarks; code will be released soon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparse voxel geometry often deteriorates into fragmented surfaces, excessive subdivision, and floating artifacts when optimized from local photometric cues and discrete visibility statistics, especially in weakly textured or sparsely observed regions. A robust, region-aware regularization derived from 2D surface priors is needed to stabilize 3D reconstruction.

Method: For each image, jointly reason over appearance, monocular depth, normals, and cross-view geometry to partition into coherent surface regions. Represent each region with an adaptively selected planar or quadratic surface model based on fitting reliability and geometric complexity. Use cross-model agreement to distinguish reliable geometry from ambiguous predictions. Lift these structured 2D priors into 3D and integrate them throughout the reconstruction pipeline, guiding surface-adaptive voxel subdivision, providing region-level depth and normal supervision, enhancing voxel pruning for reliable sparse surfaces, and suppressing off-surface floaters during post-refinement training.

Result: Extensive experiments on three public benchmarks show SurfSVR consistently improves sparse voxel reconstruction across scenes with varying visibility and geometry, achieving state-of-the-art reconstruction quality.

Conclusion: By converting image-space semantic and geometric coherence into persistent 3D structural constraints, SurfSVR provides a unified, robust sparse voxel reconstruction framework; code and models will be released soon.

Abstract: Sparse voxel reconstruction offers an efficient representation for high-fidelity 3D modeling, yet its geometry is commonly optimized from local photometric evidence and discrete visibility statistics. This often leads to fragmented surfaces, excessive subdivision, and floating artifacts, particularly in weakly textured or sparsely observed regions. We introduce SurfSVR, a novel sparse voxel reconstruction paradigm that treats 2D surface priors as explicit 3D geometric regularizers. Instead of directly lifting noisy pixel-wise depth predictions, SurfSVR first organizes each image into coherent surface regions by jointly reasoning over appearance, monocular depth, normals and cross-view geometry. Each region is then represented by an adaptively selected planar or quadratic surface model based on fitting reliability and geometric complexity, while cross-model agreement distinguishes reliable geometry from ambiguous predictions. These structured 2D priors are lifted into 3D and integrated throughout the reconstruction pipeline. They guide surface-adaptive voxel subdivision, provide region-level depth and normal supervision during optimization, enhance geometrically reliable sparse-observed surfaces in voxel pruning, and suppress off-surface floaters during post-refinement training. This unified design converts semantic and geometric coherence in image space into persistent structural constraints in 3D. Extensive experiments on 3 public benchmarks demonstrate that SurfSVR consistently improves sparse voxel reconstruction across scenes with substantially different visibility and geometry characteristics, achieving state-of-the-art reconstruction quality. Codes and models will be released soon.

</details>


### [64] [Evaluating and Calibrating Diffusion Model-derived Uncertainty for Quantitative MRI Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11942)
*Shishuai Wang,Stefan Klein,Juan A. Hernandez-Tamames,Dirk H. J. Poot*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Evaluates uncertainty maps from a diffusion-model–based qMRI pipeline. Finds uncertainty correlates with error and supports selective prediction, but raw uncertainty is miscalibrated for quantitative intervals; post-hoc bias correction and uncertainty scaling improve calibration; qualitative data show spatially meaningful patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning–based qMRI mappings lack explicit uncertainty characterization; reliable uncertainty estimates are needed for error awareness, selective prediction, and trustworthy quantitative intervals.

Method: Systematic evaluation using multiple inferences from a data-consistent diffusion-model qMRI framework. Synthetic data assessed error-awareness, high-error detection, selective prediction, and Gaussian interval calibration. Post-hoc calibration combined prediction-value–dependent bias correction with scalar uncertainty scaling. Qualitative assessment conducted on a healthy volunteer.

Result: Uncertainty from diffusion models positively correlates with mapping error; excluding high-uncertainty voxels reduces retained error (risk-coverage analysis). Raw uncertainty poorly calibrated for quantitative intervals; calibration improved via post-hoc bias correction and scalar uncertainty scaling. Spatially meaningful uncertainty patterns observed in a healthy volunteer.

Conclusion: Diffusion-model–derived uncertainty is informative for reliability assessment and selective prediction, but calibration is essential to enable quantitative interval interpretation.

Abstract: Quantitative MRI (qMRI) provides standardised tissue parameter maps, but the reliability of deep learning-based qMRI mapping methods is often not explicitly characterised. In this work we systematically evaluate uncertainty maps for quantitative MRI derived from multiple inferences of a data-consistent diffusion model-based qMRI framework. Evaluation on synthetic test data assessed error-awareness, high-error detection, selective prediction, and Gaussian interval calibration. Diffusion model-derived uncertainty was positively associated with the mapping error, while risk-coverage analysis showed that excluding high-uncertainty voxels reduced the retained error. However, the raw uncertainty was poorly calibrated for quantitative interval interpretation. Calibration was substantially improved using a post-hoc procedure combining prediction-value-dependent bias correction with scalar uncertainty scaling. Qualitative evaluation on a healthy volunteer showed spatially meaningful uncertainty patterns. These results indicate that diffusion model-derived uncertainty is informative for reliability assessment and selective prediction, but requires calibration for quantitative interval interpretation.

</details>


### [65] [Auditing Frame-Level AUC in Weakly Supervised Video Anomaly Detection: Granularity, Resolution, and Scene Bias](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11985)
*Sara Abdulaziz,Egor Bondarev*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Pooled AUC is unstable for localizing anomalies and hides margins; granularity-aware evaluation (global, per-anomaly, within-video) plus zero-shot readouts reveals localization issues, scene-sensitivity biases, and decoupled anomaly structure; a protocol is proposed and released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To interrogate the validity of pooled frame-level AUC as a sole metric for WSVAD and to expose potential biases that affect localization and cross-video comparisons.

Method: Reproduce and audit recent WSVAD models on UCF-Crime; compute frame-level scores; evaluate under three granularity readouts (global, per category, within-video) and on zero-shot scores; use paired video bootstrap for reliability; analyze normal footage for scene properties; publish granularity-aware protocol.

Result: Pooled AUC poorly predicts within-video localization; margins claimed by baselines are not supported at finer granularity; within-video AUC resolves more comparisons; learned representations show decoupling of anomaly structure and detector localization; models exhibit scene-sensitivity on normal footage; protocol released.

Conclusion: Granularity-aware evaluation reveals weaknesses of pooled AUC and highlights the need to include intra-video granularity and scene factors in WSVAD evaluation; the proposed protocol provides a more reliable and interpretable framework for comparing methods on datasets like UCF-Crime.

Abstract: Frame-level area under the ROC curve (AUC) is the dominant evaluation metric for weakly supervised video anomaly detection (WSVAD). Its standard form measures whether an anomalous frame outranks a normal frame drawn from anywhere in the test set. We refer to this comparison as pooled AUC, since it aggregates frame pairs across test videos regardless of source. Pooled AUC therefore credits both event localization and differences between video sources. We audit this protocol on UCF-Crime across recent state-of-the-art models spanning different backbone families. Holding each model's frame scores fixed, we read them under three pairing granularities: global, per anomaly category, and within each video, then repeat the same three-granularity readout on zero-shot scores computed from the models' internal representations. We assess ranking reliability with a paired video bootstrap. Three findings follow. First, pooled AUC does not reliably predict within-video anomaly localization: models with similar pooled scores exhibit large localization differences and rank reversals under stricter granularities. Second, at the benchmark's test-split size, pooled AUC lacks the resolution to support state-of-the-art margins reported in the field. Within each backbone family, it resolves no comparison at those margins, while within-video AUC resolves several over identical predictions. Learned representations further reveal that within-video anomaly structure and detector localization are decoupled. Third, on normal footage alone, every model we examine separates videos by recording properties, such as resolution and color encoding, indicating that scene sensitivity is shared across the setting rather than specific to any architecture. We publicly release a granularity-aware protocol computable from existing predictions and scene-factor annotations for UCF-Crime.

</details>


### [66] [A Remote Approach to Cashew Orchard Detection: Leveraging Active Learning with Satellite Imagery in Guinea-Bissau](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11996)
*Miguel,Sofia,Maria,Patrícia,Luke,João*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Nationwide mapping of cashew orchards in Guinea-Bissau using Sentinel-2 imagery and margin-based active learning, achieving 94.0% balanced accuracy; two datasets and a 2021 10 m resolution cashew map released openly on GitHub.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cashew production significantly influences West African economies and land use; unregulated cultivation correlates with deforestation and biodiversity loss; absence of a nationwide georeferenced cashew orchard database necessitates scalable remote sensing approaches.

Method: Sentinel-2 imagery analyzed with machine learning; margin-based active learning to optimize the training sample in terms of size and informativeness; training conducted entirely off-site. Two datasets were created, and a 2021 cashew map at 10 m resolution was produced.

Result: A nationwide cashew orchard map was produced with 94.0% balanced accuracy (off-site). Two openly accessible datasets and a 2021 10 m resolution cashew map are available on GitHub.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility of scalable nationwide cashew orchard mapping using open satellite data and active-learning-based training; provides data resources to support environmental monitoring, deforestation assessment, and policy planning in Guinea-Bissau and similar regions.

Abstract: Cashew production is a widespread economic activity in Guinea-Bissau, as well as other countries in West Africa. However, unregulated cashew production can be directly associated with increasing regionwide deforestation rates, biodiversity losses, and a fragile economic structure. There is no nationwide database for listing or georeferencing cashew orchards, so there is a clear need to remotely map their locations. In recent years, multiple methods for detecting orchards have been developed, though they have only been applied on a regional level. This work expands regional analyses to a nationwide scale. It develops a scalable and cost-effective remote approach, based on Sentinel-2 satellite imagery, using Machine Learning techniques to detect cashew orchards automatically. Margin-based Active Learning techniques were employed to develop an optimal training set in terms of the number of points and their informativeness, leading to a cashew map with 94.0% balanced accuracy obtained entirely off-site. We created two datasets and a 2021 cashew map with 10m spatial resolution that are openly accessible through GitHub. The results demonstrate the possibility of a broader cashew orchard mapping, creating a new stepping stone for this environmental application.

</details>


### [67] [LoSA: Near-Lossless Sparse Attention for Training-Free Video Diffusion Acceleration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12032)
*Enhuai Liu,Yunke Wang,Yutong Wang,Changming Sun,Chang Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LoSA provides a training-free sparse-attention method for video diffusion transformers that preserves 99% of attention mass while removing ~40% of block interactions, enabling up to 3.2x speedups with minimal quality loss, especially when combined with feature caching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video diffusion transformers incur high sampling cost due to dense self-attention over long 3D token sequences. Existing sparse attention trades fidelity for speed; there is value in a regime that preserves near-lossless performance while maximizing speed. Observations show most attention mass is concentrated in a subset of block interactions and that the high-mass support is stable across denoising steps, suggesting a fixed sparse structure may work well.

Method: LoSA is a training-free sparse-attention scheme that fixes a retained-mass threshold of 99%. It computes exact block attention masses at an early dense step, and for each head and query block selects the smallest key/value block set that achieves the threshold; these block indices are frozen and reused across all subsequent denoising steps. This avoids retraining and maintains near-lossless fidelity. Optional integration with feature caching further boosts speed.

Result: Experimental results on Wan2.1-1.3B show LoSA alone yields 1.36× speedup with a 0.06-point VBench Overall drop. When combined with feature caching (compositional setup), LoSA achieves 3.2× speedup on HunyuanVideo with a 0.02-point drop, outperforming the strongest sparse baseline (0.32-point drop at similar speed). Across three video diffusion transformers with speedups up to 3.2×, LoSA consistently delivers the best training-free speed-quality trade-off.

Conclusion: LoSA demonstrates that a training-free, threshold-based sparse-attention strategy can achieve near-lossless fidelity with substantial speedups in video diffusion transformers, particularly when paired with feature caching, establishing the best training-free speed-quality trade-off among tested models.

Abstract: Video diffusion transformers are costly to sample: every denoising step applies self-attention over a long 3D token sequence, a quadratic cost that dominates as resolution and duration grow. Sparse attention reduces this cost without retraining, but existing methods pursue aggressive sparsity, where further speedup costs disproportionately more attention fidelity. We target the opposite end of this trade-off: fix near-lossless fidelity by construction, and remove as much computation as this constraint permits. Two observations make this regime practical: roughly 40% of block interactions can be removed while retaining 99% of the attention mass, and the high-mass support remains stable across denoising steps. We propose LoSA, a training-free sparse-attention method that fixes a retained-mass threshold of 99% rather than a sparsity ratio: it measures exact block attention masses at one early dense step, keeps, for each head and query block, the smallest key/value block set meeting the threshold, and reuses the frozen block indices for all remaining steps. On Wan2.1-1.3B, LoSA alone gives a $1.36\times$ speedup with a 0.06-point VBench Overall drop. The benefit is largest under composition: combined with feature caching, LoSA reaches a $3.2\times$ speedup on HunyuanVideo at a 0.02-point drop, versus 0.32 points for the strongest sparse baseline at comparable speed. Across three video diffusion transformers and speedups up to $3.2\times$, LoSA consistently achieves the best training-free speed-quality trade-off.

</details>


### [68] [How Far from Clinical Deployment? Evaluating the Complete Unsupervised Domain Adaptation Pipeline in Medical Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12035)
*Yiheng Xiong,Luisa Gallée,Daniel Santak Wolf,Heiko Hillenhagen,Michael Götz*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: End-to-end evaluation of unsupervised domain adaptation pipelines in clinical imaging shows label-free validators often fail to identify the best target model; ensembling and limited target labeling help but don't fully close the gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In clinical deployment, the target domain is unlabeled, making model selection impossible; need to evaluate adaptation and selection jointly.

Method: Empirical study across 11 cross-domain scenarios from 9 datasets; 10 UDA algorithms; 13 label-free validators; >80k models; test strategies including ensembling and small target labeling budget.

Result: Best-adapted models exist, but validator-based selection yields large, structural gaps; no validator consistently reliable; ensembling and small labeling reduce gap but don't eliminate it.

Conclusion: Effective deployable UDA hinges on improving the selection step; addressing this aspect could significantly move UDA toward clinical practice.

Abstract: Deploying unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) in clinical practice requires choosing which algorithm to use and which of its trained models to ship. However, the deployment (target) domain is unlabeled, so models cannot be evaluated directly on it, leaving it unclear which to select. We address this by evaluating the complete UDA pipeline, considering both adaptation and label-free selection together. Our study covers eleven clinically relevant cross-domain scenarios from nine medical imaging datasets, with ten UDA algorithms and 13 label-free selection methods (validators), evaluating over 80,000 trained models in total. By this, we find that a capable adapted model usually exists, but identifying it without target labels is difficult: the validator-selected models leave a large and structural target performance gap to the best available one, with no evaluated validator consistently reliable. Towards closing it, we explore two strategies, ensembling and a small target-labeling budget; both narrow this gap but do not close it entirely. Overall, deployable UDA depends on the complete pipeline; addressing the less explored selection step could bring much of current UDA closer to clinical use.

</details>


### [69] [Localizing to Debias: A Patch-Level Benchmark and Baseline for Weakly Supervised Spatial Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12045)
*Sara Abdulaziz,Abdulrahman Al-Abri,Giacomo D'Amicantonio,Egor Bondarev*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A sparse spatio-temporal framework (SST-WSVADL) for weakly supervised video anomaly detection mitigates background bias by dynamic sparsification, coupling temporal and spatial branches with motion-aware regularization; it provides frame-level spatial annotations and evaluation protocol for auditing spatial biases on three datasets, achieving competitive results while enabling localization and interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Weakly supervised video anomaly detection (WSVAD) often relies on coarse temporal supervision and background cues, leading to spatial biases and lack of interpretability. There is a need for spatial grounding and auditability to ensure ethics and accountability in anomaly detection.

Method: Introduce SST-WSVADL, a sparse spatio-temporal model that progressively sparsifies to focus on anomaly-relevant spatio-temporal regions. It couples temporal and spatial branches end-to-end via motion-aware regularization, avoiding external detectors or prompts. It provides frame-level spatial annotations and a method-agnostic evaluation protocol for UCF-Crime, XD-Violence, and MSAD.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows SST-WSVADL is competitive with prior methods on benchmark datasets and offers localization and patch-level auditability of scene bias, enabling reproducible interpretability-oriented evaluation for WSVAD models.

Conclusion: SST-WSVADL advances ethical and accountable WSVAD by delivering spatial grounding and auditability while maintaining competitive anomaly detection performance, supported by publicly released annotations and evaluation protocol.

Abstract: Despite growing interest in weakly supervised video anomaly detection (WSVAD), current methods struggle to bridge the gap between coarse temporal supervision and fine-grained spatial reasoning. A key obstacle is the tendency of temporal detectors to latch onto background and scene-level cues rather than truly discriminative anomaly evidence. This background bias raises ethical concerns: models may inadvertently associate anomalies with societal or environmental context rather than authentic crime-related cues. Without spatial grounding, such biases remain hidden and unauditable. To address this, we propose SST-WSVADL, a sparse spatio-temporal framework that bridges temporal anomaly detection with fine-grained spatial localization. Rather than processing all spatial regions indiscriminately, SST-WSVADL progressively focuses on the most anomaly-relevant spatio-temporal regions through dynamic sparsification, naturally suppressing background dominant content while preserving discriminative evidence. The temporal and spatial branches are coupled end-to-end via motion-aware regularization that guides sparsification toward dynamically informative regions, without relying on external detectors or vision-language prompts. We publicly release frame-level spatial annotations and a method-agnostic evaluation protocol for three public datasets: UCF-Crime, XD-Violence, and MSAD. These resources enable the community to audit spatial biases in WSVAD predictions, supporting progress toward more ethical and accountable anomaly detection. Experiments demonstrate that SST-WSVADL is competitive with prior methods across benchmarks while enabling localization and patch-level auditability of scene bias, providing a reproducible foundation for interpretability-oriented evaluation of WSVAD models.

</details>


### [70] [Predicting Functions, Not Features: KANs with Function-Space Joint-Embedding Predictive Learning for Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12050)
*Yungeng Liu,Xuanzi Fang,Yuge Zhang,Shuqi Ren,Haijin Zeng,Yongyong Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FS-JEPA is a self-supervised pre-aggregation learning framework for KAN-based medical image segmentation that learns edge-function representations via multi-radius signatures and EMA targets, achieving state-of-the-art Dice (+2.25 pp) on five benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current KAN-based segmentation optimizes edge functions only after aggregation, lacking explicit pre-aggregation learning targets for individual edge functions; this limits the quality of edge representations.

Method: Introduce FS-JEPA: a masked online branch predicts structured multi-radius signatures of sampled KAN edge functions produced by a full-context EMA target branch; shared edge indices align predictions and targets; edge functions are represented by a multi-radius signature capturing local variations; train jointly with segmentation loss; inference uses only the segmentation branch.

Result: On five medical image segmentation benchmarks, FS-JEPA achieves the best average Dice and surpasses the strongest competing KAN-based method by 2.25 percentage points.

Conclusion: Function-space predictive learning in the pre-aggregation space of KANs yields richer edge-function representations and improved segmentation performance; predictive branch is discarded at inference, maintaining efficiency.

Abstract: Kolmogorov--Arnold Networks (KANs) introduce explicit functional representations by parameterizing each network edge as a learnable univariate function. However, existing KAN-based segmentation models optimize edge functions only through objectives defined after edge aggregation, leaving individual functions without an explicit pre-aggregation learning target. To address this limitation, we propose Function-Space Joint-Embedding Predictive Learning (FS-JEPA) for medical image segmentation. Our FS-JEPA framework moves predictive learning into the pre-aggregation function space of KANs. A masked online branch predicts structured signatures of sampled KAN edge functions generated by a full-context exponential moving average target branch, while shared edge indices preserve correspondence between predictions and targets. Rather than predicting an isolated edge response, we represent each sampled edge function using a multi-radius signature composed of function evaluations around its input anchor. This structured representation captures local functional variations that cannot be characterized by a single response and provides a more informative predictive target. The function-space objective is jointly optimized with the segmentation loss during training, while the predictive branch is removed at inference. Experiments on five medical image segmentation benchmarks show that our FS-JEPA achieves the best average Dice and outperforms the strongest competing KAN-based method by +2.25 percentage points.

</details>


### [71] [Do Not Forget the Obvious - RISC: A Risk-Informed Slice-Coverage Protocol for Safe Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12051)
*Fabian Hüger*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A risk-informed protocol (RISC) for stress testing and evaluation in autonomous driving; uses risk slices and coverage-qualified reporting to direct auditing and assess safety more effectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aggregate metrics may miss performance issues in high-risk scenarios; a risk-guided approach and explicit coverage reporting can improve safety assurances.

Method: Define safety-driven risk slices; tag candidate data with lightweight signals; select a compact audit set by risk; evaluate results with explicit coverage statements; optional LLM assistance in test planning; model-agnostic; applicable to perception modules, driving models, and subsystems; instantiate for monocular pedestrian perception using 1,000 frames from Zenseact Open Dataset, image statistics, and a YOLO-based detector proxy.

Result: Risk-guided selection increases critical failure discovery from 34.0% under random sampling to 98.5% in a proof-of-concept; demonstrates high efficiency of risk-guided auditing; supports model-agnostic assessment across subsystems.

Conclusion: RISC provides a lightweight, assurance-oriented evaluation layer that complements scenario categorization, coverage assessment, and broader testing-and-verification workflows; potential for broader adoption with considerations for generalizability and dataset dependency.

Abstract: Aggregate metrics may not fully reflect performance in insufficiently examined high-risk driving conditions. We propose RISC (Risk-Informed Slice Coverage), a practical protocol for risk-guided stress testing and coverage-qualified evaluation. Risk-guided stress testing directs a finite audit budget toward risk-relevant sub-datasets, called risk slices, while coverage-qualified evaluation reports results together with explicit statements about which slices are sufficiently or insufficiently covered. The protocol translates safety concerns into machine-readable risk slices, uses lightweight signals to tag candidate data, selects a compact audit set by risk, and qualifies the results using coverage evidence. An LLM can optionally support this process by surfacing relevant but potentially overlooked conditions during test planning, thereby helping engineers not to forget the obvious. RISC is model-agnostic and can be applied to perception modules, driving models, and other autonomous-driving subsystems. We instantiate the protocol for monocular pedestrian perception using 1,000 frames from the Zenseact Open Dataset, image statistics, and a YOLO-based detector proxy. In this proof-of-concept study, risk-guided selection increases critical failure discovery from 34.0% under random sampling to 98.5%. RISC provides a lightweight, assurance-oriented evaluation layer that complements scenario categorization, coverage assessment, and broader testing-and-verification workflows.

</details>


### [72] [Draw This First](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12064)
*Dazhi Zhong,Rowan Bradbury,Grant Davis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Predicts an order field to govern stroke sequencing for generating ordered vector sketches, using a latent flow-matching transformer as an image prior and a VAE to predict the order field, stroke mask, and segmentation, enabling text-guided or image-to-vector derendering with prescribed drawing order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Move beyond fixed stroke order in sketch synthesis to allow explicit control over drawing order, enabling natural language guidance and more robust vectorization of raster inputs.

Method: Use a pretrained latent flow-matching transformer as an image prior. The VAE decoder is trained to predict (i) an order field that encodes stroke sequence, (ii) a stroke mask, and (iii) a stroke segmentation. Segmentation is vectorized into polylines and sorted by the order field to produce an ordered vector sketch. The model supports both text-conditioned generation and derendering of images into ordered vectors, following text-specified draw-order instructions.

Result: Demonstrates feasibility of generating ordered vector sketches by learning an explicit order field and producing polylines ordered accordingly. Capable of producing sequences from text descriptions or by derendering images, with order guided by textual instructions.

Conclusion: The approach introduces a novel paradigm for vector sketch synthesis centered on a learnable 2D order field, enabling controllable, instruction-following vectorization and potential improvements in alignments between content and stroke order, with broad applicability to text-conditioned sketching and image-to-vector conversion.

Abstract: We invert the typical formulation of sketch generation: instead of drawing strokes in order, we predict a 2D field that defines the order in which strokes are drawn. We use a pretrained latent flow-matching transformer to supply the image prior to predict an intermediate representation, while training the VAE's decoder to predict the order field, stroke mask, and stroke segmentation. We vectorize the predicted segmentation into polylines and sort them by the field, producing an ordered vector sketch. Our model can predict an ordered vector sketch from a text description or derender an image into ordered vectors; for either, it follows text instructions specifying the order of drawing.

</details>


### [73] [Better Slots, Better Worlds: Representation Quality & Robustness in Object-Centric World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12078)
*Shukrullo Nazirjonov,Sai Prasanna,Anna Manasyan,Georg Martius*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OC world models boost planning when the object slots are well-bound; under distribution shifts, well-formed OC slots plus pretrained features yield robustness, while auxiliary proprioception/masking biases become unnecessary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the object-centric bias in world models truly aids planning and what drives its benefits, by controlling slot quality and generalization under distribution shift relative to scene-centric baselines.

Method: Controlled study of OCWMs for visual model-predictive control using offline trajectories, varying object-slot quality via unsupervised metrics (FG-ARI, mBO) and evaluating under distribution shifts; compare against scene-centric LeWM and DINO-WM; analyze dependence on auxiliary inputs (proprioception) and masking.

Result: Planning success scales with unsupervised slot-quality metrics (FG-ARI, mBO) with diminishing returns at high quality; with well-bound slots, auxiliary proprioception inputs and masking biases are largely unnecessary; under unseen distribution shifts, OCWM with well-bound slots is more robust than end-to-end LeWM, while DINO-WM remains competitive, highlighting pretrained features as key to robustness.

Conclusion: OC biases in world models improve planning when slots are well-bound, and pretrained features contribute substantially to robustness under distribution shifts; reducing reliance on additional inductive biases is possible with strong slot quality, though slot quality and pretraining are critical for generalization.

Abstract: Learning world models from offline trajectories enables agents to accomplish different tasks through planning. Object-centric (OC) representations, which decompose a scene into a set of slots that bind to its objects, have been proposed as an inductive bias for world models that are more sample-efficient and generalize better. Yet prior object-centric world models (OCWMs) take the slot encoder as given and evaluate only in-distribution, leaving open whether the object-centric bias actually delivers for planning and what within the OCWM drives it. We conduct a controlled study of OCWMs for visual model-predictive control along two axes: object-centric representation quality and generalization under distribution shift relative to scene-centric models. We find that (i) planning success correlates positively with unsupervised slot-quality metrics (FG-ARI, mBO), though the gains saturate at high slot quality; (ii) with well-bound slots, the auxiliary proprioception inputs and masking inductive bias that prior methods relied on become unnecessary; and (iii) under unseen distribution shifts, the OCWM with well-bound slots is more robust overall than the end-to-end trained scene-centric LeWM, while DINO-WM, built on similar frozen pretrained features, remains competitive -- pointing to pretrained features as a key contributor to robustness.

</details>


### [74] [Look What the Probes Dragged In! Real-World Chest X-ray Shortcuts in MedCLIP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12086)
*Nikolette Pedersen,Regitze Sydendal,Veronika Cheplygina,Théo Sourget*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MedCLIP shows high AUROC but poor calibration and layer-wise shortcuts across depths; exposes both localized and diffuse shortcuts, plus data quality issues in NIH-CXR14 and PadChest; emphasizes need for high-quality datasets and robust evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how real-world shortcuts manifest in CLIP-based medical models (MedCLIP and frozen ResNet-50) across layers and to assess calibration/robustness to spurious cues in medical imaging.

Method: Attach 17 linear probes to intermediate layers of a frozen ResNet-50 encoder within MedCLIP; train probes on NIH-CXR14 (pneumothorax) and PadChest (cardiomegaly, pneumothorax); evaluate with subgroup calibration and layer-wise confidence curves; supplement with manual image analysis to assess data quality.

Result: Final linear probes achieve high AUROC but exhibit poor calibration; layer-wise confidence analyses show shortcuts emerge at different depths: localized shortcuts (drains) in later layers; diffuse short cuts (scanner-specific noise) in earlier layers.

Conclusion: SOTA CLIP-based medical models remain vulnerable to shortcuts; dataset quality and annotation reliability are critical; emphasizes need for robust evaluation beyond AUROC and for higher-quality, well-annotated datasets; code available on GitHub.

Abstract: Vision-language models, such as contrastive language-image pre-training (CLIP)-based approaches, have reached state-of-the-art (SOTA) results in medical artificial intelligence. However, recent work reveals that CLIP-based models remain vulnerable to shortcuts. We investigate how real-world shortcuts manifest across different layers of the medical CLIP-based model, MedCLIP, and its vision encoder, a frozen ResNet-50. We attach 17 linear classification probes to the intermediate layers of the ResNet-50 and train them on three different dataset configurations and targets: NIH-CXR14 (pneumothorax) and PadChest (cardiomegaly and pneumothorax). This setup allows us to observe model behaviour during evaluation using subgroup-based calibration and layer-wise confidence curves. We find that the final linear probes achieve a high AUROC but poor calibration in the models. The layer-wise confidence analyses suggest that shortcuts emerge at different depths. Patterns consistent with localised shortcuts, such as drains, appear at later layers, while patterns consistent with diffuse shortcuts, such as scanner-specific noise patterns, emerge earlier, aligning with previous work. Finally, we conduct a manual analysis of the images, which reveals data quality issues in both NIH-CXR14 and PadChest. Our findings underscore that even SOTA models remain vulnerable to shortcuts, and the need for high-quality and well-annotated datasets to draw solid conclusions. Code can be found on our GitHub: https://github.com/nikodice4/MedCLIP_shortcuts.

</details>


### [75] [RA-ClipScore: Making Generative Model Evaluation More Interpretable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12088)
*Yifan Lu,Taras Kucherenko,Hedvig Kjellström,Judith Bütepage*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RA-CLIPScore is a new CLIP-based metric that also assesses spatial distribution alignment using dual prompts and local patch tokens, improving robustness and interpretability over FID and prior CLIP-based metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FID provides a single scalar score with limited diagnostics; CLIP-based metrics add semantic evaluation but struggle with spatial analysis and attribute-wise decoupling due to CLIP's training paradigm. There is a need for metrics that diagnose both attribute distribution and spatial priors in generated imagery.

Method: Introduce RA-CLIPScore with dual prompts to separate competing attributes and local patch tokens to capture fine-grained regional semantics. Compute alignment of generated images with the training data across attribute and spatial distributions, enabling regional analysis and detection of spatial biases.

Result: Extensive experiments show RA-CLIPScore yields more robust and interpretable evaluations than prior methods, especially under distribution misalignment or irrelevant attributes. It reveals spatial biases in generative models, and Regional Single Attribute Divergence based on RA-CLIPScore aligns better with human perception of visual diversity than existing semantic metrics.

Conclusion: RA-CLIPScore provides a stronger, more interpretable diagnostic for generative models by jointly evaluating attribute and spatial distribution alignment, outperforming traditional metrics and CLIP-based approaches in challenging settings.

Abstract: Generative models can produce images nearly indistinguishable from real data, yet rigorous and interpretable evaluation remains challenging. Conventional metrics such as FID provide only scalar scores with limited diagnostic insight. Widely adopted CLIP-based metrics enable semantic evaluation beyond simple training class labels, but inherit limitations from CLIP's training paradigm that restrict attribute-wise analysis. We propose RA-CLIPScore, a novel metric that mitigates these issues and extends CLIP-based evaluation to spatial distribution alignment, measuring whether generated objects adhere to the positional priors found in the training data. RA-CLIPScore introduces dual prompts to decouple competing attributes and leverages local patch tokens to capture fine-grained regional semantics. We evaluate image generative models on their ability to match both attribute and spatial distributions of the training data. Extensive experiments show that RA-CLIPScore provides more robust and interpretable evaluations than prior methods, particularly under distribution misalignment or partially irrelevant textual attributes. We further demonstrate how it reveals spatial biases in generative models. User evaluations confirm that Regional Single Attribute Divergence based on our RA-CLIPScore aligns more closely with human perception of visual diversity than existing semantic metrics.

</details>


### [76] [Avatar-Forever: Decoupled Parallel Training for High-Quality Real-Time Infinite Avatars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12107)
*Ruibin Li,Tao Yang,Zhiyuan Ma,Fangzhou Ai,Shilei Wen,Lei Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Decoupled parallel training for real-time infinite avatars: separate distillation-based generator training from a Recovery-oriented Rollout Training (RRT) long-horizon adapter; plus ForeverCache for chunk-wise caching; demonstrates high-quality, 27.2 FPS 768x512 on a single H100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address two main drawbacks of sequential distillation pipelines: (1) error/distribution shifts from early stages propagate and complicate training; (2) distillation-optimized objectives bias toward short-horizon quality and degrade with long rollouts.

Method: Two independent branches trained in parallel: (i) full-parameter distillation to train an efficient, high-quality generator; (ii) a lightweight long-horizon adapter trained with Recovery-oriented Rollout Training (RRT) for robustness in long-horizon, streaming conditions. Introduces ForeverCache, a chunk-wise feature caching mechanism to reduce redundant history computation during streaming inference. Built on a 22B video foundation model for unbounded audio-driven avatar generation with identity consistency, motion coherence, and visual fidelity; achieves end-to-end throughput of 27.2 FPS on a single H100 for 768x512 videos.

Result: Real-time, unbounded audio-driven avatar generation with maintained identity and motion coherence; high-resolution video output at 27.2 FPS on H100; demonstrates decoupled parallel training and caching that improve long-horizon robustness and streaming efficiency.
