Conclusion: A decoupled parallel training paradigm avoids conflicts between few-shot generation and long-horizon adaptation, simplifying training and enabling robust, real-time infinite avatar generation; ForeverCache further reduces computation during streaming inference.

Abstract: Existing streaming video systems often rely on sequential, distillation-centered training pipelines to enable few-step long-video generation. However, this paradigm suffers from two limitations. First, failures or distribution shifts introduced in earlier stages affect later optimization, complicating the training process to converge. Second, the distillation-centric objective favours short-term generation but is prone to quality degradation when autoregressive errors accumulate over long rollouts. We propose Avatar-Forever, a decoupled parallel training framework for high-quality real-time infinite interactive avatars. Instead of coupling generation efficiency and long-horizon robustness under a sequential distillation pipeline, we treat them as two independent capabilities that can be trained in parallel. One branch performs full-parameter distillation to train an efficient generator with high visual quality, while another trains a lightweight long-horizon adapter via Recovery-oriented Rollout Training (RRT), which improves generation robustness under long-horizon inference conditions. Our decoupled parallel training design simplifies the overall training process and avoids unnecessary objective conflicts between few-step generation and long-horizon adaptation. We further introduce ForeverCache, a chunk-wise feature caching mechanism to substantially reduce redundant history computation during streaming inference. Built upon a 22B video foundation model, Avatar-Forever supports unbounded audio-driven avatar generation while maintaining identity consistency, motion coherence, and visual fidelity, enabling an end-to-end throughput of high-resolution 768x512 videos at 27.2 FPS on a single H100 GPU and providing a practical path toward stable digital humans.

</details>


### [77] [SCOPE-Router: Cost-Aware Open-Set VLM Routing for Execution-Oriented Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12127)
*Tao Yu,Yifei Qu,Zhiqing Cui,Pengfei Zhou,Zhongtian Luo,Yujia Yang,Shenghua Chai,Haopeng Jin,Zhenghao Zhang,Xinming Wang,Hongzhu Yi,Wangbo Zhao,Zhenglin Wan,Yan Huang,Yeshani,Jinwen Luo,Yang You*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes a triptych of VLM routing innovations: a workflow benchmark, a dual-tower router with calibration-based behavior modeling, and a cost-aware objective; collectively improving routing under open-set and OOD conditions and enabling cost-aware decision making.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLM routing research is limited to traditional VQA evaluation, lacks calibration for open-set scenarios, and uses softmax-based training that dilutes multi-positive signals and ignores costs.

Method: 1) VLM-ExecRouterBench benchmark across Code, Agentic, and Search with 11 models across price range; 2) SCOPE-Router dual-tower router built on calibration-derived behavior profiles enabling new models to join without retraining; 3) CRM+RCCR cost-aware objective encoding cost preferences via per-pair independent scores, removing multi-positive dilution and regularizing queries by routing preferences.

Result: SCOPE-Router achieves best Rank Score on all three benchmarks, beating runner-up by 1.84 points in OOD and 6.75 points in doubly OOD open-set; CRM+RCCR applied to four routers yields Rank Score improvements of 1.25–6.21 points.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enhances VLM routing by providing standardized evaluation, scalable model integration without retraining, and cost-aware optimization, with empirical gains across benchmarks and routers.

Abstract: Model routing aims to select the most suitable model from a candidate pool for each query, balancing quality and cost. Existing VLM routing research is limited to traditional VQA evaluation, lacks systematic calibration optimization for open-set scenarios, and employs training objectives that dilute multi-positive signals via softmax normalization without incorporating cost. We address these limitations with three contributions: (1)VLM-ExecRouterBench, the first execution-oriented VLM routing benchmark covering Code, Agentic, and Search domains with 11 candidate models spanning nearly two orders of magnitude in pricing; (2)SCOPE-Router, a dual-tower router that matches queries to model behavior profiles constructed via hybrid calibration (random/diagnostic/diversity sampling), enabling new models to join routing without retraining; (3)CRM+RCCR, an architecture-agnostic cost-aware objective that encodes cost preference into continuous relevance targets through per-pair independent scoring, eliminating multi-positive dilution while regularizing queries with similar routing preferences to be closer in the routing space. Empirically, SCOPE-Router achieves the best Rank Score on all three benchmarks, surpassing the runner-up by 1.84 points under OOD settings and by 6.75 points under doubly OOD open-set evaluation. When applied to four diverse routers, CRM+RCCR improves Rank Score by 1.25--6.21 points.

</details>


### [78] [Autonomous Telerehabilitation via Skeletal Motion Prediction and Joint-Level Performance Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12145)
*Lara Pereira,João Ruivo Paulo,Pedro Santos,Paulo Peixoto*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Two-module telerehabilitation pipeline combining (i) marker-free RGB-based exercise quality classification using a self-attentive Bidirectional LSTM with MMD-NCA metric learning, and (ii) a graph-based short-term motion predictor producing per-joint deviation signals. Evaluated on PROZIS squats (96.45% mean-class accuracy) and Human3.6M (MPJPE 75.8 mm at 560 ms), outperforming baselines. Shows promise for autonomous, feedback-driven telerehabilitation; future work includes end-to-end integration and clinical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable autonomous rehabilitation systems that provide structured feedback without continuous therapist supervision by fusing action recognition and motion prediction on marker-free video.

Method: Module 1: self-attentive Bidirectional LSTM with MMD-NCA metric learning for exercise quality classification on marker-free RGB videos. Module 2: graph-based motion predictor forecasting 3D joint positions and computing per-joint position errors to generate localized deviation signals. Evaluations on PROZIS squat sequences and Human3.6M.

Result: Classifier achieves 96.45% mean-class accuracy on PROZIS squat sequences; STARS predictor attains MPJPE of 75.8 mm at 560 ms on Human3.6M, outperforming graph and recurrent baselines across horizons.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates feasibility of integrated recognition and prediction for autonomous, feedback-driven telerehabilitation, with deployment potential in assistive robotics and home-based rehab; calls for end-to-end integration and clinical validation.

Abstract: Autonomous rehabilitation systems must not only recognize human motion but also provide structured feedback to support users without continuous therapist supervision. This paper presents a telerehabilitation pipeline that integrates skeleton-based exercise quality assessment and short-term motion prediction into a two-module system operating on marker-free RGB video. A self-attentive Bidirectional LSTM performs exercise quality classification using MMD-NCA metric learning, while a graph-based motion prediction module computes per-joint position errors between predicted and observed poses, generating spatially localized deviation signals. Each module is evaluated independently on established benchmarks: the classifier achieves 96.45% mean-class accuracy on squat sequences from the PROZIS dataset, and the adopted STARS predictor achieves a mean MPJPE of 75.8 mm at 560 ms on Human3.6M, outperforming graph and recurrent baselines across all prediction horizons. The framework is designed for eventual deployment in assistive robotics and home-based rehabilitation contexts; end-to-end integration and clinical validation are important directions for future work. By combining motion recognition and prediction in a single system, this work contributes a step toward autonomous, feedback-driven telerehabilitation, for more accessible and scalable rehabilitation solutions.

</details>


### [79] [Understanding Why Foundation Models Work for Diffusion-Generated Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12155)
*Davide Cozzolino,Giovanni Poggi,Luisa Verdoliva*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Foundation-model detectors leverage non-semantic, low-to-mid frequency distributional discrepancies between real and diffusion-generated images, as shown by DDIM inversion, frequency swapping, and latent-space analysis; their decisions depend on inversion depth and reduced variance in regenerated images, indicating generalization beyond high-frequency artifact detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To uncover the cues and mechanisms by which vision foundation models detect AI-generated images and to explain their robustness and generalization across generators and degradations.

Method: 1) Build an ad hoc protocol using DDIM inversion and vary inversion depth to produce semi-identical synthetic copies of real images. 2) Apply frequency-swapping analysis to localize discriminative cues in frequency bands. 3) Conduct latent-space analysis to assess variance and effective dimensionality of regenerated images.

Result: Detector scores vary with inversion depth despite semantic identity, indicating reliance on non-semantic cues. Discriminative cues mainly reside in the low-to-mid frequency range, not solely in high-frequency artifacts. Generated images exhibit reduced variance and lower effective dimensionality, suggesting diffusion models do not fully capture real data variability.

Conclusion: Foundation-model detectors detect non-semantic distributional differences in the low-to-mid frequency spectrum, enabling robust detection across generators. This insight informs more interpretable forensic methods and potential improvements to detector design to emphasize these cues.

Abstract: Vision foundation models have recently emerged as powerful feature extractors for detecting AI-generated images, achieving strong generalization across generators and robustness to common image degradations. However, the reason behind their effectiveness is poorly understood. In this work, we investigate what cues are exploited by foundation-model-based detectors to distinguish real images from diffusion-generated ones. To this end, we design an ad hoc analysis protocol based on DDIM inversion. Given a real image we generate a sequence of synthetic copies by changing the depth of DDIM inversion. Even though most copies are semantically identical to the real reference, the detector score varies significantly across them due to subtle traces introduced by the diffusion synthesis, showing that its decision is not primarily driven by semantic failures. Through a frequency-swapping analysis, we further reveal that the discriminative cues exploited by the detectors are mainly localized in the low-to-mid frequency range, rather than only in the high-frequency range, as is the case for artifacts commonly associated with generative models. Finally, a latent-space analysis shows that regenerated images exhibit reduced variance and effective dimensionality, indicating that diffusion models do not fully reproduce the variability of real data. Overall, our results suggest that foundation-model-based detectors succeed by capturing non-semantic low-to-mid frequency distributional discrepancies between real and diffusion-generated images. These findings provide new insight into the robustness and generalization of such detectors and suggest directions for more interpretable forensic methods.

</details>


### [80] [Context Blindness in DPO: Mitigating Object Hallucination in MLLMs via Context-Calibrated Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12158)
*Byungoh Ko,Jinyoung Park,Jongha Kim,Jeehye Na,Jaewon Cho,Hyunwoo J. Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Contextual Preference Gain (CPG) is introduced to quantify how context improves a model's non-hallucinated preferences in multimodal LLMs; Context-Calibrated DPO (C^2-DPO) directly optimizes CPG while preserving preference order, yielding substantial hallucination reduction without harming reasoning, e.g., 36% relative on Object HalBench for Qwen2-VL-Instruct-2B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Object hallucination in multimodal LLMs; limitations of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and its variants in utilizing contextual information; need for a metric and method that leverages relevant context to reduce hallucinations.

Method: Define CPG as the gain in preferred responses when relevant context is provided; analyze DPO and variants for their CPG; develop C^2-DPO to maximize CPG while maintaining original preference ordering; apply across multiple benchmarks; provide code.

Result: CPG correlates with reduced hallucination; standard DPO exhibits limited CPG and underutilizes context; C^2-DPO significantly reduces hallucination across benchmarks; example: 36% relative reduction in Object HalBench hallucination rate for Qwen2-VL-Instruct-2B.

Conclusion: Maximizing contextual leverage via C^2-DPO is an effective approach to mitigate object hallucination in MLLMs without sacrificing reasoning capabilities; code available at provided GitHub.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made rapid progress, yet they still exhibit object hallucination, generating plausible but incorrect descriptions that are inconsistent with the visual input. Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) mitigates this by training models to prefer non-hallucinated responses over hallucinated ones, and recent efforts further enrich the preference data with relevant context. However, it remains unclear whether DPO actually leverages such context. To investigate this, we propose Contextual Preference Gain (CPG), a simple metric that measures how much a model's preference strengthens when relevant context is provided. We find that higher CPG consistently corresponds to lower hallucination, yet standard DPO and its variants exhibit only limited CPG, indicating that they underutilize contextual information and thus remain prone to hallucination. To address this, we propose Context-Calibrated DPO (C$^2$-DPO), which directly maximizes CPG while preserving the original preference ordering. Across multiple benchmarks, C$^2$-DPO substantially reduces hallucination without compromising general reasoning, relatively reducing the Object HalBench hallucination rate of Qwen2-VL-Instruct-2B by 36%. Code is available at https://github.com/mlvlab/C2-DPO

</details>


### [81] [TGRHuman: Text-Guided Realistic 3D Human Generation via Diffusion Renderer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12175)
*Muxin Zhang,Chaohui Yu,Yuanwang Yang,Min Wei,Zhuo Su,Kun Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TGRHuman decouples geometry and texture generation for text-to-3D human synthesis, enabling high-quality, consistent 3D humans with efficient explicit multi-view optimization, outperforming NeRF-based and other text-to-3D methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current text-to-3D human generation struggles with high-quality geometry/texture, 3D consistency, and inference efficiency, especially with NeRF-based implicit optimization. A decoupled, explicit multi-view approach is proposed to improve quality and speed.

Method: TGRHuman splits geometry and texture generation. Geometry uses a high-resolution multi-view normals generator plus a geometry-carving strategy to ensure view consistency and support loose clothing. Texture uses densely sampled surrounding views to produce spatially consistent RGB observations via a texture-prior acquisition strategy and a diffusion renderer, avoiding slow optimization.

Result: Experiments show high-quality, consistent geometry and texture with improved efficiency; TGRHuman outperforms existing text-to-3D human methods in both geometry and texture quality.

Conclusion: TGRHuman offers an effective, efficient framework for text-driven 3D human synthesis, achieving superior geometry and texture while maintaining 3D consistency.

Abstract: Realistic 3D human generation plays a crucial role in many graphics applications. However, current methods still struggle to generate high-quality human geometry and texture while maintaining 3D consistency and inference efficiency. In this work, we address these limitations by introducing TGRHuman, a novel approach for generating realistic 3D humans from text. Our method decouples geometry and texture generation to alleviate the issues commonly encountered in NeRF-based methods. Instead of relying on slow, implicit score-distillation-based optimization, we directly use explicit multi-view observation generation and optimization for efficient 3D synthesis. For geometry generation, we propose a high-resolution generative module for multi-view normals together with a geometry-carving strategy that preserves view consistency and supports loose clothing. For texture generation, we produce spatially consistent RGB observations from densely sampled surrounding views using a carefully designed texture-prior acquisition strategy and a diffusion renderer, enabling detailed human texture synthesis. Experiments show that our method can generate high-quality and consistent 3D human geometry and texture efficiently. TGRHuman outperforms existing text-to-3D human methods in both geometry and texture quality.

</details>


### [82] [Map-Det3D: Metric Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction Prior for Multi-view 3D Object Detection from Streaming Inputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12179)
*Yung-Hsu Yang,Luigi Piccinelli,Samuel Rota Bulò,Sunghwan Hong,Denis Rozumny,Johannes Schönberger,Zuria Bauer,Hermann Blum,Peter Kontschieder,Marc Pollefeys*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Map-Det3D: an online monocular 3D detector that fuses multi-view geometry from RGB into a metric 3D space using a reconstruction backbone, enabling direct 3D boxes without 2D-to-3D lifting and showing robust online performance and transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monocular 3D detection suffers from depth and absolute scale ambiguity; depth sensors add cost, power, and integration complexity. To achieve stable metric 3D detection without extra sensors, there is a need for reconstruction-informed priors that remain robust under domain shifts.

Method: Map short temporal window into multiple views and repurpose a feed-forward metric 3D reconstruction model as the geometric backbone, with object-aware tuning. The detector predicts boxes directly in metric 3D space, bypassing 2D-to-3D lifting.

Result: Experiments show strong online performance and robust transfer without adaptation across benchmarks; code and models are provided.

Conclusion: Training reconstruction priors for detection is a practical route to stable metric 3D detection from monocular video.

Abstract: Metric 3D object detection is a core capability for embodied agents, yet most reliable systems lean on depth sensors, trading away cost, power, and integration simplicity. This motivates monocular 3D detection, which avoids additional constraints, yet it faces a major obstacle: from a single image, depth, and especially absolute scale, are underconstrained. As a result, the prevailing pattern of detecting in 2D and then predicting 3D attributes is often brittle, since modest range errors can dominate 3D localization, and the learned scale prior can fail when cameras, motion, or environments undergo domain shifts. To address this, we propose Map-Det3D, an online multi-view 3D object detection model that brings detection directly into a 3D space reconstructed from RGB. We map a short temporal window into multiple views and repurpose a feed-forward metric 3D reconstruction model as our geometric backbone while tuning its object-aware capabilities. Building on this representation, Map-Det3D directly predicts boxes in metric 3D space, without the widely used 2D-to-3D lifting. Experiments across different benchmarks show that this design supports strong online performance and robust transfer without adaptation, suggesting that training reconstruction priors for detection is a practical route to stable metric 3D detection from monocular video. Code and models are available at https://royyang0714.github.io/Map-Det3D.

</details>


### [83] [GenFAR: A generalized representation of brain structure, derived from 49,246 multi-cohort MRIs via deep learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12185)
*Vishnu M. Bashyam,Guray Erus,Junhao Wen,Pratik Chaudhari,Randa Melhem,Sindhuja Govindarajan Tirumalai,Gareth Harman,Yong Fan,Colin L. Masters,Paul Maruff,Sterling C. Johnson,Jurgen Fripp,Duygu Tosun,John C. Morris,Daniel S. Marcus,Pamela LaMontagne,Tammie Benzinger,Susan R. Heckbert,Mark Espeland,Marilyn S. Albert,Andrew J. Saykin,Paul M. Thompson,Timothy J. Hohman,Susan M. Resnick,R. Nick Bryan,Murat Bilgel,Yang An,David A. Wolk,Li Shen,Haochang Shou,Ilya M. Nasrallah,Christos Davatzikos*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GenFAR: a modular, transfer-learning framework trained on 49k MRI from 11 cohorts, learning general brain representations via 17 tasks; introduces sequential task learning with Donor Score; identifies five core donor tasks; enhances downstream performance and data efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Task-specific deep learning models in neuroimaging limit knowledge transfer; a need for transferable, clinically informed representations learned from large, heterogeneous data to boost efficiency and accuracy of secondary predictors.

Method: Build GenFAR modular architecture; train on 49,246 individuals across 11 cohorts; 17 tasks spanning cognition, clinical, diagnosis, demographics, and biomarkers; use sequential learning to build on prior representations; analyze 5,000 task sequences to determine optimal sequence length (six); compute a Donor Score to quantify each task's contribution; identify five donor tasks (Age, AD/MCI, MMSE, Hypertension, Hyperlipidemia) as base; evaluate representation on downstream tasks and secondary predictors; assess sample efficiency and accuracy improvements.

Result: Aggregated, focused feature sets capturing clinically/biologically relevant brain representations; optimal sequence length found to be six tasks; Donor Score highlights five core donor tasks; base donor tasks underpin the sequential model; learned representations improve downstream task performance and data efficiency, and transfer to tasks beyond the training set.

Conclusion: GenFAR provides transferable, clinically informed brain representations via modular sequential transfer learning on large multi-cohort MRI data, enabling improved performance and sample efficiency for downstream neuroimaging tasks.

Abstract: Deep learning models for neuroimaging have largely been developed for individual tasks, limiting knowledge transfer across applications. Here we introduce GenFAR, a modular deep learning framework that learns general, clinically informed features from brain MRIs. We trained this modular architecture on 49,246 individuals across 11 cohorts, using 17 diverse classification and regression tasks spanning cognition, clinical, diagnosis, demographics, and biomarkers. This yields aggregated, focused feature sets that capture rich, clinically- and biologically-relevant brain representations. We developed a sequential learning approach where tasks progressively build on previously learned representations. Through an analysis of 5,000 task sequences, we identified an optimal sequence length of six tasks and introduced a Donor Score metric to quantify each task's contribution to downstream performance. This analysis revealed five consistently strong donor tasks (Age, AD/MCI, MMSE, Hypertension, Hyperlipidemia) that formed the base of our sequential model. We demonstrated the utility of our learned representation, in various tasks beyond those included in the training set, to serve as the foundation for specialized secondary predictors. We further showed that using the learned feature representation can substantially increase the sample efficiency of secondary deep learning training tasks and models, as well as improve their accuracy.

</details>


### [84] [HSTGFormer: Hyper Spatial-Temporal Graph Transformer for 3D Human Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12187)
*Ruochen Li,Shuang Chen,Wenke E,Farshad Arvin,Amir Atapour-Abarghouei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces HSTGFormer, a graph-enhanced Transformer that unifies spatial-temporal reasoning for monocular 3D human pose estimation via Hyper Spatial-Temporal Graph and Adaptive Dual-Scale Temporal Graph, achieving high accuracy with efficiency on standard benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing monocular 3D pose estimation methods often separate spatial and temporal reasoning, potentially weakening interdependencies and discarding frame-level structural information. A unified, local, structure-aware approach over joint-time nodes is desired.

Method: Proposes Hyper Spatial-Temporal Graph (HSTG) that extends per-frame skeleton graphs to temporal neighborhoods for localised coupled reasoning; employs Adaptive Dual-Scale Temporal Graph (ADSTG) to model both short- and long-range temporal dependencies; includes a lightweight node-wise fusion to combine the two representations for each joint-time node.

Result: Achieves strong accuracy and computational efficiency on benchmark datasets Human3.6M and MPI-INF-3DHP.

Conclusion: HSTGFormer effectively models spatial-temporal dependencies through localized graph reasoning and dual-scale temporal analysis, providing an efficient and accurate solution for monocular 3D pose estimation.

Abstract: Transformer-based methods have achieved strong performance in monocular 3D human pose estimation, but most existing approaches organise spatial and temporal reasoning as separate stages, which may weaken unified spatial-temporal interdependencies inherent in human motion and compress frame-level structural information before temporal modelling. In this paper, we propose HSTGFormer, a graph-enhanced Transformer framework that reformulates spatial-temporal reasoning as localised coupled graph aggregation over joint-time nodes. Specifically, HSTGFormer introduces a Hyper Spatial-Temporal Graph (HSTG), which decomposes global spatial-temporal reasoning into local spatial-temporal receptive fields around individual joint-time nodes by extending per-frame skeleton graphs into temporal neighbourhoods, thereby enabling structure-aware coupled reasoning while preserving local structural motion information. It further incorporates an Adaptive Dual-Scale Temporal Graph (ADSTG) to capture joint-specific temporal dependencies over complementary short- and long-range windows. A lightweight node-wise fusion module further adaptively integrates the two graph representations for each joint-time node. Experiments on Human3.6M and MPI-INF-3DHP show that HSTGFormer achieves strong accuracy with high computational efficiency.

</details>


### [85] [M-Net: Integrating Spectral Features and Physical Field Operators into Deep Learning for Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12196)
*Jing Zhu,Ye Wang,Fumin Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A math-augmented network (M-Net) augments U-Net with spectral and vector calculus priors, yielding improved segmentation on LiTS, KiTS, BraTS; ablations show the condition-number feature and Math-Attention Gate contribute to gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Purely data-driven methods miss exploitable mathematical structure in medical images; incorporating inductive biases from linear algebra and field operators can complement CNN learning.

Method: Integrate three priors into U-Net: (1) differentiable continuous spectral feature via condition number of centered local pixel matrices; (2) divergence and a discrete curl-like boundary irregularity operator computed from image gradients; (3) Math-Attention Gate (MAG) to fuse these features with CNN features at skip connections; evaluate on LiTS, KiTS, BraTS.

Result: Dice: 78.42% (liver), 76.15% (kidney), 83.67% (brain tumor). Improvements over U-Net: +12.37, +3.52, +5.55. Ablation: condition-number feature adds 2.14% vs binary invertibility; MAG adds 1.45% over simple concatenation.

Conclusion: Mathematical inductive biases are effective complementary information for medical image segmentation; continuous condition-number feature provides better gradient information; MAG preserves priors throughout network; suggests integrating linear algebra and vector calculus into deep architectures.

Abstract: Purpose: Deep learning-based medical image segmentation has achieved remarkable success, yet purely data-driven approaches often fail to exploit the rich mathematical structure inherent in medical images. We investigate whether explicit mathematical inductive biases, specifically matrix spectral analysis and vector calculus operators, can enhance segmentation beyond data-driven learning alone. Methods: We propose M-Net (Math-Augmented Network), which integrates three complementary mathematical priors into U-Net: (1) continuous spectral features derived from the condition number of centered local pixel matrices, providing a differentiable measure of texture ill-conditioning; (2) physical field operators (divergence and a discrete curl-like boundary irregularity operator) computed from image gradient fields, capturing focal intensity extrema and edge non-smoothness; and (3) a Math-Attention Gate (MAG) that adaptively fuses mathematical features with CNN-extracted deep features at skip connections. Results: Experiments on three benchmarks (LiTS, KiTS, and BraTS) show that M-Net achieves Dice scores of 78.42%, 76.15%, and 83.67%, outperforming baseline U-Net by 12.37%, 3.52%, and 5.55% on liver, kidney, and brain tumor segmentation, respectively. Ablations reveal that the condition-number feature contributes a 2.14% gain over binary invertibility features, while MAG adds 1.45% over simple concatenation. Conclusion: M-Net establishes that mathematical inductive biases provide effective complementary information for medical image segmentation. The continuous condition-number feature offers superior gradient information over discrete alternatives, and MAG preserves these priors throughout the network. This work opens avenues for integrating linear algebra and vector calculus into deep architectures for medical imaging.

</details>


### [86] [GeoFlow: Efficient Driving Video Generation via Geometry-Aligned Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12203)
*Jiazheng Liu,Hang Li,Jiawei Zhang,Jiahe Li,Xiaohan Yu,Shengyin Fan,Jin Zheng,Xiao Bai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GeoFlow introduces a geometry-aligned prior (GAP) for driving video generation, leveraging multi-view geometry and spatially-adaptive noise to reduce sampling steps and improve efficiency in training and inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard diffusion/flow-based driving video generation initializes frames from independent Gaussian noise, ignoring rich spatiotemporal correlations, leading to long sampling trajectories and geometric inconsistencies.

Method: Construct a Geometry-Aligned Prior (GAP) by combining multi-view geometry with spatially-adaptive noise injection to align the source distribution with the data distribution; integrate GAP into baseline diffusion/flow models; perform targeted fine-tuning to enable few-step generation; fully converge training to drastically reduce inference steps.

Result: Experiments show that a few hours of fine-tuning on baseline models significantly improves quality for few-step generation; with fully converged training, the required number of inference steps for state-of-the-art video generation is drastically reduced.

Conclusion: Geometry-aware priors bridge the gap between source and data distributions, enabling efficient driving video generation with fewer inference steps and more consistent geometry.

Abstract: Generative models like Diffusion Models and Flow Matching have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in synthesizing high-fidelity driving videos, but are severely constrained by high inference latency due to the requirement of extensive sampling steps. We argue that this inefficiency stems from the prevailing reliance on a standard Gaussian source distribution, where consecutive frames are initialized as independent Gaussian noise. This paradigm disregards the rich spatiotemporal correlations inherent in driving videos, compelling the model to regenerate deterministic scene structures existing in previous frames from noise, which is both computationally redundant and prone to geometric inconsistency. To address this problem, we propose GeoFlow, a novel framework designed to achieve efficient driving video generation by harnessing explicit geometric priors. Instead of sampling from standard Gaussian noise, we leverage multi-view geometry and spatially-adaptive noise injection to construct a Geometry-Aligned Prior (GAP) distribution as starting point. This initialization bridges the gap between source distribution and data distribution, yielding a significantly straighter and shorter sampling trajectory. Extensive experiments demonstrate that GeoFlow can achieve remarkable efficiency of both training and inference: merely several hours of fine-tuning on baseline models can significantly boost few-step generation quality, while fully converged training drastically reduces number of inference steps required for state-of-the-art video generation.

</details>


### [87] [Generation as Auxiliary Supervision: Enhancing Visual Understanding at Zero Inference Overhead via Decoupled Embedding Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12209)
*Zhongbin Guo,Jiahao Xie,Dongling Xiao,Qianle Wang,Ruiqi Lu,Xiaomin He,Wanxuan Sun,Cheng Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GAS uses a generation-guided training signal via a cross-modal Next Embedding Prediction (NEP) task within a decoupled Mixture-of-Transformers to strengthen the visual representation in multimodal models, yielding improved perception and spatial understanding with no inference overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Align generation objectives with continuous visual representations in multimodal LLMs and transfer generation benefits to understanding without incurring extra inference cost.

Method: Adopt NEP as a cross-modal generation task inside a decoupled Mixture-of-Transformers: a shared lower trunk with parallel upper layers; generation losses update the shared visual pathway but do not backpropagate into understanding layers; design correlated generation tasks requiring deep cognitive grounding to maximize synergy.

Result: Improved aggregate multimodal understanding across scales, with the most reliable gains in perception and spatial comprehension; the auxiliary generation branch is discarded at inference, yielding zero overhead; extensive comparisons and analyses elucidate when generation helps.

Conclusion: Generation-guided training is a practical route to stronger multimodal understanding, enabling generation benefits to enrich perception/spatial tasks during training without affecting inference cost; promising across model scales and training stages, subject to further empirical validation.

Abstract: While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved remarkable progress, visual understanding and generation are typically treated as divergent objectives. Existing unified frameworks often rely on discrete visual tokenization or diffusion objectives whose generative targets differ from the continuous representations consumed by visual understanding models, making direct transfer to enhance existing pretrained MLLMs non-trivial. In this work, we present GAS, a generation-guided training framework that reinterprets visual generation as auxiliary supervision for representation learning. Concretely, GAS adapts Next Embedding Prediction (NEP) as a cross-modal generation paradigm within a decoupled Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) architecture. By maintaining a shared lower trunk and parallel upper layers, GAS lets generation losses enrich the shared visual pathway with finer spatial precision and stronger visual retention while shielding the upper understanding layers from direct generation gradients. To maximize this synergy, we further construct highly correlated generation tasks that demand deep cognitive grounding rather than generic synthesis alone. Across model scales and training stages, GAS improves aggregate multimodal understanding, with its most reliable gains on perception and spatial comprehension. Crucially, because the auxiliary generation branch is discarded after training, these gains incur zero inference overhead. Extensive controlled comparisons and representation-level analyses further clarify when and why generation-guided training benefits understanding, and demonstrate the feasibility of generation-guided training as a practical route to stronger multimodal understanding.

</details>


### [88] [SCOUT: Unlocking Enhanced Spatial Reasoning via Structured Chain-of-Thought and Multi-Objective Process Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12220)
*Zile Zhou,Huining Yuan,Weichen Zhang,Xinlei Chen,Xiao-ping Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SCOUT introduces a structured chain-of-thought framework with explicit 3D perception for robust spatial reasoning in vision-language models, paired with a multi-objective RL training regime and a synthetic SCOUT-24k dataset; it yields substantial gains on general and complex spatial tasks and even surpasses GPT-4o at 7B, with strong out-of-domain generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address a bottleneck in robust spatial reasoning in vision-language models, where existing RL methods suffer from poor credit assignment across reasoning steps and structured approaches miss depth perception necessary for 3D understanding.

Method: A structured Chain-of-Thought (CoT) framework that models 3D environmental perception; a novel reinforcement learning algorithm with multi-objective process rewards and a tailored advantage estimation method; a synthetic SCOUT-24k dataset for structured spatial reasoning CoT.

Result: SCOUT-3B achieves 16.85% improvement on general spatial benchmarks and 6.3% on complex spatial tasks; SCOUT-7B outperforms GPT-4o by 4.28%; SCOUT-7B trained on single image shows robust out-of-domain generalization to multi-image and video scenarios.

Conclusion: SCOUT represents a significant step toward next-generation spatially-aware vision-language models, demonstrating strong performance gains, scalable model sizes, and generalization capabilities.

Abstract: Existing Vision-Language Models (VLMs) exhibits a critical bottleneck in robust spatial reasoning. Recent reinforcement learning (RL) methods aim to close this gap with verifiable outcomes, yet they suffer from poor credit assignment across intermediate reasoning steps. Concurrently, structured reasoning approaches overlook the critical depth perception necessary for comprehensive 3D understanding. To address these challenges, we propose SCOUT (Structured Chain-Of-Thought Utilizing Process-Supervised RL Training). Specifically, we design a structured Chain-of-Thought (CoT) framework that explicitly models 3D environmental perception to ensure robust spatial understanding and reasoning. Furthermore, we introduce a novel RL algorithm featuring multi-objective process rewards and a tailored advantage estimation method, facilitating fine-grained credit assignment across distinct segments of the reasoning trajectory. To support our framework, we develop SCOUT-24k, a structured spatial reasoning CoT dataset synthesized through a customized pipeline. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that SCOUT-3B improves upon baseline models by 16.85% and 6.3% on general spatial benchmarks and complex spatial reasoning tasks respectively. Notably, our larger SCOUT-7B even outperforms GPT-4o by a margin of 4.28%. Moreover, despite being trained exclusively on single image, SCOUT-7B exhibits robust out-of-domain generalization to multi-image and video scenarios. These empirical results render SCOUT as a critical step towards next generation of spatially-aware VLMs.

</details>


### [89] [Few-Shot Ordinal Learning for Day-Wise Freshness Estimation with Hyperspectral Fish Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12230)
*Kazi Nabiul Alam,Pooneh Bagheri Zadeh,Akbar Sheikh-Akbari*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A first few-shot learning framework for hyperspectral imaging (HSI) based food freshness estimation, addressing per-fillet variability and label scarcity. It uses episodic tasks per fillet, a CORAL-style ordinal head with cumulative thresholding, plus monotonicity and smoothness constraints. On a 16-day salmon dataset with unseen fillets, it achieves MAE 1.58 days and 72.3% accuracy within ±2 days using only three labeled days per fillet, outperforming regression and label-distribution baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome inter-fillet variability and scarce labeled data in HSI-based freshness prediction. Existing deep learning methods rely on full supervision and dense annotations per product, which is impractical for per-fillet labeling.

Method: Proposes a few-shot learning framework where each fillet is an episodic task. Employs a CORAL-style ordinal prediction head to model the aging as a cumulative threshold process. Adds biologically grounded constraints for monotonicity and embedding smoothness to guide plausible freshness trajectories.

Result: On a 16-day salmon HSI dataset under an unseen-fillet protocol, the approach obtains mean absolute error (MAE) of 1.58 days and 2-day accuracy of 72.3% using only three labeled days per fillet, substantially surpassing scalar regression and label-distribution baselines under the same protocol.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the viability of few-shot learning for HSI-based food quality estimation and suggests that ordinal, monotonic, and smoothness constraints can substantially reduce labeling burden while maintaining predictive performance; potential applicability to other perishables and broader HSI-based quality tasks.

Abstract: Non-destructive food quality assessment has increasingly benefited from hyperspectral imaging (HSI), which captures spectral signatures linked to biochemical changes during storage. Estimating day-wise freshness, however, remains challenging owing to strong inter-fillet variability and scarce labelled data per product. All existing deep learning approaches for HSI-based freshness prediction operate under full supervision, requiring densely annotated training sets that are costly to obtain at the individual-product level. We introduce, to the best of our knowledge, the first few-shot learning framework for HSI-based food quality estimation. Each fillet defines a distinct episodic task, and a CORAL-style ordinal prediction head captures the ranked nature of freshness progression through cumulative threshold modelling. Biologically grounded monotonicity and embedding smoothness constraints further guide predictions toward plausible trajectories. On a 16-day salmon HSI dataset under a strict unseen-fillet protocol, our method achieves a mean absolute error of 1.58 days and 2-day accuracy of 72.3% with only three labelled days per fillet, substantially outperforming scalar regression and label-distribution baselines under an identical unseen-fillet protocol.

</details>


### [90] [ScaleVid: Geometry-Aware Video Object Scaling with Mesh-Free Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12232)
*Youze Huang,Penghui Ruan,Bojia Zi,Xianbiao Qi,Shihao Zhao,Rong Xiao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A two-stage, geometry-aware video object scaling framework using pseudo-sources to decouple foreground transformation from background preservation, achieving geometry-consistent, temporally coherent scaling without explicit 3D reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable geometry-aware, temporally coherent video object scaling that surpasses 2D text-guided methods, depth-guided coarse control, and costly mesh-based 3D reconstruction by avoiding explicit 3D inference at test time.

Method: Progressive two-stage training with pseudo-source reconstruction. Stage 1 learns planar foreground-background transformations for robust composition; Stage 2 introduces object-centric 3D deformation guidance for geometry-aware scaling. The approach does not rely on mesh-pixel alignment or explicit 3D reconstruction during inference. Training uses geometrically perturbed pseudo-sources derived from real videos, with the original complete videos as reconstruction targets. Evaluation uses paired-geometry and real-background benchmarks, including in-the-wild videos.

Result: Demonstrates superior geometric consistency, foreground fidelity, and background preservation, with faster and more practical inference than methods requiring explicit 3D reconstruction. Verified on in-the-wild videos across constructed benchmarks.

Conclusion: The framework enables realistic, geometry-aware video scaling without explicit 3D reconstruction, offering robust performance and practical inference for real-video synthesis.

Abstract: Geometry-aware video object scaling aims to anisotropically resize the object along object-centric axes while preserving geometric plausibility, temporal coherence, and background consistency. Existing text-guided methods mainly operate in the 2D image plane, while depth-guided approaches provide coarse control and mesh-based methods require costly 3D reconstruction. We present a progressive two-stage training framework that decouples geometry-aware foreground transformation from background preservation and realistic video composition, without mesh-pixel alignment and explicit 3D reconstruction at inference. In both stages, geometrically perturbed pseudo-sources are constructed from real videos, while the original complete videos are retained as reconstruction targets. The first stage uses planar transformations to learn robust foreground-background composition, whereas the second introduces object-centric 3D deformation guidance for geometry-aware scaling. This pseudo-source reconstruction formulation enables real-video synthesis without paired real-world scaling targets. We construct complementary paired-geometry and real-background benchmarks and further evaluate on in-the-wild videos. Extensive experiments demonstrate superior geometric consistency, foreground fidelity, and background preservation, together with faster and more practical inference than methods requiring explicit 3D reconstruction.

</details>


### [91] [HAMP-LIC: Hessian-Aware Mixed-Precision Post-Training Quantization for Learned Image Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12239)
*Yuefeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A Hessian-aware mixed-precision post-training quantization (PTQ) framework for learned image compression (LIC) that enables aggressive compression with minimal rate-distortion loss and cross-platform reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LIC models achieve strong rate-distortion performance but suffer high computational complexity and cross-platform encoding-decoding mismatches. Uniform fixed-precision quantization at low bit widths degrades quality because it ignores layer-wise quantization sensitivities.

Method: A four-stage optimization: (1) estimate block-wise sensitivities from the Hessian trace to capture second-order importance; (2) a task-aware refinement module adjusts sensitivities by jointly considering quantization distortion and rate-distortion performance; (3) allocate bit widths under a global model-size constraint guided by the refined sensitivities; (4) block-wise reconstruction using a small calibration set to suppress quantization error.

Result: On representative LIC models (Minnen2018, Cheng2020), HAMP-LIC achieves up to 4.85× model compression with only 0.59% BD-rate loss, outperforms existing fixed- and mixed-precision PTQ methods across multiple datasets, and eliminates cross-platform encoding-decoding errors.

Conclusion: HAMP-LIC provides an effective, hardware-robust PTQ framework enabling efficient and accurate low-bit LIC deployment by incorporating Hessian-based sensitivity, task-aware refinement, constrained bit-width allocation, and calibration-based reconstruction.

Abstract: Use this plain-text version for the arXiv abstract field: Learned image compression (LIC) models achieve strong rate-distortion performance but are hindered by high computational complexity and encoding-decoding mismatches across heterogeneous hardware platforms. Uniform fixed-precision quantization alleviates these issues but suffers severe quality degradation at low bit widths because it ignores differences in the quantization sensitivities of individual layers. To enable efficient and accurate low-bit deployment of pretrained LIC models, we propose HAMP-LIC, a Hessian-aware mixed-precision post-training quantization (PTQ) framework with a four-stage optimization strategy. First, block-wise sensitivity is estimated from the Hessian trace to capture second-order importance. Second, a task-aware refinement module adjusts these sensitivities by jointly considering quantization distortion and rate-distortion performance. Third, guided by the refined sensitivity profile, bit widths are allocated under a global model-size constraint to balance efficiency and reconstruction quality. Finally, block-wise reconstruction using a small calibration set further suppresses quantization error. Experiments on representative LIC models, including Minnen2018 and Cheng2020, demonstrate that HAMP-LIC achieves up to 4.85x model compression with as little as 0.59% BD-rate loss. It consistently outperforms existing fixed- and mixed-precision PTQ methods across multiple datasets while completely eliminating cross-platform encoding-decoding errors.

</details>


### [92] [Automated Borehole Core Analysis with Report-Derived Weak Labels and Supervised Crack Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12252)
*Usama Imdad,Ali Khan,Luke Lu,Zubair Khalid,Arif Mahmood*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Hybrid weakly supervised + fully supervised crack segmentation for borehole core images; leverages report-derived spacing labels and a gated U-Net to achieve strong crack segmentation and reasonable alignment with log-derived references.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the absence of native pixel-level crack annotations in borehole archives by exploiting existing text reports for spacing supervision and leveraging annotated imagery for precise crack localization.

Method: 1) Extract spacing categories from log text to create weak interval-level labels; train a DINO encoder on unlabeled core crops and use a manually verified subset to identify label inconsistencies. 2) Manually annotate 5,087 core-row images for fully supervised crack segmentation using a gated U-Net that fuses PiDiNet edge maps with Mask R-CNN masks via a learned spatial gate. Deterministic post-processing converts crack locations to spacing categories. Separate rule-based branches estimate core-relative bedding angles and lithological color descriptors, compared against log reports.

Result: Segmentation: F1 = 0.860; crack-class IoU = 0.754 (highest among evaluated configurations). Spacing categories derived from post-processing. Bedding angle agreement with log reports: 75.4%; lithological color descriptor agreement: 84.7% (on 1,200 evaluated images). The references are from reports, so agreement reflects consistency with recorded observations rather than independent physical validation.

Conclusion: The framework effectively combines weak supervision from report text with fully supervised segmentation for crack localization, enabling automated extraction of defect-spacing information from borehole archives. Validation is restricted to consistency with report-derived references rather than ground-truth physical measurements; potential improvements include broader validation and ablation studies.

Abstract: Borehole archives commonly contain core tray photographs and corresponding digital log reports, but no native pixel-level crack annotations. We investigate two complementary approaches for extracting defect-spacing information from these archives. First, structured spacing categories recovered from the report text layer provide weak interval-level labels for classification. A DINO encoder trained on unlabeled core crops supplies domain-specific representations, and a manually verified subset is used to identify label inconsistencies. Second, we manually annotate 5,087 extracted core-row images and evaluate fully supervised crack-segmentation models. Our gated U-Net combines PiDiNet edge maps with Mask R-CNN masks through a learned spatial gating mechanism. This configuration achieves an F1 score of 0.860 and a crack-class IoU of 0.754, the highest result among the evaluated segmentation configurations. Deterministic post-processing converts predicted crack locations into defect-spacing categories. Separate rule-based branches estimate core-relative bedding angles and lithological color descriptors; their predictions agree with log-report references on 75.4% and 84.7% of 1,200 evaluated images, respectively. Because these references are extracted from existing reports, the reported values measure agreement with recorded geological observations rather than independent physical accuracy. The resulting framework combines report-derived weak supervision for spacing classification with fully supervised segmentation for image-based crack localization.

</details>


### [93] [Diagram-MMU: A Multi-Modal Benchmark for Scientific Diagrams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12262)
*Weihao Bo,Shan Zhang,Yanpeng Sun,Jie Liu,Yongke Yao,Jinhao Du,Wei He,Kai Zou,Zechao Li,Jingdong Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces Diagram-MMU, a 3.7k diagram benchmark with 18.3k questions for evaluating MLLMs on diagram parsing/understanding across six domains. Findings: diagram-to-code is harder than diagram QA; agentic settings help parsing/editing but harm QA; Claude-4 Opus performs best across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As multimodal LLMs advance in scientific writing, there is a need to rigorously assess and improve diagram understanding and the ability to convert diagrams into code (e.g., LaTeX TikZ). The study exposes gaps in diagram-to-code generation and the robustness of QA under agentic prompting.

Method: Construct a multi-domain diagram benchmark (Diagram-MMU) with 3.7k curated diagrams and 18.3k human-validated questions across six domains; evaluate 12 MLLMs on three tasks—diagram-to-code parsing, diagram-to-code editing, and diagram question answering—under agentic settings per task.

Result: Diagram-to-code tasks are more challenging than diagram QA; models reason over diagrams but struggle to parse/edit them. Under agentic settings, parsing/editing improve while QA degrades; Claude-4 Opus consistently improves across all three tasks.

Conclusion: There is a need for methods to boost diagram-to-code generation in MLLMs. Agentic prompting entails trade-offs across tasks. Claude-4 Opus demonstrates robust cross-task improvements. Project page: https://vi-ocean.github.io/projects/diagram-mmu.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have been growing the capability for scientific writing and collaboration. For example, OpenAI Prism is a free workspace for scientific writing and collaboration. One important feature in Prism is turning scientific diagrams directly into LaTeX TikZ code. In this paper, we build a benchmark, Diagram-MMU, a multi-modal benchmark designed to assess MLLMs' ability for scientific diagram parsing and understanding. Diagram-MMU features 3.7k curated diagrams and 18.3k human-validated questions across six domains. It evaluates MLLMs on three tasks common in vibe writing workspaces: diagram-to-code parsing, diagram-to-code editing, and diagram question answering, alongside agentic settings per task. The evaluation of 12 MLLMs reveals that diagram-to-code tasks are more challenging than diagram question answering: models can reason well over diagrams but struggle to parse and edit them, underscoring the need for methods to enhance MLLMs' capability in diagram-to-code generation. Under agentic settings, most models improve parsing and editing performance but degrade on question answering, while Claude-4.6 Opus consistently improves across all three tasks. Project Page: https://vi-ocean.github.io/projects/diagram-mmu.

</details>


### [94] [A Neighborhood Attention Transformer Network for Enhanced 3D Segmentation of the Left Anterior Descending Artery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12274)
*Rafi Ibn Sultan,Chengyin Li,Yiannos Demetriou,Ahmed I. Ghanem,Joshua P. Kim,Justine Cunningham,Hassan Bagher-Ebadian,Dongxiao Zhu,Kundan S. Thind*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Transformer-based NA-UNETR for LAD segmentation in low-contrast 3D CT; uses Neighborhood Attention and Dilated NA blocks with LoRA fine-tuning and uncertainty-guided loss; achieves improved Dice and boundary metrics over nnU-Net and Swin UNETR; validated on internal and ImageCAS datasets; ablations support key components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Left Anterior Descending artery is extremely small, has poor soft-tissue contrast, and shows high inter-patient variability; accurate LAD delineation is essential for cardiac-sparing radiotherapy planning, but manual contours have limited agreement. There is a need for a model that can leverage local detail and global context while performing well under data scarcity.

Method: Propose NA-UNETR, a 3D transformer-based segmentation model with Neighborhood Attention (NA) and Dilated NA (DiNA) blocks to capture fine local structure and long-range context. Pretrained on 1,000 CTA volumes of general coronary anatomy; fine-tuned with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) for parameter-efficient adaptation on 20 free-breathing institutional CT scans. Training uses a composite Dice-Focal and Hausdorff loss, dynamically balanced via homoscedastic uncertainty.

Result: NA-UNETR achieved 45.64% Dice, 38.16 mm HD95, and 10.01 mm ASD on the internal dataset, improving Dice by 3.10 points over nnU-Net and reducing HD95 by 2.96 mm compared with Swin UNETR; demonstrated strongest boundary accuracy and improved centerline stability. On ImageCAS, it achieved 79.49% Dice, 8.89 mm HD95, and 1.02 mm ASD. Ablation studies showed contributions from residual blocks, variable kernels, and uncertainty-weighted loss.

Conclusion: NA-UNETR balances local precision and global context for thin, low-contrast LAD structures, yielding a computationally efficient framework suitable for substructure-level cardiac segmentation in radiotherapy planning.

Abstract: Background: Accurate segmentation of the Left Anterior Descending (LAD) artery in 3D free-breathing, non-contrast CT is critical for cardiac dose sparing in thoracic radiotherapy. The LAD is extremely small, has poor soft-tissue contrast, and varies substantially across patients; even manual contours show limited inter-observer agreement, underscoring the ambiguity of the vessel boundaries. Purpose: To develop a transformer-based framework that improves LAD delineation in low-contrast, imbalanced CT through local-global context modeling and uncertainty-guided optimization. Methods: We propose NA-UNETR, a 3D transformer-based segmentation model whose Neighborhood Attention (NA) and Dilated NA (DiNA) blocks jointly capture fine structural detail and long-range context. Given the scarcity of annotated LAD data, the model is pretrained on 1,000 CTA volumes of general coronary anatomy and fine-tuned with LoRA-based parameter-efficient adaptation on 20 free-breathing institutional CT scans. A composite Dice-Focal and Hausdorff loss, dynamically balanced via homoscedastic uncertainty, improves overlap and boundary accuracy. Results: NA-UNETR reached 45.64% Dice, 38.16 mm HD95, and 10.01 mm ASD, improving Dice by 3.10 percentage points over nnU-Net and reducing HD95 by 2.96 mm relative to Swin UNETR, with the strongest boundary accuracy among all models and improved centerline stability. On ImageCAS it achieved 79.49% Dice, 8.89 mm HD95, and 1.02 mm ASD. Ablations confirmed that residual blocks, variable kernels, and uncertainty-weighted loss each contributed. Conclusions: NA-UNETR balances local precision and global context for thin, low-contrast LAD structures, offering a computationally efficient framework for substructure-level cardiac segmentation in radiotherapy planning.

</details>


### [95] [XYZFlow:Scaling Multi dimensional Shortcut Flows for Efficient Generative Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12276)
*Jinxiu Liu,Xuanming Liu,Kangfu Mei,Yandong Wen,Weiyang Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: XYZFlow: multidimensional scaling of flow matching for efficient high-fidelity image generation, using temporal (full denoising history) and spatial (Next Shortcut Prediction) conditioning to improve speed/quality without heavy teacher distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models yield high-quality images but are slow due to iterative sampling. Distilling pretrained models into few-step samplers is brittle and depends on teacher quality. A method that improves efficiency without heavy distillation is needed.

Method: Introduce XYZFlow with two orthogonal dimensions: temporal scaling using non-Markovian conditioning on the full denoising history, and spatial scaling via Next Shortcut Prediction that sequentially generates patches using prior patches' denoising trajectories as priors.

Result: XYZFlow achieves state-of-the-art performance with 7.2–8.5× teacher speedups and competitive FID. Next Shortcut Prediction delivers superior quality–latency trade-offs compared to model scaling or step reduction.

Conclusion: Rethinking efficient generation as multidimensional flow matching enhances expressivity and learnability of probability paths; autoregressive modeling acts as implicit flow straightening; the approach offers strong efficiency gains with competitive quality, and the proposed spatial/temporal conditioning further improves latency-accuracy trade-offs.

Abstract: High-fidelity image generation faces a trade-off between speed and quality. Diffusion models produce strong visuals but require costly iterative sampling. Existing efficient methods mainly distill pretrained models into few-step samplers, a challenging process that depends heavily on teacher-model quality. In this paper, we introduce XYZFlow, a framework that rethinks efficient generation through multidimensional scaling of flow matching. Unlike single-step mappings, XYZFlow enhances expressivity by making probability paths more identifiable and learnable through structured multidimensional conditioning. We view autoregressive modeling as implicit flow straightening, where richer context reduces trajectory ambiguity. XYZFlow realizes this idea through two orthogonal dimensions: temporal scaling, which uses non-Markovian conditioning on the full denoising history; and spatial scaling, enabled by Next Shortcut Prediction, which sequentially generates patches using preceding patches' denoising trajectories as priors. Experiments show that XYZFlow achieves state-of-the-art performance, with 7.2-8.5X teacher speedups and competitive FID, while Next Shortcut Prediction delivers superior quality-latency trade-offs over model scaling or step reduction.

</details>


### [96] [Curvature-Aware Zeroth-Order Optimization for Memory-Efficient Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12279)
*Junming Zhang,Shuyu Yin,Peilin Liu,Rendong Ying,Fei Wen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CAZO: a curvature-aware zeroth-order fine-tuning method for test-time adaptation that freezes the backbone and trains minimal adapter parameters using forward-only gradient estimates. It uses a sliding-average diagonal Hessian to form a covariance for anisotropic perturbation sampling, improving stability and memory efficiency; reports state-of-the-art results with strong accuracy/memory trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-order TTA methods reduce memory and enable on-device deployment but suffer high gradient-variance. Exploiting the loss landscape curvature, specifically a low-rank Hessian pattern during adaptation, can stabilize gradient estimates and reduce sample complexity.

Method: Observe persistent low-rank Hessian structure during adaptation. Estimate the diagonal Hessian via a sliding-average. Construct a covariance matrix for anisotropic perturbation sampling. Perform forward-only gradient estimation to update a small set of adapter parameters while freezing pretrained weights.

Result: CAZO significantly outperforms existing TTA methods on benchmark tasks, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy with favorable memory efficiency. Code is released at the provided repository.

Conclusion: Curvature-aware zeroth-order optimization effectively mitigates gradient variance in ZO-TTA by leveraging a diagonal Hessian proxy and anisotropic sampling, enabling BP-free, memory-efficient adaptation. Future work could explore non-diagonal Hessian structures and robustness across architectures.

Abstract: Test-time adaptation (TTA) aims to enhance the cross-domain performance of pre-trained models by adapting to unlabeled test data. While most existing TTA methods rely on backpropagation (BP) for finetuning, BP-free methods such as zeroth-order (ZO) methods are more desired in practical on-device scenarios. ZO methods rely only on forward computation, which can largely reduce the complexity and memory overhead of on-device deployment. However, ZO methods suffer from much higher variance compared with first-order methods in estimating the gradient. To address this, we propose an improved ZO method to substantially boost the performance of ZO optimization based TTA. First, we provide an observation to reveal the persistent low-rank Hessian structure of the loss during the adaptation process. Based on this insight, we then propose a loss-landscape curvature-aware zeroth-order (CAZO) method, which leverages a sliding-average estimation of the diagonal Hessian to construct a covariance matrix for anisotropic perturbation sampling. CAZO operates by freezing pretrained weights and optimizing minimal adapter parameters via forward-only passes based gradient estimation, which can substantially reduce the memory overhead compared to BP-based methods. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CAZO significantly outperforms existing TTA methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance while maintaining an excellent balance between accuracy and memory efficiency. Code is available at https://github.com/Hollyming/CAZO.

</details>


### [97] [Beyond Trial-and-Error: Agentic Optimization for Image-to-Video Adherence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12290)
*Aman Tyagi,Hemanth Boinpally,Jonathan Chen,Douglas Gebert,Steven Hickson*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A two-stage agentic framework to control I2V generation: (1) iterative prompt optimization with an mLLM incorporating DSG and CMQ evaluations for semantic adherence and artifact detection, and (2) Bayesian optimization over seeds and CFG scales guided by Video-Text Adherence (VTA).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern black-box Image-to-Video models suffer from limited controllability and high stochasticity, causing costly trial-and-error and unreliable outputs in professional workflows.

Method: Stage 1 uses an iterative prompt optimization loop with a multimodal LLM, supplemented by Davidsonian Scene Graph (DSG) queries for semantic adherence and Common Mistake Questions (CMQ) for artifact detection. Stage 2 performs Bayesian optimization to co-optimize stochastic seeds and CFG scales, guided by quality metrics including the Video-Text Adherence (VTA) score derived from DSG and CMQ.

Result: The agentic framework significantly outperforms unguided searches in human preference studies, with videos generated via the approach being strongly preferred and achieving win rates up to 69%.

Conclusion: Provides a practical, extensible methodology to improve predictability and control of state-of-the-art video generation models, moving the field toward production-ready tools.

Abstract: Modern black-box Image-to-Video (I2V) models offer powerful capabilities in automated content creation, yet their lack of fine-grained control and reliability presents significant challenges in professional workflows. Their inherent stochasticity causes minor variations in textual prompts or hyperparameters to yield drastically different outputs often necessitating inefficient, brute-force trial-and-error processes. To address these limitations, we introduce the ``Agentic Self-Improvement" framework, which reframes video synthesis into a closed-loop, goal-directed optimization. Our framework systematically navigates the generation parameter space using a novel two-stage approach. In the first stage, an iterative prompt optimization loop uses a multimodal Large Language Model (mLLM) to refine the input prompt. This refinement implements two automated evaluations: Davidsonian Scene Graph (DSG) queries ensure semantic adherence, and Common Mistake Questions (CMQ) for artifact detection. At the second stage, we use Bayesian optimization to efficiently co-optimize stochastic seeds and CFG scales. This search is guided by a suite of quality metrics, including the novel Video-Text Adherence (VTA) score derived from the DSG and CMQ evaluations. Our framework significantly outperforms unguided search methods: in human preference studies, videos generated via our agentic approach were strongly preferred over baseline outputs, achieving win rates up to 69\%. This work provides a practical and extensible methodology for enhancing the predictability and control of state-of-the-art video generation models, moving the field beyond speculative curiosities toward reliable, production-ready tools.

</details>


### [98] [Class Activation Mapping in Explainable Computer Vision: A Method-Centered Review of CNN, Transformer, and Foundation-Model-Era Visual Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12299)
*AmirHossein Eshghi,Hamid Saadatfar,Seyyed Ali Hoseini,AmirMohsen Eshghi,Siavash Arjomand Bigdel*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CAM research has evolved from low-resolution, single-class explanations in CNNs to multi-layer, token-aware, and foundation-model-aware approaches, with a taxonomy based on attribution, architecture, and evaluation; 57 papers (2016–present) are surveyed, revealing trends and fragmented evaluation protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To synthesize a comprehensive, method-centered review of CAM-type explanations, establish a taxonomy, evaluate existing methods, and illuminate gaps and directions for future work.

Method: Systematic literature review of 57 CAM-related papers from 2016 onward, followed by taxonomy construction that classifies methods by attribution mechanism, architectural dependence, and evaluation objective; coverage includes gradient-based CAMs, gradient-free methods, high-resolution upscaling, weak supervision, transformer/token attribution, causal/debiasing methods, and foundation-model-era methods (CLIP, DINO, SAM, feature-distribution comparisons).

Result: A structured corpus of 57 CAM papers, a multi-axis taxonomy, and a synthesis of trends and gaps; key trend: move toward comparative, multi-layer, probabilistic, token-aware, and foundation-model-aware explanations; evaluation remains fragmented across faithfulness, localization, robustness, cost, and trust.

Conclusion: Future work should address unified evaluation protocols and cross-cutting gaps, improving faithfulness, localization accuracy, robustness, and efficiency, while bridging between CNN-based and foundation-model-based CAM approaches.

Abstract: Class activation mapping (CAM) is one of the most widely used visual explanation families in explainable artificial intelligence. Its purpose is intuitive: it converts internal model evidence into a heatmap that highlights the image regions, convolutional channels, tokens, or patches that support a target class or concept. Since the first CAM formulation in 2016, the field has moved far beyond global-average-pooled CNN classifiers. CAM-style methods now include gradient-based post-hoc explanations, gradient-free score and ablation methods, high-resolution upscaling, weakly supervised localization and segmentation, transformer token attribution, causal and debiasing methods, and foundation-model-era approaches that use CLIP, DINO, SAM, or feature-distribution comparisons. This review synthesizes a strict corpus of 57 method-centered papers published from 2016 onward. The paper develops a taxonomy that separates methods by attribution mechanism, architectural dependence, and evaluation objective. It then reviews gradient-based CAMs, recent and hybrid CAM-style methods, and model-based or architecture-aware methods. Across the corpus, the main trend is clear: the field is shifting from explaining one class score in one low-resolution CNN layer toward comparative, multi-layer, probabilistic, token-aware, and foundation-model-aware explanations. At the same time, evaluation remains fragmented. Faithfulness, localization, robustness, computational cost, and human trust are often measured with different protocols. The review therefore emphasizes not only what each method contributes, but also which gap it leaves open and which later methods attempt to close that gap.

</details>


### [99] [DreamFly: Causal Memory and Receding-Horizon Diffusion Planning for Aerial Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12308)
*Yan Deng,Fei Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Diffusion-based aerial VLN DreamFly with causally aligned historical memory, receding-horizon planning, and LiteStop, achieving state-of-the-art SR/SPL on OpenFly by leveraging past context, future-action supervision, and explicit termination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome partial observability and limited planning horizon in aerial VLN by leveraging temporal history, structured action forecasting, and explicit stop decision.

Method: A DreamFly framework built on Dream-VLA; introduces a causally aligned historical memory using only past observations; formulates navigation as receding-horizon diffusion planning where a K-step action chunk is predicted but only the first action is executed before replanning; LiteStop estimates stop probability directly from action logits at the initial all-mask state to decouple termination from action generation.

Result: On OpenFly benchmark, DreamFly achieves 32.04%/29.46% SR and 28.22%/23.54% SPL on test-seen/test-unseen, outperforming all compared methods with the lowest navigation error.

Conclusion: Jointly modeling historical context, future action structure, and explicit termination yields significant gains in aerial VLN, validating the proposed architectural and planning choices.

Abstract: Aerial vision-language navigation (VLN) requires an embodied agent to integrate visual evidence over time, plan future actions, and determine when it has reached a navigation goal under partial observability. Although recent VLA models offer a promising perception-to-action paradigm, adapting them to aerial navigation remains challenging due to limited historical context, short planning horizons, and unreliable implicit termination. To address these challenges, we propose DreamFly, a diffusion-based aerial VLN framework built on Dream-VLA. DreamFly introduces a causally aligned historical memory that augments the current visual representation using only observations preceding the current decision step, enabling temporal reasoning without future information leakage. We further formulate navigation as receding-horizon diffusion planning, where the policy predicts a $K$-step action chunk but executes only the first action before replanning. This plan-$K$, execute-one strategy uses future actions as auxiliary planning targets while preserving closed-loop visual feedback. Finally, LiteStop estimates the stop probability directly from action logits at the initial all-mask state, decoupling explicit termination from action generation. Experiments on the OpenFly benchmark demonstrate consistent improvements in seen and unseen environments. DreamFly achieves 32.04%/29.46% SR and 28.22%/23.54% SPL on the test-seen/test-unseen splits, respectively, outperforming all compared methods on both metrics while attaining the lowest navigation error. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of jointly modeling historical context, future action structure, and explicit termination for aerial VLN.

</details>


### [100] [AVA-Encoder: Towards Agent-Native Video Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12313)
*Chuyue Li,Jinpeng Yu,Haozhe Wang,Tian Xueyun,Zhijing Zhang,Bingnan Li,Shuqi Gu,Kan Ren,Jiaming Liu,Ruihua Hua*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AVA-Encoder learns agent-native video representations by converting videos to a knowledge graph with text descriptions and assets, enabling agentic reconstruction and editing via textual gradients; achieves significant gains and reduces prompt tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lack of structured, film-faithful video representations that support agentic reasoning and manipulation; current representations are either unstructured or not directly usable for planning and editing in agents.

Method: Encode video into a hierarchical knowledge graph with state nodes (text) and an asset layer (images, audio, video); use typed edges to preserve relations; reconstruct video from the KG; drive learning with a textual-gradient optimization where feedback is expressed as natural-language directions for a Data-Independent Encoding Policy Pseudo-Training outer loop and optional Data-Dependent KG Representation Refinement inner loop at test time.

Result: On benchmarks, AVA-Encoder achieves about 20.7 percentage points improvement over the strongest external baseline; in a policy-only setting, its pseudo-trained shot-level policy outperforms a carefully human-tuned policy while using 74.3% fewer system-prompt tokens.

Conclusion: AVA-Encoder proposes a novel, structured agent-centric video representation that enables efficient reasoning and editing, showing substantial performance gains and contributing a new dataset and benchmark for high-quality film KG representations.

Abstract: Creative agents still lack an effective way to learn from high-quality human films, limiting their ability to produce cinematic-grade videos. A key challenge is the absence of a structured video representation that is both faithful to film content and directly usable for agentic reasoning and manipulation.
