To address the challenge, we propose the Agentic Video Auto-Encoder (AVA-Encoder), a framework for learning agent-native video representations via agentic auto-encoding.
  AVA-Encoder transforms a video into a knowledge graph (KG) representation and then reconstructs it back into video. Its hierarchy and state nodes store structured text, while a linked asset layer holds generated images, audio, and video. Typed edges preserve the relations between these text descriptions and assets in a form that agents can easily understand, query, and edit. The video reconstruction differences drive a textual-gradient optimization framework, which expresses evaluation feedback as natural-language update directions for Data-Independent Encoding Policy Pseudo-Training in the outer loop and optional Data-Dependent KG Representation Refinement in the test-time inner loop.
  Extensive experiments show that AVA-Encoder improves by 20.7 percentage points over the strongest external baseline. In the controlled policy-only setting, its pseudo-trained shot-level Agentic Video Encoder policy also outperforms a carefully human-tuned policy while using 74.3% fewer system-prompt tokens. We release the complete AVA-Encoder framework, a reliable agentic video reconstruction benchmark, and the first dataset of high-quality film KG representations.

</details>


### [101] [StateFlow: Building, Evolving, and Accessing 3D World States for Previsualization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12314)
*Yuyang Yin,Zixiang Li,Longxuan Deng,Hongkai Li,Shifang Zhao,Junnan Liu,Weirong Huang,Mengyu Wang,Tianxiao Fu,Yikai Wang,Peng-Shuai Wang,Xiaojie Jin,Yao Zhao,Yunchao Wei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: StateFlow introduces a persistent, state-centric 3D world for generative previsualization, separating world construction, evolution, and access to enable iterative editing and higher-quality video/game prototyping, rather than relying on one-shot image/video synthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative methods rely on single prompts to jointly control multiple scene factors, offering weak controllability and limited support for iterative editing. A persistent working state that explicitly encodes geometry, appearance, and camera configuration enables reuse and progressive refinement across edits in domains like film, games, architecture, and urban design.

Method: Three-stage pipeline: 1) state construction via prior-guided, conflict-aware dual-view initialization to lift 2D content into a coherent 3D world; 2) state evolution translating user edits into structured state transitions while preserving world memory to avoid full-scene regeneration; 3) state access using render-feedback reflection to refine camera plans into feasible trajectories, with optional integration of off-the-shelf video models for higher fidelity.

Result: Experiments show StateFlow can produce high-quality 3D worlds suitable for video creation and game-like prototyping, demonstrating improved controllability, memory reuse, and editing efficiency compared to traditional one-shot generation.

Conclusion: A persistent, state-centric framework addresses the core bottlenecks of controllability and editability in generative previsualization by maintaining a coherent 3D world state and a three-stage workflow that separates construction, evolution, and access, enabling iterative refinement and reuse of scene elements.

Abstract: Previsualization is an intermediate layer between ideas and production in film, games, architecture, and urban design. It lets creators iteratively refine scenes, actions, cameras, and spatial-temporal dynamics. Yet existing generative methods rely on simple prompts to jointly control all of these factors through one-shot image or video synthesis, offering weak controllability and limited support for iterative editing. Fundamentally, a world comprises multiple elements with geometry, appearance, and other attributes, together with cameras. Different frames are produced through local modifications or recombinations of this shared state, which is otherwise largely reused. Therefore, we argue that the missing component is an explicit and persistent working state. To address this, we present StateFlow, a state-centric framework for generative previsualization. Rather than generating videos in one shot, StateFlow uses an editable 3D world to organize scene structure, evolution, and cameras, while off-the-shelf video models enhance visual quality when higher fidelity is desired. This world is maintained as a persistent structured 3D state of scene elements and camera configurations, serving as the core working representation for previsualization. Built on this insight, StateFlow has three stages to construct, evolve, and access the world state. State construction lifts generated 2D content into a coherent 3D world through prior-guided, conflict-aware dual-view initialization, while State evolution translates user intent into structured state transitions while preserving world memory, avoiding full-scene regeneration for each edit. State access uses render-feedback reflection to refine camera plans into visually feasible trajectories, avoiding reliance on VLM semantics alone. Experiments show that StateFlow produces high-quality 3D worlds for video creation and game-like prototyping.

</details>


<div id='cs.CG'></div>

# cs.CG [[Back]](#toc)

### [102] [A Probabilistic Interpretation of the Ball Mapper Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11397)
*John Rick Manzanares,Jay-Anne Bulauan*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Probabilistic Ball Mapper (PBM) extends Ball Mapper by assigning each data point a probability distribution over containing metric balls, yielding a PSD, mass-normalized soft-overlap matrix and enabling transport-based comparisons across covers while preserving interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable quantitative, mass-aware comparison of Ball Mapper representations, address unnormalized overlaps, and provide stability guarantees under perturbations and landmark motion.

Method: Define two assignment schemes (uniform-on-support and localized radial-basis) to create a Markov kernel from data to the Ball Mapper cover. Push the empirical distribution through the kernel to obtain vertex distributions. Draw twice per point to produce a soft-overlap matrix that is symmetric, nonnegative, PSD and has the vertex distribution as marginals. Compare graphs on a common cover directly via vertex/overlap distributions; for independently fitted covers, use Wasserstein and fused Gromov–Wasserstein-type discrepancies accounting for vertex mass and geometry. Derive perturbation bounds for fixed covers, controlled by assignment sensitivity, data perturbation, and cover-boundary mass; for recomputed covers, state a transport-based stability principle due to landmark motion.

Result: The framework yields a mass-normalized, probability-valued representation where the overlap matrix is PSD and has the vertex distribution as marginals. It enables direct comparison of vertex and overlap distributions across common covers and, for different covers, transport-based discrepancy measures (Wasserstein, fused GW) that incorporate landmark geometry and intrinsic graph relations. Perturbation bounds quantify sensitivity to rule changes and data perturbations; stability is linked to landmark motion.

Conclusion: PBM transforms Ball Mapper into a probability-valued representation that preserves geometric interpretability and computational simplicity, enabling principled, quantitative comparisons across covers and datasets.

Abstract: We introduce Probabilistic Ball Mapper, a formulation of Ball Mapper in which each data point is assigned a probability distribution supported only on the metric balls that contain it. This assignment defines both a partition subordinate to the Ball Mapper cover and a Markov kernel from the finite data space to the cover. We study two assignment schemes: a uniform-on-support rule and a localized radial-basis rule that incorporates distance to landmarks while preserving the underlying cover. Pushing the empirical data distribution through the kernel produces a probability distribution over vertices. Drawing twice, conditionally and independently, from each pointwise distribution produces a soft-overlap matrix. This matrix is symmetric, nonnegative, positive semidefinite, and has the vertex distribution as both marginals. It therefore provides a mass-normalized refinement of classical Ball Mapper overlap rather than another unnormalized edge count. For graphs constructed on a common cover, the vertex and overlap distributions can be compared directly. For independently fitted covers, we formulate Wasserstein and fused Gromov--Wasserstein-type discrepancies that account for vertex mass, landmark geometry when a common ambient metric is available, and intrinsic graph relations. For a fixed cover, we derive explicit perturbation bounds controlled by the sensitivity of the assignment rule, the magnitude of the data perturbation, and the data mass near cover boundaries. When the cover is recomputed, landmark motion creates an additional source of variation, for which we state a transport-based stability principle rather than an unconditional theorem. The resulting framework turns Ball Mapper into a probability-valued representation suitable for quantitative comparison while retaining its geometric interpretability and computational simplicity.

</details>


### [103] [Topology-Preserving Meshing of Implicit Scalar Fields via Monotonicity Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12142)
*Tanner Finken,Jixian Li,Bei Wang,Hanqi Guo,Joshua A. Levine*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Monotonic-edge based PL reconstruction for implicit 2D scalar fields to extract a Morse-Smale complex, with a refinement strategy to fix monotonicity violations; achieves topology-correct critical points and improved separatrix geometry with modest evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MSC extraction traditionally assumes explicit discretizations; implicit field representations challenge topology preservation. A method that builds a topology-preserving PL mesh from implicit data enables reliable MSC extraction.

Method: Construct a triangulated PL mesh from implicit field evaluations by enforcing edge monotonicity with respect to the underlying scalar field; introduce a refinement procedure to mitigate monotonicity violations; require only pointwise evaluations and modest mesh refinement; perform targeted refinements to improve separatrix geometry and MSC fidelity.

Result: The produced PL meshes are correct with respect to critical points (topology preserved) in experiments; additional targeted refinement improves geometric fidelity of MSC separatrices.

Conclusion: A monotonicity-guided PL reconstruction provides a topology-consistent MSC extraction from implicit 2D scalar fields using minimal data and refinement, with potential for improved MSC geometry through targeted refinements.

Abstract: Topological analysis of scalar fields yields structures such as the Morse-Smale complex (MSC) that summarize salient features across multiple scales. Existing MSC extraction algorithms typically assume an explicit representation of the input field, such as a discretely sampled mesh. However, recent advances in visualization have popularized implicit field representations, for which these assumptions no longer hold. In this work, we address the problem of extracting an MSC from an implicitly defined 2D scalar field. We present a method for constructing a triangulated piecewise-linear (PL) mesh that aims to preserve the critical points of an underlying implicit scalar field. Our central insight is that if all edges are monotonic with respect to the underlying field, then the resulting PL approximation is topologically consistent with respect to critical points. Based on this insight, we introduce a refinement procedure that mitigates monotonicity violations. Requiring only pointwise evaluations and modest mesh refinement, the approach produces PL meshes that are correct with regards to critical points in our experiments. Finally, we demonstrate that additional targeted refinement improves the geometric fidelity of MSC separatrices.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [104] [Dynamic Governance of Multi-LLM Agent Systems for Collaborative Conversational Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11207)
*Alexander Liss,Nicholas Desmond,Santiago Gil Gallego*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A governance layer for opposing LLMs (Experience Orchestrator) uses a Contextual Bandit, PID controller, and POMDP to coordinate behavior in a simulated financial-site interaction, yielding a 32-point lift in high-intent advisor contact vs naive control; results hinge on governance policy rather than initial conditions and require live-traffic validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When two LLM agents pursue conflicting objectives without a shared goal, interaction collapses. A control-theoretic governance layer could substitute for the missing goal function to sustain productive trajectories.

Method: In a 60k-simulation study, EO comprises: (1) a Contextual Bandit selecting content arms based on real-world web analytics; (2) a PID controller enforcing behavioral consistency via dynamic schema constraints; (3) a POMDP belief tracker modeling visitor intent. The setting is a simulated financial-services site where a site agent nudges visitors toward advisor contact while visitors resist.

Result: EO achieves +32 percentage points in high-intent advisor contact rate (78.1% vs 46.1% with naive LLM control). The Contextual Bandit explains 97% of the between-factor outcome variance, indicating governance policy is the main determinant of trajectory outcomes. Two visitor-regime insights emerge: (a) for non-convertible visitors, governance makes the difference between functionality and dysfunction; (b) for near-aligned visitors, an empathetic naive LLM suffices. All results are conditioned on LLM-to-LLM simulation.

Conclusion: The Experience Orchestrator demonstrates that a governance layer can substitute for a missing shared goal in multi-agent LLM interactions and steer trajectories toward high-intent conversions. However, validation on live traffic and calibration of the PID to real human unpredictability are necessary before practical deployment; findings are contingent on simulated conditions.

Abstract: When two LLM agents with structurally opposed objectives interact across multiple turns, the absence of a shared goal function produces not competition but collapse: the visitor capitulates, the site agent stops varying its approach, and the conversation terminates without achieving either agent's stated objective. This paper asks whether a control-theoretic governance layer can substitute for that missing goal function. The Experience Orchestrator (EO) addresses this in a simulated financial services environment where a site agent guides a visitor toward advisor contact while the visitor maintains psychologically realistic resistance. EO governs the joint trajectory through three mechanisms: a Contextual Bandit (CB) that selects content arms calibrated from real-world web analytics, a PID controller that enforces behavioral consistency via dynamic schema constraints, and a POMDP belief tracker that maintains a probabilistic model of visitor intent. Across 60,000 simulations, EO achieves a +32 percentage point lift in high-intent advisor contact rate (78.1% vs. 46.1% over a naive LLM control), with CB variant selection accounting for 97% of between-factor outcome variance -- confirming that the governance policy, not environmental initial conditions, determines where trajectories end up. Persona-level analysis reveals two distinct regimes: for visitors with no natural inclination toward conversion, the governance layer is the difference between a functional system and a non-functional one; for visitors already near alignment, a naive LLM's empathetic defaults are largely sufficient. All findings are conditional on LLM-to-LLM simulation. The PID controller has not been calibrated against real human unpredictability, and validating EO on live traffic is the critical next step.

</details>


### [105] [Distribird: Literature-Informed Prior Distribution Design for Bayesian Model Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11210)
*Patrik P. Süli,György Eigner,Roland Hollós*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Distribird automates the construction of Bayesian priors for process-based models from published literature via a multi-agent pipeline, producing traceable, validity-checked priors with local LLM privacy. It matches open-weight model baselines in prior quality and rejects out-of-scope requests, emphasizing transparency and scientific rigor over marginal accuracy gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior specification for Bayesian calibration is challenging and time-consuming; uniform priors are common but not informative. There is a need for automated, transparent, and privacy-preserving methods to build literature-informed priors.

Method: A multi-agent system searches literature for a parameter’s physical description and domain context, extracts reported values, weights them by domain relevance, and fits a distribution using AIC model selection. If literature is missing, it falls back to uninformative priors. A built-in validity layer rejects out-of-scope requests. All LLM calls run locally, with only generated search terms reaching public databases.

Result: Evaluated on 24 parameters across 10 domains, comparing three open-weight LLMs (Qwen3.6 27B, Gemma 4 31B, Mistral Small 4 119B) to a single-prompt baseline. The full pipeline matches the baseline in prior quality; priors are traceable to specific papers and values; the validity layer rejects out-of-scope requests; nothing sensitive is transmitted to third-party providers; all LLM calls are local.

Conclusion: For scientific use, the emphasis on transparency, traceability, and privacy—enabled by the validity layer and local computation—outweigh potential marginal gains in point-estimate accuracy.

Abstract: Bayesian calibration of process-based models requires a prior distribution for each model parameter. Despite decades of methodological work, researchers almost always fall back on uniform priors. The main reason is that building informative priors from scientific literature is slow and needs both domain and statistical expertise. We present \textbf{Distribird}, an agentic web application that automates this process. Given a parameter name, physical description, and domain context, Distribird deploys a multi-agent pipeline that searches the literature, extracts and weights reported values by domain relevance, and fits a probability distribution via AIC model selection. When no literature is available, the system falls back to sensible uninformative alternatives, and clearly reports both the evidence behind and the confidence level of every prior it produces. It is designed for the problems where the models have physically interpretable parameters, where domain knowledge exists in the published literature. We evaluate the tool on 24~parameters across 10 scientific domains comparing three open-weight models (Qwen3.6 27B, Gemma 4 31B, Mistral Small 4 119B) with a single-prompt LLM baseline. On prior quality the full pipeline \emph{matches} this baseline. Every prior is traced to the specific papers and values from which it was constructed; a built-in validity layer declines to produce priors for out-of-scope requests, whereas the single-prompt baseline returns confident but unfounded priors for them in 11 of 30~model--parameter cases; and every language-model call runs locally, so no parameter description or unpublished modelling detail is transmitted to a third-party LLM provider (only generated search terms reach the public literature databases). For scientific use, we argue these properties matter more than a marginal improvement in point-estimate accuracy.

</details>


### [106] [A Forced-Structure Reduction and Verifiable Bounds for Conway's 99-Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11211)
*Aalok Thakkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An analysis of an abstract tackling Conway's 99-graph problem via computational and structural reductions: exhaustive circulant/abelian bounds, a 12-regular reduction to a known srg(9,4,1,2), automorphism-orbit checks, and a best-verified artifact around 69.43%. The work is intriguing but requires rigorous justification of the reductions and full reproducibility to be persuasive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate methodological soundness, reproducibility, and the strength of the claimed reductions and partial results in the abstract.

Method: Critically assess the logical flow: (i) constraint-bound proofs for circulant/abelian cases, (ii) forced-structure reductions turning the problem into a smaller SRG, (iii) prescribed-automorphism orbit framework validation on known graphs, (iv) interpretation of partial-credit metrics and their implications for non-existence proofs.

Result: Provide a concise appraisal of the novelty and potential gaps: the reductions are plausible but require formal justification; CP-SAT encoding and validation against srg(9,4,1,2) is a key test; automorphism framework appears sound but needs broader verification; the 69.43% artifact is a meaningful bound within a search space but not a non-existence proof, and the claim about provable bounds below 4950 being non-existence needs careful framing.

Conclusion: The abstract presents a cohesive, multi-method approach with plausible reductions and checks. For acceptance, it should supply detailed proofs of the reductions, full reproducibility of the computational experiments, and independent confirmation of the CP-SAT models and automorphism checks.

Abstract: Conway's 99-graph problem asks whether a strongly regular graph with parameters $\mathrm{srg}(99,14,1,2)$ exists. We report a systematic, fully reproducible attack by an autonomous AI research agent, scored under the track's partial-credit metric. Our verifiable contributions are: (1) an exhaustive proof that no circulant graph on $\mathbb{Z}/99$ satisfies more than $3366/4950=68.0\%$ of the constraints ($33$ of $49$ difference-classes), with the same ceiling for the other abelian group of order $99$; (2) a forced-structure reduction: $λ=1$ makes each neighbourhood a perfect matching and $μ=2$ puts the outer vertices in bijection with non-matched neighbour-pairs, collapsing existence to a $12$-regular graph on $84$ vertices, encoded for CP-SAT and validated by recovering the unique $\mathrm{srg}(9,4,1,2)$; (3) a validated prescribed-automorphism orbit-existence framework (fixed-point-free and single-fixed-point actions, checked on $\mathrm{srg}(9,4,1,2)$ and the Paley graph $\mathrm{srg}(13,6,2,3)$), and (4) a best verified artifact at $69.43\%$, with evidence that this is a robust frontier (fourteen distinct methods, none exceeding it) entangled with the open question, since any provable bound below $4950$ is a non-existence proof.

</details>


### [107] [Detecting a Route Flip Is Easier Than Knowing Whether to Fix It: Causal Route-Mediated Damage in Quantized Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11212)
*Parvel Gu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Quantifies routing-side damage in top-k MoE under simulated 4-bit KV-cache quantization; finds routing-mediated damage (RMF) around 0.31; flip detection exists but sign of loss remains unpredictable; no mitigation proposed; pre-registered, multi-architecture replication; results sensitive to architecture and quantization, with signed-flip predictability as an empirical barrier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand and quantify how discontinuous Top-k MoE routing interacts with low-precision KV-cache quantization, to assess damage, detection limits, and the feasibility of mitigation across architectures.

Method: Four-run apparatus to estimate the route-mediated fraction (RMF) of quantization damage; token-level attribution by mechanism; pre-registered probes across three architectures; experiments on OLMoE-1B-7B at 4-bit KV; includes a deployable router margin for flip detection, pre-registered re-execution, and same-checkpoint flag swaps; evaluation with an int4 KV kernel and held-out replication.

Result: RMF ~ 0.31 (discovery 0.31 [0.20, 0.41]; mean 0.313±0.020); pre-registered re-execution 0.231; AUC for flip detection 0.772 but cannot distinguish harmful vs helpful flips; no predictor of flip loss sign above chance; real int4 results compatible with the dose curve but underpowered (CI includes zero, -0.111 to 0.394); held-out replication and near-cancelling tax; pre-registered impossibility exclusion narrowly misses.

Conclusion: No mitigation is proposed. The work provides a causal framework and empirical baselines with a detection-limit result; findings are cross-architecture but gate/normalization choices and damage magnitude rather than route-recoverability drive outcomes; significant barriers remain for predicting or repairing beneficial vs harmful flips and for generalizing mitigation.

Abstract: Top-k Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routing is discontinuous, so a deployment-motivated numerical disturbance -- simulated 4-bit KV-cache quantization read by a protected BF16 gate -- pushes tokens across decision boundaries and flips which experts fire. This paper proposes no new mitigation; it supplies a causal apparatus, empirical findings, and a detection-limit result. A four-run apparatus prices the route-mediated fraction (RMF) of quantization damage, a token-level attribution decomposes it by mechanism, and pre-registered probes carry the findings across three architectures. On OLMoE-1B-7B at 4-bit KV (pilot), about a third of the damage is routing-mediated: RMF ~ 0.31 (discovery 0.31 [0.20, 0.41]; process-replicated mean 0.313 +/- 0.020; pre-registered re-execution 0.231). The deployable router margin detects that a flip occurred (AUC 0.772) but cannot tell a harmful flip from a helpful one (at chance): among the tested local, inference-observable router statistics we find no predictor of a flip's loss sign above chance -- an empirical benefit-detection barrier bounding selective repair restricted to this feature family. The signed-flip tax and sign-inseparability carry cross-model; the clean-reference remedy's payout is architecture-modulated; a controlled same-checkpoint flag-swap re-scopes the gate's normalization convention to a damage-magnitude moderator, not a route-recoverability mechanism. A real int4 KV kernel yields a fraction compatible with the fake-quant dose curve but underpowered (95% CI [-0.111, 0.394] includes zero) -- ruling out gross disagreement, not an independent replication. Hypotheses, thresholds, and evaluations were pre-registered before measurement, with misses reported; a pre-registered held-out read replicates the partition and the near-cancelling tax out of sample, while the strict impossibility exclusion narrowly misses.

</details>


### [108] [Poor Man's Agentic Modeling: Simulating Large LLM-Agent Societies on a Laptop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11215)
*Igor Itkin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A scalable surrogate-model approach for simulating large societies of LLM agents: replace each agent with a low-parameter model fitted from cheap queries, enabling N-scale analyses on a laptop. Introduces an interaction order x memory taxonomy to connect perception/memory to an effective theory and predict surrogate error trends; validated across EconAgent and seven other LLM simulations with decisions cloned from genuine elicitations, matching predicted error trends without free parameters; two refutations explained by curvature and saturated response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulating many LLM agents is computationally expensive, but studies typically ask macroscopic questions (phase behavior, stylized facts, scaling with N). A surrogate modeling framework is proposed to study large N behavior with cheap, controlled experiments, enabling principled prediction of how surrogate error scales with N.

Method: Replace each LLM agent with a fitted low-parameter surrogate learned from a few hundred to a few thousand cheap queries. Run the society for any N on a laptop. Develop an interaction order × memory taxonomy that maps perception and memory to an effective theory and yields predicted N-dependent surrogate error. Validate on EconAgent’s faithful reimplementation and seven additional LLM simulations, with agent decisions cloned from genuine LLM elicitations (notably DeepSeek) at low cost. Compare predicted error trends cell-by-cell and interpret refuted predictions through the theory with no free parameters.

Result: The predicted N-dependent error trends hold at a fine-grained (cell-by-cell) level across the tested simulations. Two predictions that fail—a strongly saturating response and a curvature-related effect—are precisely accounted for by the theory, with no free parameters. The surrogate models reproduce macroscopic dynamics consistent with the full LLM simulations, and the refutations are quantitatively explained by the proposed framework.

Conclusion: A practical, low-cost surrogate modeling approach enables scalable analyses of large LLM-agent societies. The interaction order × memory taxonomy provides an effective theory linking perception and memory to N-scaling of surrogate error, with robust validation across multiple simulations, including EconAgent. The framework yields quantitative predictions and explanations for both successes and certain failures, highlighting the method’s predictive power and limitations.

Abstract: Simulating societies of many large language model (LLM) agents is expensive, yet the questions asked of such simulations are usually macroscopic: phase behaviour, stylised facts, and scaling with the number of agents $N$, not the cognition of any single agent. We turn a statistical-physics observation into a method: replace each LLM agent by a low-parameter model fitted from a few hundred to a few thousand cheap queries, then run the society at any $N$ on a laptop. Whether this works is decided before the simulation runs, chiefly by what each agent perceives. We introduce an [interaction order x memory] taxonomy that maps perception and memory to an effective theory and a predicted $N$-trend of the surrogate error. We validate it on a faithful reimplementation of the LLM macroeconomy EconAgent and seven further named LLM simulations, with agent decisions cloned from genuine LLM elicitations (primarily DeepSeek) for a few dollars; the predicted error trends hold cell by cell, and the two refuted predictions, both on a strongly saturating response and traced to its curvature, are themselves matched quantitatively by the theory with no free parameters.

</details>


### [109] [AutoWorldModel-Bench: A State-Centric Benchmark for Automated World-Model Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11216)
*Marjan Moodi,Xuankang Zhu,Fernando De Mesentier Silva,Harold Chaput,Mohammad Reza Taesiri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Open-ended benchmark AutoWorldModel-Bench: frontier coding agents autonomously improve a world-model starter under a fixed compute budget across eight game environments with a unified structured-state representation. In 64 sessions, Codex-5.4 and Claude Opus 4.6 improve 63 starters; 91% of winning edits are non-trivial research-style modifications rather than hyperparameter tweaks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World modeling is unsettled; evaluating autonomous researchers can reveal robust search strategies and enable open-ended AI research without predefined objectives. A structured, perception-isolated state representation enables rapid iteration and focuses on dynamics modeling.

Method: A closed-loop benchmark where eight games provide ground-truth entity state converted to a shared tensor format. Agents independently modify the world-model starter within a fixed compute budget, iterating across 64 sessions. Winning edits are categorized by whether they add a new objective, representation, rollout procedure, or architectural change (as opposed to hyperparameter tweaks).

Result: Agents achieved improvements in 63 of 64 sessions. 91% of winning edits introduced non-trivial research-style changes (new objectives/representations/rollouts/architectures). The benchmark isolates dynamics modeling from perception and enables minutes-per-run iteration.

Conclusion: AutoWorldModel-Bench provides a platform to evaluate frontier coding agents on open-ended research tasks rather than engineering-to-spec objectives, illustrating the potential for autonomous research-driven progress in world-modeling.

Abstract: World modeling is an unsettled field: architectures, training objectives, and state representations interact in complex ways, and no single recipe dominates across environments. This makes it an ideal testbed for AI coding agents acting as autonomous researchers--a setting in which the improvement direction is not specified in advance, unlike the engineering-to-spec tasks that dominate current agent benchmarks. We introduce AutoWorldModel-Bench, a closed-loop benchmark in which frontier coding agents autonomously improve a provided world-model starter under a fixed compute budget. The benchmark spans eight game environments under a unified structured-state representation--ground-truth entity state extracted from each game and consumed through a shared tensor format--which isolates dynamics modeling from perception and enables minutes-per-run iteration. Across 64 sessions, Codex-5.4 and Claude Opus 4.6 improve their starter on 63; in 91% of sessions the winning edit is a non-trivial research-style modification--a new objective, representation, rollout procedure, or architectural change--rather than a hyperparameter tweak. Our benchmark offers a setting in which frontier coding agents can be evaluated on open-ended research rather than engineering-to-spec problems.

</details>


### [110] [MaSRead: Content-Addressed Reading of Replicated Latent Stores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11218)
*Carlos Baquero,Luís Brito,João Resende*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MaSRead enables selective, content-based reads from replicated latent stores by routing through tag-derived word sets and decod ing chosen fragments with a hard attention mask, enabling multi-hop query retrieval despite fragmentation and accumulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CRDT-based latent stores merge fragments in a way that hinders later read access; there is a need for a read path that can selectively reconstruct relevant fragments without being overwhelmed by unrelated data.

Method: MaSRead routes via opaque keyed tag sets derived from fragment words, decodes selected fragments under a restrictive attention mask, and uses lexical graph traversal to reach fragments needed for a multi-hop query. It supports various store topologies (chain, pipeline, symmetric, hub, natural language) and relies on fragment-length-driven decoding with one read per visited fragment.

Result: The approach recovers visited fragments in isolation, remains effective as unrelated fragments accumulate, and transfers to another model family. End-to-end routing depends on the store, and decoding scales with fragment length rather than total store size. Limits include potential misses from lexical routing and a read bound by the frozen reader; performance is contingent on connectivity through content.

Conclusion: A replicated latent store becomes selectively readable for later queries when needed fragments connect to the query through content, enabling read access without requiring global colocation or deterministic addressing.

Abstract: Independent agents that reason in latent space can share computed state as key-value cache fragments rather than text. Merged by a conflict-free replicated data type, these fragments form a store that converges under any delivery order or duplication. Yet a later query, unknown at encode time, cannot reliably read the merged cache: colocated fragments interfere, so colocation is not addressability. MaSRead addresses the read to content. It routes through opaque keyed tag sets derived from fragment words and decodes each selected fragment under a hard attention mask that hides the rest. Under lexical connectivity, a graph walk reaches the fragments required by a multi-hop query. Across chain, pipeline, symmetric, hub, and natural-language stores, MaSRead recovers visited fragments in isolation, remains effective as unrelated fragments accumulate, and transfers to another model family. After routing, materialized decoding depends on fragment length rather than total store size; end-to-end work still includes store-dependent routing and one read per visited fragment. The limits are explicit: lexical routing can miss disconnected evidence, and answer composition remains bounded by the frozen reader. Thus a replicated latent store becomes selectively readable for later queries when the needed fragments connect to the query through content.

</details>


### [111] [From Monolithic to Modular: Segment-level Automatic Prompt Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11219)
*Nikita Kulin,Viktor Zhuravlev,Artur Khairullin,Sergey Muravyov,Ilya Makarov,Daniil Sukhorukov,Ekaterina Averkova*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Segment-level Automatic Prompt Optimization (SAPO) decomposes prompts into role, context, tasks, and output format, and optimizes each segment using top-5/bottom-5 signals. It uses a two-stage process with a single LLM and static meta-prompts, achieving the best average scores across multiple tasks against strong APO baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monolithic APO often improves some behaviors while degrading others due to global prompt rewrites. Segment-level optimization aims to balance cross-task behaviors by targeting specific prompt segments, reducing negative interference.

Method: Decompose prompts into role, context, tasks, and output format. Use static meta-prompts and structured outputs for segmentation, weakness analysis, and candidate generation. Employ a train/validation protocol. Two-stage generation: (1) segment-level diagnosis and recommendation extraction; (2) candidate synthesis constrained by weak/strong segment signals. Evaluation uses SQuADv2, TweetEval, XSUM, CommonGen, GSM8K on GPT-3.5-Turbo and GPT-4o-mini; compare against Zero-shot and strong APO baselines (APE, OPRO, EvoPrompt, GEPA, StraGO).

Result: SAPO achieves the best average score across tasks against Zero-shot and strong APO baselines, outperforming methods such as APE, OPRO, EvoPrompt, GEPA, and StraGO.

Conclusion: Segment-level APO can outperform monolithic approaches by isolating and optimizing individual prompt segments with targeted signals, demonstrating strong cross-task generalization across models.

Abstract: Automatic Prompt Optimization (APO) often rewrites prompts monolithically, which can improve one behavior while degrading others. We present SAPO, a segment-level APO method that decomposes prompts into role, context, tasks, and output format, then applies targeted improvements based on top-5 and bottom-5 examples. The optimization loop uses one LLM with static meta-prompts and structured outputs for segmentation, weakness analysis, and candidate generation. We describe a train/validation protocol and a two-stage generation process: (1) segment-level diagnosis and recommendation extraction, (2) candidate synthesis constrained by weak/strong segment signals. Using the evaluation setup across SQuADv2, TweetEval, XSUM, CommonGen, and GSM8K on GPT-3.5-Turbo and GPT-4o-mini, SAPO achieves the best average score against Zero-shot and strong APO baselines including APE, OPRO, EvoPrompt, GEPA, and StraGO.

</details>


### [112] [LLMs in Process Diagram Engineering: From Optimal PFDs to Validated P&IDs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11220)
*Timur Zakarin,Sergei Voitov,Sergei Shumilin,Evgeny Burnaev*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hybrid GA-LLM pipeline for end-to-end PFD->P&ID automation: GA+LLM optimizes PFD topology under flow constraints; an LLM-based agent, constrained by an engineering SDK, converts the PFD into a validated, executable P&ID, achieving 100% execution success and domain compliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual generation of PFDs and their conversion to P&ID is time-consuming and error-prone. An AI-driven end-to-end pipeline could automate topology exploration, reduce labor, and improve consistency while ensuring compliance with process- and safety-related constraints.

Method: Stage 1: PFD synthesis evaluated across four methods; the hybrid GA + LLM approach produced the optimal valid PFD topology with the lowest loss and satisfied outlet flow parameters without engineering-rule violations. Stage 2: An LLM-based agent transforms the generated PFD into a source-grounded P&ID via a restricted engineering SDK, yielding validated, executable modifications with 100% execution success and adherence to domain rules and reference graphs.

Result: Demonstrates an end-to-end AI pipeline capable of generating deployable PFDs and P&IDs with validated outputs; GA/LLM hybrid method outperforms alternatives in topology optimization; LLM-based transformation ensures executable, compliant P&ID generation.

Conclusion: The unified GA/LLM synthesis plus LLM-driven transformation agent presents a feasible route toward autonomous process design automation, delivering validated, deployable artifacts and substantial reductions in manual engineering effort.

Abstract: Nowadays, the creation of a process flow diagram (PFD) and its subsequent transformation into a piping and instrumentation diagram (P&ID) is predominantly performed manually. Applying artificial intelligence in the task could potentially lead not only to process automation and time savings, but also to financial gains by exploring numerous diagram's topology options and reducing manual labor. This research presents P&ID Pilot - a practical end-to-end AI pipeline capable of handling flowsheet developing for both stages. The first stage focuses on PFD synthesis, whereas the second is directed toward modifying the generated PFD into P&ID. After comparing four different methods, the hybrid approach combining genetic algorithms (GA) and large language models (LLM) is shown to generate the optimal valid PFD topology, achieving the lowest loss value among all the methods, while satisfying the required outlet flow parameters without engineering-rule violations. For the second stage, the proposed LLM-based agent successfully transforms the generated PFD into a source-grounded P&ID by producing validated, executable modifications through a restricted engineering software development kit, achieving 100% execution success while maintaining compliance with domain-specific rules and reference graph structures. This unified pipeline - coupling GA/LLM-driven synthesis with an LLM-based transformation agent - offers a feasible path toward end-to-end process design automation by producing validated, deployable outputs and substantially reduces manual engineering effort.

</details>


### [113] [A Conceptual Framework for Refining Influence Knowledge from Simulation Evidence in Cyber-Physical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11221)
*Barbara da Silva Oliveira,Julien Deantoni,Nicolas Ferry*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces 'Influences', a conceptual framework to iteratively refine CPS simulation campaigns by capturing environment-mediated interactions, demonstrated on a Simulink/Gazebo mobile-robot co-simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CPS are built by multiple stakeholders and their emergent behaviour depends on interactions between artefacts and the operational environment. Many environment-mediated interactions remain unmodelled due to complexity, time, or domain know-how, hindering interpretation of simulation results.

Method: Proposes a conceptual framework of Influences to explicitly represent environment-mediated interactions and support iterative, incremental refinement of simulation campaigns; validated via a case study with a mobile robot implemented in Simulink/Gazebo co-simulation.

Result: The case study demonstrates how the Influences concept helps uncover environment-mediated interactions beyond direct sensing/actuation, enabling deeper understanding of CPS behaviour and enabling refinement of simulation campaigns.

Conclusion: Influences offer a structured approach to progressively improve CPS simulation studies and interpretation, with potential to enhance cross-domain collaboration; future work includes broader application and quantitative evaluation.

Abstract: Cyber-physical systems (CPS) are typically developed by multiple stakeholders who produce artefacts tailored to their specific domains of expertise. The behaviour of these systems emerges from the interaction between those artefacts and their operational environment. Simulation and co-simulation have become essential approaches for analysing CPS behaviour and, through simulation campaigns, developers can explore system responses under changing conditions, including interactions with the environment. However, the lack of details and understanding of some environmentmediated interactions (typically the ones beyond direct sensing and actuation), which remain unmodelled due to their complexity, a lack of time, or a lack of domain experience, hinders the proper comprehension and exploitation of simulation results. To address these limitations, we propose a conceptual framework leveraging the novel concept of Influences to support the iterative and incremental refinement of simulation campaigns and deepen the understanding of the system behaviour. We demonstrate the proposed approach through a case study involving a mobile robot implemented using Simulink/Gazebo co-simulation.

</details>


### [114] [Harnessing agent memory to build lifelong AI partners for materials scientists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11224)
*Siyu Liu,Bo Hu,Beilin Ye,He Cao,David J. Srolovitz,Tongqi Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a self-evolving, persistent memory framework for materials science that stores observations, protocols, and skills as inspectable, portable memory to enable lifelong, cross-model expertise and improve task success and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fragmented, model-specific memory in materials research hinders reproducibility and knowledge transfer. A durable, portable memory could enhance collaboration across AI agents and over time.

Method: Introduce a memory framework that stores scientific observations, failure boundaries, protocols, and validation checks as inspectable facts and executable skills. Evaluate across three computational settings: (1) 49 real-world materials-tool-use questions with 138 executable subtasks; (2) elemental-solid equation-of-state calculations; (3) 13 practical material simulation workflows. Metrics include task success, error reduction, token trace burden, and tool-call reductions.

Result: Memory nearly doubles GPT-5.2 task success without model-parameter updates (in 49 questions). In elemental-solid EOS tasks, memory converts wavefunction-init failures into pre-execution guardrails, improving Correct/Partial/Error from 22/1/4 to 25/2/0 and avoiding 92% of repeated errors. In 13 workflows, remembered skills reduce trace tokens by ~50% and tool calls by >2x by the third round, while preserving outputs for band-gap, phonon, vacancy, and work-function analyses.

Conclusion: Agent memory can serve as a durable, portable scientific asset that outlives any single model or agent stack, enabling reproducibility and knowledge transfer in materials research.

Abstract: Materials research advances through accumulated experience - scripts that work, protocols that are trusted, warnings attached to failed calculations or experiments, and judgement that links a new question to an old result. This experience is essential for reproducibility and knowledge transfer, yet it is usually fragmented across notebooks, repositories, job logs and individual memory, and it is rarely portable across artificial-intelligence agents. Here we argue that a lifelong AI partner for materials science can be designed around persistent memory rather than around a particular agent implementation. We introduce a self-evolving memory framework that stores scientific experience as inspectable facts and executable skills, so that observations, failure boundaries, protocols and validation checks can be retrieved, revised and migrated across models. We evaluate the idea in three computational settings that expose different layers of materials-research competence. In 49 real-world materials-tool-use questions comprising 138 executable subtasks, memory nearly doubles GPT-5.2 task success without model-parameter updates. In elemental-solid equation-of-state calculations, memory converts a wavefunction-initialization failure into a pre-execution guardrail, improving outcomes from 22/1/4 to 25/2/0 Correct/Partial/Error and avoiding 92% of repeated errors. In 13 practical material simulation workflows, remembered skills and failure facts halve the aggregate trace burden (tokens) and reduce tool calls by over a factor of two by the third round, while preserving physically meaningful outputs in band-gap, phonon, vacancy and work-function analyses. These results show that agent memory can serve as a durable scientific asset; a portable, self-improving record of materials-research experience that outlives any single model or agent stack.

</details>


### [115] [Identity from the Outside: A Conceptual Framework and Research Program for AI Personality Clones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11225)
*Luc E. Brunet*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A theoretical framework for AI identity clones using a six-term factorization and a state-space model to analyze indiscernibility, introducing the delegate as a bounded clone, and advocating climate fidelity (matching conditional response distributions) as the appropriate long-horizon criterion; outlines an experimental program.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reframe personal-identity questions for AI systems in operational terms, distinguishing fidelity to a target, generic human-likeness, and individuality beyond the conscious self.

Method: Six-term factorization of identity: substrate, dispositions, memory, update dynamics, context, exogenous contingencies; model as a state-space; define indiscernibility as one minus a judge's distinguishing advantage; estimate local sensitivities via randomized ablation; use analogies to lambda-calculus, linear typing, and bisimulation; distinguish product clone, delegate, and individual; map literature, propose experiments, and present a central conjecture.

Result: Provides a formal framework and a central conjecture: given some knowledge about an agent's persistence and consequences on its stakes, versionability tends to degrade long-horizon indiscernibility; climate fidelity is proposed as the correct long-horizon criterion; introduces the delegate as a bounded, task-limited clone; maps empirical literature to the three criteria and outlines an experimental program.

Conclusion: The approach clarifies the implications of linearity for identity, elevates climate fidelity as a robust long-horizon metric, and offers a concrete agenda (conceptual decomposition, empirical mapping, and experimental protocols) for studying AI identity clones.

Abstract: AI "personality clones" force a re-examination of personal identity in operational terms. Setting aside the hard problem of consciousness, we approach identity through the indiscernibility of manifestations, as assessed by an observer over a duration. We distinguish three criteria that "identity" conflates: fidelity to a target person, generic human-likeness, and individuality. We propose a six-term factorization of observed identity (substrate, dispositions, memory, update dynamics, context, exogenous contingencies), with a state-space formulation. Indiscernibility is defined as one minus a judge's distinguishing advantage, and the factorization's coefficients become local sensitivities estimable by randomized ablation. The central claim is a conditional conjecture: given hypotheses about the agent's information on its own persistence and about consequences bearing on its own stakes, versionability tends to degrade long-horizon indiscernibility. An analogy with lambda-calculus, linear typing, and bisimulation clarifies what linearity does and does not establish. Between product-clone and individual we identify a third object, the delegate: a task-limited, bounded-lifespan partial clone ending in a bandwidth-limited testament. We map the empirical literature onto the three criteria, propose an experimental program, and argue that the correct long-horizon criterion is not trajectory fidelity but climate fidelity: matching the conditional distribution of a person's possible responses. The best clone is the one that diverges from the original as the original would have diverged from itself.

</details>


### [116] [Cutting AI Datacenter Energy with Reinforcement Learning: Measured Power Control of LLM Training from One GPU to the Fleet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11226)
*Eliseo Curcio*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An RL-based meta-controller adapts PPO-driven LM generation parameters to measured GPU power, reducing power-violation incidents while boosting output and energy efficiency; performance scales across 7B/14B/72B but actuator-sharding can break control at 72B; redesigns (actuator authority, occupancy vs. volume) restore strong performance and enable near-2x nameplate oversubscription with a low-cost operator pilot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Characterize and improve GPU power behavior during reinforcement-learning post-training of large language models, where static caps and reactive throttling waste throughput and energy, and shipping datacenters lack power-aware generation control.

Method: Instrument PPO/RLHF-type training across 7B, 14B, and 72B scales on 1–4 A100 GPUs with half-second power telemetry (380k+ samples). Train a PPO meta-controller to adjust generation parameters based on measured power. Evaluate on full 500-step traces, perform actuator-authority sweeps, test a redesigned actuator, and validate on live 72B rollout workloads across multiple replications. Analyze realistic measurement windows and a 16-GPU fleet; quantify economics and carbon impact. 

Result: The controller reduces power-limit violations by 89.8% on a 500-step 7B trace, while increasing tokens by 18.1% and energy efficiency by 26.2% (tokens/MWh). At 72B scale with the same controller, authority collapses due to model sharding. An actuator-authority sweep reveals 17–22% power authority retention under generation concurrency; using an actuator redesign and a thresholded controller yields 35.7% more output than a static safe baseline with 2.27%±1.08% budget violations and 87.2% fewer violations versus uncontrolled operation, achieving best throughput and energy per token among constrained controllers; an adaptive threshold rule matches it in one of three conditions. Realistic windows damp transients from 23.6% to 1.6% at 30s and zero at 5 minutes; a 16-GPU fleet achieves zero violations at 30s+, with peak demand 50–56% of nameplate. Approximately twofold oversubscription of nameplate appears feasible for this fleet mix, pending operator validation. The work also quantifies economic and carbon consequences and proposes a low-cost operator pilot.

Conclusion: RL-based adaptive power control for PPO-style LM training can deliver substantial throughput gains and energy efficiency with markedly fewer power violations, even at scale, but initiation challenges arise from actuator authority under model sharding at very large scales. An actuator-aware redesign and threshold-based policy restore robust performance, enabling near-nameplate oversubscription in fleet settings and suggesting practical operator pilots to realize economic and carbon benefits.

Abstract: Reinforcement-learning post-training dominates modern language-model development, yet its power behavior on GPU hardware has not been characterized, and datacenters manage GPU power with workload-blind mechanisms, static caps and reactive throttling, that slow hardware indiscriminately. We instrument GRPO training with half-second power telemetry at 7B, 14B, and 72B scales on one to four A100s (380,000+ samples), and train a PPO meta-controller that adapts the workload's own generation parameters to measured power. Against the full 500-step 7B trace, the controller cuts power-limit violations by 89.8% while increasing token output by 18.1% and energy efficiency by 26.2% (tokens per MWh). Deployed live at 72B, the same controller family yields replicated null results, diagnosed as the group-size actuator losing authority under model sharding. An actuator-authority sweep shows the same parameters applied as generation concurrency retain 17-22% power authority, isolating an occupancy-versus-volume principle; a controller rebuilt on that actuator controls a live 72B rollout-generation workload across three replications: 35.7% more output than a static safe baseline at 2.27 +/- 1.08% budget violations, 87.2% fewer violations than uncontrolled operation, and the best mean throughput and energy per token among constrained controllers, with an adaptive threshold rule matching it in one of three operating conditions. Under realistic measurement windows the original 72B transients fall from 23.6% at half-second resolution to 1.6% at 30 s and zero at 5 min; a composed 16-GPU fleet shows zero violations at 30 s and longer, with peak demand at 50-56% of nameplate. For this fleet mix, roughly twofold oversubscription of nameplate appears feasible, subject to operator validation. We quantify the economic and carbon consequences and specify a low-cost operator pilot.

</details>


### [117] [Forecasting Side Effects of Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11227)
*Chong Yong Ong,Alson Wei Jie Sim,Peixin Zhang,Jun Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Activation steering exhibits forecastable, structured side effects across 67 behaviors in 3 open-weight LMs; magnitude depends on target behavior; direction is predictable from unsteered representations, enabling proactive safety auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety and deployability concerns: unintended side effects of activation steering can hinder real-world deployment; need to forecast and audit cross-behavior interactions before steering.

Method: Construct a cross-effect matrix mapping steering-induced changes across a taxonomy of 67 behaviors in three open-weight language models; analyze the structure and asymmetry of side effects; compare against similarity-based heuristics; assess predictability of direction from unsteered representations; evaluate against baselines.

Result: Side effects are common, structured, and often asymmetric; existing similarity-based heuristics fail to account for these interactions; side effects are largely predictable before steering; magnitude depends on the target behavior, while direction can be forecasted from unsteered representations with higher accuracy than simple baselines.

Conclusion: Activation steering exhibits systematic, forecastable side effects, enabling proactive safety auditing and more informed deployment of steering interventions.

Abstract: Activation steering modifies a language model by adding a learned direction to its hidden activations, enabling targeted behavioral changes without retraining. While effective, steering often produces unintended side effects on other behaviors, making it difficult to deploy safely. We therefore ask: can these side effects be forecasted before steering is applied? We answer this question by constructing a cross-effect matrix over a taxonomy of 67 behaviors across three open-weight language models. We find that side effects are common, structured, and often asymmetric, revealing interactions that cannot be explained by existing similarity-based heuristics. Despite this complexity, we show that side effects are largely predictable before steering is performed. Their magnitude depends primarily on the target behavior, while their direction can be forecasted from the model's unsteered representations with substantially higher accuracy than simple baselines. Our results demonstrate that activation steering has systematic and forecastable side effects, enabling proactive safety auditing and more informed deployment of steering interventions.

</details>


### [118] [Synchronizing Beliefs with Second-Order Theory-of-Mind in Human-Autonomy Teams (Extended Version)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11229)
*Jack Mirenzi,Henny Admoni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An informed teacher who models the learner and uses structured understanding statements outperforms learner-driven preference selection in reward learning; teacher-model drift harms the advantage, but second-order ToM-2 statements mitigate drift, especially when teacher errors are directional.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard preference-based reward learning treats the teacher as a passive oracle, underutilizing the teacher's knowledge of the objective. As reward-feature dimensionality grows, the teacher's potential advantage increases. The work reframes preference learning as a human-autonomy team problem to preserve instructor knowledge and improve data efficiency.

Method: Propose a two-model framework: (1) teacher maintains a model of the learner to design an informative curriculum, and (2) learner maintains a second-order model of the teacher's model, emitting structured understanding statements that synchronize the two models. Evaluate via simulation with alternating teachers, comparing teacher-driven curriculum against learner-led selection and testing the impact of drift and understanding statements (including ToM-2 vs mean-belief statements).

Result: An informed teacher outperforms learner-led selection in simulations; drift in teacher models under alternating teachers erodes this advantage; introducing understanding statements repairs it, with ToM-2 statements outperforming mean-belief statements when teacher’s error about the learner is concentrated in a particular direction.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling the teacher–learner interaction with second-order understanding statements can enhance preference-based reward learning by leveraging objective knowledge, maintaining synchronization between agents, and mitigating teacher-model drift; ToM-2 formulations are particularly effective under directional error.


Abstract: Comparative feedback, asking people which of two behaviors they prefer, has become a standard way to align robot and agent behavior with human intent when the reward itself cannot be specified directly. Preference-based reward learning typically casts the human teacher as a passive oracle answering learner-generated queries. We argue this forfeits the teacher's defining advantage: knowledge of the objective. A teacher who knows the target can construct training examples more efficiently than any learner-driven acquisition strategy, an advantage that widens as the reward's feature dimension grows. However, exploiting this advantage requires an accurate model of what the learner currently knows. We therefore recast preference learning as a human-autonomy team problem coupling two behavioral models: the teacher maintains a model of the learner to design an informative curriculum, and the learner maintains a second-order model of the teacher's model, emitting structured preference constraints (understanding statements) that keep the teacher's model of the learner synchronized. In simulation, an informed teacher outperforms learner-led selection; teacher-model drift under alternating teachers erodes this advantage; and understanding statements repair it, with second-order (ToM-2) statements outperforming mean-belief statements when the teacher's error about the learner is concentrated in a particular direction rather than spread evenly.

</details>


### [119] [The Edge-based Contiguous p-median Problem with Connections to Logistics Districting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11230)
*Zeyad Kassem,Adolfo R. Escobedo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ECpM partitions road networks into k compact, contiguous edge-territories using two MIP formulations: (i) exponential cut-set constraints with branch-and-cut; (ii) polynomial shortest-path constraints solved by standard solvers. SPC-based model dramatically speeds up relative to cut-set B&C (up to 17x) and yields strong performance on large networks; SPC constraints are supervalid for the EpM model and reveal structural links to edge-based districting (EBD).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve edge-based p-median formulations by enforcing contiguity efficiently, enabling scalable, high-quality road-network partitioning with contiguity and compactness.

Method: Develop two binary programming models for ECpM. Model A uses cut-set based contiguity constraints requiring an exponential number of constraints; coupled with a branch-and-cut separation procedure. Model B uses polynomial shortest-path contiguity constraints, solvable by off-the-shelf solvers. Evaluate on road networks with ~2.7k nodes and ~3.4k edges, with models having >9.6 million binary variables. Compare B&C vs SPC in terms of time and feasibility; analyze SPC as supervalid for EpM and study ECpM–EBD connections.

Result: SPC-based approach achieved up to 17x speedup over the cut-set B&C approach. The SPC constraints also act as supervalid inequalities for EpM, potentially cutting off some integer-feasible solutions of EpM. The study reveals structural links between ECpM and EBD and demonstrates that an SPC-based EBD model solves most instances to optimality, whereas a cut-set-based model struggles to find feasibility within 12 hours.

Conclusion: Polynomial shortest-path contiguity constraints offer scalable, effective modeling for edge-based partitioning with contiguity; SPC constraints extend beyond ECpM to provide insights for EBD and reveal a path to robust, scalable solutions on large road networks.

Abstract: This paper introduces the edge-based contiguous p-median (ECpM) problem to partition the roads in a network into a given number of compact and contiguous territories. Two binary programming models are introduced, both of which incorporate a network distance. The first model requires an exponential number of cut set-based constraints to model contiguity; it is paired with a separation scheme that usually generates only a small number of these constraints, namely, a branch-and-cut (B&C) algorithm. The second model utilizes a polynomial number of shortest-path constraints to model contiguity and can be solved with off-the-shelf solvers. The respective solution approaches are tested on road networks with over 2,700 nodes and close to 3,400 edges, yielding models with over 9.6 million binary variables. Solving the model based on shortest path contiguity (SPC) constraints via standard branch and bound attains speedups in computational time of up to 17x relative to the cut set-based B&C implementation. In addition, the SPC constraints are demonstrated to be supervalid inequalities of the edge-based p-median (EpM) model (i.e., for which contiguity is not explicitly required), meaning that they may cut off integer-feasible solutions and some, but not all, of the optimal solutions of this simpler problem. Finally, the paper explores structural insights and connections between ECpM and the edge-based districting (EBD) problem, which enforces an additional work balance criterion. An existing model that utilizes cut set-based contiguity constraints was unable to find a feasible solution within 12 hours for any of the tested instances, while an SPC-based EBD model was able to solve most of these to optimality.

</details>


### [120] [LinearKV: One Cached State Suffices for Position-Independent Caching in Hybrid LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11231)
*Yirui Liu,Ruoling Qi,Longwen Wang,Xuaner Wu,Jian Chen,Yuxin Jin,Jiawei Shao,Xuelong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LinearKV provides a training-free hybrid-PIC framework for LLMs with linear recurrence layers, enabling token-indexed PIC compatibility by decoupled initialization; a single cached state initializer suffices and is cheaper, while exact composition (HYPIC) is not generally beneficial and can be harmful on some architectures; empirical results show near-full quality recovery on some models and significant latency reductions across LongBench QA and RULER at 8K-32K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend PIC to hybrid LLMs that replace most attention with linear recurrences lacking token-indexed KV, enabling practical caching-based acceleration without retraining.

Method: Propose LinearKV with decoupled initialization: map K local states to a single initial state per linear layer, while full-attention layers still concatenate KV. Show compatibility with existing PIC selectors; compare against exact composition baseline (HYPIC) across multiple models and tasks.

Result: Single cached state initializer recovers near full quality on some models (up to 92%); exact composition reduces performance on Mamba-2 under EPIC to 46.6% vs 86.8% for single-state; LinearKV also reduces time-to-first-token to 0.46x full prefill, cheaper than exact composition; results hold across LongBench QA and RULER 8K-32K.

Conclusion: A simple, training-free, single-state initializer suffices for many hybrid models, enabling efficient PIC while preserving quality; exact compositional approaches may be unnecessary or harmful in some architectures.

Abstract: LLM serving is increasingly accelerated by position-independent caching (PIC). Existing PIC methods, however, are built for full-attention models, where a token-indexed KV cache underlies its core operations: matching reusable token chunks, concatenating their KV entries, and selectively recomputing a few tokens to restore cross-chunk context. Hybrid LLMs break these primitives---they replace most attention layers with linear recurrences that expose only a fixed-size state, leaving no token-indexed KV to concatenate or to locally repair. This raises a natural question: can PIC benefit hybrid models, and what would it take? We present LinearKV, a training-free hybrid-PIC framework. Its key insight is a \emph{decoupled initialization}: each linear layer maps its $K$ matched local states to a single initial state, while full-attention layers concatenate their KV as before. LinearKV is therefore compatible with existing PIC methods, reusing their token selection and recomputation as-is. Under this framework, we find that a \emph{single cached state} suffices as the linear layer's initializer. The algebraically principled alternative---composing all $K$ cached states into the exact full-prefix state, as concurrent work HYPIC does---is unnecessary and, on some architectures, even harmful. We compare the two across three hybrid models and three PIC selectors. On the two GDN models the two tie, both recovering most of full quality (up to $92\%$); on the Mamba-2 model, exact composition instead collapses under every selector---under EPIC, for instance, it recovers only $46.6\%$ of full quality, versus $86.8\%$ for a single cached block initializer. A single state initializer is also cheaper, cutting time-to-first-token to $0.46\times$ full prefill versus a further $5$--$17\%$ overhead for exact composition; results hold across LongBench QA and RULER at 8K--32K.

</details>


### [121] [InfraBench: Evaluating Infrastructure Agents Across Layers, Lifecycle, and Risk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11234)
*Yuan Gao,Zeren Yang,Junnan Li,Shawn,Zhong,Ahmed Dajani,Mai Zheng,Andrea Arpaci-Dusseau,Remzi Arpaci-Dusseau*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: InfraBench is a benchmark suite to evaluate AI agents on realistic infrastructure tasks across the full system stack and lifecycle; results show no agent reaches full score; mean effective scores 40-88%; three repeats reveal fragility; per-check scoring reveals durable changes and invariants issues; public leaderboard at infraben.ch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify AI agents' ability to manage real-world infrastructure complexity and provide a standardized, risk-aware evaluation across tasks and lifecycle.

Method: InfraBench benchmark suite with live leaderboard and evaluation harness; 15 agent-model configurations; tasks spanning full stack and lifecycle; repeat each task three times; per-check scoring; fine-grained risk assessment.

Result: No agent achieves full score; mean scores 40-88% with SE 6-12; top configs pass only a fraction of attempts; short-term objectives achieved but durable changes, invariants broken, unsafe side effects, uncleaned state left.

Conclusion: InfraBench is publicly available; provides evaluation framework and leaderboard; results indicate substantial room for improvement in durable, safe, and clean infrastructure management by AI agents.

Abstract: Managing modern computing infrastructure has become a steadily harder problem due to the ever-increasing complexity. Recent advances in AI agents create a timely opportunity to automate infrastructure management tasks, but it remains unclear how well such agents can handle real-world infrastructure complexity. We present InfraBench, a benchmark suite for evaluating AI agents on realistic infrastructure tasks across the full system stack and full operational lifecycle with fine-grained risk assessment. Experiments with 15 agent-model configurations show that even the strongest agent cannot secure a full score across all tasks. Mean effective scores range from roughly 40% to 88% (with per-configuration standard errors of 6-12 points), repeating every task three times reveals that top configurations still pass only a fraction of their attempts, and per-check scoring exposes a general failure pattern: agents may routinely satisfy short-term objectives while leaving non-durable changes, broken distributed invariants, unsafe side effects, and uncleaned state behind. INFRABENCH, including its live leaderboard, tasks, and evaluation harness, is publicly available at infraben.ch.

</details>


### [122] [CORA-Diff: Confidence-Oriented Residual Acceptance for Efficient Diffusion Language Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11235)
*Yifan Wu,Yufeng Zhang,Kenli Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A training-free, native-trajectory gating method (CORA-Diff) accelerates diffusion language-model decoding by selectively accepting residual tokens with confidence and persistence signals, reducing repeated denoising while preserving or improving task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion LM decoders often perform fixed-horizon, blockwise decoding that still denoises all positions. Many predictions stabilize early, but decoding remains until all positions are resolved. Existing accelerators rely on filters, modified scores, dependency models, or caches. The goal is to exploit native trajectory signals to identify residual positions that will match the dense endpoint, enabling early termination without backbone changes or learned components.

Method: CORA-Diff is training-free and applies confidence-and-persistence gating only to unresolved positions, preserving the original transfer rule and visibility of accepted tokens. The block terminates when all positions are resolved. No backbone modifications, learned acceptance model, or logit tweaks are needed. A calibration subset selects a single operating point (frozen) for all evaluations. The approach relies on high-confidence, persistent predictions being predictive of the fixed-horizon endpoint, supported by paired post-intervention trajectories.

Result: Under a matched Learn2PD-style LLaDA protocol, CORA-Diff achieves the lowest runtime across eight task-length settings. Task scores match or exceed dense decoding in five settings, with at most a 1.22-point drop. Incremental speedups of 2.70x (GSM8K) and 3.32x (HumanEval) over EOS-aware dense decoding; up to 13.14x under a fixed-horizon 1024/1024 mechanism-isolation protocol; and transfers to Dream at 3.18x–3.53x without retuning. These results indicate native confidence and persistence yield reliable residual acceptance, reducing repeated denoising while preserving quality.

Conclusion: CORA-Diff demonstrates that native trajectory signals can reliably identify residual positions to terminate decoding early, achieving significant runtime savings with minimal or no loss in task performance and without backbone changes or learned components. The method leverages confidence and persistence to guide residual acceptance and shows strong generalization across task lengths and domains.

Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) update many tokens in parallel, yet practical decoders often use a fixed denoising horizon. Many predictions stabilize early, but blockwise decoding continues until all positions are resolved, causing repeated dense forward passes. Existing accelerators often rely on learned filters, modified scores, dependency models, or cache-specific mechanisms. We ask whether native trajectory signals can identify residual positions likely to match the deterministic dense endpoint. We propose CORA-Diff, a training-free method that preserves the original transfer rule and applies confidence-and-persistence gating only to positions that rule leaves unresolved. Accepted tokens remain visible as context, and the block terminates once all positions are resolved. This requires no backbone change, learned acceptance model, or logit modification. Our theory explains why high-confidence, persistent predictions are more likely to match the fixed-horizon dense endpoint, and paired post-intervention trajectories provide direct empirical support. We select one operating point on a separate GSM8K calibration subset and freeze it for all evaluations. Under a matched Learn2PD-style LLaDA protocol, CORA-Diff has the lowest measured runtime in all eight task-length settings. Task scores match or exceed dense decoding in five settings, and the largest observed drop is 1.22 points. Its incremental speedups over EOS-aware dense decoding are 2.70x and 3.32x on GSM8K and HumanEval. It also reaches 13.14x under the fixed-horizon 1024/1024 mechanism-isolation protocol and transfers to Dream without retuning at 3.18x-3.53x. These results show that native confidence and persistence enable reliable residual acceptance, reducing repeated denoising computation while preserving task quality.

</details>


### [123] [Geometry-aware Incremental Neural Operator for Long-Horizon PDE prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11237)
*Jiaquan Zhang,Shuxu Chen,Haifan Meng,Yi Lu,Zhihan Lyu,Fan Mo,Wei Dong,Yang Yang,Chaoning Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI
