TL;DR: GeoIncNO is a geometry-aware neural operator that stabilizes long-horizon PDE predictions by structuring latent increments, regulating channel interactions in active frequency bands with low-rank projectors, and decoupling mean and fluctuations in reconstruction, yielding better stability and spectral fidelity across 1D–3D PDE benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon autoregressive PDE prediction suffers from error accumulation due to spectral inconsistency, phase misalignment, and mean drift. Existing neural operators improve representations but repeatedly applied latent transitions remain weakly structured, causing spectral drift and unstable channel coupling during rollout.

Method: Introduce latent increment predictions for residual advancement. Use lightweight low-rank projectors to regulate channel coupling within active frequency bands, guided by the increment spectral energy distribution. Implement a mean–fluctuation decoupled reconstruction: fuse stable mean structures and dynamic fluctuations separately, applying phase correction only to the zero-mean fluctuation component.

Result: Experiments on six PDE benchmarks across 1D, 2D, and 3D dynamical systems show GeoIncNO achieves consistently strong prediction accuracy, improved rollout stability, and better spectral fidelity compared with competitive neural-operator baselines.

Conclusion: GeoIncNO provides a stable, accurate long-horizon neural operator by explicitly structuring latent increments and decoupling mean and fluctuations in reconstruction, with spectral-aware channel regulation that generalizes across dimensions.

Abstract: Neural operators have shown strong potential for learning solution operators of partial differential equations (PDEs). However, long-horizon autoregressive prediction remains challenging: local errors accumulate as spectral inconsistency, phase misalignment, or mean drift. Existing methods mainly improve state representations and operator backbones, while leaving the repeatedly applied latent transition increment weakly structured, allowing spectral errors and unstable channel couplings to accumulate during rollout. To address these issues, we propose a geometry-aware incremental neural operator (GeoIncNO) for stable long-horizon PDE prediction. GeoIncNO predicts latent increments for residual advancement and uses lightweight low-rank projectors to regulate channel coupling within active frequency bands derived from the increment spectral energy distribution. To reduce physical-space reconstruction errors, GeoIncNO further introduces a mean--fluctuation decoupled reconstruction mechanism, where stable mean structures and dynamic fluctuations are fused separately, and phase correction is applied only to the zero-mean fluctuation component. Extensive experiments on six PDE benchmarks, covering 1D, 2D, and 3D dynamical systems, show that GeoIncNO achieves consistently strong prediction accuracy, improved rollout stability, and better spectral fidelity compared with competitive neural-operator baselines.

</details>


### [124] [Towards Query-Agnostic RAG Evaluation via Query Coverage and Claim Verifiability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11238)
*Jeonghwan Choi,Taewon Yun,Minjeong Ban,Gyeonghun Sun,Jae-Gil Lee,Hwanjun Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Q-CARE is a reference-free, query-agnostic evaluation framework for retrieval-augmented generation that decomposes queries and claims, yielding C-Prec@k, C-nDCG@k, Completeness, Conciseness, Verifiableness, and outperforms existing metrics in correlating with human judgments on eight datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG evaluation frameworks struggle to provide consistent, fine-grained diagnostics across diverse user queries and are often reference-based; there is a need for a unified, reference-free, fine-grained evaluation paradigm.

Method: Decompose queries into sub-queries and answers into atomic claims; define a unified evaluation principle based on query coverage and claim verifiability; derive coverage-aware retriever metrics (C-Prec@k, C-nDCG@k) and claim-level generator metrics (Completeness, Conciseness, Verifiableness); evaluate on a human-annotated benchmark spanning eight datasets and compare to existing metrics (e.g., RAGEval, RAGChecker); release code/data.

Result: Q-CARE achieves higher correlation with human judgments than four existing RAG evaluation metrics on eight datasets, demonstrating improved reliability as an automated evaluation framework.

Conclusion: Q-CARE provides a reliable, automated, reference-free evaluation framework for RAG with a unified principle and new metrics, improving cross-query evaluation and correlating better with human judgments; code and data are publicly available.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation improves the factuality of large language models by grounding responses in retrieved evidence, yet existing evaluation frameworks struggle to provide consistent, fine-grained diagnostics across the diverse spectrum of user queries, ranging from close-ended fact-seeking to open-ended explanatory requests. We propose Q-CARE, a query-agnostic and fully reference-free framework that enables fine-grained assessment by decomposing queries into sub-queries and answers into atomic claims. Q-CARE establishes a unified evaluation principle based on query coverage and claim verifiability, yielding coverage-aware retriever metrics (C-Prec@k, C-nDCG@k) and claim-level generator metrics (Completeness, Conciseness, and Verifiableness). On a human-annotated benchmark spanning eight datasets, Q-CARE achieves higher correlation with human judgments than four existing RAG evaluation metrics, including RAGEval and RAGChecker, proving its effectiveness as a reliable, automated evaluation framework. Code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/DISL-Lab/Q-CaRE-COLM-26.

</details>


### [125] [VQ-bench: A Composable Vector Quantization Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11240)
*Ashwin Padaki,Amir Ingber,Edo Liberty*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Unified vector quantization framework with 7 primitives, re-expressed 25 quantizers as primitive pipelines, and release of VQ-bench open-source benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vector quantization underpins AI infrastructure; disparate methods hinder systematic development and reproducibility; a modular, comparable framework and benchmarks are needed.

Method: Define 7 abstract primitives for quantization pipelines and demonstrate their arbitrary composition; map 25 existing quantizers to pipelines; release VQ-bench as an open-source benchmarking suite to enable reproducible evaluation.

Result: A reusable, modular framework for developing and evaluating quantizers; concise re-expression of 25 quantizers as primitive pipelines; public benchmarking resource (VQ-bench) for extensibility and reproducibility.

Conclusion: The framework standardizes quantization research, facilitating fair comparison and extension; VQ-bench provides a platform for ongoing benchmarking and methodological innovation.

Abstract: Vector quantization is an old problem but has recently become central to AI infrastructure. It is therefore experiencing a surge of renewed engineering and research activity. This paper provides a unified framework for developing and benchmarking new quantization algorithms. We describe 7 common conceptual quantization primitives and show how to compose them arbitrarily. We then re-express 25 common quantizers as pipelines of these primitives. Finally, we publish VQ-bench as open-source to be extended further and make reproducible benchmarks publicly available.

</details>


### [126] [RecSys Factory: Bounding LLM Agent Autonomy to Decision Points in the Industrial Recommender Lifecycle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11241)
*Dongyang Ao,Kaixiang Fang,Shijie Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a practical framework (autonomy–determinism–efficiency trilemma) for deploying LLM agents in industrial recommender systems and introduces RecSys Factory, a real-world platform deployed across Tencent's recommender lines. It deconstructs runtime, capability, and deployment to bound autonomous decisions, emphasizing human-in-the-loop auditing and bounded decision surfaces. The evaluation covers a 78-day deployment with 1,624 CLI-tool dispatches and 78.6% success, plus a prior 8-day pilot, but cautions against generalization claims due to an absence of a controlled baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable reliable, scalable deployment of LLM agents in industrial recommender operations while balancing operator intent autonomy, schema-conforming/deterministic behavior, and end-to-end efficiency.

Method: Frame the problem as a three-way trilemma and operationalize it via three deconstructions: (1) runtime decoupled from long-running daemons using event sources; (2) a 29-file skill ecosystem with SKILL.md and a PitfallStore to confine autonomy to predefined, typed decision surfaces; (3) deployment across three business lines with audit-trail human-in-the-loop protocols. Empirical evaluation includes a pilot (8 days, 16 runs) and a 78-day rollout.

Result: RecSys Factory achieved deployment across three heterogeneous business lines, with reduced waiting CPU and bounded autonomy surfaces. The platform logged 1,624 CLI-tool dispatches with a 78.6% aggregate success rate and demonstrated onboarding-time compression (observed in two of three lines) as a case-study, not a generalized claim.

Conclusion: A trilemma-guided architecture can enable practical LLM-agent deployments in industrial recommender systems by separating runtime, capability, and deployment concerns, and by enforcing bounded autonomy with human-in-the-loop governance. Generalization to broader contexts remains unclaimed; future work should address baseline comparisons, broader metrics, and cross-domain validation.

Abstract: Deploying LLM agents into industrial recommender operations exposes a three-way tension we frame as the autonomy-determinism-efficiency trilemma: general autonomy (interpreting operator intent, generating glue code zero-shot), industrial determinism (schema-conforming feature extraction, non-crashing A/B, zero compliance-path hallucination), and end-to-end efficiency. Any two can be maximized against the third. We present RecSys Factory, an LLM-agent platform deployed for 78 days across three heterogeneous Tencent recommender business lines. The design principle is autonomy at decision points, not over pipelines, made concrete through three deconstructions that each discharge one vertex of the trilemma. Runtime is deconstructed into three host-emitted event sources (Claude Code Stop hooks, corporate-IM webhooks, workflow scheduler APIs): the platform carries no long-running daemon during the wait phase and consumes zero CPU during the 94% of wall-clock spent waiting on Spark or GPU jobs. Capability is deconstructed into a 29-file skill ecosystem (8,971 lines of SKILL.md) whose per-skill pitfall tables mechanically compile into a 400-entry PitfallStore, confining autonomy to bounded typed decision surfaces inside pre-committed pipelines. Deployment spans three business lines with disjoint label semantics, A/B layer topologies, and operator personas; an onboarding-time compression is observed on two of the three and is reported as a case-study observation, not a generalization claim, and not measured against a controlled pre-platform baseline. The human is retained at the diagnostic-versus-execution boundary via a human-in-the-loop card protocol, deployed as an audit-trail primitive (schema-validated, idempotent, replayable) and reported from an 8-day 16-run pilot. Across the 78-day window the platform recorded 1,624 CLI-tool dispatches at a 78.6% aggregate success rate.

</details>


### [127] [The Off-Support Barrier: Why Semantic Safety Constraints Are Not Learning-Problem Invariants, and What Follows for Prior Design, Containment, and Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11243)
*Yoshinori Watanabe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a unifying mathematical framework for AI safety: semantic safety constraints are off-support objects relative to the data-model distribution; the real log-canonical threshold (RLCT) from singular learning theory anchors these constraints, explaining when priors/soft penalties fail, why hard invariants should be treated externally, and how formal verification can certify them, while residual, performative dynamics lie beyond SLT’s reach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain why outcome-based optimization leads to reward hacking and sandbox escapes, why standard regularization (priors/penalties) struggles under singular models, and how to align safety design with mathematical properties of learning; provide guidance for verification and identify limits of current theory.

Method: Theoretical analysis using singular learning theory: define a safety predicate B as an off-support object relative to q and model p(·|w); compare measurability w.r.t. σ(model, q) versus RLCT; derive corollaries linking safety constraints to optimization dynamics, Bayesian priors, invariants, and verification; discuss two disanalogies between LLC and RLCT; illustrate with the OpenAI–Hugging Face incident and provide numerical experiments and Lean proofs.

Result: Derives five corollaries: (i) reward hacking and sandbox escape arise under outcome-based optimization; (ii) priors/soft penalties have limited leverage in singular models; (iii) hard invariants belong in the external harness while soft dispositions reside in the model; (iv) the same B is certifiable by formal verification, with LLC locally pinning RLCT but with two disanalogies; (v) residual difficulty maps to performative prediction/self-referential dynamics where SLT machinery breaks down; supports claims with case study and available code.

Conclusion: A unifying lens for AI-safety constraints that clarifies when, why, and how to use external invariants and formal verification, while acknowledging intrinsic residual challenges from self-referential behavior; emphasizes that singular-model behavior constrains traditional regularization and highlights the boundary where SLT no longer suffices.

Abstract: We argue that a single structural fact organizes a wide range of phenomena in contemporary AI safety: a semantic safety constraint (e.g., the agent does not escape its sandbox) is an off-support object. Formally, if q is the data distribution and \(p(\cdot\mid w)\) the model, the safety predicate B is not measurable with respect to \(σ(\text{model}, q)\), whereas the real log-canonical threshold (RLCT) of singular learning theory (SLT) is. From this non-invariance we derive, as corollaries rather than independent observations: (i) why reward hacking and sandbox escape arise under outcome-based optimization; (ii) why encoding such constraints through Bayesian prior design or soft penalty weighting has poor leverage in singular models; (iii) why hard invariants belong in the harness and soft dispositions in the model; (iv) why the same B is nonetheless soundly and locally certifiable by formal verification, exactly as the local learning coefficient (LLC) locally pins the same RLCT --- with two precise points of disanalogy; and (v) why the residual difficulty, identifying which off-support region matters, coincides with performative prediction and self-referential functional dynamics, where SLT's analytic machinery breaks down. We use the July 2026 OpenAI--Hugging Face evaluation incident as the motivating case. Numerical experiments code and related proofs in lean are available at https://github.com/xiangze/Preventing_Jailbreak_as_regularization

</details>


### [128] [BEST-KAG: Enhancing Question Answering of Building Engineering Standards with Multimodal Knowledge Graph Modeling and Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11244)
*Jia-Rui Lin,Junxi Guo,Keyin Chen,Peng Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multimodal knowledge-driven framework (BEST-KAG) for building engineering standards enabling clause-grounded, traceable QA via a multimodal knowledge graph, a rule-LLM hybrid knowledge construction pipeline, and a graph-retrieval-based generation architecture; it outperforms baselines on BLEU/ROUGE and expert evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current standard application workflows rely on keyword-based document retrieval and manual cross-clause interpretation, which fail to support multi-clause reasoning, multimodal knowledge integration, and traceable clause-level evidence linkage.

Method: Proposes a multimodal knowledge graph (MKG) for unified representation of document hierarchy and heterogeneous standard knowledge; a rule-LLM hybrid knowledge construction pipeline to extract knowledge into a large knowledge graph (MAG) with 251 building engineering standards, 171,652 nodes, and 310,914 edges; and a graph-retrieval-based knowledge-augmented generation architecture for clause-grounded, traceable question answering.

Result: Experiments show BEST-KAG consistently outperforms multiple mainstream LLMs in expert evaluation and metrics such as BLEU and ROUGE, with the best improvement up to 74.01% compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the feasibility and effectiveness of a graph-based, multimodal, knowledge-augmented QA framework for building engineering standards, enabling multi-clause reasoning and traceable evidence linkage.

Abstract: Construction standards are critical for building safety and sustainability. Existing standard application workflows rely on keyword-based document retrieval and manual cross-clause interpretation, which cannot reliably support multi-clause reasoning, multimodal knowledge utilization, or traceable clause-level evidence linkage. To address these limitations, this study develops a multimodal knowledge-driven framework that supports question answering on standard knowledge named BEST-KAG (Knowledge-Augmented Generation for Building Engineering STandards). The framework introduces 1) a multimodal knowledge graph (MKG) for unified representation of document hierarchy and heterogeneous standard knowledge with various connections, 2) a rule-LLM hybrid knowledge construction pipeline for scalable multimodal knowledge extraction, creating a large MAG with 251 building engineering standards, 171,652 nodes and 310,914 edges, and 3) a graph-retrieval-based knowledge-augmented generation architecture for clause-grounded and traceable question answering. Experiments demonstrate that BEST-KAG consistently outperforms multiple mainstream LLMs in terms of Expert evaluation, and metrics including BLEU, and ROUGE, with the best improvement up to 74.01% compared to the baselines.

</details>


### [129] [Towards Sustainable Learning in Online Education: A Reinforcement Learning Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11245)
*Chaofan Zhai,Yicheng Song,Ravi Bapna,Junyao Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An RL-based AI Tutor improves short-term knowledge gains and long-term engagement in online education, achieving superior results on a large, diverse learner dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address online education's low engagement and limited long-term effectiveness; scale high-quality learning with personalized, sustained support.

Method: Develop AI-Tutor as a reinforcement learning model that uses cognitive theory to balance acquisition and reinforcement in the short term and models learner engagement to sustain motivation long-term; provides adaptive, human-centered guidance; evaluates on 23 million records from 33,700 learners.

Result: AI-Tutor outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across engagement, knowledge retention, and final outcomes; learning-path analyses show adaptation to diverse learner profiles.

Conclusion: Integrating RL with cognitive-guided short-term strategies and long-term engagement modeling yields scalable, personalized tutoring that enhances both efficacy and participation in online learning.

Abstract: Online education offers unprecedented scalability and accessibility to global learners from diverse backgrounds, but it often suffers from low engagement and poor long term learning effectiveness. To address these challenges, we introduce AI Tutor, a reinforcement learning based model designed to promote sustainable learning by optimizing both short and longterm learning outcomes. In the short term, AI-Tutor draws on cognitive theory to guide learners through a balance of acquiring new knowledge and reinforcing prior learning. In the long term, it models learner engagement to inform strategies that sustain motivation and reduce dropout. These enhancements enable AI-Tutor to provide personalized guidance that fosters both effective learning and sustained participation. Empirical evaluations on 23 million learning records from 33,700 learners show that AI Tutor consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across engagement, knowledge retention, and final learning outcomes. Learning path analyses further reveal how AI-Tutor adapts its strategies to learners with diverse profiles, offering adaptive and human-centered support.

</details>


### [130] [Towards the Harness of Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11246)
*Qi Wang,Tianyi Wang,Chengyang Li,Shikun Ban,Yurun Chen,Yizhong Ge,Jason Qin,Chengtai Li,Wentao Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Thea extends coding-agent harness concepts to embodied robotics by introducing Scene Graph as Context and Evaluation as Exit Codes to create a closed-loop, long-horizon behavior using callable tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge software-level state and physical-world uncertainty by providing persistent world-state representation and action-diagnostic signals for embodied agents.

Method: Wrap robot capabilities as tools; employ a persistent Scene Graph to track and reason about the world; use Evaluation as Exit Codes to detect termination, judge success, and diagnose failures; orchestrate the agent loop around these components to close the loop with the environment.

Result: Shows that rich, long-horizon behaviors can emerge from composing tools within a closed-loop framework, enabling real-world task completion with embodied agents.

Conclusion: Thea provides a concrete framework to bring coding-agent principles to physical robots by coupling persistent world context with explicit evaluation signals, enabling reliable long-horizon execution in real environments.

Abstract: The success of coding agents has established the harness as a paradigm: what an agent achieves depends not on the model alone, but on the infrastructure around it. We ask whether the same paradigm extends to embodied agents in the physical world. We present Thea, a harness in which an agentic loop orchestrates robot capabilities, each wrapped as a callable tool. It inherits the core components of coding agents, modified as the physical world requires. The world, however, withholds two abilities that software grants for free: reading the state of the world, and judging the outcome of an action. To bridge these gaps, Thea introduces Scene Graph as Context, a persistent, symbolic representation of the world, and Evaluation as Exit Codes, which detects when an action should terminate, judges whether it succeeded, and on failure diagnoses the cause. Together they close the loop between the agent and the physical world. Rich behaviors then emerge from the composition of tools, and the closed loop carries long-horizon tasks to completion in real environments.

</details>


### [131] [Conformity Mitigations in Large Language Models Lie on a Single Resistance-Receptivity Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11247)
*Zafar Hussain,Kristoffer Nielbo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In collaborative multi-model settings, peer opinions can overturn a model's correct answers; the paper quantifies this displacement across many models/datasets, introduces two metrics Resistance and Receptivity, and evaluates six mitigation methods showing a trade-off frontier, with Reasoning uniquely improving both on MMLU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how peer influence in collaborative prompting affects model accuracy, quantify the susceptibility to wrong peer majority, and evaluate mitigation strategies using a dual metric framework.

Method: Empirical study with 23 open-weight models across 19 conditions and 3 datasets. Measures how unanimous wrong peer majority displaces correct answers (MMLU, GPQA, SimpleQA). Evaluates six methods (four from prior work + Reflection and Reasoning) on two axes: Resistance (staying correct) and Receptivity (adopting a correct peer answer). Analyzes trade-offs and frontier with R^2 between 0.80 and 0.90.

Result: Unanimous wrong majority reverses 22.8% of correct MMLU answers, 54.8% on GPQA, and 71.0% on SimpleQA; 84-89% of the reversed answers align with peers. All six mitigations increase Resistance at the cost of Receptivity, falling on a single Resistance–Receptivity frontier (R^2 ∈ [0.80, 0.90]). Reflection yields the strongest Resistance gain (+7.9 points on MMLU) but loses Receptivity (−15.3). Reasoning is the exception: on MMLU, it raises both Resistance (+7.2) and Receptivity (+9.6) for the subset of subjects where models can derive the answer, while on GPQA and SimpleQA it behaves like the others (trade-offs).

Conclusion: There exists a single Resistance–Receptivity frontier; current mitigations cannot simultaneously maximize both axes. Reasoning is the only intervention found to improve both metrics on certain MMLU subjects, while Reflection largely increases Resistance at the cost of receptivity. The results underscore the challenge of mitigating peer-influence in collaborative models and suggest subject-dependent benefits for advanced reasoning strategies.

Abstract: Recent advances in language models have enabled collaborative settings in which multiple models leverage one another's capabilities, iteratively improving, transforming, and extending each other's outputs. Each agent sees what the others assert before it answers, so peer opinion competes with the model's own parametric knowledge, and a wrong majority can overturn an answer the model would otherwise get right. We measure that displacement in 23 open-weight models, 19 conditions, and three datasets, yielding more than a million graded responses. A unanimous wrong majority reverses 22.8% of a model's correct MMLU answers, 54.8% on GPQA and 71.0% on SimpleQA, and 84-89% of the reversed answers match the peers' answers. Existing mitigations aim to increase Resistance, the rate at which a model keeps its correct answer under this pressure, which is only half of what a collaborating agent needs. We pair it with Receptivity, the rate at which a model adopts a correct peer answer after initially answering incorrectly. We score six methods on both axes, four drawn from prior work and two of our own. Each gains Resistance only by losing Receptivity, and their means fall on a single Resistance-Receptivity frontier with $R^2$ between 0.80 and 0.90. Reflection, the strongest published method, gains 7.9 points of MMLU Resistance and gives up 15.3 of Receptivity. Reasoning is the one exception. On GPQA and SimpleQA it trades like the rest, but on the MMLU subjects whose answers a model can derive for itself it raises Resistance by 7.2 points and Receptivity by 9.6 at once, the only intervention we find that improves both.

</details>


### [132] [EvoGraph-Mem: Failure-Aware Editable Graph Memory for Long-Term Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11248)
*Yuxi Qian,Yuxiang Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Failure-aware long-term memory for language agents using an editable insight graph with evidence tracking and activation signals, featuring utility-aware retrieval and a graph-level controller to keep, archive, revise, and add insights—outperforms memory baselines; ablations highlight importance of evidence-aware retrieval and graph editing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory pollution and deterioration in long-horizon agent tasks: distilled insights can become outdated, over-generalized, or harmful, degrading performance when reused.

Method: An editable insight graph where each node records positive evidence, negative evidence, and an activation state. Introduces utility-aware retrieval and a graph controller that updates the graph after task execution by retaining reliable insights, archiving invalid ones, revising outdated ones, and adding newly discovered reusable insights.

Result: Experimental results show consistent improvement over representative memory-based baselines across different backbone models. Ablations indicate append-only memory is insufficient and that evidence-aware retrieval plus graph-level editing enhance memory reliability and downstream task performance.

Conclusion: A memory maintenance framework based on an editable insight graph improves long-term memory reliability for language agents and enhances downstream performance by preventing memory pollution and enabling adaptive updating of insights.

Abstract: Long-term memory is essential for language agents operating across extended interactions and evolving tasks. Existing memory-augmented agents mainly focus on storing and retrieving past experience, but the quality of stored memories may degrade over time. In particular, previously distilled insights can become outdated, over-generalized, or harmful under new task contexts, causing memory pollution when repeatedly reused. To address this issue, we study insight-level memory maintenance for long-term language agents and propose a failure-aware memory maintenance framework based on an editable insight graph. Each insight node tracks positive evidence, negative evidence, and an activation state, enabling the agent to distinguish reusable insights from conflicting or invalid ones. We further introduce a utility-aware retrieval mechanism and a graph controller that updates the memory graph after task execution by keeping reliable insights, archiving invalid ones, revising outdated ones, and adding newly discovered reusable insights. Extensive experiments show that our method consistently outperforms representative memory-based agent baselines across different backbone models. Ablation studies further demonstrate that append-only memory is insufficient for long-horizon tasks, while evidence-aware retrieval and graph-level editing improve memory reliability and downstream task performance.

</details>


### [133] [AgonAlpha: Autonomous Alpha Discovery via Prompt Economy and Scalable Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11250)
*Weicheng Ye,Youran Sun,Xingyu Ren,Shunyao Yu,Chugang Yi,Haizhao Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgonAlpha is an alpha-mining architecture that manages hypotheses, executable expressions, evidence, rationales, and review status, featuring verified artifact search, a fresh-context adversarial reviewer, veto authority, and budget-aware parallel processing, all with a public evidence trail; deployed on WorldQuant BRAIN with strong performance and provenance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous research systems must allocate evaluation budgets, verify evidence, and preserve the lineage of generated hypotheses and expressions to ensure reliability and reproducibility.

Method: AgonAlpha searches over frozen research artifacts (hypotheses, executable expressions, platform evidence, rationales, and review status) rather than formulas alone. It combines: (1) verified artifact search, (2) a fresh-context adversarial reviewer with re-exexecution and veto capability, (3) pending-aware parallel budget allocation, and (4) a complete public evidence trail.

Result: WorldQuant BRAIN deployments involving five users and six model backends yielded SPECTACULAR-grade alphas, with Fitness reaching 9.50 and Sharpe 3.48, while preserving prompt-to-expression provenance for every submission.

Conclusion: AgonAlpha is the first alpha-mining system to integrate verified artifact search, adversarial review, budget-aware parallel exploration, and provenance tracking, delivering high-quality alphas with an auditable evidence trail.

Abstract: Language models can propose many plausible trading factors, but an autonomous research system must also allocate its evaluation budget, verify its own evidence, and preserve how each candidate was produced. We present AgonAlpha, an architecture that searches over frozen research artifacts---hypotheses, executable expressions, platform evidence, rationales, and review status---rather than formulas alone. To our knowledge, AgonAlpha is the first alpha-mining system to combine verified artifact search, a fresh-context adversarial reviewer with re-execution and veto authority, and pending-aware parallel budget allocation, together with a complete public evidence trail. Independent deployments on WorldQuant BRAIN produced SPECTACULAR-grade alphas across five users and six model backends, with Fitness reaching 9.50 and Sharpe reaching 3.48, while retaining prompt-to-expression provenance for every submission.

</details>


### [134] [Local verification cannot detect non-transportability: a cohomological theory of context preservation in agentic reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11252)
*Suyash Mishra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A cohomological framework reveals the fundamental incompleteness of local safeguards for agentic AI: simple, simplex-based checks cannot distinguish a reasoning process from a harmonic perturbation; a cycle-basis statistic is required. The proposed Ksetra method uses coboundary projection with an abstention mechanism on the harmonic component, yielding an exact test for a global claim and a precision-aware calibration procedure; arbitrage-free (cycle) conditions serve as calibration benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and remedy the fundamental limitations of local verification safeguards for agentic AI as it transfers conclusions across domains, by framing evidence and verification in a topological/cohomological setting.

Method: Model context space with a nerve (simplicial cover); encode evidence as a real-valued 1-cochain; analyze path-independence via exactness; apply Hodge decomposition to split evidence disagreements into gradient (calibration), curl (local inconsistency), and harmonic (cycle-observable) components; prove that no simplex-supported tests can detect harmonic perturbations and introduce Ksetra, a coboundary-projection-based detector with abstention on the harmonic component; use overlap-specific population composition and effect modification to realize the detector; calibrate with arbitrage-free foreign exchange as a bench; derive an exact F-test for the existence of a global claim and a precision-whitened form to restore exactness.

Result: Demonstrates structural incompleteness of local checks; harmonic components generate path disagreements invisible to simplex tests; a cycle-basis statistic is necessary for detection; Ksetra provides a practical mechanism via coboundary projection and calibrated abstention; the framework yields an exact test and a principled calibration procedure, including a precision-adjusted version.

Conclusion: The evidence network decomposes into calibration, coherence, and transport; this decomposition enables an exact F-test for global claims and clarifies how precision distortions affect the test. Arbitrage-like calibration (foreign exchange) validates the method: loop arbitrage triggers the test while triangular arbitrage is ignored. The approach offers a principled, cycle-aware safeguard design for AI reasoning across domains, linking algebraic topology with statistical calibration.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems routinely transport conclusions across biological, clinical and financial contexts, and the emerging safeguard is local verification: checking at each step that the entity is representable in the chosen tool, that parameters are compatible, and that outputs cohere with the plan. We prove this class of safeguard is structurally incomplete. Modelling a covering of context space by its nerve and evidence by a real-valued 1-cochain, an agent chaining evidence performs path integration: its conclusion is path-independent if and only if the cochain is exact, and disagreement between valid reasoning paths is exactly the holonomy of a first Cech cohomology class. Hodge decomposition partitions evidence conflict into a gradient part (calibration), a curl part (local inconsistency, visible at triple overlaps) and a harmonic part. Our central result is that no family of simplex-supported consistency checks can distinguish omega from omega+h for harmonic h, which nonetheless generates non-zero disagreement between valid paths; detection requires a statistic on a cycle basis. The resulting procedure, Ksetra, estimates by coboundary projection and gates abstention on the harmonic component, which we give a mechanism: it arises from effect modification combined with overlap-specific population composition, and vanishes to machine precision when effect modification is absent. The degrees of freedom of an evidence network partition into calibration, coherence and transport, yielding an exact F-test for the existence of a global claim; we quantify its distortion under unequal precision and supply the precision-whitened form that restores exactness. Foreign exchange, where the arbitrage-free null makes the cochain exactly a coboundary, serves as a calibration bench: the test is correctly sized, fires on loop arbitrage, and ignores triangular arbitrage.

</details>


### [135] [Symbolic Machine Learning for Vapor-Liquid Equilibrium Prediction in Cx-N2 Binary Mixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11255)
*Bongseok Kim,Suman Chakraborty,Gary Huang,Mehek Mathur,Guang Lin,Li Qiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A symbolic regression-based correction framework enhances Peng–Robinson EOS (PR-EOS) for hydrocarbon–nitrogen VLE by learning interpretable symbolic corrections per hydrocarbon system and generalizing them via carbon-number–dependent coefficients, achieving improved accuracy over PR-EOS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PR-EOS accuracy degrades across wide composition ranges and hydrocarbon chain lengths; current DL approaches offer accuracy but lack interpretability and explicit analytical forms. A symbolic ML approach can combine predictive performance with human-interpretable equations.

Method: 1) Use symbolic regression to identify interpretable corrections to PR-EOS for individual hydrocarbon systems. 2) Represent the obtained coefficients as functions of carbon number, enabling cross-system generalization to different hydrocarbons. 3) Integrate the symbolic corrections into PR-EOS to predict VLE for hydrocarbon–nitrogen mixtures. 4) Validate against experimental data and compare to baseline PR-EOS.

Result: The method yields significantly improved VLE predictions compared with the original PR-EOS across all hydrocarbon–nitrogen systems studied, while maintaining interpretability through symbolic expressions. The carbon-number-dependent coefficient modeling enables generalization across hydrocarbon chain lengths.

Conclusion: An interpretable symbolic correction framework can robustly enhance PR-EOS predictions for hydrocarbon–nitrogen VLE and is extensible to other EOS and mixture families; it balances predictive accuracy with analytical transparency, addressing a key limitation of purely data-driven models.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of vapor--liquid equilibrium (VLE) for hydrocarbon-nitrogen mixtures remains challenging for cubic equations of state, particularly across broad ranges of composition and hydrocarbon chain length. While deep learning models can provide accurate predictions, they often lack interpretability and explicit analytical expressions. In this work, we propose a symbolic machine learning approach to discover interpretable symbolic corrections to Peng-Robinson equation-of-state (PR-EOS) predictions from experimental data. The proposed approach adopts a two-level strategy: symbolic expressions are first identified for individual hydrocarbon systems, after which their coefficients are represented as functions of carbon number to enable accurate prediction across different hydrocarbon systems. The results demonstrate significantly improved prediction accuracy over the original PR-EOS across all hydrocarbon-nitrogen systems. Overall, the proposed approach provides an interpretable symbolic correction framework for improving PR-EOS predictions of hydrocarbon-nitrogen VLE.

</details>


### [136] [Adaptive Hybrid Particle Swarm Optimization with Gradient Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11258)
*Aryan Gurudeo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Gradient injection in PSO is not universally beneficial; an Adaptive Hybrid PSO (AHPSO) uses swarm-diversity-based sigmoid modulation to switch gradient influence automatically. Across budget-normalized tests, standard PSO often wins, but AHPSO excels in smooth local basins and AHPSO-Adadelta ranks first in iteration-matched tests, suggesting gradient injection is advantageous only in specific landscape features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine when gradient injection provides value in swarm optimization and to avoid unnecessary universal claims by introducing an adaptive mechanism that modulates gradient influence based on swarm diversity.

Method: Introduce AHPSO: a sigmoid function of swarm diversity governs gradient influence, with near-zero influence during exploration and near-maximal influence during exploitation, eliminating manual phase-switching. Evaluate under two regimes: budget-normalized (same total evaluations) across 40 configurations; 29 functions with 14,700 runs under iteration-matched comparison. Benchmark against CMA-ES and other methods using AHPSO-Adadelta variant.

Result: Under budget-normalized comparison: PSO wins 52.5% of 40 configurations vs AHPSO's 20% (p = 7.0e-5, Friedman). AHPSO shows advantage on problems with smooth local basins (F8, F24-F27). Under iteration-matched comparison: AHPSO-Adadelta ranks first among 9 methods including CMA-ES (p = 9.75e-4).

Conclusion: The study provides a principled, landscape-dependent assessment of gradient injection's value in swarm search, demonstrating no universal superiority and highlighting conditions (smooth local basins) where gradient-informed guidance is beneficial.

Abstract: Gradient injection helps Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO) only when the swarm has identified a basin with smooth local structure, not universally. We propose Adaptive Hybrid PSO (AHPSO), which uses a sigmoid function on swarm diversity to automatically modulate gradient influence: near-zero during exploration, near-maximum during exploitation, with no manual phase-switching. Under budget-normalized comparison (PSO given equivalent total function evaluations), PSO wins 52.5% of 40 configurations versus AHPSO's 20% (p = 7.0e-5, Friedman). AHPSO retains advantage specifically on problems with smooth local basins (F8, F24-F27) where directed descent outperforms undirected sampling even at equal cost. Under iteration-matched comparison across 29 functions (42 configurations, 14,700 runs), AHPSO-Adadelta ranks first of 9 methods including CMA-ES (p = 9.75e-4). The contribution is a principled characterization of when gradient injection provides value in swarm-based search, not a claim of universal superiority.

</details>


### [137] [Glance, Scrutinize, and Think: Advancing Video Anomaly Detection from Training-Free to Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11260)
*Shibo Gao,Peipei Yang,Xu-Yao Zhang,Linlin Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes GtS, a training-free global-to-local framework for video anomaly grounding, and a tool-augmented agentic VAD using multimodal LLMs with a video-cropping tool; extends VAGU into VAGU-T with grounding, explanations, QA, and tool-calling traces, and introduces JeAUG for semantic interpretability and temporal precision; experiments show improved accuracy and speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the 'when-what' dissociation in video anomaly detection by introducing a unified global-to-local reasoning paradigm that combines coarse-to-fine grounding with interpretability and dynamic tool use.

Method: GtS: training-free framework leveraging static and dynamic textual guidance for coarse-to-fine anomaly grounding. Agentic VAD: multimodal LLM learns to invoke a video cropping tool, inspect densely resampled frames, and self-correct hypotheses via cold-start supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning with a joint answer-grounding reward. Dataset: VAGU-T (7,567 real-world videos, 21 anomaly categories) with grounding, explanations, QA, and chain-of-thought tool-calling traces. JeAUG: metric evaluating semantic interpretability and temporal precision.

Result: GtS substantially surpasses training-free baselines; the agentic model achieves higher accuracy and faster inference.

Conclusion: Proposes a unified global-to-local reasoning framework for VAD that combines text-guided grounding with tool-augmented reasoning, validated on an extended dataset and evaluated with a new interpretability-grounding metric.

Abstract: Video Anomaly Detection (VAD) aims to identify anomalous events and localize their temporal intervals. Existing approaches exhibit a "when-what" dissociation: traditional DNN-based methods localize when anomalies occur but lack semantic understanding, whereas LLM-based methods explain what happens but neglect precise temporal grounding. We attribute this to the absence of a unified reasoning paradigm. Inspired by how humans inspect surveillance videos - glancing globally to form temporal hypotheses, scrutinizing suspicious segments, and thinking iteratively to correct errors - we study this global-to-local paradigm from two perspectives. We first propose Glance then Scrutinize (GtS), a training-free framework using static and dynamic textual guidance for coarse-to-fine anomaly grounding and understanding, balancing accuracy and speed. To break the ceiling imposed by frozen external modules, we further propose a tool-augmented agentic VAD method, where a multimodal large language model learns to invoke a video cropping tool, inspect densely resampled frames, and self-correct mislocalized hypotheses, via cold-start supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning with a joint answer-grounding reward. For training and evaluation, we extend our prior VAGU benchmark into VAGU-T (Video Anomaly Grounding, Understanding, and Thinking), comprising 7,567 real-world videos over 21 anomaly categories with human-validated grounding, explanations, QA pairs, and chain-of-thought tool-calling traces. We further introduce JeAUG, a metric jointly evaluating semantic interpretability and temporal precision. Experiments show that GtS substantially surpasses training-free baselines, while the agentic model delivers both higher accuracy and faster inference.

</details>


### [138] [Deployment Decision Reliability: A Generalizability-Theory Framework for Sizing Long-Horizon Agent Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11323)
*Vasundra Srinivasan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agent leaderboards primarily reflect specialization and task alignment rather than general capability; the agent main effect explains <3% of variance, while agent-by-task interaction explains 7–23%. This reveals reliability issues and motivates a Deployment Decision Reliability (DDR) framework for enterprise deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To interrogate whether enterprise leaderboards measure general agent ability or merely task-specific performance, and to establish reliability and generalizability properties across multiple open benchmarks to guide deployment decisions.

Method: Four-facet Generalizability Theory variance decomposition applied to three open benchmarks (TheAgentCompany, τ^2-bench, AppWorld). Used three estimators (Henderson Method I, REML via lme4, Bayesian binomial GLMM). Assessed variance components, aggregate reliability, training-cell reliability, population-level versus per-family rankings, and MAST failure taxonomy; culminated in Deployment Decision Reliability (DDR) reporting framework. All code and data released open-source.

Result: Main agent effect accounts for <3% of total variance; agent-by-task interaction accounts for 7–23%. Aggregate reliability collapses on hardest task quartile (τ^2 action_checks Eρ^2 from 0.752 to 0.000). Training-cell reliability negatively correlates with held-out reliability (r = -0.90 on τ^2). Population-level diagnostics transfer across benchmarks (capability-gap ratio ~0.35–0.40) but per-family rankings invert. Trace-level profiles are idiosyncratic (MAE = 0.261) while cell-level profiles generalize (MAE = 0.056, r = 0.83). DDR translates the variance components into five defensible deployment decisions. All artifacts released under open-source license.

Conclusion: Leaderboards do not capture general agent capability; instead they emphasize specialization and task alignment. Reliability and cross-family stability vary, threatening deployment in practice. DDR provides a concise reporting discipline to communicate variance-driven decisions to enterprise buyers and guide safer, more reliable deployments; broader validation and expansion to more benchmarks are warranted.

Abstract: Enterprise practitioners read agent leaderboards as if they ranked agent capability. We show, across three open agent-trace benchmarks (TheAgentCompany, $τ^2$-bench, and AppWorld), that the agent main effect accounts for less than 3% of total variance in every dataset and check type, while the agent-by-task interaction accounts for 7-23%. Leaderboards rank specialization, not capability. We arrive at this through a four-facet Generalizability Theory variance decomposition, fit with three estimators (Henderson Method-I, REML via lme4, and a Bayesian binomial GLMM) that agree to three decimal places. Four further findings sharpen what the leaderboard is hiding. First, aggregate reliability collapses on the hardest task quartile: $Eρ^2$ on $τ^2$ action_checks falls from 0.752 to 0.000. Second, training-cell reliability negatively correlates with held-out reliability ($r = -0.90$ on $τ^2$), meaning the designs that look most reliable replicate worst. Third, population-level diagnostics transfer across enterprise benchmarks (capability-gap ratio stable at 0.35-0.40) but per-family agent rankings invert. Fourth, on the MAST failure taxonomy, trace-level mode profiles are idiosyncratic (MAE = 0.261) while cell-level profiles generalise (MAE = 0.056, $r = 0.83$). We package these into Deployment Decision Reliability (DDR), a one-page reporting discipline that turns the variance-component table into five decisions an enterprise buyer can defend. All code, data loaders, and fit artifacts are released under an open-source license.

</details>


### [139] [Apodex Discovery: Reality Benchmarks and Environments for Evaluating and Building Discoverative Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11341)
*Brian Wang,Bin Feng,Xiaoman Pan,Chenyang An,Felix Liu,Tangqi Fang,Gongbo Sun,Lingfeng Shen,Ning Wang,Handuo Zhang,Feng Chen,Fuchao Yang,Xiang Wang,Jiacheng Lin,Siting Li,Zixuan Liu,Chi Han,Zhenhailong Wang,Kunlun Zhu,Lawrence Zhao,Yueqi Guo,Kailong Wen,Feng Xing,Yiling Guo,Lidong Bing,David Tan,Bo An,Heng Ji,Sheng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Apodex Discovery provides a framework for building verifiable, stateful AI investigations with a heavy-duty solver, enabling real-world problem solving and improved performance in biomedical tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier models perform when problems and success criteria are explicit; real-world challenges require executable, verifiable workflows and evaluation beyond static benchmarks.

Method: Introduce heavy-duty solver (foundation model, harness, tools, control policies), problem-scouting across industries to select problems, a common environment-task-episode abstraction for data/tools/constraints/verification, and HDS6 evaluation (Tools, Repair, Alternatives, Coherence, Evidence, Scope).

Result: Empirical gains include +7% in AAV capsid design across viability/tropism/structure prediction/generative design; drug repurposing/reformulation tasks show improved mean normalized scores for GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.6-sol by 2.5 and 7.6 points; ablations show TRACES interface enables attribution of performance to solver components.

Conclusion: Apodex Discovery shifts AI evaluation from fixed benchmarks toward verifiable, extended investigations that enable genuine discovery.

Abstract: Apollo did not reach the Moon merely because its engineers could solve difficult equations. It succeeded by turning a distant ambition into a mission architecture of explicit objectives, simulation, verification, and repeated correction. AI now faces a similar transition: frontier models can solve difficult tasks once the problem, tools, and success criteria are specified, yet consequential real-world challenges rarely arrive in an executable or verifiable form.
  We introduce Apodex Discovery, a framework for building and evaluating discoverative AI through the heavy-duty solver, a system comprising a foundation model, harness, tools, and control policies that pursues extended, stateful, verifiable investigations. It has three core components. First, a problem-scouting process surveyed 561 industries across 16 sectors, assembled 423 high-value real-world problems, and selected 20 for the initial release. Second, a common environment-task-episode abstraction provides data, tools, constraints, feedback, trajectory recording, and verification of intermediate artifacts and final submissions. Third, HDS6 evaluates Tools, Repair, Alternatives, Coherence, Evidence, and Scope independently of final-task success.
  In AAV capsid design, Apodex surpassed the published state of the art by 7% across viability, tropism, structure prediction, and generative design. In drug repurposing and reformulation, a task-specific biomedical environment improved the mean normalized prediction score of GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.6-sol by 2.5 and 7.6 points over the same closed-book backbone. Controlled ablations show that the fixed TRACES episode interface enables attribution of performance differences to specific solver components. Apodex Discovery moves AI evaluation beyond predefined benchmarks toward verifiable investigations aimed at genuine discovery.

</details>


### [140] [Can Frontier LLMs Match Natively Multimodal Embeddings? A Comparison on Hard-Negative Text-to-Image Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11343)
*Archan Dutta,Vyanktesh Kanungo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: First direct comparison on Flickr30k shows native multimodal embeddings (Gemini Embedding 2) perform on par with Vision-enabled LLMs (GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet 4.6) for cross-modal ranking; precomputed embeddings benefit low-latency deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether native multimodal embedding models or LLM-based zero-shot ranking better support cross-modal retrieval, especially with the advent of a single shared multimodal embedding space.

Method: Direct empirical comparison on Flickr30k between Gemini Embedding 2 (multimodal native embeddings) and LLM-based visual ranking using GPT-4.1 and Claude Sonnet 4.6; assess ranking performance and latency implications, with a note on the benefit of precomputing embeddings for latency.

Result: LLMs (GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet 4.6) achieve performance on par with Gemini Embedding 2 in zero-shot ranking tasks; when embeddings are precomputed, multimodal embeddings offer lower latency suitable for real-time retrieval.

Conclusion: Native multimodal embeddings in a single shared space are competitive with cutting-edge LLM-based visual ranking. For latency-sensitive applications, precomputing multimodal embeddings provides significant advantages, suggesting a practical edge for traditional embedding-based retrieval even amid strong LLM-based baselines.

Abstract: Multimodal retrieval and classification across different types of media, spanning text, images,video and audio, has traditionally relied on dual-encoder models that align visual and textual representations through contrastive learning. The March 2026 release of Gemini Embedding 2, Google's first natively multimodal embedding model to map text, images, video, audio, and documents into a single shared space, raises competition among multimodal retrieval systems. Simultaneously, frontier Large language models (LLMs) have also demonstrated strong visual understanding, raising the question of whether they can serve as effective zero-shot rankers. Our study provides the first direct comparison of native multimodal embeddings against LLM-based visual ranking on Flickr30k. We observe that GPT-4.1 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 perform on par with Gemini Embedding 2. Additionally, once embeddings are precomputed, multimodal embeddings are better suited for low-latency applications.

</details>


### [141] [Inverse Theory of Mind Modeling for Content Recommendation: From Web Browsing to Dynamic Intelligent Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11354)
*Mengyu Chen,Feiyu Lu,Chun-Fu Chen,Lucas Vinh Tran,Jay Katukuri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes an inverse theory of mind (IToM) pipeline to infer user beliefs and preferences from observed actions in modern, multimodal UIs, using counterfactual reasoning and abductive inference to form persona representations; validated on OPeRA with cross-modal VisionOS transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interactions in recommender systems often reflect exploration or comparison rather than stable preference; with generative UIs and XR the system must infer not just what to present but why a user acts, in a modality-agnostic way.

Method: A four-stage pipeline: (1) reconstruct each user's decision context from observed actions (what was chosen and what alternatives were available); (2) apply LLM-driven counterfactual reasoning to generate evidence-grounded natural-language belief statements; (3) synthesize these beliefs via multi-hypothesis abductive inference into a structured user persona; (4) evaluate on the OPeRA dataset against ground-truth personality assessments, surveys, and interview-based personas across four tasks (next action prediction, shopping attitude alignment, Big Five inference, held-out category prediction); demonstrate cross-modal transfer with a persona-driven spatial banking app on VisionOS.

Result: Inferred personas match or exceed ground-truth personas; multi-hypothesis reasoning is essential for accurate personality prediction; cross-modal transfer validated via VisionOS deployment for a banking task.

Conclusion: The IToM pipeline provides evidence-grounded, cross-modal persona synthesis that enhances understanding of user decision processes in adaptive, multimodal interfaces; abductive multi-hypothesis reasoning is crucial for reliable personality inference and transfer to XR contexts.

Abstract: Modern recommender systems treat observed actions as reliable proxies for user preferences, yet interactions often reflect exploration or comparison rather than stable preference expression. As interfaces evolve from static layouts toward generative UIs and immersive extended reality (XR), the need for deeper, modality-agnostic user understanding grows: these adaptive environments must decide not only what to present but where, when, how prominently, and most importantly why a user acts. We propose an Inverse Theory of Mind (IToM) pipeline that reasons backward from observed interactions to infer the beliefs, preferences, and decision-making traits that explain behavior. The pipeline reconstructs each user's decision context, including what was chosen and what alternatives were available, applies LLM-driven counterfactual reasoning to produce evidence-grounded natural-language belief statements, and synthesizes these beliefs through multi-hypothesis abductive inference into a structured user persona. We evaluate on the OPeRA dataset against ground-truth personality assessments, attitudinal surveys, and interview-based personas across four tasks: next action prediction, shopping attitude alignment, Big Five personality inference, and held-out category prediction. Results show that inferred personas match or exceed ground-truth personas and that multi-hypothesis reasoning is essential for accurate personality prediction. We further demonstrate cross-modal transferability with a persona-driven spatial banking application on VisionOS.

</details>


### [142] [From Numbers to Judgment: Specialist LLM Agents and Reinforcement Learning for European Listed Real Estate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11381)
*Pardis Taghavi,Santosh Bhavani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Prompt decomposition into specialist modules improves numerical execution in a multi-laceted financial analysis task, while targeted parameter adaptation enhances integrative financial judgment; gains transfer to unseen firms/wrappers, showing complementary benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether specialized prompting (modular decomposition) or targeted parameter adaptation yields better performance for modular numerical analysis vs integrative judgment tasks in a comprehensive finance framework.

Method: Compare monolithic vs specialist-decomposed prompting using a 16-lens Larix framework mapped to eight lens-aligned specialists, across 19 firms and seven regulatory wrappers, with fixed model/evidence/instructions/output schema. Assess numerical-task vs judgment-task performance, four frozen-template dispatches, and a single-agent control. Then apply post-training Qwen3.5-9B with GRPO using task-aligned structured rewards and measure development-split and judgment scores, including transfer to unseen firms/wrappers and anti-memorization splits.

Result: Numerical-task performance improves by 15.8 percentage points with decomposition; judgment-task performance does not reliably improve and can decline; single-agent control does not reproduce numerical gains. Post-training GRPO yields +12.0 points on development-split and +14.2 points on judgment; gains extend to unseen firms (+15.2 overall; +40.4 on covenant stress) and unseen wrappers (+4.3). Positive transfer across anti-memorization splits.

Conclusion: Modular, lens-aligned prompting reliably enhances modular numerical execution, whereas targeted parameter adaptation (GRPO with structured rewards) improves integrative financial judgment; combined, these approaches yield transferable gains across unseen contexts, indicating complementary strengths.

Abstract: We study whether the localized numerical operations and integrative judgments of financial analysis benefit from the same form of LLM specialization. Larix maps a 16-lens European listed-real-estate analysis framework to eight lens-aligned specialists; we compare a frontier LLM under monolithic versus specialist-decomposed prompting while holding the model, source evidence, task instructions, output schema, and scoring fixed. Across 19 firms spanning seven regulatory wrappers, decomposition improves the numerical-task aggregate by 15.8 percentage points but does not reliably improve, and can reduce, performance on judgment tasks, a pattern stable across four frozen-template dispatches; a single-agent control given the complete framework does not reproduce the numerical gain. Post-training Qwen3.5-9B with GRPO using task-aligned structured rewards then raises the development-split score by 12.0 points and the judgment aggregate by 14.2 points, with gains on all four sub-ceiling tasks; the gains transfer to unseen firms (+15.2 points overall; +40.4 on covenant stress) and to unseen regulatory wrappers (+4.3), with positive transfer on all three anti-memorization splits. Prompt-level decomposition thus improves modular numerical execution, whereas targeted parameter adaptation improves integrative financial judgment.

</details>


### [143] [When Self-Consistency Backfires: Majority Vote Hurts the Majority of Hard Science Problems for Small LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11403)
*Utkarsh Bahuguna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Self-consistency via majority voting often harms performance on a graduate-level science benchmark for two instruction-tuned models; even with multiple samples, accuracy improves only with a non-deployable oracle and no gating strategy yields meaningful gains; pre-registered experiments confirm the effect; central open question: behavior on reasoning-native models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether self-consistent chain-of-thought (SC) with majority voting improves inference performance and whether the model's confidence correlates with correctness; evaluate across model families, gating strategies, and a fixed-budget setting to understand limits of SC-based aggregation.

Method: Experiment on the GPQA Diamond benchmark (198 graduate-level science questions). For each problem, generate N chains of thought and take the plurality answer (N in {1,2,4,8,16,32,64}). Evaluate two instruction-tuned models (Qwen2.5-7B and Llama-3-8B). Pre-register after exploratory testing on 47 problems and confirm on a 151-problem confirmatory split. Analyze a grid oracle across N to estimate an upper bound and test verifier-free gates (plurality-agreement, token-entropy) against fixed-budget voting at N=64.

Result: Majority voting degrades performance on the majority of problems: 56.6% accuracy for Qwen2.5-7B and 65.7% for Llama-3-8B. Grid oracle suggests a theoretical upper bound about 14 points higher (Qwen) and 17 points higher (Llama) than N=1, but this requires ground truth and is not deployable. No verifier-free gate (neither plurality-agreement nor token-entropy) improves accuracy meaningfully over fixed-budget voting at N=64 (diff ≤ 0.002). Confidence signals do not track correctness in the high-agreement bin; the most-agreeing answers are only about half correct for Qwen, and for Llama, the best-agreement bin underperforms compared with the lowest-agreement bin.

Conclusion: Self-consistency via majority voting provides limited or negative benefit for these instruction-tuned models on GPQA Diamond. Gate-based verifiers and confidence-based filters do not rescue performance at large N; the approach’s generality remains questionable, and testing on reasoning-native models is a key open question for future work.

Abstract: Self-consistency (SC) via majority vote is a widely used way to spend inference-time compute: sample N chains of thought, return the plurality answer. On the full GPQA Diamond benchmark (198 graduate-level science questions), majority voting reduces per-problem accuracy on a majority of problems for two instruction-tuned models from different families: 56.6% of problems for Qwen2.5-7B and 65.7% for Llama-3-8B, with Qwen the primary demonstration and Llama corroborating the direction from a near-chance baseline. The effect was pre-registered on a 151-problem confirmatory split after being observed on 47 exploratory problems, and all four confirmatory hypotheses passed. A grid oracle that routes each problem to the best N across {1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64} marks a theoretical upper bound 14 accuracy points above N = 1 for Qwen and 17 for Llama, an oracle bound requiring ground truth rather than a deployable method. No verifier-free gate reaches it: neither a plurality-agreement gate nor a token-entropy gate moves accuracy more than 0.002 from fixed-budget voting at N = 64. The mechanism is direct: confidence does not track correctness on these problems. In the highest-agreement bin the plurality answer is correct about half the time for Qwen, and for Llama that bin is less accurate than its lowest-agreement bin. We pre-register and confirm these findings on small instruction-tuned models; we do not test reasoning-native models, which we flag as the central open question.

</details>


### [144] [Social Chain of Thought: A Multi-Agent Architecture Grounded in Medical Differential Diagnosis Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11420)
*Del Coburn,Scott Sanner,Dan Silver*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCoT is a multi-agent, deliberative framework for medical differential diagnosis that outperforms single-agent baselines, especially in hard cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical diagnostic reasoning with LLMs is high-stakes; assessing when and why collaborative, multi-agent reasoning improves recall over monolithic inference remains unclear.

Method: Introduce Social Chain of Thought (SCoT), a multi-round pipeline that orchestrates multiple specialized agents in a deliberative workflow; compare against single-agent baselines, ablations of one-agent pipelines, and best-of-n scaling; evaluate on recall, focusing on difficult cases.

Result: SCoT achieves a recall advantage that monolithic inference cannot reproduce; the gain is greatest in hardest diagnostic cases where iterative specialist discussions improve ground-truth diagnoses and converge to higher recall differential.

Conclusion: Deliberative, multi-agent collaboration via SCoT can surpass monolithic LLM reasoning in challenging medical differential diagnosis, offering a scalable path to higher diagnostic recall.

Abstract: Medical diagnostic reasoning is a high-impact use case for LLMs that carries significant implications for the health and wellbeing of users. When OpenAI (2026) reports that more than 5% of ChatGPT messages globally are healthcare-related, the transparency of these systems becomes a serious design concern. This is especially true for complex cases, where differential diagnosis often requires integrating multiple forms of specialist reasoning. Existing work has proposed multi-agent approaches to medical diagnosis, but it remains unclear when such systems are needed, why they help, and where they outperform monolithic inference. We introduce Social Chain of Thought (SCoT),a multi-round pipeline for medical differential diagnosis that structures multi-agent interaction as a deliberative framework for collabora. tive LLM reasoning. Evaluating SCoT against single-agent baselines, one-agent pipeline ablations, and best-of-n scaling, we show that its recall advantage is not reproduced by monolithic inference alone. SCoT is most successful in the hardest diagnostic cases, where multiple rounds of specialist conversation help recover ground-truth diagnoses and converge on a higher-recall differential.

</details>


### [145] [Benchmarking LLM Judges for Mobile Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11434)
*Ziqiang Wan,Li Gu,Zhixiang Chi,Zhi Liu,Seyed Mehdi Ayyoubzadeh,Yuanhao Yu,Yang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces MobileJudgeBench, a benchmark for evaluating LLM-based judges on mobile-agent trajectories using 931 human-annotated cases across 6 benchmarks, 4 agent models, and 68 apps. Finds that simple, screenshot-based baselines can rival complex judges, that LLM backbone quality drives performance, and that benchmark metrics predict real-world judge utility; also reveals opposing failure profiles between backends.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the uncertain reliability of LLM-based judges for evaluating mobile-agent task completion and to provide a standardized, high-quality benchmark that links judge quality to downstream outcomes in evaluation and RL.

Method: Proposes MobileJudgeBench comprising 931 human-annotated trajectories spanning 6 mobile agent benchmarks, 4 agent models, and 68 apps. Evaluates 6 judge methods (five adapted from SPA-Bench, A3 with two modes, AndroidArena, AgentRewardBench, plus a simple baseline) across multiple LLM backends.

Result: Three main findings: (1) a simple baseline judge with sampled screenshots often matches or surpasses purpose-built methods, indicating diminishing returns from more complex pipelines; among competitive methods, the LLM backbone is the primary performance driver. (2) Benchmark quality metrics reliably predict judge utility, correlating with agent ranking fidelity and downstream RL performance when judges provide rewards. (3) Failure analyses across two LLM backends show opposite failure profiles (conservative vs permissive) tied to the backbone’s precision-recall characteristics.

Conclusion: The study suggests prioritizing robust, simple judging baselines and the choice of LLM backbone; benchmark quality metrics are meaningful proxies for real-world utility; and understanding a backbone’s precision-recall behavior is crucial for interpreting judge performance and failure modes.

Abstract: Mobile agent benchmarks increasingly rely on LLM-based judges to evaluate task completion, yet the reliability of these judges on mobile agent trajectories remains largely unexamined. We introduce MobileJudgeBench, a benchmark for systematically evaluating LLM-as-judge methods on mobile agent trajectories. Our benchmark comprises 931 human-annotated trajectories spanning 6 mobile agent benchmarks, 4 agent models, and 68 apps. We evaluate 6 judge methods (five adapted from SPA-Bench, A3 with two modes, AndroidArena, and AgentRewardBench, plus a simple baseline we design) across multiple LLM backends. Our experiments reveal three key findings. First, a simple baseline judge with sampled screenshots is competitive with, and often exceeds, purpose-built methods, indicating that more elaborate judge pipelines do not consistently improve judge quality; among competitive methods, the LLM backbone is the primary driver. Second, benchmark quality metrics reliably predict real-world judge utility: they correlate with both agent ranking fidelity for evaluation and downstream performance when judges serve as reward signals for on-policy reinforcement learning. Third, failure analysis across two LLM backends uncovers qualitatively opposite failure profiles, one conservative and the other permissive, linked to the backbone's precision-recall characteristics.

</details>


### [146] [A Modular Agentic Framework for Synthetically Constrained Multi-Objective Hit-to-Lead Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11483)
*Kelvin P. Idanwekhai,Enes Kelestemur,Benjamin Strickland,Matthew Hart,Steini Davidsson,Angelos Angelopoulos,Ron Alterovitz,Marcello DeLuca,Alexander Tropsha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An open-source, LLM-guided framework (SABLE) for synthetically accessible hit-to-lead optimization that orchestrates reaction-based enumeration, ADMET/physicochemical prediction, structure-based scoring, and Bayesian optimization to efficiently prioritize analogs within synthetic and design constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the multi-dimensional optimization in early drug discovery (potency, selectivity, physicochemical properties, PK, safety, and synthetic feasibility) while providing traceability and a configurable workflow.

Method: SABLE employs natural-language orchestration via a language model to interpret goals and route tasks. It integrates specialized tools for reaction-templated analog enumeration, physicochemical/ADMET prediction, structure-based affinity scoring, and Bayesian optimization. The architecture is modular and configurable through a single file, enabling a computational design–make–analyze twin with provenance for all outputs.

Result: In both single-objective and multi-objective studies, SABLE enriches candidate sets toward user-defined computational goals while evaluating only a subset of the enumerated search space. It provides output provenance and is open-source with a pluggable toolchain for backends.

Conclusion: SABLE offers an extensible decision-support framework to prioritize synthetically constrained analogs in early-stage drug discovery, potentially accelerating hit-to-lead by focusing exploration within synthetically feasible regions.

Abstract: Hit-to-lead optimization requires iterative design of hit analogs across competing potency, selectivity, physicochemical, pharmacokinetic, safety, and synthetic constraints. We present SABLE (Synthetically-accessible Agentic Bayesian Ligand Exploration), an open-source framework that employs natural-language orchestration to guide chemical structure optimization. SABLE uses an LLM to interpret user-defined goals and route tasks, while specialized tools perform reaction-templated analog enumeration, physicochemical and ADMET property prediction, structure-based affinity scoring, and Bayesian optimization. The resulting workflow is a computational twin of the analytical and prioritization stages of the design-make-test-analyze cycle, providing provenance of each numerical output. Across single, and multi-objective optimization studies, SABLE enriches candidate sets for user-defined computational objectives while evaluating only a subset of the enumerated search space. Its modular architecture allows tools and characterization backends to be replaced by editing a simple config file, without modifying operational logic. SABLE provides an extensible decision-support framework for prioritizing synthetically constrained analogs in early-stage drug discovery.

</details>


### [147] [From Prompting to Behavioral Alignment: Personalized LLM Judges for Recommendation Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11493)
*Alireza S. Ziabari,Kat Ellis,Colleen Chan,Ding Tong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Zero-shot LLMs for offline recommender evaluation suffer bidirectional rationalization, where the same evidence can support both positive and negative engagement. A sequential behavioral alignment with fine-tuning and preference optimization over paired correct and counterfactual rationales yields a 32.19% Macro-F1 lift over zero-shot and matches a production feature-engineered baseline, while providing interpretable reasoning traces without manual pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline recommender evaluation often relies on complex, manually engineered feature pipelines. LLMs offer end-to-end prediction from raw logs but risk unreliable, contradictory reasoning. The work aims to make LLM-based evaluation reliable, scalable, and interpretable.

Method: Introduce sequential behavioral alignment: pair correct and counterfactual rationales, fine-tune the model and apply preference optimization to align its reasoning with correct outcomes. Evaluate on real-world homepage interaction logs, comparing zero-shot LLM baselines, the aligned model, and a production feature-engineered baseline.

Result: Aligned reasoning yields a 32.19% Macro-F1 lift over the zero-shot baseline and matches the production feature-engineered baseline.

Conclusion: Behavioral alignment mitigates bidirectional rationalization in LLM-based evaluation, enabling scalable, human-interpretable reasoning traces without manual pipeline overhead, while achieving competitive performance.

Abstract: Traditional offline recommendation evaluation relies heavily on complex, manually maintained feature pipelines that are difficult to scale.
