While Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a promising alternative by predicting user engagement directly from raw text logs, empirical analysis in this study identifies a critical failure mode termed bidirectional rationalization. In a zero-shot setting, LLMs are found to convincingly argue for both positive and negative user engagement outcomes on the exact same item with identical evidence, highlighting the unreliability of off-the-shelf LLMs in predicting user engagement. To resolve this, we develop and apply a sequential behavioral alignment framework pairing fine-tuning with preference optimization over paired correct and counterfactual rationales. Evaluated on real-world homepage interaction logs, this aligned reasoning approach achieves a 32.19\% lift in Macro-F1 score over the zero-shot baseline and matches the production feature-engineered baseline. The results demonstrate that behavioral alignment mitigates bidirectional rationalization while delivering human-interpretable reasoning traces without manual pipeline overhead.

</details>


### [148] [Localizing Safety Alignment: MLP Layers and Mid-Network Blocks Encode Refusal Behavior in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11583)
*Mingyu Zong,Sampad Mohanty,Bhaskar Krishnamachari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Safety alignment in open-weight LLMs is highly localized in MLP weights, with mid-network MLP blocks (layers 8–11) dominating malicious/refusal transfer; transplantation results are non-additive and benchmark-dependent, suggesting targeted, block-level interventions could improve safety with efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To pinpoint where safety-aligned refusal is encoded, given brittle, network-wide views of alignment; identify specific components and configurations that carry refusal signals to inform targeted safety interventions.

Method: Transplant weights from aligned models into matched unaligned base models at multiple granularities (attention weights, MLP weights, contiguous layer regions, and MLP blocks). Use two open-weight model pairs and four safety benchmarks; evaluate how grafts affect malicious-prompt refusal and benign refusal; employ greedy searches to identify influential blocks/layers and examine transfer orders across benchmarks.

Result: Across model pairs, refusal transfer is dominated by MLP weights; replacing MLPs yields substantially more malicious-prompt refusal than replacing attention, by at least 2.7x across benchmarks. Within the MLP stack, refusal-relevant parameters concentrate mid-network, with block spanning layers 8–11 selected first in all greedy searches. The composition of safety-relevant components is non-additive: adding more aligned blocks can reduce refusal performance, and selective subsets can outperform full MLP transplantation for certain refusals. Transfer orders vary by source benchmark, indicating a benchmark-dependent precision-coverage trade-off.

Conclusion: Safety alignment in current LLMs is localized and interaction-sensitive. This brittleness implies potential for efficient, targeted safety interventions by focusing on specific MLP blocks, while also cautioning that effects are benchmark-dependent and may not generalize across models.

Abstract: Safety alignment in large language models is often treated as a distributed property of the entire network, yet its practical brittleness suggests that refusal behavior may be concentrated in a smaller set of parameters. This work addresses where safety-aligned refusal is encoded by transplanting weights from aligned models into matched unaligned base models at multiple levels of granularity. Using two open-weight model pairs and four safety benchmarks, we conducted experiments to compare the effects of replacing attention weights, MLP weights, contiguous layer regions, and MLP blocks. Across both model families, refusal transfer is dominated by MLP weights: replacing MLP parameters recovers substantially more malicious-prompt refusal than replacing attention parameters, with gains of at least 2.7 times more across benchmarks. Within the MLP stack, refusal-relevant parameters exhibit a consistent mid-network concentration, as the block spanning layers 8-11 is selected first in all six greedy searches over model-dataset pairs. The results also show that the composition of safety-relevant components is non-additive: in five of six greedy trajectories, adding more aligned blocks can reduce refusal performance, and selective block subsets can outperform full MLP transplantation on malicious refusal, benign over-refusal, or both. Finally, greedy orders transferred to OR-Bench vary with the source benchmark used to derive them, indicating a benchmark-dependent precision-coverage trade-off. These results suggest that safety alignment in current LLMs is both localized and interaction-sensitive, offering insight into alignment brittleness and potential avenues for targeted safety interventions.

</details>


### [149] [EnterpriseRAG: Benchmarking LLM Instruction Adherence and Robustness under Non-Ideal Enterprise Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11584)
*Huiqi Miao,Xinbao Sun,Bo Wang,Fanyu Meng,Lijun Mei,Na Wu,Di Jin,Chao Deng,Junlan Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces EnterpriseRAG, a benchmark to quantify reliability of enterprise RAG deployments under noisy retrieval, knowledge gaps, and factual conflicts; evaluates 13 LLMs and reveals a large gap between per-constraint success and holistic instruction adherence, providing a reproducible framework for deployment decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise RAG deployments exhibit a reliability gap: meeting all multi-dimensional constraints is far harder than satisfying individual constraints. Existing clean-retrieval benchmarks fail to reflect production conditions, where noisy documents and complex instructions cooccur.

Method: Develops EnterpriseRAG, a benchmark of 983 expert-validated samples across six domains. Systematically simulates three failure modes (retrieval noise, knowledge gaps, factual conflicts) coupled with complex instructions. Evaluates 13 state-of-the-art LLMs on per-constraint and holistic compliance to measure instruction adherence.

Result: Finds a severe instruction adherence collapse: high per-constraint satisfaction masks low holistic compliance, with a 57-point gap and only 26.8% of responses meeting all requirements. Knowledge gaps and factual conflicts remain major barriers even with reasoning-enhanced inference.

Conclusion: Production RAG requires explicit context-aware protocols and calibrated judgment. EnterpriseRAG offers a reproducible benchmark and evaluation framework to measure and close these gaps, informing deployment decisions for enterprise-scale RAG systems; the authors will release the benchmark and framework upon publication.

Abstract: Enterprise RAG deployments face a critical reliability gap: while LLMs satisfy 80% of individual constraints, only 26.8% of responses meet all requirements simultaneously, revealing a 57-point orchestration gap. Existing benchmarks assume clean retrieval with simple queries, failing to capture production conditions where noisy documents and multi-dimensional constraints coexist. We introduce EnterpriseRAG, a benchmark of 983 expert-validated samples across six domains that systematically simulates three failure modes absent from prior work: retrieval noise, knowledge gaps, and factual conflicts, coupled with complex instructions. Evaluation of 13 state-of-the-art LLMs reveals a severe instruction adherence collapse, where high per-constraint satisfaction masks low holistic compliance. Critical findings expose deep barriers under knowledge gaps and factual conflicts, even with reasoning-enhanced inference, indicating production RAG requires explicit context-aware protocols and calibrated judgment. EnterpriseRAG provides a reproducible foundation for measuring and closing these gaps, directly informing deployment decisions for enterprise-scale RAG systems. We will release the benchmark and evaluation framework upon publication.

</details>


### [150] [CoAdapt-GUI: Joint Workflow Context and Policy Adaptation for Unseen GUI Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11588)
*Linqiang Guo,Li Gu,Zihuan Jiang,Zhixiang Chi,Siobhan Reid,Ziqiang Wang,Yuanhao Yu,Wei Liu,Yang Wang,Tse-Hsun,Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoAdapt-GUI is a test-time adaptation framework for mobile GUI agents that separates transferable workflow context from target-app specifics and jointly adapts policy via a LoRA adapter on a frozen vision-language model, enabling better generalization to unseen apps with limited target interaction and no demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile GUI agents generalize poorly to novel apps. There is a need for adaptation using only target app rollouts/rewards without demonstrations, within a limited interaction budget, while avoiding transferring source-interfaces state.

Method: Two components: (1) workflow context adaptation that preserves transferable procedures, failure modes, and verification rules while excluding app-bound source details; (2) policy adaptation via task-context-matched group-relative optimization updating a LoRA adapter on a frozen vision-language model, using the agent's own target-app data.

Result: On unseen apps, CoAdapt-GUI achieves 45.0% AndroidWorld-Generalization vs 37.5% for a Policy-Only TTA baseline; AndroidWorld Plus performance improves from 38.6% to 52.9%, indicating substantial gains from transfer-constrained workflow context and further gains from joint policy adaptation.

Conclusion: Transfer-constrained workflow context provides meaningful gains; joint policy adaptation further improves held-out performance, demonstrating effective generalization to novel apps under limited target interaction without demonstrations.

Abstract: Mobile GUI agents remain brittle when deployed to applications absent from source training. We study novel-app generalization under a limited target interaction budget and without target demonstrations. We introduce CoAdapt-GUI, a test-time adaptation (TTA) framework that jointly adapts structured workflow context and policy from the agent's own target-app rollouts and rewards. The workflow context retains transferable procedures, failure modes, and verification rules while excluding app-bound source details. This separation allows reusable workflow knowledge to guide adaptation without transferring source-interface state. For policy adaptation, task-context-matched group-relative optimization updates a LoRA adapter on a frozen vision-language model. Across two unseen-app evaluations, CoAdapt-GUI reaches 45.0% on AndroidWorld-Generalization, compared with 37.5% for the reported Policy-Only TTA baseline, and raises AndroidWorld Plus performance from 38.6% to 52.9%. These results show that transfer-constrained workflow context provides substantial gains and that joint policy adaptation further improves held-out performance.

</details>


### [151] [Learning from Online User Feedback for Shopping Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11604)
*Haobo Zhang,Kelong Mao,Sulong Xu,Simiu Gu,Zhicheng Dou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LOFA learns from real online interaction logs by combining RL over observable purchase outcomes with feedback-aware on-policy distillation to convert user dialogues into dense token-level supervision, yielding improved recommendations, response usefulness, and user-satisfaction alignment without manual annotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods rely on offline signals or synthetic data and overlook the rich supervision in natural conversational feedback. Online feedback is heterogeneous, sparse, and noisy, making it hard to derive reliable learning signals automatically. A learning framework that directly leverages real online logs can better capture user preferences and behavior.

Method: LOFA integrates two objectives: (1) reinforcement learning guided by verifiable purchase outcomes to optimize long-term success, and (2) feedback-aware on-policy distillation that identifies user directives within dialogue and converts them into dense token-level supervision. This combination leverages collaborative patterns (from data) and user-specific preferences (from directives) without requiring manual annotation.

Result: Experiments on real-world e-commerce logs show that LOFA consistently outperforms strong baselines in three metrics: recommendation quality, response helpfulness, and user-satisfaction alignment.

Conclusion: Learning shopping agents from real online user feedback via RL on outcomes and on-policy distillation for dialogue directives is effective, offering a robust approach to utilize authentic user interactions without manual labeling.

Abstract: Large language model-based shopping agents are increasingly deployed in real-world e-commerce platforms, generating massive amounts of user interaction logs that provide valuable supervision for improving these agents. However, existing approaches primarily rely on offline training signals, such as user-item interactions or synthetic preference data, while largely overlooking the rich supervision contained in users' natural conversational feedback. Moreover, the available online feedback is heterogeneous, sparse, and noisy, making it difficult to transform into reliable learning signals automatically. To address these challenges, we propose LOFA, a framework that enables shopping agents to learn directly from real online interaction logs without human annotation. LOFA combines reinforcement learning over verifiable purchase outcomes with feedback-aware on-policy distillation, which identifies users'in-dialogue directives and converts them into dense token-level supervision. These complementary objectives capture both collaborative behavioral patterns and user-specific preferences. Extensive experiments on real-world e-commerce logs demonstrate that LOFA consistently improves recommendation quality, response helpfulness, and user-satisfaction alignment over strong baselines, highlighting the effectiveness of learning shopping agents from real online user feedback.

</details>


### [152] [Foresight Without Seeing: Latent Futures for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11605)
*Jiakai Huang,Zhongbo Wu,Zheng Zhang,Zihan Wang,Shan You,Tao Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ForeWAM is a dynamics-conditioned direct-policy WAM that injects predictive dynamics via a single Future-KV prefill into action generation, avoiding explicit future video decoding. It uses dynamics registers trained by a frozen latent action teacher and achieves high LIBERO performance with low inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between explicit-future WAMs (expensive, access to predicted scene evolution) and direct-policy WAMs (efficient, but lack predictive dynamics access). Enable exposure of predictive dynamics to the Action DiT without generating future videos at inference time.

Method: Introduce ForeWAM with a Future-KV module that performs one Video DiT prefill over the current visual latent and stochastic future slots, reusing layer-wise K-V states during action denoising. Learn dynamics registers via supervision from a frozen latent action teacher. Use ground-truth futures and teacher only in training; deployment requires neither future video generation nor teacher.

Result: Achieves average success rates of 96.7% (standard) and 96.9% (accelerated) on LIBERO; 61.6% on LIBERO-Plus for the standard variant.

Conclusion: Direct-policy WAMs can retain efficient action prediction while exposing predictive dynamics to the action pathway without explicit future video generation, approaching explicit-future WAM performance with lower inference cost.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) couple future visual prediction with robot action generation, enabling policies to model how the physical world evolves during interaction. Existing WAMs differ in how predictive dynamics are exposed to the action pathway. Explicit-future WAMs provide direct access to predicted scene evolution, but incur substantial inference costs from iterative video denoising. In contrast, direct-policy WAMs efficiently predict actions from the current observation but lack an explicit inference-time interface for exposing predictive dynamics to the Action DiT. To bridge this gap, we propose ForeWAM, a dynamics-conditioned direct-policy WAM that provides predictive context for action generation without decoding future videos. At its core, Future-KV performs a single Video DiT prefill over the current visual latent and stochastic future slots, and reuses the resulting layer-wise key-value states throughout action denoising. We further introduce dynamics registers supervised by a frozen latent action teacher, encouraging the implicit future states to capture interaction-induced transitions such as object motion, contact changes, and task progress. Ground-truth future observations and the teacher are used only during training; deployment requires neither and performs no future video generation. Without embodied robot data pretraining, the standard and accelerated variants of ForeWAM achieve average success rates of 96.7% and 96.9% on LIBERO, respectively. The standard variant further achieves 61.6% success on LIBERO-Plus. These results demonstrate that direct-policy WAMs can retain efficient action prediction while exposing predictive dynamics to the action pathway without explicitly generating future observations.

</details>


### [153] [MBA: Multimodal Benchmark and Agents for Real-World Business Ideation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11616)
*Hojun Choi,Jaeyo Shin,Suin Lee,Hyunjung Shim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces MBA-Bench, a multimodal benchmark for business ideation and two LoRA-tuned agents (MBA-b and MBA-k) that optimize creativity and feasibility to outperform caption-only and multimodal baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world business ideation relies on multimodal context beyond text; existing work lacks a standard benchmark and evaluation framework for multimodal ideation agents.

Method: Automatic image captioning; GPT-4o generates five reference ideas per three business questions via retrieval query generation, market evidence retrieval, and evidence-augmented synthesis. Evaluation uses MLLM-as-a-Judge across six criteria; defines blind (MBA-b) and known (MBA-k) settings. Training uses LoRA-based supervised fine-tuning followed by group relative policy optimization with rewards (creativity/feasibility); MBA-k additionally optimizes six disclosed criteria. Two baselines compare caption-only and multimodal inputs.

Result: MBA-b and MBA-k outperform caption baselines by 63.9% and 77.1%, and multimodal baselines by 25.6% and 35.8%, respectively; MBA-k approaches closed-source performance on several metrics.

Conclusion: Multimodal business-ideation agents trained with task-specific rewards and alignment objectives can significantly surpass text-only and generic multimodal baselines, validating MBA-Bench as an effective standard for multimodal ideation research.

Abstract: Agentic systems powered by large language models (LLMs) have opened new opportunities for business ideation. Yet existing approaches remain confined to a text-only paradigm, despite the inherently multimodal nature of real-world contexts. We thus introduce MBA-Bench, the first multimodal benchmark for training and evaluating business ideation agents, comprising 30K samples across six domains, each domain characterized by distinct visual cues not fully conveyed by text alone. Concretely, we automatically caption images and employ GPT-4o to generate five reference ideas for each of three business questions through retrieval query generation, market evidence retrieval, and evidence-augmented synthesis. Following prior work, we evaluate agents across six business-oriented criteria using MLLM-as-a-Judge. To consider settings where criteria are hidden or disclosed, we present MBA-b and MBA-k for blind and known, respectively. We train both with two novel reward objectives---creativity and feasibility---while MBA-k further optimizes the six disclosed criteria for eight in total. Both are trained via LoRA-based supervised fine-tuning followed by group relative policy optimization with these setting-specific rewards. For extensive experiments on MBA-Bench, we set up two baselines accommodating either captions only or multimodal inputs, with the latter nearing closed-source performance on several metrics. MBA-b and MBA-k outperform caption baselines by 63.9% and 77.1%, and multimodal baselines by 25.6% and 35.8%, respectively.

</details>


### [154] [Making AI-Generated Feedback Matter: From Provision to Student Enactment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11625)
*Omar Alsaiari,Nilufar Baghaei,Jason M. Lodge,Dragan Gaševi'c,Naomi Winstone,Hassan Khosravi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Enacted Feedback, a structured, AI-assisted feedback workflow, yields higher uptake of AI-generated feedback and improves self-assessment confidence and work quality relative to unguided (Directed) or self-directed (Self-Directed) workflows. AI access alone is insufficient; purposeful workflow design is essential for productive feedback use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the dual challenge of scalable, high-quality, timely, individualized feedback and helping students interpret, evaluate, and act on feedback by leveraging generative AI within structured workflows.

Method: Large-scale quasi-experimental sequential cohort study (n=13,037 students; 51,296 authored resources) comparing three AI-mediated feedback workflows: Directed Feedback (n=3,723) with AI comments but no structured support; Self-Directed Feedback (n=3,951) with optional AI-supported dialogue; Enacted Feedback (n=5,363) with prompts to select feedback suggestions, evaluate relevance, and engage in targeted, AI-supported dialogue anchored to those selections. Outcomes include uptake of AI feedback, self-assessment confidence, and submitted-work quality.

Result: Enacted Feedback showed significantly higher AI-feedback uptake (26.2%) than Directed (14.1%) and Self-Directed (0.1%), and also higher self-assessment confidence and submitted-work quality than both comparison groups.

Conclusion: The educational value of AI-generated feedback hinges on workflow design that actively engages learners in judgement, dialogue, and improvement. AI access alone is insufficient; designing AI feedback systems that position learners as active participants in feedback literacy is essential.

Abstract: Feedback processes strongly influence student learning, yet their educational value depends on addressing two distinct challenges: providing high-quality, timely, and individualised feedback at scale, and supporting students to interpret, evaluate, and act on that feedback productively. Generative AI offers a credible means of addressing the provision challenge, but students' uptake of AI-generated feedback remains limited. We conducted a large-scale quasi-experimental sequential cohort study comparing three AI-mediated feedback workflows across 13,037 students and 51,296 student-authored resources. In Directed Feedback (n = 3,723), students received AI-generated feedback comments without structured support. In Self-Directed Feedback (n = 3,951), students could initiate optional AI-supported dialogue. In Enacted Feedback (n = 5,363), students were prompted to select feedback suggestions, evaluate their relevance, and engage in targeted AI-supported dialogue anchored to those selections. Enacted Feedback was associated with significantly higher uptake of AI-generated feedback, with an estimated probability of 26.2%, compared with 14.1% for Directed Feedback and 0.1% for Self-Directed Feedback. It was also associated with significantly higher self-assessment confidence and submitted-work quality than both comparison conditions. These findings suggest that the educational value of AI-generated feedback depends not only on the quality of feedback comments, but also on workflows that actively structure students' enactment of feedback literacy processes. The results have implications for the design of AI feedback systems that position learners as active participants in judgement, dialogue, and improvement rather than passive recipients of comments. Overall findings show that AI access alone is insufficient; purposeful workflow design is central to productive feedback use.

</details>


### [155] [CLAIM: Leading Open-domain Active Clarification of Large Language Models with Uncertainty Measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11631)
*Kuangzhao Yang,Ziliang Zhao,Zhicheng Dou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CLAIM uses entropy of cross-model disagreements to drive active clarification in open-domain HCI, generating synthetic data for a unified clarification policy trained via supervised fine-tuning and group-relative policy optimization, without manual annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ambiguity in user queries in open-domain interactions leads to poor, overgeneralized answers. Existing methods depend on costly labeled data and preference alignments, hindering generalization and scalability.

Method: 1) Estimate uncertainty via entropy of answers across multiple models. 2) Create entropy-driven synthetic data using semantic clustering and reasoning-based judgments to automatically annotate clarification needs. 3) Train a unified clarification decision model as a structured decision generation problem using supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and group-relative policy optimization (GRPO).

Result: CLAIM learns stable, generalizable clarification strategies without manual labels, providing a low-cost, robust solution for proactive understanding in real-world open-domain LLM interactions.

Conclusion: Offers an annotation-free, scalable framework for proactive clarification in open-domain HCI, with potential applicability to other uncertain-decision settings.

Abstract: In open-domain human-computer interaction scenarios, large language models (LLMs) frequently encounter user queries that are ambiguous or incomplete. In such cases, directly producing an answer often leads to overgeneralized, erroneous, or low-information responses. In contrast, asking clarifying questions can substantially improve interaction quality. However, existing approaches still rely heavily on manually annotated data or preference alignment to address two fundamental challenges: when clarification is necessary, and which aspect of the query should be clarified. This reliance incurs high annotation costs and limits generalization. To address these challenges, we propose CLAIM, an uncertainty-driven framework for active clarification learning in open-domain settings. CLAIM eliminates the need for explicit human preference annotations by quantifying query uncertainty through the entropy induced by answer disagreements across multiple models. This uncertainty signal is then used to construct high-quality synthetic data, enabling the training of a unified clarification decision model through a combination of supervised learning and reinforcement learning. Specifically, we propose an entropy-driven synthetic data generation pipeline that integrates entropy-based uncertainty estimation with semantic clustering and reasoning-based judgments, enabling reliable automatic annotation of clarification requirements. To train CLAIM, we formulate the clarification process as a structured decision generation problem and adopt a training paradigm that combines supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). Experimental results demonstrate that CLAIM can learn stable and generalizable clarification strategies without relying on manually labeled data, offering a low-cost and robust solution for proactive understanding in real-world open-domain interactions with LLMs.

</details>


### [156] [XBridge: Entity-Grounded Latent Bridge for Heterogeneous LLM Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11676)
*Wooseong Yang,Wei-Chieh Huang,Weizhi Zhang,Yu Wang,Philip S. Yu,Junhyun Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: XBRIDGE enables decode-free cross-architecture LLM communication using lexical anchors and latent enrichment to ground entities, achieving lower latency and better performance than text-based or KV-sharing baselines across heterogeneous model families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-architecture communication suffers from entity grounding issues: cross-attention bridges transferring continuous representations risk rare-token compression collapse, losing entity identity. Decoding to text loses internal representations; cross-architecture latent transfer requires grounding and efficiency.

Method: Lexical Anchor Mapping (LAM) maps sender context tokens to the receiver's vocabulary to create discrete entity anchors. Latent Enrichment Bridge (LEB) lets the receiver query the sender's hidden states for contextual enrichment. The bridge uses the receiver's self-attention to ground contextual signals to specific entities. Decode-free and discrete anchors avoid token-level collapse.

Result: Empirical evaluation across three model families (Llama, Qwen, Mistral) and seven benchmarks in both communication directions: XBRIDGE outperforms text-based communication on all tasks, with ~11x lower latency. In same-architecture settings, it exceeds a KV-sharing baseline on six of seven tasks. LEB uses 264M parameters (3.8% of the receiver) and training on a small balanced set, with negligible inference overhead.

Conclusion: XBRIDGE provides an efficient, decode-free, ground-traced cross-architecture communication protocol. It achieves consistent gains across model families and tasks with minimal training overhead, leveraging discrete lexical anchors and latent state enrichment to maintain entity identity.

Abstract: Heterogeneous multi-agent LLM systems, where agents are powered by different model families, can outperform homogeneous configurations by reducing redundant reasoning patterns. Yet existing communication protocols either operate through text, discarding the sender's internal representations, or require architectural homogeneity for latent-level transfer. We identify the entity grounding problem in cross-architecture communication: cross-attention bridges that transfer continuous representations across different LLM families suffer from rare-token compression collapse, where entity identity is lost in the continuous bottleneck (bridge-only F1 ~30%). We propose XBRIDGE, a decode-free communication protocol that addresses this through two mechanisms. Lexical Anchor Mapping (LAM) maps the sender's original context tokens to the receiver's vocabulary, providing discrete entity anchors. A Latent Enrichment Bridge (LEB) lets the receiver query the sender's hidden states for contextual enrichment. The entity anchors ground the bridge's contextual signals to specific entities through the receiver's own self-attention. Across three model families (Llama, Qwen, and Mistral), seven benchmarks, and both communication directions, XBRIDGE outperforms text-based communication on all seven tasks for each model pair while achieving 11x lower latency, and in a same-architecture setting it also exceeds a KV-sharing baseline on six of seven tasks. LEB requires only 264M trainable parameters (3.8% of the receiver), is trained on a small balanced sample set, and adds negligible inference overhead.

</details>


### [157] [AgenticTwin: An Agentic LLM Framework Integrated with Digital Twin for Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11679)
*Touseef Hasan,Mounika Ghanta,Souvika Sarkar,Ujjwal Guin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgenticTwin combines LLM-driven reasoning with a digital twin anomaly pipeline to aid operators. It grounds explanations in classifier outputs, enables natural-language querying, and uses a synthetic-anomaly benchmark on real weather data. Lightweight open-source LLMs are evaluated. Results show improved diagnosis, retrieval, and mitigation through structured agent collaboration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve interpretability and decision support in digital twin–driven anomaly analysis by combining knowledge-grounded LLM reasoning with cyber-physical sensing, and to provide a controllable benchmark for operator queries.

Method: Propose the AgenticTwin framework that grounds LLM explanations on outputs from a digital twin–based anomaly classifier, supports natural-language questions by operators, and introduces a benchmark pipeline using synthetic anomalies injected into a real-world weather sensor dataset. Evaluate lightweight, open-source LLMs for practicality, and assess diagnosis quality, contextual retrieval, and mitigation.

Result: Structured agent collaboration and knowledge-grounded reasoning yield improvements in diagnosis quality, contextual retrieval, and mitigation quality across diverse anomaly scenarios. Lightweight open-source LLMs show feasibility for practical deployment.

Conclusion: LLM-assisted agentic digital-twin analysis improves operator understanding and response to anomalies; the framework and benchmark provide a viable path for integrating reasoning and domain grounding in cyber-physical anomaly analysis, with attention to model efficiency.

Abstract: Digital twins are increasingly used to monitor and simulate the behavior of cyber-physical systems. Even with skilled operators, interpreting anomalies detected within digital twin pipelines is challenging, as the sheer complexity and volume of raw sensor data make thorough analysis difficult. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) offer promising capabilities for reasoning and explanation, yet their integration into digital twin-driven anomaly analysis remains underexplored. In this work, we propose AgenticTwin, an agentic framework that integrates LLM-driven reasoning with a digital twin-based anomaly detection pipeline. The framework grounds LLM-generated explanations in outputs from a digital twin-driven anomaly classifier and enables human operators to ask relevant natural-language questions about the system. Beyond the framework itself, we introduce a benchmark-oriented evaluation pipeline constructed over synthetic anomalies injected into a real-world weather sensor dataset, enabling controlled generation of operator queries over anomaly events. We further evaluate the feasibility of deploying lightweight, open-source LLMs for practical cyber-physical environments. Experimental results demonstrate that structured agent collaboration and knowledge-grounded reasoning improve diagnosis quality, contextual retrieval, and mitigation quality across diverse possible anomaly scenarios.

</details>


### [158] [FrontierFinance: A Challenging Benchmark for Measuring Frontier Intelligence of Finance Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11683)
*Yuhao Zhang,O. Ozan Koyluoglu,Thejas Venkatesh,Richard Diehl Martinez,Vishank Bhatia,Arash Alidoust,Ashwin Paranjape*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces FrontierFinance, a broad, open benchmark for expert investor-research tasks, with 220 queries and 11,543 rubrics across six use cases, highlighting the impact of tool harness on performance and cost. The top performer (Samaya) leads at 56.0%, with Claude Fable 5 at 49.2% and Kimi K3 at 46.4%; best open-weight model nears proprietary with much lower cost; hardest tasks are Screening & Discovery and Sector/Macro. Dataset and grading code are public.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks focus on financial data extraction and do not capture the full, long-form, open-ended investor-workflow tasks. A broad, open benchmark is needed to fairly assess frontier models and agent systems and to reveal the true cost-quality trade-offs in real-world research settings.

Method: An open benchmark ( FrontierFinance ) with 220 expert-crafted queries and 11,543 source-attributed rubrics covering six use cases across the full investor workflow. Evaluation uses publicly available data and a common harness to compare frontier models and agent systems. Metrics are performance percentages on tasks, costs are compared across systems, and rubrics are source-attributed to ensure traceability.

Result: Samaya's in-house system achieves 56.0%, Claude Fable 5 at 49.2%, and Kimi K3 at 46.4% (open-weight). The best open-weight model approaches proprietary performance at ~4.5x lower cost. Hardest use cases (Screening & Discovery; Sector/Industry & Macro) reach 33% and 39% respectively. The dataset and grading code are publicly available.

Conclusion: Tool harness quality is a major determinant of performance, not just model capability. FrontierFinance is broader and harder than existing benchmarks, revealing cost–quality trade-offs and encouraging the use of open data and in-harness evaluation for investment-research AI.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly deployed for professional investment research, yet no benchmark captures the complexity of the full investor workflow. Existing benchmarks mainly target financial data extraction, a narrow slice that current models have largely saturated, while reference-based metrics and generic LLM-as-a-judge scoring fall short on the open-ended, long-form answers that real analyst queries demand. We introduce FrontierFinance, a fully open benchmark of 220 expert-crafted queries and 11,543 source-attributed rubrics spanning six crucial use cases across the full investor workflow. FrontierFinance is both broader and harder than existing public finance benchmarks. Evaluating frontier models and agent systems under a common harness restricted to publicly available data, we find that the tool harness, not the model alone, strongly shapes quality and efficiency; that Samaya's in-house system leads at 56.0%, ahead of the strongest frontier model (Claude Fable 5, 49.2%) at roughly 2.2x lower cost; and that the best open-weight model (Kimi K3, 46.4%) nearly matches the best proprietary model at 4.5x lower cost. Screening & Discovery and Sector, Industry & Macro remain the hardest use cases across all systems, where even the best systems reach only 33% and 39%. We make the dataset and grading code publicly available.

</details>


### [159] [HUGIN: Enhancing Vision-Language Planning for Autonomous Logistics Sorting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11692)
*Xikai Sun,Cangtian Zhou,Kebin Liu,Ke Ma,Xu Wang,Zaishu Chen,Haotian Wang,Li Liu,Yunhao Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HUGIN improves joint multi-scene understanding for autonomous logistics sorting using vision-language models through endogenous data augmentation and global context ranking, introducing SortingBench and achieving strong gains (e.g., Qwen3-VL-8B accuracy 63.6%→78.8%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address Joint Multi-Scene Understanding (JMSU) for autonomous logistics sorting, where cross-scene supervision is scarce and long visual context disperses attention. Leverage open-world VLMs to enable planning with multiple camera views.

Method: Two components: (1) Endogenous Data Augmentation that recombines verified atomic facts under operating constraints; (2) Global Context Ranking that aligns instruction representations with complete visual context rather than partial contexts. Also introduces SortingBench dataset from four system layouts and evaluates across five open VLMs.

Result: HUGIN consistently outperforms baselines across five open VLMs; notable improvement on SortingBench (e.g., Qwen3-VL-8B from 63.6% to 78.8%). Additional ablations confirm efficacy of each component and JMSU spillover benefits in embodied tasks. Deployment tests with over 15,000 packages demonstrate real-world viability.

Conclusion: HUGIN effectively enables VLM-based planning for autonomous logistics sorting by leveraging JMSU-friendly training and context-aware inference; SortingBench provides a relevant benchmark for industrial sorting, and the approach shows practical viability in large-scale deployment.

Abstract: Autonomous logistics sorting systems (ALSS) are an important industrial application of embodied AI, which requires joint planning over spatially disjoint camera views. We formulate this setting as Joint Multi-Scene Understanding (JMSU). With open-world visual understanding and task-planning capabilities, vision-language models (VLMs) are promising candidates for JMSU. However, directly applying existing VLMs to JMSU is non-trivial due to scarce cross-scene supervision and attention dispersion caused by long visual context in JMSU. To address these challenges, we propose HUGIN, a training framework with two complementary components. Endogenous Data Augmentation recombines verified atomic facts under operating constraints, while Global Context Ranking aligns the instruction representation more strongly with the complete visual context than with a partial visual context. To support ongoing research, we construct a high-quality industrial sorting dataset and benchmark named SortingBench from four layouts of autonomous logistics sorting systems. Across five open VLMs, HUGIN consistently outperforms matched baselines; for example, the accuracy on SortingBench of Qwen3-VL-8B increases from 63.6% to 78.8%. Additional experiments verify the effectiveness of each component and JMSU's spillover benefits in embodied tasks. Deployment tests involving more than 15,000 packages support the practical viability of VLM-based planning for autonomous logistics sorting.

</details>


### [160] [Making Your LLMs More Objective: Stabilizing LLM Safety Behavior Across Traits with Trait-Invariant Safety Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11705)
*Lang Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Trait-induced safety variation in aligned LLMs; introduces metrics and subspace analysis; proposes TraSN, via TIST self-distillation, to achieve trait-invariant safety with maintained capability; improves harmful-request safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety decisions in LLMs depend on system-prompt traits, causing inconsistent safety behavior. Quantifying and mitigating this trait-induced variability is essential for robust alignment.

Method: Define Trait-Induced Deviation and Trait-Induced Flip Rate to measure trait effects; conduct representation-level subspace analysis showing low-dimensional perturbations; propose Trait-Invariant Safety Tuning (TIST) using self-distillation to align trait-conditioned behavior with no-trait behavior; instantiate Trait-Subspace Neutralization (TraSN) to enforce invariance within the identified trait subspace.

Result: TraSN improves trait-invariant safety and strengthens safety for harmful requests while preserving general capabilities.

Conclusion: Traits are a critical factor in LLM safety; addressing them via TraSN/TIST yields more robust, trait-invariant alignment without sacrificing performance.

Abstract: Aligned large language models (LLMs) are expected to exhibit safety behavior based on the content of the user request: they should refuse unsafe requests and comply with safe ones. However, we show that the same request can elicit substantially different safety decisions under different traits assigned in the system prompt, a failure mode we call trait-induced safety variation. To measure this failure, we introduce refusal-based metrics: Trait-Induced Deviation measures dataset-level deviation from the no-trait baseline, while Trait-Induced Flip Rate measures whether the same request receives different safety decisions across traits. We then provide a representation-level analysis of the mechanism behind trait-induced safety shifts and find that traits perturb the model's safety representations within a low-dimensional subspace. To achieve trait-invariant safety, where safety behavior remains stable across traits, we introduce Trait-Invariant Safety Tuning (TIST), a simple yet effective self-distillation framework that aligns an LLM's trait-conditioned behavior with its no-trait behavior. Guided by our analysis, we further propose Trait-Subspace Neutralization (TraSN), an instantiation of TIST, which enforces invariance only within the identified trait subspace. Experiments show that TraSN improves trait-invariant safety and strengthens harmful-request safety while preserving general capability. Our results highlight traits as an important factor in LLM safety and robust model behavior.

</details>


### [161] [Proportional Analogies on Probability Distributions via Bayesian Updating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11724)
*Pierre-Alexandre Murena*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a proportional analogy framework for probability distributions via Bayesian updating, extending analogical reasoning from Boolean/symbolic/real domains to probabilistic domains, with exponential-family focus and Gaussian-mixture-based extension to general distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill a gap in analogical reasoning research by defining and studying proportional analogies for probability distributions, grounded in Bayesian updating and aiming for a principled, axiomatic framework across distribution families.

Method: Define proportional analogies as transformability between distributions via Bayesian updating induced by a set of observations. Analyze this framework for standard exponential-family distributions and show how Gaussian mixture approximations can extend the approach to arbitrary distributions.

Result: The framework yields a principled way to relate distributions through updating, with concrete analysis for common exponential-family members and a practical pathway to general distributions using Gaussian mixtures.

Conclusion: Extends proportional analogies to the probabilistic domain, providing a Bayesian updating-based notion of analogy and paving the way for broad applicability in probabilistic modeling and reasoning.

Abstract: Analogies are quaternary relations of the form "A is to B as C is to D". Among the various formalizations of analogical reasoning, proportional analogies provide an important axiomatic framework by characterizing valid analogies through a set of postulates. While proportional analogies have been extensively studied over Boolean, symbolic, and real-valued domains, their extension to probability distributions remains largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce a notion of proportional analogy for probability distributions based on Bayesian updating. Our approach builds upon the idea that two distributions are related whenever one can be transformed into the other through Bayesian updating induced by a suitable set of observations. We investigate this framework for several standard members of the exponential family and discuss how it naturally extends to arbitrary probability distributions through Gaussian mixture approximations.

</details>


### [162] [Harness-IF: Evaluating Instruction Following Across Instruction Surfaces in Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11727)
*Zining Huang,Haoran Que,Hong Zeng,Ge Zhang,Zuo Wang,Jin Chen,Haodong Wang,Zhongfei Hou,Changxin Pu,Shen Yan,Wenhao Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Rule-level compliance evaluation (Harness-IF) with AP-Acc reveals that models show substantial but overstated adherence to operational rules; precedence is not solely driven by prompt depth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing instruction-following benchmarks conflate compliance with incidental task success and cannot distinguish whether a rule is followed due to genuine adherence or coincidence; the study introduces operational-rule scoring to disentangle these components.

Method: Create Harness-IF: 60 realistic multi-turn coding items drawn from a 642-rule library; evaluate 256 rules for execution evidence across five surfaces a deployed agent reads. Use Against-Prior Accuracy (AP-Acc): re-run tasks with a withheld rule across nine probe builds to isolate rules that oppose unprompted defaults. Test 12 frontier models.

Result: Accuracy of rule verdicts ranges 72.1–85.9%; AP-Acc ranges 66.1–78.6%. All models perform worse on against-prior rules by 3.6–7.4 points (mean 5.81). Prior-control maintains the top build but reorders adjacent ranks. Aggregated scores inflate true compliance by model-specific margin. A counterbalanced conflict pilot on nine builds shows precedence does not scale with prompt depth; system prompts, project files, and user instructions outrank tool and skill descriptions.

Conclusion: Rule-level evaluation with counterfactual controls reveals that aggregate compliance metrics overstate genuine compliance; benchmarking should incorporate rule-level and counterfactual analyses to properly assess alignment and instruction-following fidelity.

Abstract: When a coding agent obeys a rule, it may simply have been going to do that anyway. Existing instruction-following benchmarks cannot tell the difference: they concentrate rules in the user turn, while coding-agent benchmarks emphasize final task success. We introduce Harness-IF, which scores operational rules one at a time from execution evidence: 60 realistic multi-turn coding items drawn from a 642-rule library, 256 rules receiving verdicts, placed on the five configurable surfaces a deployed agent reads. To separate compliance from coincidence we introduce Against-Prior Accuracy (AP-Acc), which scores only rules labeled as opposing unprompted defaults, observed by re-running tasks with the rule withheld across nine probe builds and curated otherwise. Across 12 frontier models, accuracy spans 72.1-85.9% and AP-Acc 66.1-78.6%; every model is worse on against-prior rules, by 3.6 to 7.4 points (mean 5.81), and the direction survives a common-support analysis with item-clustered intervals. Aggregate scores therefore overstate compliance by a model-specific margin: prior control leaves the top build unchanged and exchanges three adjacent rank pairs. A counterbalanced conflict pilot on nine separate builds adds a second result: pooled precedence does not follow prompt depth, with system prompts, project files, and user instructions ahead of tool and skill descriptions.

</details>


### [163] [HyperANFIS: Enhancing Rule Representation and Interpretability in Adaptive Neuro-Fuzzy Systems via Hyperbolic Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11768)
*Haoran Pei,Zhao Su,Zetao Lin,Haoran Li,Jun Shen,Qi Zhu,Lan Guo,Qingguo Zhou,Binbin Yong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HyperANFIS extends ANFIS to hyperbolic space, achieving higher predictive accuracy and better-quality interpretable rules while preserving the original architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional ANFIS operates in Euclidean space, which constrains representation capacity and predictive performance. A hyperbolic geometry could enable richer rule representations and improved inference without sacrificing interpretability.

Method: Introduce HyperANFIS, a hyperbolic extension of ANFIS that carries out rule-prototype learning, rule activation, and consequent aggregation in hyperbolic space while preserving interpretable IF-THEN rules.

Result: Empirical results show HyperANFIS consistently outperforms standard ANFIS and its variants across multiple datasets and produces higher-quality fuzzy rules, along with better inter-rule collaboration and credibility of the rules.

Conclusion: Hyperbolic geometry enhances fuzzy inference in ANFIS, yielding stronger performance and more credible, interpretable rules without sacrificing the core architecture.

Abstract: The adaptive neuro-fuzzy inference system (ANFIS) is an interpretable reasoning framework capable of generating explicit IF-THEN fuzzy rules, making it suitable for tasks requiring transparent reasoning. However, existing ANFIS models generally construct rule antecedents and perform inference in Euclidean space, limiting their representational capacity and predictive performance. To address this issue, we propose Hyperbolic ANFIS (HyperANFIS), a hyperbolic extension of ANFIS. HyperANFIS preserves the fuzzy semantics and core architecture of conventional ANFIS while performing rule-prototype learning, rule activation, and consequent aggregation in hyperbolic space. It also retains the ability to generate interpretable IF-THEN rules. By exploiting the representational properties of hyperbolic geometry, HyperANFIS strengthens the fuzzy inference process, thereby improving predictive accuracy, inter-rule collaboration, and the credibility of its interpretable rules. Experimental results show that HyperANFIS consistently outperforms the standard ANFIS baseline and various ANFIS variants across all datasets, while also generating higher-quality fuzzy rules.

</details>


### [164] [The Sleeping Agent: What Gist-Based Context Compression Loses and Why](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11775)
*Nicholas E. Kyrkewood*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Gist-based context compression via SWC helps multi-hop and single-hop factual retrieval but harms temporal queries; a targeted one-sentence prompt tweak that preserves temporal expressions largely mitigates this deficit, revealing memory-type dependent effects of gist compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how gist-based context compression affects different memory retrieval types in long-horizon language model agents, using sleep-inspired Salience-Weighted Consolidation (SWC) as a diagnostic probe.

Method: Apply SWC to ten LoCoMo conversations, evaluating four conditions on 1,935 matched questions (1,501 in primary aggregate). Use a temperature of 0. Analyze task-type interactions (multi-hop, single-hop factual, temporal). Identify a mechanism where gist abstraction discards dates/times; perform a one-sentence prompt modification to preserve temporal expressions; conduct preservation analysis across all conversations to quantify temporal, named-entity, and event preservation; report judge-accuracy change with bootstrapped or reported interval.

Result: Gist compression outperforms truncation on multi-hop reasoning and single-hop factual questions; temporal questions remain substantially harder under compression, scoring below full-context references. Temporal-expression preservation increased ~20× with the prompt tweak (3.05% → 62.39%), while NE and event preservation changed little (×1.02, ×1.11). The prompt modification yields a +0.314 [0.254, 0.375] improvement in judge accuracy for category-2 temporal questions in the matched set.

Conclusion: Temporal information preservation is the critical lever for temporal reasoning under gist compression; a precise prompt modifier can restore performance, highlighting the memory-type specificity of gist-based compression. SWC proves useful as a diagnostic framework for disentangling memory retrieval types in long-horizon LMs. Code and results are available at the provided GitHub link.

Abstract: Gist-based context compression---summarising older conversation history into compact representations---is a common approach in long-horizon language model agents, yet its effect on different types of memory retrieval is poorly understood. We use Salience-Weighted Consolidation (SWC), a biologically-inspired compression framework motivated by sleep-based memory consolidation, as a diagnostic probe to study when gist compression helps and when it hurts. SWC scores conversation history by salience, partitions it into priority tiers, and applies structured gist abstraction to mid-priority content. Evaluating four conditions on all ten LoCoMo conversations---1,935 matched text-only questions in total, 1,501 used in the primary aggregate after excluding Category 5 (adversarial) questions---at temperature 0, we find a consistent task-type interaction: gist compression substantially outperforms truncation on multi-hop reasoning and single-hop factual questions, but temporal questions remain substantially harder under compression, with compressed conditions scoring well below the full-context reference on the conversations where both are evaluated. We trace this failure to a specific mechanism: the gist abstraction prompt preserves relational and event structure while discarding dates and times. A preservation analysis across all ten conversations confirms the mechanism: an approximately 20-fold increase in temporal expression preservation (3.05% to 62.39%) with a one-sentence prompt modification, while named entity and event preservation rates barely change (x1.02 and x1.11), demonstrating that the fix is a precision instrument. The prompt modification recovers +0.314 [0.254, 0.375] judge accuracy on category-2 (temporal) questions in the matched set. Code and results: https://github.com/kyrkewood/sleeping-agent.

</details>


### [165] [Agent Skills Can Be Harmful: An Empirical Study of Skill-Induced Failures in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11888)
*Gen Dong,Yanjie Gao,Liqun Li,Tianyin Xu,Yu Hua,Fan Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces a differential analysis framework to attribute skill-induced failures in LLM agents, using SkillTriage to analyze 307 skill-related issues (125 functional, 182 efficiency regressions) across SkillsBench and SWE-Skills-Bench; key findings include that functional failures arise from seemingly relevant skills causing missing or incorrect task elements, efficiency regressions are not just due to prompt length, and excessive verification/complex pipelines drive many cases; proposes topics and tooling to enable safer, cost-aware skill reuse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address conflicting and mixed effects of agent skills reported in prior work by providing a framework to diagnose when and why skills harm performance or increase cost, enabling safer and more cost-effective skill reuse.

Method: Develop a differential analysis framework that compares a target skill-guided run to a no-skill or semantically matched reference run on the same task, or a cheaper variant. Instantiate on SkillsBench and SWE-Skills-Bench to collect skill-induced failures. Create SkillTriage, a taxonomy-guided tool that normalizes paired cases, extracts differential evidence, and generates triage reports.

Result: Identification of 307 skill-induced failures: 125 functional failures and 182 efficiency regressions. Major findings: (1) Functional failures often stem from seemingly relevant skills causing omissions or incorrect implementations; (2) Efficiency regressions are not explained solely by prompt length; (3) Excessive Procedure is a dominant source, with 67 cases from excessive verification and 30 from heavy pipelines. Based on these, propose research topics and tooling improvements for safer and cost-aware skill reuse.

Conclusion: Differential analysis and taxonomy-guided triage enable systematic attribution of faults and costs to agent skills, guiding safer design and tooling improvements to mitigate functional errors and efficiency regressions while reducing unnecessary procedural overhead.

Abstract: Agent skills are the de facto mechanism for extending LLM agents with reusable guidance. A skill can shape the agent's task execution, including planning, tool use, problem-solving, and validation. Prior work reported mixed results of agent skills: some skills improve task success rates, while others have no effect, increase token use and execution time, and even reduce success rates. This paper presents a comprehensive analysis of skill-induced agent failures by attributing task failures and cost regressions to specific loaded skills. We introduce a differential analysis framework that attributes a failure or regression to a skill by comparing a target skill-guided run against a no-skill or semantically matched skill reference run that solves the same task, or solves it more cheaply. We instantiate this framework on SkillsBench and SWE-Skills-Bench, yielding 307 skill-induced failures, including 125 functional failures and 182 efficiency regressions. We also build SkillTriage, a taxonomy-guided attribution tool that normalizes paired cases, extracts differential evidence, and produces triage reports. Our major findings include: (1) Skill induced functional failures are rarely caused by obviously irrelevant skills; instead, seemingly relevant skills often make the agent incorrectly implement or omit task-required implementation elements. (2) Skill-induced efficiency regressions are not explained by prompt length alone. (3) The largest sources within Excessive Procedure are excessive verification and heavy implementation pipelines, contributing 67 and 30 cases, respectively. This shows that skills often turn validation checklists and construction recipes into mandatory work. Based on our findings, we propose research topics and tooling improvements for safer and more cost-aware skill reuse.

</details>


### [166] [Policy-as-logic for robust reasoning over rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11905)
*Rahul Nair,Bastian Lipka,Elizabeth Daly*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hybrid symbolic approach grounds policies for generative AI: express policies in formal logic, leverage language models for fact extraction to ground predicates, and use an answer-set solver for reasoning, yielding interpretable, auditable, robust responses with significant token efficiency (~10x) compared to policy-as-prompt/code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensure responses respect written policies/rules while remaining interpretable, auditable, and robust in the face of input variations by combining symbolic reasoning with generative models.

Method: Express policies in formal logic; use language models to extract facts to ground predicates; employ an answer-set solver for logical reasoning; separate extraction (LM-based) from reasoning (symbolic solver) to enable auditable outputs.

Result: Outputs are interpretable, auditable, and robust to input perturbations; outperforms policy-as-prompt and policy-as-code in most cases; approximately 10x reduction in token usage.

Conclusion: Structured reasoning that combines symbolic solvers with generative models can yield robust, auditable decisions for objective criteria in policy-constrained generative AI.

Abstract: In many practical applications of generative AI systems, from tax rules to airline baggage allowance, responses to natural language queries must respect written policies or rules. We present a hybrid symbolic approach that expresses policies in formal logic and at inference time exploits the representation power of language models for fact extraction to ground predicates, and an answer set solver for reasoning such that responses are interpretable, auditable, and as we show, accurate and robust under input perturbations. Specifically, we show this separation of extraction and reasoning steps outperforms policy-as-prompt and policy-as-code methods in most cases with ~10x reduction in token usage. The results point to the value of structured reasoning and symbolic solvers in conjunction with generative models to make robust decisions involving objective criteria.

</details>


### [167] [OEIS Open: How many conjectures can language models turn into theorems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11941)
*Tom Adamczewski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OEIS Open is a benchmark of 492 OEIS conjectures formalized in Lean; evaluates general LMs with a minimal toolset under cost-limited budgets; achieves modest success (30% overall, 147/492; 44% on a 100-conjecture subset at a higher budget); arXiv literature access and advanced agent loops did not notably improve results; demonstrates autonomous, low-cost conjecture resolution but with limitations and uncertain mathematical significance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide a reproducible, scalable benchmark for evaluating language models on formal mathematical conjecture resolution, enabling secure, tool-assisted evaluation and fair comparison across models.

Method: Formalize 492 OEIS conjectures in Lean (by Tsoukalas et al.), build an open-source evaluation framework, run generic LMs with a minimal, secure toolset, and enforce per-attempt budgets. Include OEIS Open Lite (100 conjectures) for cheaper evaluation. Test with/without access to a large arXiv corpus (476k papers) and with different agent-loop strategies to assess improvements in performance.

Result: With a minimal toolset, LMs resolve 147 of 492 conjectures at $50 per attempt (≈30% overall; OEIS Open). On OEIS Open Lite (100 conjectures), the best LM achieves 44% at $200 per attempt. Providing arXiv literature access or more sophisticated agent loops did not increase performance. The conjectures are of uncertain mathematical significance, but the study demonstrates autonomous, cost-effective conjecture solving by LMs.

Conclusion: The benchmark demonstrates potential for autonomous LM-driven exploration of open mathematical conjectures at modest cost, while highlighting limitations in current capabilities, susceptibility to dataset difficulty, and the limited impact of external literature or complex agent strategies. Future work should explore richer toolchains, per-conject difficulty analyses, broader datasets, and deeper ablations to understand where LMs succeed or fail.

Abstract: We construct OEIS Open, a benchmark based on 492 open mathematical conjectures from the OEIS, formalized in Lean by Tsoukalas et al. Whereas these conjectures had previously been attempted only with a bespoke agent, our open-source evaluation code runs any generic language model (LM) against them, and is secure against LM cheating attempts. We find that LMs equipped with a minimal set of tools resolve 147 of these conjectures with a budget of \$50 per attempt, scoring 30% on OEIS Open. OEIS Open Lite is a random subset of 100 conjectures for cheaper evaluation. When evaluated with a budget of \$200 per attempt, the best current LM scores 44% on OEIS Open Lite. Giving LMs access to the mathematics literature via 476,000 papers from arXiv did not increase performance on OEIS Open Lite, and nor did using more sophisticated agent loops. The conjectures covered in this work are of uncertain mathematical significance, and most have likely received little previous attention. Nevertheless, our results show that LMs can resolve open research conjectures autonomously and at modest cost.

</details>


### [168] [ExRole: From Team Trajectories to Executable Roles in Multi-Agent Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11949)
*Zhou Liu,Chaoyang Han,Zewei Pan,Zeli Su,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ExRole learns trajectory-based role prototypes to turn roles into future-aware, executable control variables, yielding substantial EM/F1 gains on multi-agent benchmarks and demonstrating transferable, role-conditioned behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Roles in multi-agent systems are often hand-written prompt labels that are disconnected from learnable behavior. An effective role should be a trainable, executable control variable that summarizes predictive behavior, guides interaction, and connects to the trainable capacity behind that behavior.

Method: ExRole learns future-aware role prototypes from prefix-local team traces (trajectory-to-role mapping), resolves them into readable instructions and token-aligned role markers, and (optionally) routes shared LoRA rank slots with turn-aligned credit. It evaluates on MuSiQue and 2WikiMultiHopQA to compare against single-agent search and various baselines, with objective metrics like EM and F1.

Result: ExRole yields large performance gains over single-agent search: 15.0/14.4 and 13.5/16.1 EM/F1 points on MuSiQue and 2WikiMultiHopQA, respectively. Against the strongest non-ExRole controls, gains are 11.5/11.6 and 7.7/9.7 points. Across both benchmarks, trajectory-induced role conditioning consistently outperforms role-free, manual, random, and shuffled alternatives. Role-Agent-Turn interventions show the induced roles capture transferable behavioral specialization beyond fixed identities or turn positions.

Conclusion: Trajectory-induced roles serve as effective, interpretable, and transferable executable controls for coordinating language-model agents, providing measurable gains over baseline role frameworks and demonstrating the value of learning role prototypes from trajectory data.

Abstract: Roles provide an interpretable interface for organizing language-model agents, yet most multi-agent systems treat them as hand-written prompt labels disconnected from learned behavior and parameter updates. We argue that a useful role should instead be an executable control variable: it should summarize behavior predictive of future utility, guide subsequent interaction, and identify the trainable capacity responsible for that behavior. We introduce ExRole, a trajectory-to-role framework that learns future-aware role prototypes from prefix-local team traces, resolves them into readable instructions and token-aligned role markers, and optionally routes shared LoRA rank slots with turn-aligned credit. Across MuSiQue and 2WikiMultiHopQA, ExRole improves over single-agent search by 15.0/14.4 and 13.5/16.1 EM/F1 points, respectively. Against the strongest non-ExRole controls, the corresponding gains remain 11.5/11.6 and 7.7/9.7 points. Across both benchmarks, the controlled results consistently favor trajectory-induced role conditioning over role-free, manual, random, and shuffled alternatives. Role-Agent-Turn interventions further show that the induced roles capture transferable behavioral specialization beyond fixed agent identities or turn positions.

</details>


### [169] [Retry, Switch, or Abstain? Learning Strategy-Aware Tool-Use Policies via Controlled Error Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11977)
*Chaoran Chen,Vy Nguyen,Ziji Zhang,Abhinav Gullapalli,Ziyi Wang,Yuxuan Lu,Dakuo Wang,Jing Huang,Zhou Yu,Jin Lai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BENCH2ROBUST turns error-free tool-use benchmarks into staged stochastic environments to study and close the robustness gap in tool usage; combining Bayesian Tool Memory (BTM) with reinforcement learning (RL) yields stronger resilience to tool failures than either alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world tool-using LLM agents face unreliable tools (transient, persistent, or silent failures). Current benchmarks assume perfect tool calls; there is a need to understand and improve recovery strategies (retry, switch, or stop) under controlled failure scenarios.

Method: Convert failure-free benchmarks into stochastic, solvability-controlled episodes where failures force retries, path switching, or stopping. Evaluate two interventions: (1) structured runtime recovery via Bayesian Tool Memory (BTM); (2) curriculum-controlled reinforcement learning. Test across 7 models from 4 families and two multi-turn benchmarks; assess on held-out Retail tasks.

Result: Tool failures create a near-universal robustness gap. BTM improves robustness on Retail tasks by up to 16.8 percentage points without retraining; RL provides complementary recovery behavior that remains beneficial without inference-time BTM. The combination of both approaches yields 40.8–45.5% robustness under injected failures while preserving failure-free performance.

Conclusion: Robust tool use benefits from a hybrid approach that blends environment-specific recovery knowledge (BTM) with learned recovery behavior (RL); together they substantially close the robustness gap under varied failure conditions.

Abstract: Tool-using LLM agents are commonly trained and evaluated in environments where tool calls succeed reliably, yet deployed tools can fail transiently, persistently, or silently. Robust recovery therefore requires more than repeated retries: an agent may need to retry the same path, switch to an alternative, or recognize that no viable path remains. We present BENCH2ROBUST, a framework that converts failure-free tool-use benchmarks into controlled stochastic environments with scenario-controlled solvability, where episodes explicitly require retrying, switching, or stopping after available paths are exhausted. We use BENCH2ROBUST to study two complementary interventions: structured runtime recovery context through Bayesian Tool Memory (BTM), and curriculum-controlled reinforcement learning. Across 7 models from 4 families and two multi-turn benchmark families, tool failures produce a near-universal robustness gap. On held-out Retail tasks, BTM improves robustness by up to 16.8 percentage points without retraining, while RL learns complementary recovery behavior that remains beneficial without inference-time BTM. Combining the two reaches 40.8-45.5% under injection while preserving failure-free performance. These results suggest that robust tool use benefits from combining environment-specific recovery knowledge with learned recovery behavior.

</details>


### [170] [Claim-Level Reliability Assessment for Efficient Test-Time Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11994)
*Sen Xu,Wei Wang,Shixi Liu,Jixin Min,Yingwei Dai,Zhibin Yin,Yirong Chen,Junlin Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CLR is a training-free framework that shifts test-time compute from solution sampling to targeted verification by condensing reasoning into decision-critical claims, enabling semantic falsification and improving reliability metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whole-trace evaluation can obscure decisive errors; under fixed model capabilities, a reliable falsification approach is needed to identify critical flaws with minimal computation.

Method: Introduce claim-level falsification and CLR; condense traces into claims; focus on negative evidence; nonlinear reliability scoring; test across multiple LLMs and benchmarks with matched budgets.

Result: CLR generally improves pass@1 and self-consistency across four LLMs and four benchmarks; on GPT-OSS-20B/CMIMC25, surpasses pass@1 by 27.15 percentage points and raises self-consistency from 77.50% to 82.19% with 37% fewer tokens.

Conclusion: Claim-level falsification with CLR provides scalable, training-free verification that reduces token use while improving accuracy and consistency; supports a shift from solution construction to semantic falsification.

Abstract: We propose claim-level falsification as a principle for test-time scaling and instantiate it through Claim-Level Reliability Assessment (CLR), a training-free framework that reallocates test-time compute from additional solution sampling to targeted verification. Since whole-trace evaluation often obscures decisive errors due to signal dilution from routine tokens, CLR condenses each reasoning trace into a compact set of decision-critical claims, thereby isolating its logical anchors. Furthermore, recognizing the inherent difficulty of generating entirely correct solutions under fixed model capabilities, CLR shifts the focus to semantic falsification. This approach exploits a fundamental asymmetry between solution construction and claim refutation. Constructing a valid solution requires a flawless reasoning path, whereas refuting an incorrect claim requires identifying only a single decisive flaw. This targeted search for negative evidence systematically compresses the survival space of high-confidence incorrect traces, effectively suppressing erroneous consensus via nonlinear reliability scoring. Across four LLMs and four reasoning benchmarks under matched budgets, CLR generally improves upon pass@1 and self-consistency. On GPT-OSS-20B/CMIMC25, for instance, CLR exceeds pass@1 by 27.15 percentage-points and raises self-consistency accuracy from 77.50\% to 82.19\% with 37.0\% fewer tokens.

</details>


### [171] [CTBench: Evaluating Troubleshooting Capabilities of AI Agents in Realistic Telecom Network Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12002)
*Xingyu Yan,Tingting Dai,Antonio De Domenico,Mohamed Sana,Nicola Piovesan,Changchang Li,Bowen Liu,Kun Jiang,Mengjie Zhang,Dingcheng Shan,Jing-Cheng Pang,Chenwei Wu,Sijie Wu,Lianying Chao,Haoran Cai,Jiantao Ye,Xubin Li,Simon Mark Lucas,Xin Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CTBench is a public benchmark for evaluating telecom troubleshooting agents, focusing on root-cause analysis and path restoration with expert-grounded metrics and evidence requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a gap in realistically evaluating agents under partial observability in diverse telecom environments, including multiple vendors and interfaces, which current benchmarks fail to capture.

Method: CTBench builds tasks by experts, annotated with rich metadata and golden evidence steps; uses expert-grounded metrics that evaluate final answers and diagnostic evidence; evaluates agent performance with representative harness-model combinations and analyzes root-cause vs. path-restoration capabilities.

Result: Agents identify endpoints well in path-restoration tasks but underperform on root-cause analysis, especially for interface state, link-layer, service-management, and other operational faults; many correct final answers lack evidence-grounded diagnoses; path restoration is more resource-intensive, yet higher resource use does not reliably improve diagnostic quality.

Conclusion: CTBench exposes critical gaps in current telecom troubleshooting agents, highlighting the need for better handling of partial observability, operational faults, and evidence-grounded reasoning; results suggest focusing on improving root-cause analytics and evidence generation, with careful resource-management considerations.

Abstract: Agents are increasingly considered for automating network operations and maintenance, where engineers must diagnose network faults, optimize configurations to enhance services, and reduce operational costs while acting under strict constraints. However, existing evaluations fail to accurately model real network characteristics or assess agents under partially observable telecom environments with diverse vendors, devices, protocols, and interfaces. In this paper, we introduce CTBench, a public benchmark for assessing whether an agent behaves like a competent telecom troubleshooting engineer. CTBench focuses on root cause analysis and path restoration. Each task is constructed by experts and annotated with rich task metadata, including golden evidence steps. CTBench uses expert-grounded metrics that evaluate both final answers and the diagnostic evidence. Experiments with representative harness-model combinations show that state-of-the-art agents perform very well at identifying endpoints in path-restoration tasks but, more generally, underperform in root cause analysis. In particular, agents struggle with interface state, link-layer, service-management, and other operational faults. Most importantly, even when agents produce plausible or correct final answers, they often fail to provide the evidence-grounded diagnoses required in operational practice. Our results further show that path restoration is generally more resource expensive, yet larger resource usage does not necessarily translate into better diagnosis.

</details>


### [172] [Mechanist: AI as a Scientific Instrument for Discovering the Mechanisms of Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12036)
