*Mengru Wang,Junfeng Fang,Shuofei Qiao,Zhenqian Xu,Haoming Xu,Haoxiong Wang,Shumin Deng,Linyi Yang,Zhixiang Cui,Xin Xu,Yunzhi Yao,Buqiang Xu,Fei Shen,Haozhe Luo,Yunxiang Wei,Ningyu Zhang,Julian McAuley,Tat Seng Chua,Huajun Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Mechanist is an autonomous AI research assistant that discovers, explains, and steers AI intelligence mechanisms using an interpretable knowledge graph and a library of 32 methods, surpassing prior AI-scientist systems in hypothesis quality and experimental reliability, with concrete case studies on safety risks, belief mechanisms, and practical interventions including DNA sequence design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a gap between observed model capabilities and underlying mechanisms; manual mechanistic exploration is slow and limited. As AI development accelerates, a self-guided, autonomous mechanistic discovery framework is needed to understand, validate, and control AI across domains.

Method: Build Mechanist, an agentic system that uses AI as a scientific instrument. It constructs an interpretability-focused knowledge graph of ~13,000 papers and integrates it with a multidisciplinary database of ~43 million papers across 26 fields. It curates a library of 32 foundational methods for mechanism analysis, causal intervention, and validation. Mechanist autonomously generates mechanism hypotheses, designs and executes experiments, and validates findings. It is evaluated against Claude Code and existing AI-scientist systems, showing more valuable hypotheses and more reliable experiments. The paper demonstrates a progression from discovering model behaviors to explaining and controlling AI models, including a discovered safety risk, a mechanism theory of belief, and interventions that improve model performance and steer foundation models toward designing DNA sequences with specified properties.

Result: Mechanist yields more valuable mechanism hypotheses and executes experiments more reliably than baselines; it uncovers counterintuitive safety risks, develops a mechanism theory of belief, and provides practical interventions that improve performance and enable targeted DNA sequence design.

Conclusion: Mechanist advances autonomous, interpretable mechanistic science of AI by bridging behavior discovery to explanation and control, enabling safer, more capable AI through cross-domain knowledge integration and actionable interventions, including directed DNA sequence generation.

Abstract: AI models have achieved remarkable success across diverse domains, yet the mechanisms underlying their capabilities and the risks they may pose remain poorly understood. As AI development becomes faster and increasingly automated, mechanistic exploration remains largely manual, widening the gap between what models can do and our ability to understand and control them. To bridge this gap, we introduce Mechanist, an agentic system that uses AI as a scientific instrument for the autonomous discovery of mechanisms underlying AI intelligence. To support autonomous mechanistic discovery, we construct an interpretability-focused knowledge graph of approximately 13,000 papers and integrate it with a multidisciplinary database of 43 million papers spanning 26 fields. We further curate a library of 32 foundational methods for mechanism analysis, causal intervention, and validation. Compared with Claude Code and existing AI-scientist systems, Mechanist generates more valuable mechanism hypotheses and executes experiments more reliably. Mechanist also demonstrates a progression from discovering model behaviors to explaining and controlling AI models. Specifically, Mechanist first uncovers a counterintuitive safety risk in scientific laboratories, showing that unsafe traits can transfer across modalities through apparently safe training data. Mechanist then develops a mechanism theory of belief, revealing how models represent world knowledge, form beliefs, infer the beliefs of others, and how these mechanisms emerge during pretraining. Finally, Mechanist translates these mechanistic insights into practical interventions that improve model performance across diverse scenarios and steer scientific foundation models toward generating DNA sequences with specified properties.

</details>


### [173] [Graph-Structured Rubrics: Compiling Rubrics into Typed Evaluation Graphs for LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12097)
*Xi Chen,Jie Mu,Mo Xuan,Qun Shao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GSR converts rubrics into a typed, response-independent evaluation graph to formalize criterion composition and improve scoring accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To fix implicit, ad hoc composition in rubric-based evaluation and enable exact, consistent aggregation across criteria before responses are observed.

Method: Compile rubric into a graph with typed criterion nodes, operators (transformation, reduction, gating), and a Readout mapping to a final score or preference; validate graphs for type correctness; evaluate pointwise (dimensions separately) and pairwise (one judgment per candidate per criterion); test on GPT-OSS-120B against Prometheus-style baselines.

Result: GSR yields 0.62–6.75 percentage point improvements in exact score agreement over Prometheus in four pointwise datasets and achieves the highest end-to-end pairwise accuracy on two preference benchmarks under native policies.

Conclusion: Graph-Structured Rubrics provide a principled, compilable framework for rubric-based evaluation that improves accuracy and clarity by making criterion composition explicit and machine-checkable.

Abstract: Rubric-based evaluators commonly treat rubrics as prompt context or flat criteria: they specify what to judge but leave criterion composition implicit, even when natural-language rules state it. We introduce Graph-Structured Rubrics (GSR), which compiles a rubric into a response-independent typed evaluation graph before observing responses. Criterion nodes elicit judgments; transformation, reduction, and gating operators compose them through named ports; and a task-specific output mapping, termed Readout, converts the unique sink into a score or preference. Compilation rejects malformed or type-incompatible graphs. Pointwise evaluation judges rubric dimensions separately before graph aggregation; pairwise evaluation reuses the graph with one judgment for each candidate under every criterion. Under GPT-OSS-120B, GSR improves exact score agreement by 0.62--6.75 percentage points over Prometheus-style scoring on four pointwise datasets and achieves the numerically highest end-to-end pairwise accuracy on two preference benchmarks under native tie and abstention policies.

</details>


### [174] [GUIDE: Governed Unified Intelligence for Document-to-Artifact Generation in Enterprise Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12133)
*Shivali Dalmia,Sumukha Thoppanahalli,Mohammadreza Sediqin,Abhishek Mukherji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GUIDE: a governed multi-agent framework for enterprise guideline documents that automates extraction and artifact generation with provenance, achieving high success and fast turnaround.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise guideline documents are heterogeneous and multimodal; current LLM/VLM systems hallucinate and degrade tables; manual processing is time-consuming (2-3 days per doc).

Method: Six specialized agents (parsing, VLM-driven extraction, consistency checking, evaluation, HITL escalation, persona-tailored artifact synthesis) operate on a shared versioned rule store with schema-validated contracts and provenance tracking; end-to-end governance.

Result: Evaluated on 120 real-world documents: 96% document success; 3,896 rules extracted with 71.4% auto-approved; 812 deployment-ready artifacts produced; turnaround 40–125 minutes per document.

Conclusion: GUIDE provides end-to-end governance for multimodal enterprise guidelines, reducing manual effort and enabling rapid, artifact-ready outcomes with provenance and contract-driven collaboration.

Abstract: Enterprise guideline documents are heterogeneous and multimodal, combining narrative text, complex tables, and embedded images. Existing LLM and VLM systems face hallucinated content, table structure degradation, and lack governed workflows extending beyond extraction to validation and artifact generation. This leaves enterprises to perform this manually, consuming 2-3 days per document. To address this, we introduce GUIDE, a governed multi-agent framework built on a shared versioned rule store with schema-validated inter-agent contracts and end-to-end provenance tracking. Six specialized agents handle parsing, VLM-driven extraction, consistency checking, evaluation, human-in-the-loop (HITL) escalation, and persona-tailored artifact synthesis. Evaluated on 120 real-world enterprise guideline documents, GUIDE achieves 96% document success, extracts 3,896 rules with 71.4% auto-approved, produces 812 deployment-ready artifacts, and reduces turnaround to 40-125 minutes per document.

</details>


### [175] [Who Thinks Best Depends on How Long You Let Them: Budget-Dependent Rankings in LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12150)
*Rodrigo Guedes de Souza,Alison R. Panisson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Budget variation across token budgets reveals non-monotone accuracy, ranking reversals, and substantial model complementarity; budget-aware evaluation is needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Challenging the assumption of stable model rankings under fixed inference budgets; investigate how token budget affects accuracy, model rankings, and cross-domain transfer.

Method: Seven budgets from 64 to 4096 tokens; four models; three reasoning benchmarks; 56,476 inferences; analyze non-monotonicity, cross-model overlap, and rank reversals (McNemar p<0.01); conduct oracle analysis of model complementarity; develop a budget-aware router; assess within-domain vs cross-domain transfer.

Result: Non-monotone behavior in 3–19% of items; cross-model overlap 6–14%; model rankings reverse across budgets (p<0.01); oracle complementarity up to +27.8 percentage points, strongest at constrained budgets; budget-aware router captures 14.1% of the oracle gap; budget features improve within-domain performance by +1.6 to +5.7 percentage points but hurt transfer by -1.2 points.

Conclusion: Budget-conditioned evaluation protocols are warranted; treat budget as a dimension in evaluation and consider ensembling across budgets to leverage model complementarity.

Abstract: Standard evaluation of large language models assumes stable model rankings across inference conditions. We challenge this assumption by varying the token generation budget, i.e., the maximum tokens a model may produce, across seven levels (64--4,096), evaluating four models on three reasoning benchmarks (56,476 inferences). We report four findings: (i) 3--19% of items exhibit non-monotone behavior (accuracy decreasing with more budget), even after controlling for truncation, and this phenomenon is model-specific (cross-model overlap: 6--14%). (ii) Model rankings reverse across budgets on all benchmarks ($p {<} 0.01$, McNemar). (iii) Oracle analysis reveals model complementarity up to $+27.8$pp, most pronounced at constrained budgets. (iv) A budget-aware router captures 14.1% of the oracle gap cross-domain; budget features help within-domain ($+1.6$ to $+5.7$pp) but are domain-specific and hurt transfer ($-1.2$pp). These results argue for budget-conditioned evaluation protocols.

</details>


### [176] [How to Spend Your Oracle Budget: Practical Guidance for Protein Structure Prediction Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12192)
*Aleksandra Kalisz,Jack Simons,Krisztina Sinkovics,Noam Ghenassia,Shikha Surana,Henry Moss,Paul Duckworth*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Benchmarking oracle-guided guidance methods for protein structure prediction under oracle-budget constraints shows no universal winner; O3 excels at very low budgets, while FK-steering and DPO improve with higher budgets; provides practical, budget-aware recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Protein structure predictors with foundation models can be unreliable on certain targets. External or biological oracles can flag/correct failures but are expensive, making budget-aware guidance essential. The goal is to compare guidance methods and provide practical recommendations under oracle-budget constraints.

Method: Benchmark FK-steering, DPO, Best K-of-N sampling, and Optimisation Over Outputs (O3) on protein structure models; extend O3 to work with protein structure predictors; evaluate on two targets (calmodulin 1CLL and E. coli aspartate transcarbamoylase 9EEH) using external oracles.

Result: No single method dominates across budgets or oracles. O3 is most effective at low oracle budgets; FK-steering and DPO improve with larger budgets. The study provides practical recommendations for practitioners operating under real-world oracle-budget constraints.

Conclusion: This work offers the first practical reference for budget-aware oracle guidance in protein structure prediction, informing method choice depending on the available oracle budget.

Abstract: Foundation models for protein structure prediction remain unreliable on certain targets. External oracles can flag and correct these failures, but biological oracles are expensive, making oracle budget a critical constraint. Existing guidance methods, such as FK-steering, DPO, and Best K-of-N sampling, differ in how they spend this budget, yet no systematic comparison exists to guide method selection. To bridge this gap, we benchmark these methods alongside the recently proposed Optimisation Over Outputs (O3), which applies off-the-shelf optimisers within a generative model's latent subspace. We extend the usage of O3 to protein structure prediction models. Overall, our work provides the first practical reference for oracle budget-aware guidance. Our evaluation on two protein targets, calmodulin (1CLL) and E. coli aspartate transcarbamoylase (9EEH), reveals that no single method consistently dominates across all budgets and oracles. Specifically, O3 proves most effective at low oracle budgets, while FK-steering and DPO demonstrate improved performance as the budget increases. We distil these findings into actionable recommendations for practitioners operating under real-world oracle-budget constraints.

</details>


### [177] [An Agentic Workflow for Legacy HPC Modernization: Converting the Two-Electron-Integral Core of GAMESS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12249)
*Yuzhong Shen,Masha Sosonkina,Peng Xu,Mark S. Gordon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic workflow for large-scale modernization of legacy Fortran: three prompt-specialized roles operate under a self-authored, version-controlled spec with human gates and a domain-specific verification oracle; applied to GAMESS Fortran 77 to 2008 conversion across 12 files, with 56,448 lines and 225 subroutines; validated by 51 tests and 612 test runs showing zero chemistry-relevant differences and passing CI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Volume and risk of manual modernization across massive legacy codebases make per-file transformations infeasible. An agentic, scalable workflow with safety guards aims to extend automated modernization to production-scale software, preserving scientific results.

Method: Three prompt-specialized agent roles operate in isolated worktrees under a self-authored, version-controlled specification. Humans provide a small number of gates. A domain-specific verification oracle defines the boundary of safe delegation. Case study on GAMESS: migrating 12 Fortran 77 files (56,448 lines, 225 subroutines) to Fortran 2008. Agents ran across four model generations in 12 source files; bit-for-bit reproducibility used as merge criterion against a canonical energy suite; 51-test validation battery plus 2 additional calculations; 612 test runs.

Result: All 12 source files pass the 51-test validation battery (49 standard GAMESS tests plus 2 additional) and 612 test runs show zero chemistry-relevant differences compared to canonical energies; all files also pass Jenkins CI tests.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that an agentic, model-assisted workflow with a domain-specific verification oracle can scale production-scale modernization of legacy scientific software while preserving exact scientific results and CI-compatible reproducibility; the boundary of safe delegation aligns with the domain oracle’s visibility.

Abstract: Modernizing legacy Fortran is a problem of volume: the transformations are individually routine, but the codebases can be enormous, and across much of computational science the work simply goes undone. We propose an agentic workflow that takes this work on at production scale, and we set out to measure how far such delegation can reach. In this work, three prompt-specialized agent roles operate under a version-controlled specification that the agents themselves authored and revised, while humans hold a small number of gates. The arrangement is kept safe by an exact verification oracle inherited from the domain, and the boundary of safe delegation lies exactly where that oracle stops seeing.
  We apply the proposed workflow in a case study, converting the two-electron-integral routines of GAMESS (General Atomic and Molecular Electronic Structure System), a mature quantum-chemistry package with a 48-year development history, from fixed-form Fortran 77 to free-form Fortran 2008. The scope of this work was twelve source files, 56,448 lines, and 225 subroutines for computing electron repulsion integrals. The agents ran as three Claude Code roles in isolated worktrees, and the work spanned four Claude model generations. Because the GAMESS group ships a standard test suite whose printed energies its user community treats as canonical, we could adopt bit-for-bit reproduction of those energies as the merge criterion, where a deviation in the twelfth decimal place counts as a failure rather than drift. All twelve source files pass a 51-test validation battery comprising the 49 standard GAMESS tests and two additional calculations, and across 612 test runs the number of chemistry-relevant differences is zero, and every file also passes the Jenkins tests that are used for continuous integration.

</details>


### [178] [VAKRA: Evaluating Multi-Hop Reasoning Across APIs and Retrieval Under Tool-Use Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12282)
*Ankita Rajaram Naik,Anupama Murthi,Benjamin Elder,Siyu Huo,Raavi Gupta,Abhinav Jain,Praveen Venkateswaran,Abdulhamid Adebayo,Danish Contractor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VAKRA is a benchmark of 8k executable APIs across 62 domains testing three difficulty levels: diverse API interactions, multi-hop compositional reasoning over APIs, and multi-source reasoning under natural-language tool-use policy constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address gaps where existing benchmarks evaluate API interaction and knowledge retrieval in isolation, by evaluating integrated, live API interactions with reasoning over structured tools and documents in enterprise-like contexts.

Method: Construct and curate 8k executable APIs across 62 domains; define three task settings with increasing difficulty (diverse API interaction styles, multi-hop API reasoning, multi-source pondering with policy constraints); verify correctness by re-executing model-predicted tool calls against live APIs; employ a fixed ReAct harness to isolate model capabilities from agent architecture; evaluate frontier and open-weight models.

Result: Best model achieves ~70.4% on single-hop endpoint-like tasks; drops to 50–51% on compositional APIs; performance degrades by >50% with increased reasoning depth; policy-constrained questions show severe failures (as low as 2.4% on unanswerable queries); trace analysis pinpoints failures to language-mediated reasoning (entity disambiguation, cross-source grounding) rather than tool invocation.

Conclusion: VAKRA reveals substantial gaps in API-grounded reasoning, especially for multi-hop and policy-constrained tasks; current models struggle with language-grounding and cross-source grounding more than tool-use mechanics; code and dataset are publicly released for benchmarking and research progress.

Abstract: Agents deployed in enterprise settings must reason across structured APIs and document collections, yet existing benchmarks evaluate these capabilities in isolation. We introduce VAKRA (e\textbf{V}aluating \textbf{A}PI and \textbf{K}nowledge \textbf{R}etrieval \textbf{A}gents), a benchmark of over $8{,}000$ executable APIs across $62$ domains with tasks spanning three settings of increasing difficulty: diverse API interaction styles, multi-hop reasoning over structured APIs, and multi-source reasoning with natural-language tool-use policy constraints. Correctness is verified by re-executing predicted tool calls against live APIs, accommodating multiple valid paths. Using a fixed ReAct harness to isolate model capabilities from agent architecture, we evaluate frontier and open-weight models and find that even the best model achieves only 70.4\% on single-hop endpoint-style tasks and drops to 50--51\% on compositional APIs; performance degrades by over 50\% as reasoning depth increases, and policy-constrained questions expose severe failures (as low as 2.4\% on unanswerable queries). Trace analysis shows failures concentrate at language-mediated reasoning - entity disambiguation, cross-source grounding, rather than tool invocation mechanics. Code is available https://github.com/IBM/VAKRA. Dataset is available https://huggingface.co/datasets/ibm-research/VAKRA

</details>


### [179] [Constructing Dynamic Master Logic Models as Knowledge Graphs for Complex System Diagnostics Using Retrieval-Augmented Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12304)
*Saman Marandi,Yu-Shu Hu,Mohammad Modarres*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Automates construction of Dynamic Master Logic models as Knowledge Graphs (KG-DML) from system descriptions using Retrieval-Augmented Generation and LLMs; scalable to large, complex systems; validated on a reactor subsystem; enables diagnostic/reliability workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DML offers a hierarchical, functional-to-structural mapping of system behavior but relies on expert interpretation, hindering scalability for complex systems. Automating KG-DML construction aims to improve scalability, consistency, and usability for diagnostics and safety analysis.

Method: Extend automated KG-DML construction across a DML hierarchy using targeted retrieval and RAG with LLMs; preserve functional dependencies and logical relationships; multi-level validation of precision/recall, gate consistency, and structural integrity; apply to the Low-Pressure Coolant Injection system of a decommissioned Boiling Water Reactor and assess repeatability.

Result: Demonstrates consistent reconstruction across repeated runs; KG-DML supports diagnostic reasoning, safety assessment, upward failure propagation, and downward dependency tracing; provides executable functional models from technical documentation for diagnostic and reliability analyses.

Conclusion: Automated KG-DML construction is a viable path to transform technical documentation into executable functional models for diagnostics and reliability in large, complex systems; the framework scales beyond small-scale demonstrations.

Abstract: Dynamic Master Logic (DML) provides a hierarchical framework for representing system behavior by linking functional objectives to underlying structural elements. However, DML construction typically relies on expert interpretation of technical documentation, limiting scalability for complex systems. This study presents a framework for automated construction of DML models from system descriptions and their representation as Knowledge Graphs (KG-DML), using Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Large Language Models as enabling tools. Building on prior work with small-scale systems, the framework extends automated KG-DML construction and evaluation to substantially larger and more complex systems. Model construction proceeds across the DML hierarchy using targeted retrieval while preserving functional dependencies and explicit logical relationships. The resulting KG-DML supports diagnostic reasoning, safety assessment, upward failure propagation, and downward dependency tracing. A multi-level validation methodology evaluates layer-specific precision and recall, logical gate consistency, and overall structural integrity. Application to the Low-Pressure Coolant Injection system of a decommissioned Boiling Water Reactor demonstrates consistent reconstruction across repeated runs. The results show that automated KG-DML construction can transform technical documentation into executable functional models for diagnostic and reliability analysis.

</details>


### [180] [Reproducing and Stress-Testing Two Approaches to LLM Reasoning Reliability: Test-Time Probability Aggregation and Logic-Representation Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08514)
*Minhan Cho,Jimin Kweon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Independent replication of RPC and LCF yields limited cross-domain gains; RPC's advantage over self-consistency is not robust across four new domains, and LCF shows weak, model-dependent effects with inconsistent statistical significance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To independently reproduce and stress-test two LLM-reliability methods (RPC and LCF) whose original evaluations were authored by their developers and lacked public code, to assess generalizability across models and domains.

Method: Reproduce RPC's published-path aggregation; re-implement LCF's projector, contrastive, and intervention pipeline; extend both to four new domains (text-to-SQL, legal extraction, fallacy identification, precedent grading); compare across BIRD budget settings and multiple models; directly probe LCF representations.

Result: RPC replicated on authors’ grid; across four new domains its edge over self-consistency is non-significant (paired p ≥ 0.28); in BIRD, with varying budget K, edge scales with K but largest observed gap (+2.5 accuracy at K=32, p=0.16) reverses to -0.25 with n=200. LCF’s logic-validity direction is present but weak (0.82 separability vs 0.95 semantic-control); its only reported positive effect (Qwen3 ΔProb) is not significant (p=0.56), though it reduces ΔProb on two of the three other models.

Conclusion: Independent replication provides mixed support for cross-domain reliability claims: RPC gains are not robust beyond the original domains; LCF yields small, inconsistent improvements that depend on model and domain. Public release of code and broader, powered evaluations are needed to substantiate generalizable reliability gains.

Abstract: We independently reproduce two recent methods for making large language model (LLM) reasoning more reliable, and stress-test them across domains and models (RPC across four new task domains with Qwen3-8B, LCF across four 7-8B models). The first, RPC, aggregates token probabilities and self-consistency at inference; the second, LCF, trains projectors that split hidden states into "content" and "logic" and edits the logic part toward a valid region. Validating such reliability claims matters because the original evaluations are run by each method's own authors and were never independently reproduced or stress-tested across models and domains, and LCF shipped no public code. We re-run RPC's published-path aggregation and re-implement LCF's projector, contrastive, and intervention pipeline, then extend both to text-to-SQL, legal extraction, fallacy identification, and precedent grading, and probe LCF's representation directly. RPC reproduces the original grid exactly on the authors' released reasoning paths; on four new domains its edge over self-consistency is never significant (ties or small mixed differences, paired p >= 0.28), and on BIRD, the one domain where we vary the budget, the edge grows with K as predicted but its largest gap (+2.5 accuracy at K=32, p=0.16) reverses to -0.25 when we enlarge the sample to n=200. LCF's logic-validity direction is real but weak (0.82 separability at the single best sub-layer versus 0.95 for a semantic-attribute control); its one positive effect (Qwen3 $Δ$Prob) is not significant (p=0.56), while it significantly reduces $Δ$Prob on two of the other three models.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [181] [Adaptation of Generalist Robot Policies with Minimal Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11363)
*Shreyas Kowshik,Sreyas Venkataraman,Leo Wang,Niharika Pant,Max Simchowitz,Aviral Kumar*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MiDAS enables rapid robot policy adaptation from minimal human input by combining offline behavior cloning from a single demonstration with online residual RL, achieving strong performance across LIBERO, RoboCasa, and a bimanual YAM platform, including ~6 hours of online improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address data inefficiency in robot learning by enabling autonomous improvement after minimal human guidance, bridging offline pretraining with online learning under sparse rewards and weak exploration.

Method: MiDAS: offline-to-online RL. First anchor a pre-trained policy (VLA) to the target task via behavior cloning using few demonstrations, then improve with value-based online RL on a residual policy. Evaluated across LIBERO, RoboCasa, and a bimanual YAM setup.

Result: MiDAS attains task success from a single demonstration, substantially outperforming baselines, and generalizes beyond demonstrated conditions; demonstrates robustness improvements and new behaviors after ~6 hours of online interaction.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that reliable robot policy adaptation from a single task demonstration is feasible via minimal-human-guided offline-to-online RL, with MiDAS as an effective general recipe for rapid adaptation.

Abstract: A central goal in robot learning is to move beyond task-specific human data collection toward robots that improve through autonomous interaction. Yet fully autonomous learning remains difficult with current policies: sparse rewards and weak zero-shot exploration make it unlikely that a robot will discover successful behavior from scratch. We study minimal-data adaptation, a regime in which a pre-trained robot policy must learn a new task from as little as one demonstration followed by autonomous online interaction. This setting serves as the closest tractable proxy for fully autonomous improvement, allowing us to study whether minimal human guidance can bootstrap autonomous learning and what algorithmic ingredients make it feasible. We build MiDAS, a simple offline-to-online RL recipe that first anchors a pre-trained VLA to the target task with behavior cloning on single/few demonstrations, then improves it through value-based online RL on a residual policy parameterization. Across LIBERO and RoboCasa, MiDAS recovers strong task performance from as little as one demonstration, substantially outperforming baselines and generalizing beyond demonstrated conditions. We further evaluate MiDAS on a bimanual YAM platform. Starting from a fragile low-success policy obtained from a single demonstration, MiDAS improves its robustness and learns new successful behaviors over ~6 hours of online interaction. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of reliable robot policy adaptation from a single task demonstration.

</details>


### [182] [Top-down Traffic Scenario Generation via Joint Initial-Goal Diffusion and Trajectory Infilling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11407)
*Da Saem Lee,Yash Vardhan Pant,Sebastian Fischmeister*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TrafficDiffuser introduces a top-down framework that jointly models initial and goal states to generate high-level, interpretable traffic scenarios, turning trajectory generation into a simple infilling task and improving realism and compatibility with existing trajectory models on Argoverse 2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce costly real-world data collection for autonomous vehicle simulators by providing scalable, interpretable initial states and a flexible trajectory generation workflow.

Method: A framework that jointly models high-level scenarios defined by initial and goal state pairs (top-down approach). Trajectories are generated via infilling, enabling constraint-based conditioning (trajectory modes) and easy integration with existing trajectory generation models. Evaluation on Argoverse 2 demonstrates robustness and interpretability of the generated scenarios.

Result: TrafficDiffuser yields better initialization outputs than the next-best method, reducing speed distribution distance by 55.3% and off-road rate by 2.8% on Argoverse 2, indicating improved realism and adherence to real-world distributions.

Conclusion: High-level, interpretable initial-goal state generation coupled with infilling facilitates scalable traffic scenario synthesis, improves compatibility with downstream trajectory models, and enhances realism in simulated autonomous vehicle data.

Abstract: Robust traffic simulators are crucial for developing and testing autonomous vehicles to reduce the costly, labor-intensive real-world data collection process and the need for physical presence on the road. However, existing simulators require agents' initial states to generate trajectories, which limits scalability and diversity due to restrictions on the given initial states. While data-driven agent initialization has been widely studied, the generated initial states are not interpretable in terms of why the agents are initialized at those specific locations. Given known initial states, trajectory generation is also a challenging problem, as the model must learn the variability of the destination and how agents should reach it over time. In this paper, we propose TrafficDiffuser, a top-down traffic scenario generation framework that generates high-level traffic scenarios, defined by initial and goal state pairs, by jointly modeling them. The high-level scenario generation makes initial states better interpretable and reduces trajectory generation into as simple as an infilling problem. We demonstrate how the generated high-level traffic scenarios can be used, including constraining based on different trajectory modes and integrating them with existing trajectory generation models. We conduct extensive experiments on the Argoverse 2 motion prediction dataset to evaluate how well the generated outputs capture real-world distributions. In addition to generating goal states, TrafficDiffuser outperforms the next-best approach for agent initialization, reducing speed distribution distance by 55.3% and the off-road rate by 2.8%.

</details>


### [183] [From Self-Normal-Positioning to Omni-Directional Tracking: Real-Time Surface Modeling Enabled Probe Tilt Control for Robotic Ultrasound Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11409)
*Xihan Ma,Haichong Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An omni-directional robotic ultrasound system that integrates RGB-D sensing and local-surface modeling to control probe orientation beyond normal to the surface, enabling non-normal views such as cardiac ultrasound with high angular accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitation of conventional robotic US that assumes normal probe orientation, enabling real-time surface sensing and maintenance of desired orientation for diagnostic views like echocardiography.

Method: Fuse multi-view RGB-D data into a local surface model with a quadratic surface estimate; encode the desired imaging direction relative to surface normal; implement task-space orientation control to track arbitrary probe angles; evaluate on flat surfaces, phantom target-angle recovery, and in-vivo heart view acquisition.

Result: Mean angular tracking error 1.06 ± 0.66 deg; capable of tilting up to 44.39 ± 2.59 deg from the surface normal; acquired the desired heart chamber view in phantom and in-vivo experiments.

Conclusion: The framework enables robust, real-time omni-directional probe orientation in robotic US, improving imaging flexibility and potential diagnostic reliability; suggests broader applicability to non-normal ultrasound orientations.

Abstract: Ultrasound (US) provides real-time, radiation-free imaging, but the image quality depends strongly on how the probe is oriented against the patient body. Robotic US can reduce operator workload and improve acquisition consistency; however, most existing systems focus on normal positioning, where the probe is maintained perpendicular to the local surface. This constraint is inadequate for examinations like echocardiography, where obtaining a diagnostic view requires a non-normal probe angle. Consequently, a clinically useful robotic system must sense the local surface in real-time and preserve the desired probe orientation. Here, we propose an omni-directional probe-orientation control framework that integrates RGB-D perception, local-surface modeling, and task-space orientation control. The surface model fuses multi-view point clouds and provides a quadratic estimate of the local surface. A desired imaging direction is then encoded relative to the normal, enabling the probe to track arbitrary angles. The framework was evaluated through flat-surface tracking, phantom target-angle recovery, and in-vivo tracking of an expert selected view. Results show that the mean angular tracking error was 1.06 +- 0.66 deg. The system recovered a non-normal tilt angle of up to 44.39 +- 2.59 deg relative to the surface normal, and acquired the desired heart chamber view in the phantom and in-vivo experiments.

</details>


### [184] [Locomotion Variability and User Experience in Smart Wheelchair Human-Robot Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11417)
*Sean Kille,Adina M. Panchea,Balint Varga,Sören Hohmann*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Preserving natural movement variability in shared-control assistive robotics yields better user experience without compromising task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human movement variability is structured and task-relevant; eliminating variability may reduce sense of agency and interaction quality; need to explore variability-aware assistive designs.

Method: User study with an intelligent powered wheelchair under three conditions: no assist, conventional variability-reducing assist, and variability-preserving assist ; measure task performance, movement variability, and subjective experience.

Result: Performance similar across conditions; variability-preserving condition improves perceived agency and usefulness; indicates variability can be beneficial for user experience in HRI.

Conclusion: Variability-aware assistance respects embodied movement structure and can enhance autonomy and satisfaction, guiding design of assistive robots away from noise elimination toward supporting natural variability.

Abstract: Human movement is inherently variable, with variability structured according to task relevance: movements are typically more consistent at task-critical points and more flexible elsewhere. In human-robot interaction (HRI), however, model-based assistance strategies commonly assume deterministic human behavior and suppress such variability, potentially altering how interactions are experienced and lowering sense of agency. While movement variability is increasingly recognized as functionally meaningful, its deliberate preservation in assisted interaction, and its consequences for user experience, remain underexplored. In this paper, we empirically investigate how different assistance strategies shape human movement variability, task performance, and subjective interaction experience in a shared control setting. We introduce an autonomy-supportive shared control strategy that preserves users' natural movement structure. This approach is evaluated in a user study in which participants push an intelligent powered wheelchair under three conditions: no assistance, conventional variability-reducing assistance, and variability-preserving assistance. While task-relevant performance remained comparable across assisted modes, preserving natural movement variability led to more favorable interaction experiences. In particular, participants reported significantly higher perceived agency compared to conventional assistance and highest perceived usefulness. These findings suggest that variability-aware assistance can support both performance and user autonomy in physical human-robot collaboration. More broadly, the results highlight the importance of designing assistive robotic systems that respect the embodied structure of human movement rather than treating variability as noise to be neglected or eliminated.

</details>


### [185] [Herding End-to-End Autonomous Driving via Neuro-Symbolic Safety Guards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11451)
*Simón Patiño Idarraga,Erick Silva,Rehana Yasmin,Ali Shoker*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A lightweight neuro-symbolic safety guard sits at the final command interface of a trained driving agent to enforce explicit safety rules with minimal intrusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end agents learn statistical patterns and lack enforcement of hard safety constraints; decision-making is opaque and prone to safety violations.

Method: Attach a lightweight safety guard to the final command interface. Before a command is issued, the guard checks it against explicit safety rules and replaces it with the nearest safe alternative if needed. This intervention is directly executable and traceable to the triggering rule. The guard requires no retraining and has no learned components.

Result: On Fail2Drive and Bench2Drive using the TFv6 driver as a case study, the guard improves Success Rate by 15% and reduces safety-critical collisions by up to 53%, while preserving the original Driving Score.

Conclusion: The proposed plug-and-play safety guard provides enforceable safety constraints without retraining, achieving substantial safety gains with minimal intervention and preserving overall driving performance.

Abstract: Modern end-to-end driving agents can achieve high average performance yet still violate basic traffic rules that a human driver would never miss. The reason is structural: they learn statistical patterns rather than the physical conditions that guarantee safe driving, leaving their decision-making process opaque and safety constraints unenforced. We introduce a neuro-symbolic safety guard, a lightweight module that attaches to the final command interface of an already-trained agent. Immediately before a command reaches the vehicle, it checks the command against explicit safety rules and, only when necessary, replaces it with the nearest safe alternative. Each intervention is directly executable and traceable to the rule that triggered it, while the guard itself requires no retraining and adds no learned component. Evaluated on the long-tail benchmarks Fail2Drive and Bench2Drive using the state-of-the-art TransFuser v6 (TFv6) as a case study, the guard improves Success Rate by 15% and reduces safety-critical collisions by up to 53%, while preserving the original Driving Score.

</details>


### [186] [Koopman Representation of Nonlinear Virtual Environments in Kinesthetic Haptic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11461)
*Yanting Zhou,Jozsef Kövecses,James Richard Forbes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Koopman-based lifting of nonlinear VE dynamics yields a linear-in-observables model that supports closed-loop stability analysis and robust haptic control, demonstrated on a Duffing-oscillator VE with simulations, experiments, and multi-user testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlinear, high-precision haptic VEs are difficult to analyze and control; traditional passivity-based stability methods can be conservative and sensitive to modeling uncertainty. A linear representation of nonlinear dynamics could enable straightforward stability analysis and more robust control.

Method: Represent the nonlinear VE via Koopman operator lifting to a higher-dimensional space of observable functions, where the dynamics are approximately linear. Use a linear combination of lifted states to capture the nonlinearity. Apply closed-loop stability analysis to the overall haptic system and validate with Duffing-oscillator VE through simulations, experiments, and a multi-user study; compare against traditional methods.

Result: The approach provides an effective linear representation of the VE nonlinear dynamics, validated by simulations and experiments on a Duffing oscillator. A multi-user study supports the effectiveness. The Koopman-based CL stability analysis is less conservative than passivity-based methods and shows robustness to modeling uncertainties in the haptic device.

Conclusion: Koopman lifting offers a practical, robust framework for representing nonlinear VEs in haptic systems and enables less-conservative CL stability analysis. The method is demonstrated on a Duffing VE with positive experimental and user-study results, suggesting applicability to more complex nonlinear Haptic-VEs and potential extension to real-time control and uncertainty handling.

Abstract: Rendering haptic feedback with nonlinear virtual environments (VEs) is important in many applications that require highly accurate force feedback. This paper considers the use of the Koopman operator to represent a nonlinear VE interacting with a haptic system. Simulation and experimental results demonstrated that the proposed method provides an effective representation of the nonlinear dynamics of a Duffing-oscillator VE. A multi-user study further confirmed this conclusion. In addition, a closed-loop (CL) stability analysis is performed leveraging the Koopman representation of the nonlinear VE to access stability of the overall haptic system. This alternative way of representing nonlinear VEs enables a convenient CL stability analysis that is less conservative than traditional passivity-based methods. Since a linear combination of all lifted states is used to represent the nonlinearity, such representation is also more robust to uncertainties in the modeling of the haptic device than a traditional nonlinear model.

</details>


### [187] [Keep the Future, Drop the Rollout: RIFT for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11521)
*Chushan Zhang,Jinguang Tong,Xuesong Li,Yikai Wang,Hongdong Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RIFT enables rollout-free future conditioning with near-rollout performance and substantial latency reductions across LIBERO and RoboTwin.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address deployment latency in world action models caused by iterative video rollouts. Determine whether action generation needs the evolving rollout trajectory or only its future representation.

Method: Evaluate four WAMs on LIBERO tasks with closed-loop interventions that perturb the future cache, and analyze Joint and Cosmos-2 performance. Propose RIFT (Rollout-free Imagination via Future Tokens) which uses learned anticipation tokens to construct a complete future K/V cache in one backbone pass while preserving the original future-read interface.

Result: Across LIBERO, RIFT achieves 98.8% success, close to rollout-based Joint, IDM, and LingBot-VA (98.4–98.6%), while reducing action-chunk latency by 68.2%–89.1%. On RoboTwin 2.0, RIFT attains 92.9/92.6% in clean/randomized scenes—the highest among evaluated methods. Using a fixed final-clean K/V cache in Joint/Cosmos-2 yields 1.7–1.9 cm end-effector error and 97.9%–98.2% success, indicating cache consumption can be decoupled from production.

Conclusion: Rollout-free conditioning is viable: models can reuse or construct a complete future K/V cache without iterative rollout during deployment, achieving comparable performance with substantially lower latency.

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) condition robot actions on predicted futures, but iterative video rollout increases deployment latency. We ask whether action generation requires the evolving rollout trajectory or only its future representation. Across four WAMs on all 40 LIBERO tasks, paired closed-loop interventions show that masking or reassigning future-cache values changes execution and reduces success, indicating sensitivity to future values and their assigned positions. For Joint and Cosmos-2, however, replaying one fixed final-clean key/value (K/V) cache nearly preserves unmodified execution, with $1.7$ to $1.9$~cm end-effector average displacement error and $97.9\%$ to $98.2\%$ success. This separates cache consumption from production: these models can reuse a fixed cache but still require iterative rollout to construct it. We therefore propose RIFT (\emph{Rollout-free Imagination via Future Tokens}), which uses learned anticipation tokens to construct a complete future K/V cache in one backbone pass while retaining the original future-read interface. On LIBERO, RIFT achieves $98.8\%$ success, close to rollout-based Joint, IDM, and LingBot-VA at $98.4\%$ to $98.6\%$, while reducing action-chunk latency by $68.2\%$ to $89.1\%$. On RoboTwin~2.0, RIFT reaches $92.9/92.6\%$ on clean/randomized scenes, the highest observed among the evaluated methods. These results support rollout-free future conditioning without iterative video generation at deployment.

</details>


### [188] [RoadWeaver: Large-Scale Lane-Level HD Map Generation from Scratch for Autonomous Driving Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11580)
*Yueyuan Li,Zexi Chen,Weijie Xi,Mingyang Jiang,Songan Zhang,Hanyang Zhuang,Ming Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RoadWeaver is a coarse-to-fine, from-scratch generator that creates large-scale, lane-level HD maps for autonomous driving, improving scalability and alignment over existing maps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous driving simulation needs diverse, scalable lane-level HD maps for long-horizon evaluation. Existing approaches rely on handcrafted or reconstructed real-world maps or produce only local road structures, limiting scalability and completeness.

Method: A coarse-to-fine pipeline: (1) synthesize a global road layout; (2) expand it into a connected road network; (3) construct lane-level geometry with topologically consistent lane connectivity; produces complete HD maps ready for simulators; code and implementation to be released.

Result: Demonstrates high-quality maps: 99.8% reachability, 10.7% dead-end ratio, endpoint alignment error 0.24 m. Outperforms SOTA by 94.4% in endpoint alignment error; generation time 1.39–3.50 s; maps directly deployable in driving simulators.

Conclusion: RoadWeaver enables scalable, from-scratch generation of complete HD maps for long-horizon autonomous driving evaluation, offering fast generation and accurate lane connectivity; code and implementation to be released post-acceptance.

Abstract: Autonomous driving simulation requires diverse and scalable lane-level HD maps to support long-horizon evaluation across complex road networks. Existing approaches either rely on handcrafted or reconstructed real-world maps, which limits scalability, or generate only local road structures rather than complete HD maps. We present RoadWeaver, a coarse-to-fine framework for from-scratch generation of diverse, large-scale HD maps. RoadWeaver first synthesizes a global road layout, expands it into a connected road network, and then constructs lane-level geometry with topologically consistent lane connectivity. Experimental results show that RoadWeaver achieves a 99.8\% reachability, a 10.7\% dead-end ratio, and an endpoint alignment error of 0.24 m. Compared with SOTA generation methods, it reduces endpoint alignment error by 94.4\% while generating complete HD maps in 1.39--3.50 s. The generated maps can be directly deployed in driving simulators, providing scalable simulation environments for future closed-loop evaluation of autonomous driving systems. The training code and an out-of-the-box implementation of RoadWeaver will be released upon acceptance.

</details>


### [189] [Video2Track: From Real-World Interaction Videos to Steerable Adversarial Closed-Track Testing for Automated Driving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11592)
*Mengjie Tian,Xinrui Zhang,Tianyu Li,Peizhi Zhang,Guirong Zhou,Haojie Feng,Junpeng Huang,Qixiang Zhang,Lu Xiong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Video2Track translates real-world driving interactions from videos into steerable, adversarial closed-track tests for automated driving systems, via a two-module pipeline: (1) semantic mapping from videos to a track topology using a vision-language model and retrieval-augmented grounding; (2) dynamic testing generating multi-agent trajectories through a conditional diffusion model, regulated by a Stackelberg-game-based adversarial objective. It enables reproducible yet realistic interactions and configurable risk/interaction styles for ADS validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing closed-track testing often relies on scripted protocols or predefined trajectories, failing to capture the natural complexity of public-road traffic and limiting reproducibility of real-world interactions.

Method: Module 1: scenario semantic mapping — extract structured semantics from driving videos with a vision-language model and ground onto a closed-track topology library via retrieval-augmented generation to identify compatible map segments and interaction anchors. Module 2: dynamic interactive testing — condition on the grounded topology and anchors to generate diverse multi-agent trajectories via a conditional diffusion model, while regulating interaction intensity via a Stackelberg game with a parameterized adversarial objective.

Result: Closed-track experiments show the framework can faithfully reproduce representative real-world interaction scenarios and generate executable scenario variants with controllable risk levels and interaction styles, offering a scalable approach for realistic and steerable ADS validation.

Conclusion: Video2Track provides a scalable, controllable pathway to validate ADS by converting real-world video scenes into executable, adversarial closed-track scenarios, enabling reproducible yet diverse evaluations with tunable risk and interaction styles.

Abstract: Closed-track testing plays a fundamental role in the verification and validation of automated driving systems (ADS), particularly for safety-critical scenarios, by enabling reproducible evaluation under controlled conditions. However, most existing approaches still rely on standardized protocols or predefined trajectories, leading to overly scripted interactions and limited ability to reproduce the natural complexity of public-road traffic. To address this limitation, we propose Video2Track, a framework that transfers real-world interactive driving scenarios from videos into steerable adversarial closed-track testing. The framework consists of two tightly coupled modules. The first is a scenario semantic mapping module, which extracts structured semantics from driving videos using a vision-language model and grounds them onto a closed-track topology library via retrieval-augmented generation, thereby identifying compatible map segments and interaction anchors. The second is a dynamic interactive testing module, which conditions on the grounded topology and anchors to generate diverse multi-agent trajectories through a conditional diffusion model, while regulating interaction intensity via a Stackelberg game with a parameterized adversarial objective. Closed-track experiments demonstrate that the proposed framework can faithfully reproduce representative real-world interaction scenarios and generate executable scenario variants with controllable risk levels and interaction styles, providing a scalable approach for realistic and steerable ADS validation.

</details>


### [190] [IoT-Enabled Autonomous Maritime Navigation in Smart Ports: A Curriculum-Guided Shared Policy Learning Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11597)
*Yuqing Lin,Rangya Zhang,Kum Fai Yuen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A curriculum-guided reinforcement learning framework with a shared recurrent policy enables onboard autonomous navigation for IoT maritime devices in smart ports, combining centralized offline training with full onboard execution to improve reliability, collision avoidance, and generalization in dense traffic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensure safe, scalable navigation for IoT-enabled autonomous vessels in congested waterways under partial observability; address edge constraints and scalable deployment in smart port ecosystems.

Method: Curriculum-guided reinforcement learning with a shared recurrent policy; centralized training offline; onboard execution; edge-level decision-making; simulations across realistic port environments; robust to high-density and unseen scenarios.

Result: Improved navigation reliability and collision avoidance; greater training stability; better generalization to unseen high-density traffic; demonstrates scalability of IoT-edge decision-making in smart ports.

Conclusion: Curriculum-guided shared learning offers a practical solution for deploying IoT-enabled autonomous maritime devices in smart ports, enabling scalable, robust onboard navigation.

Abstract: As smart port infrastructures increasingly rely on autonomous maritime devices enabled by the Internet of Things (IoT), ensuring reliable onboard navigation intelligence has become a critical challenge for safe and scalable operations in congested waterways. This paper investigates onboard autonomous navigation for such IoT devices under partial observability and dense traffic conditions. A curriculum-guided reinforcement learning framework with a shared recurrent policy is developed to enhance temporal reasoning, deployment scalability, and robustness of edge-level decision-making. Centralized training is adopted as an offline design-time strategy, while all navigation actions are executed fully onboard, consistent with IoT edge intelligence paradigms. Extensive simulations in multiple realistic port environments demonstrate that the proposed approach improves navigation reliability, collision avoidance, and training stability compared with standard baseline methods, and generalizes effectively to previously unseen high-density scenarios. The results indicate that curriculum-guided shared learning provides a practical solution for scalable deployment of IoT-enabled autonomous maritime devices in smart port operations.

</details>


### [191] [StellaVLA: In-Context Structured Demonstration for Generalizable Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11671)
*Siyu Xu,Yunke Wang,Zijian Wang,Dihao Zhu,Chenghao Xia,Chengbin Du,Daochang Liu,Tao Huang,Chang Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: StellaVLA enables test-time adaptation for Vision-Language-Action models via a single retrieved, structured demonstration, improving OOD performance without extra data or fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models often fail out-of-distribution; adaptation typically requires costly data collection and fine-tuning. Zero-shot, embodiment-agnostic adaptation is needed to maintain performance across scenes, viewpoints, and objects.

Method: At test time, retrieve a single demonstration and convert raw trajectories into a structured in-context guide (task plan, sub-goal descriptions, verbalized 3D motion). A parallel training objective internalizes reasoning (action-and-language) during training; inference uses only the action expert, preserving real-time control. Demonstrations are transferable across real robots, human hands, or XR demonstrations.

Result: On VLA-Arena, StellaVLA ranks first with an overall score of 0.63 (vs 0.44 and 0.22 for π0.5 and LingBot-VLA). It also leads LIBERO (98.8% average success) and LIBERO-Plus (85.1%). Real-robot benchmarks show successful adaptation using human/robot and XR demos as in-context guidance.

Conclusion: Structured, automated demonstrations as in-context guidance enable OOD adaptation for VLA models, improving transferability across embodiments and reducing annotation needs, while maintaining fast online control.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models can follow instructions and manipulate objects, but their performance often collapses out of distribution (OOD), when the scene, viewpoint, or object differs from training. Adapting to each new situation typically requires collecting more data and fine-tuning. We present StellaVLA, a framework that instead adapts at test time by conditioning on a single retrieved demonstration. The key idea is to move beyond imitating what an expert did and instead convey why: an automated offline pipeline converts each raw trajectory into a structured demonstration, e.g., a task plan, sub-goal descriptions, and verbalized 3D motion, at zero human-annotation cost. Provided as in-context guidance, this structured demonstration lets the policy reason about the task rather than mimic a pixel trajectory, which also makes it transferable across embodiments (real-robot, human-hand, or XR demonstrations). A parallel dual-training design internalizes this reasoning during training through a joint action-and-language objective, while inference uses the action expert alone, preserving real-time, high-frequency control with no added latency. On the VLA-Arena leaderboard(Aug 1, 2026), StellaVLA ranks first with an overall score of 0.63, versus 0.44 and 0.22 for the strong prior models ($π_{0.5}$ and LingBot-VLA), and it further leads on LIBERO with 98.8% average success rate and LIBERO-Plus with 85.1% success rate. Our real-robot benchmark demonstrates that StellaVLA can use both human/robot demos and human-to-robot (XR) demos as in-context structured demonstration to help VLA model adapt to OOD tasks.

</details>


### [192] [ContactIPM: A Structure-Exploiting Interior-Point Solver for Contact-Implicit Trajectory Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11731)
*Yucheng Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A unified primal–dual MPCC solver (ContactIPM) for contact-implicit trajectory optimization using barrier-relaxed interior methods, stagewise elimination, and Riccati recursion, achieving strong speedups over CRISP/IMPACT while maintaining robustness in closed-loop tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MPCCs from contact-implicit trajectory optimization suffer from degeneracy that degrades standard primal–dual solvers. Existing contact-specific methods improve robustness but miss stagewise optimal-control factorization; structure-exploiting solvers don’t handle complementarity. A method combining these capabilities aims to improve robustness and efficiency.

Method: ContactIPM identifies complementary inequality pairs and embeds them via a barrier-coupled elastic interior relaxation. It eliminates slack and dual variables stagewise, and solves the reduced Newton system with a Riccati recursion. A fixed multi-phase MPCC recovery schedule provides four continuation/restart attempts from naive initializations. Termination is determined by the unrelaxed physical complementarity residual.

Result: Compared with two contact-specific MPCC solvers (CRISP, IMPACT) under matched conditions: on four fixed CRISP benchmarks, ContactIPM is 2.17–8.87× faster across 20 repetitions and shows higher success on Push Box/Push-T. Against IMPACT, ContactIPM is 2.96× faster on Push T and 4.91× faster on Cart Transport, but 4.46× slower on Push Box. In 50 closed-loop Push Box rollouts with disturbances, the abstract text provided is cut off, so full results are not stated here.

Conclusion: The paper presents a unified primal–dual ContactIPM that blends contact-specific robustness with structure-exploiting optimal-control techniques, delivering substantial speedups over CRISP/IMPACT and showing robustness in closed-loop tests, though the provided abstract truncates before final robustness results.

Abstract: Contact-implicit trajectory optimization avoids prescribing contact sequences, but yields mathematical programs with complementarity constraints (MPCCs) whose degeneracy challenges conventional primal--dual solvers. Existing contact-specific methods improve robustness to this degeneracy but do not leverage a stagewise optimal-control factorization and primal--dual consistency, while structure-exploiting optimal-control solvers are not designed for complementarity constraints. We show that these capabilities can be combined in a single primal--dual method. ContactIPM identifies complementary inequality pairs, embeds them through a barrier-coupled elastic interior relaxation, eliminates slack and dual variables stagewise, and solves the reduced Newton system using a Riccati recursion. A fixed multi-phase MPCC recovery schedule provides four continuation and restart attempts from naive initializations, while termination is gated by the unrelaxed physical complementarity residual.
  We compare ContactIPM with two contact-specific MPCC solvers, CRISP and IMPACT, using matched benchmark conditions and common post-solve acceptance criteria. On four fixed CRISP benchmark cases, ContactIPM is $2.17$--$8.87\times$ faster over 20 paired timing repetitions per case and achieves higher success on the Push Box and Push-T robustness suites. Against IMPACT, ContactIPM is \(2.96\times\) faster on Push T and \(4.91\times\) faster on Cart Transport, but \(4.46\times\) slower on Push Box. In 50 closed-loop Push Box rollouts spanning model mismatch, measurement noise, initial-pose errors, and state resets,

</details>


### [193] [G0.5: One Autoregressive Stream for Robot Reasoning and Action](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11739)
*Yicheng Liu,Zibin Dong,Baijun Ye,Tianyuan Yuan,Tao Jiang,Anqi Yang,Shicheng Cao,Haonan Liu,Yue Sun,Zihan Guo,Xiao Liu,Dong Ke,Changxun Pan,Chenru Wu,Tailai Cheng,Xiaoshu Ren,Xinlei Zhang,Jianning Cui,Zijie Zhao,Haoyu Zhang,Kaiming Xu,Haodong Yang,Bowen Zhang,Jiahui Niu,Shaoting Zhu,Shiduo Zhang,Hang Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: G0.5 unifies reasoning and action in a single autoregressive VLA model, enabling end-to-end instruction following and action through a cross-embodiment action tokenizer, native chain-of-thought reasoning, and visual memory, achieving strong real-world and simulated results without extra fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the separation between vision-language models and separate action experts by training a single VLA model that reasons and acts end-to-end, leveraging a unified objective.

Method: A single transformer decoder emits reasoning and action tokens; cross-embodiment action tokenizer maps robot actions into a shared vocabulary; chain-of-thought stream interleaves task decomposition, grounding, and action hints with action tokens; visual memory module injects multi-second history via the vision encoder; pretrained on robot data + VQA; evaluation across seven regimes.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art across 7 regimes: real-world fine-tuning on R1lite/R1pro (76.7% vs 53.3% π0.5 and 24.4% GR00T-N1.7), BEHAVIOR Challenge (31.4% vs 26.3% and 26.1%), DROID zero-shot (82.5%), LIBERO (98.9%), RoboTwin 2.0 (93.3%), SimplerEnv-Bridge (87.3%).

Conclusion: End-to-end, single-model VLA with shared weights and memory can carry over pretrained capabilities to physical robots, enabling instruction-following and controllable action granularity and horizons without fine-tuning; data-efficient transfer and robustness to out-of-distribution scenes are observed.

Abstract: The prevailing recipe for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models couples a pretrained VLM with a separately trained flow-matching action expert. This makes the VLM a context encoder rather than a decision-maker. We introduce G0.5, a pretrained autoregressive VLA in which a single transformer decoder emits reasoning and action tokens under a single objective. Three components make this tractable at foundation-model scale: a learnable cross-embodiment action tokenizer that maps heterogeneous robot actions into a shared vocabulary; a native chain-of-thought stream interleaving task decomposition, object grounding, and action hints with action tokens; and a visual memory module that injects multi-second history through the vision encoder. Because reasoning and action share a single set of weights, the pretrained VLM's capabilities carry over to physical behavior: the model follows instructions closely, and prompts directly steer action granularity, task horizon, and out-of-distribution scene handling without further training. Pretrained on a large collection of robot datasets together with VQA samples, G0.5 surpasses state-of-the-art models across 7 independent regimes: real-world fine-tuning on R1lite and R1pro robots (76.7\% vs.\ 53.3\% for $π_{0.5}$ and 24.4\% for GR00T-N1.7), the 2025 BEHAVIOR Challenge on 50 long-horizon household mobile manipulation tasks using a generalist policy (31.4\% vs.\ 26.3\% for $π_{0.5}$ and 26.1\% for the challenge winner), DROID post-training followed by zero-shot transfer to an unseen environment and objects (82.5\%), a language-following Pick-and-Place benchmark, LIBERO (98.9\%), RoboTwin 2.0 (93.3\%), and SimplerEnv-Bridge (87.3\%).

</details>


### [194] [Policy-Induced Hand Priors in Humanoid Dual-Arm Manipulation: Diagnosing and Mitigating Initial-Pose Dependence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11769)
*Chaeyeon Jung,Juyoun Park*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Initial-pose strongly shapes hand selection in vision-language-action policies for humanoid dual-arm manipulation; pose-policy interactions cause varied success, while data augmentation and broader pose coverage improve robustness; a pose-conditioned hand prior exists and a local arm configuration acts as a causal handle on hand choice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prevent misleading aggregate success metrics from hiding pose-specific failures; understand how initial configuration and observation influence hand selection to build more robust VLA policies.

Method: Empirical study across multiple VLA policies and 17 initial configurations. Introduces HandPriorScore, residual hand bias, and target responsiveness to quantify early hand preference. Analyzes initial arm configurations, wrist-camera effects, and policy performance. Examines training data composition, including expanded initial-pose coverage, targeted augmentation around low-performing configurations, and varying exposure to simulated/real data. Compares training configurations to assess pose coverage, simulation ratio, and observation availability.

Result: There are strong initial-pose–policy interactions: the same pose yields substantially different success rates across policies, and a single policy varies significantly across poses. Some initial arm configurations induce or suppress asymmetric hand preference, with effects differing by policy. Wrist-camera observations influence hand selection and task performance. Expanding initial-pose coverage improves robustness; targeted augmentation around a low-performing configuration raises its success rate. Sufficient exposure to the target simulation task helps; the impact of real or auxiliary data depends on pose coverage, simulation ratio, and observation availability.

Conclusion: The work characterizes a pose-conditioned hand prior and identifies a localized initial arm configuration as a causal handle on hand selection, showing how data coverage and training composition affect initial-pose robustness.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies are expected to operate robustly across variations in the robot's initial configuration, yet aggregate task success can conceal pose-specific failures and inappropriate hand selection. This work investigates initial-pose dependence in VLA-based humanoid dual-arm manipulation. We characterize the initial-condition-dependent early hand preference as a policy-induced hand prior and quantify it using HandPriorScore, residual hand bias, and target responsiveness. Evaluations across multiple policies and 17 initial configurations reveal strong initial-pose--policy interactions: the same pose produces substantially different success rates across policies, while a single policy exhibits large performance variation across poses. Specific initial arm configurations can suppress or induce an asymmetric hand preference, with the resulting effect varying in direction and strength across policies. Wrist-camera observations also influence hand selection and task performance. Expanding initial-pose coverage in the training dataset substantially improves robustness, while targeted augmentation around a low-performing configuration increases its success rate. Comparisons across training configurations show that sufficient exposure to the target simulation task is beneficial, whereas the effect of real or auxiliary data depends on pose coverage, simulation ratio, and observation availability. These findings characterize a pose-conditioned hand prior, identify a localized initial arm configuration as a causal handle on hand-selection behavior, and demonstrate how data coverage and training composition affect initial-pose robustness.

</details>


### [195] [Enhancing Visual Domain Robustness in Behaviour Cloning via Saliency-Guided Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11870)
*Zheyu Zhuang,Ruiyu Wang,Nils Ingelhag,Ville Kyrki,Danica Kragic*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces RoboSaGA, a saliency-guided, pixel-level augmentation for vision-based behavior cloning that uses adaptive superimposition to improve robustness to visual domain shifts while preserving in-domain performance; no architecture changes required; code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional augmentations (e.g., Random Crop, Color Jitter) fail under substantial domain shifts (shadows, distractors, backgrounds) in BC. Task-critical semantics and agent–target interactions must be preserved, but existing superimposition methods risk corrupting these. A saliency-guided approach aims to augment only non-critical regions.

Method: RoboSaGA dynamically adjusts augmentation intensity at the pixel level using policy-driven saliency. It aggressively augments task-irrelevant regions while preserving task-critical information, applying superimposition within the BC pipeline without changing model architecture or adding new learning objectives.

Result: Experiments in simulated and real-world settings show preserved in-domain performance with substantially improved robustness to domain shifts, including distractors, background changes, lighting, and shadow variations.

Conclusion: Saliency-guided, pixel-level augmentation within the superimposition family is effective for vision-based BC, enabling stronger generalization under visual shifts without sacrificing in-domain accuracy and with easy integration.

Abstract: In vision-based behavior cloning (BC), conventional image augmentations such as Random Crop and Color Jitter often fall short under substantial visual domain shifts, including changes in shadows, distractors, and backgrounds. Superimposition-based augmentations, which blend in-domain and out-of-domain images, have shown promise for improving generalization in computer vision, but their suitability for BC remains uncertain because task-critical semantics, spatiotemporal relationships, and agent-target interactions must be preserved. To address this, we introduce RoboSaGA, a Saliency-Guided Augmentation method within the superimposition family tailored for vision-based BC. RoboSaGA dynamically adjusts augmentation intensity at the pixel level using policy-driven saliency, enabling aggressive augmentation in task-irrelevant regions while preserving task-critical information. It integrates seamlessly into existing architectures without requiring structural modifications or additional learning objectives. Experiments in both simulated and real-world settings show that RoboSaGA preserves in-domain performance while substantially improving robustness to visual domain shifts, including distractor and background changes, as well as lighting and shadow variations. Code is available at https://github.com/Zheyu-Zhuang/RoboSaGA.

</details>


### [196] [D3D-GEN: Robot-Aware Domain-Grounded Interactive 3D World Generation for Social Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11876)
*Anh Duc Do,Volodymyr Scherbyna,Tai Duc Nguyen,Spaarsh Thakkar,Zhengcheng Shen,Teham Buiyan,Archan Misra,Linh Kästner*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces D3D-GEN, a domain-grounded world generator for embodied AI simulations that combines a domain agent with a retrieval-augmented generation pipeline to produce interactive 3D environments for Isaac Sim and Gazebo without fixed 3D model databases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the realism–simulability trade-off in simulation environments for social navigation, enabling rapid creation of domain-specific, interactive worlds with minimal reliance on fixed assets.

Method: A research agent collects public-domain data to build a persistent domain database; a RAG pipeline queries a semantic database to generate floorplans and object placements, producing fully interactive 3D worlds loadable by Isaac Sim and Gazebo; local web frontend for rapid generation.

Result: Built domain databases for indoor residential, hospital, and office; generated dozens of environments per domain; outputs are loadable simulators; reusable, extensible semantic data sources.

Conclusion: Offers a scalable, domain-grounded, data-driven world generation approach that reduces asset curation effort; potential improvements include evaluation against realism, dynamics, and generalization across domains; integration with other simulators and continuous domain knowledge updates.

Abstract: Training and validation of Embodied AI for social navigation critically depends on realistic simulation environments, yet many current approaches fail to find a balance between realism and simulability. We propose D3D-GEN, a novel world generation system that combines a domain agent with a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline grounded in that domain. Our system enables users to rapidly generate domain-grounded, fully interactive 3D worlds by automating both the collection of domain knowledge and the synthesis of realistic floorplans and object placements, without dependence on any fixed 3D model database. Given a domain description prompt, the research agent collects publicly accessible domain-specific data and constructs a persistent domain database. Using this database, our RAG pipeline generates plausible floorplans and object placements by dynamically querying a user-provided semantic database, which can be easily extended or modified. The output is a fully interactive 3D world loadable by the popular simulators Isaac Sim and Gazebo. With our approach, we have built databases for several common domains (indoor residential, hospital, office) and generated dozens of distinct, plausible simulation environments for each domain.
