We present D3D-GEN with a local web frontend that facilitates rapid, interactive world generation for robot simulation.

</details>


### [197] [Scalable Multi-Agent Maze Traversal with Local Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11895)
*Julian Rau,Jahir Argote-Gerald,Grace McFassel,Genki Miyauchi,Paul Trodden,Roderich Groß*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Distributed multi-agent maze solving in unknown graphs: a leader-follower, sequential-exploration algorithm guarantees completeness, asymptotic makespan optimality w.r.t. full knowledge, and scalable time/space complexity; simulations with up to 625 agents show improved fuel efficiency and advantage over a naive baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explore and navigate complex, maze-like environments (caves, pipes) where graph topology is unknown and communication is limited. Mult-agent coordination is needed to localize and reach an undisclosed goal while avoiding collisions.

Method: Agents enter at a start node and coordinate via local communication using leader-follower links and leader switching. At any time, a single agent executes a single-agent maze solver while others collaborate indirectly. The graph may be cyclic and unknown; the approach is distributed and relies on local information to expand explored area and synchronize goals.

Result: The algorithm is proven complete. Its makespan is asymptotically equivalent (in the number of agents) to an optimal full-knowledge strategy. Time and space complexity are derived. Simulations with up to 625 agents show a decreasing average sum-of-fuels as the agent count grows, and the method outperforms a naive baseline where all agents run the single-agent solver independently.

Conclusion: This work introduces a scalable, provably complete multi-agent navigation framework for unknown, maze-like graphs. By leveraging leader-follower coordination and single-agent exploration, it achieves near-optimal makespan with increasing agents, and reduces resource usage, demonstrating practical viability for large-scale deployments.

Abstract: Cave networks, pipe systems, and similar maze-like environments pose significant challenges for multi-agent navigation in unknown settings with limited communication. We propose a distributed algorithm that enables agents to collectively traverse an unknown, possibly cyclic graph. Agents enter sequentially at a designated start node and are tasked to localize and reach an undisclosed goal while avoiding collisions. They coordinate via local communication using leader-follower relationships and leader switching. At any moment in time, exploration is performed by only one of the agents, which runs a single-agent maze solver. We prove that the algorithm is complete, that its makespan is asymptotically equivalent (in the number of agents) to that of an optimal full-knowledge strategy, and derive its time and space complexity. Simulations with up to $625$ agents show a decreasing average sum-of-fuels as the number of agents increases and demonstrate that the proposed approach outperforms a naïve baseline in which all agents independently execute the single-agent solver.

</details>


### [198] [DaViNCi: A Dataset Towards Outdoor Vision-and-Language Navigation with Continuous Actions and Dynamic Elements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11901)
*Zihao Xie,Pingrui Lai,Yitong Wu,Hua Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: First outdoor VLN dataset with continuous actions and dynamic elements; reveals robustness limits of current methods and underscores importance of action granularity and dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between outdoor VLN benchmarks that rely on fixed discrete graphs and real-world outdoor environments by introducing continuous action control and dynamic elements to enable better sim-to-real transfer.

Method: Construct DaViNCi with six outdoor maps totaling 6,933 trajectories; evaluate agents in both discrete and continuous action settings; analyze effects of action granularity and presence of dynamic elements; provide a public website for dataset access.

Result: Significant drop in success rate (>10%) in discrete environments compared to prior datasets; an even larger drop in continuous settings; findings clarify the impact of action granularity and dynamic elements on VLN performance.

Conclusion: DaViNCi advances outdoor VLN research by providing a realistic, challenging benchmark with continuous control and dynamic factors, encouraging development of more robust, real-world-ready agents.

Abstract: Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) has progressively expanded from indoor to outdoor environments. However, existing outdoor VLN datasets still rely on fixed discrete topological graphs for construction. It fails to align with the rapidly changing real-world outdoor environments and impedes the sim-to-real transfer of VLN agents. To address this limitation, we propose DaViNCi (\textbf{D}yn\textbf{a}mic \textbf{Vi}sion-and-Language \textbf{N}avigation in \textbf{C}ont\textbf{i}nuous Environment), the first outdoor VLN dataset that simultaneously introduces both continuous and dynamic factors. The agent not only moves in the outdoor environment using continuous actions but is also required to handle unpredictable dynamic elements. The dataset encompasses six distinct maps with a total of 6,933 trajectories. Through comprehensive comparative experiments, we find that the success rate on DaViNCi decreased by more than 10\% in discrete environments compared to previous datasets. And there is an even greater decline in continuous settings, demonstrating the challenge of DaViNCi. Furthermore, we clarify the impact of action granularity and dynamic elements. These results demonstrate the practical value of DaViNCi in advancing outdoor VLN toward more realistic environments. The website is https://xzh0312.github.io/DaViNCi/.

</details>


### [199] [Learning Loco-Manipulation From SMPC Demonstrations With Sparse Offline-to-Online RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12063)
*Martin Schuck,Maks Sorokin,Simone Manni,Duy Ta,Angela P. Schoellig,Marco Hutter,Simon Le Cleac'H,Jan Brüdigam*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An SMPC-generated offline dataset enables efficient off-policy RL with sparse rewards to learn loco-manipulation; combining a high-level learned policy with a low-level stability controller yields robust sim-to-real skills across morphologies (Spot with arm, G1 humanoid).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense reward shaping in RL for locomotion/manipulation is a major bottleneck. A scalable, automated data source is needed to drive exploration and enable rapid transfer from simulation to varied real-world morphologies.

Method: Use Sample-based Model Predictive Control (SMPC) in simulation as an automated expert to generate large offline datasets. Train an off-policy RL agent with purely sparse task rewards. Integrate a high-level policy with a low-level dynamic stability controller to align behavior with task objectives, aiming to surpass the teacher. Validate robustness via deployment on different morphologies (arm-equipped Spot, G1 humanoid).

Result: The approach yields learned loco-manipulation policies that, when combined with a low-level controller, achieve true task objectives and surpass the original SMPC teacher. Demonstrated successful sim-to-real transfer across multiple morphologies, indicating strong robustness.

Conclusion: An offline-SMPC-generated data pipeline can dramatically reduce manual reward shaping, enable efficient off-policy RL with sparse rewards, and produce robust loco-manipulation skills transferable across diverse robots.

Abstract: Integrating locomotion and manipulation is essential for robot autonomy, but scaling standard Reinforcement Learning (RL) to complex tasks is severely bottlenecked by the slow, manual process of dense reward shaping. To bypass this limitation, we leverage Sample-based Model Predictive Control (SMPC) entirely in simulation as an automated, rapidly tunable expert to generate massive offline datasets. Because this data solves the fundamental exploration problem, we can train an off-policy RL agent using purely sparse task rewards, drastically reducing the time required to learn new skills and eliminating the need for manual tuning. Integrating this high-level agent with a low-level dynamic stability controller yields more optimal behaviors that strictly align with true task objectives, ultimately allowing the learned policies to surpass the original optimal control teacher. We validate the robustness of this sim-to-real framework by successfully deploying complex loco-manipulation skills across different morphologies, including an arm-equipped Spot quadruped and a G1 humanoid.

</details>


### [200] [HandEdit: A Unified Benchmark for Egocentric Human-to-Robot Dexterous Hand Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12122)
*Zhenjie Yang,Xingyu Jiao,Guopeng Zhong,Shuzhe Yang,Shi Che,Chao Wu,Chenyu Jiang,Dongjie Zhang,Yideng Zhang,Zheng Zhang,Muyun Jiang,Haisheng Su,Shuang Jin,Donghang Zhang,Chao Yang,Li Chen,Hongyang Li,Zuxuan Wu,Yu-Gang Jiang,Xiaosong Jia,Junchi Yan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A large-scale, embodiment-aware image-editing dataset and benchmark (HandEdit) to translate egocentric human hands/arms into 26 robotic URDF configurations, enabling scalable dexterous-learning via 200M edits and URDF-conditioned evaluation across two tracks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between human hand appearance/kinematics and dexterous robot hands; reduce data collection cost for embodiment-aware learning by leveraging abundant egocentric human videos, addressing the missing embodiment priors in general image-editing models.

Method: HandEdit aggregates over 200M editing instances from five source datasets to cover 26 URDFs (13 hand-only, 13 hand-arm). It defines a unified benchmark with two tracks (Hand-only, Hand-Arm) and URDF-conditioned evaluation. The study evaluates 11 image-editing baselines using a multi-dimensional metric suite (generic similarity metrics, VLM-based judgments, and embodiment-aware metrics).

Result: Demonstrates feasibility of large-scale embodiment-aware editing for robotics; a comprehensive benchmark and evaluation of existing baselines reveal strengths and gaps, establishing HandEdit as a new resource for embedding-aware editing and scalable learning from human videos.

Conclusion: HandEdit provides a critical resource at the intersection of image editing and robotics, enabling embodiment-aware editing models and scalable dexterous robotic learning from human data, and advancing generalizable Embodied AI.

Abstract: Robotic manipulation with dexterous hands is a cornerstone of Embodied AI, yet its progress is stifled by the high cost of collecting embodiment-aware teleoperation data. While abundant egocentric videos of human hands offer a scalable alternative, the profound discrepancies in appearance, articulation, and camera viewpoints between human and robotic data raise significant challenges for co-training. Though existing general image-editing models demonstrate strong capabilities, they lack necessary embodiment-specific priors to fully bridge this gap. In this work, we present HandEdit, a unified large-scale embodiment-aware image-editing dataset and benchmark specifically designed to transform human hands and arms into various dexterous robotic embodiments within egocentric frames. HandEdit comprises over 200M editing instances derived from five diverse source datasets, covering 26 distinct URDFs, including 13 hand-only and 13 hand-arm configurations. Alongside the dataset, we establish a unified benchmark protocol with two tracks: Hand-only and Hand-Arm, supporting URDF-conditioned evaluation. We conduct extensive evaluations of 11 representative image-editing baselines using a multi-dimensional metric suite, including generic similarity metrics, VLM-based judgment, and embodiment-aware metrics. HandEdit serves as a critical resource at the intersection of image editing and robotics: it advances embodiment-aware editing models while enabling scalable dexterous robotic learning from abundant human video data, paving the way for more generalizable Embodied AI.

</details>


### [201] [Learning-Based Behavior Planning for Automated Driving: Real-World Integration and Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12198)
*Jean-Pierre Busch,Guido Linden,Jan Bergmann,Lutz Eckstein*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Hybrid ML-based motion planner with an optimization-based safety layer; open-loop validation on real urban data and deployment on a research vehicle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address explainability, trust, and safety assurance for learning-based motion planning in autonomous driving by combining learning with verifiable, deterministic control.

Method: A deep neural network interprets traffic scenes and proposes driving actions; an optimization-based supervision layer validates proposals and enforces drivability/safety constraints; integrates for potential closed-loop operation; evaluated on real-world urban data and deployed on vehicle Karl.

Result: Open-loop evaluations show feasibility; system integration considerations; real-world deployment results reported on Karl; demonstrates potential for stable closed-loop operation, though details are not provided.

Conclusion: Hybrid ML-plus-optimization architecture improves explainability and safety guarantees while retaining learning-based planning capabilities; compelling approach for verifiable autonomous driving systems.

Abstract: Recent research in machine and deep learning has shown the potential of learningbased motion planning approaches to improve the driving behavior of automated vehicles, especially in complex environments. However, their complex nature and lack of transparency can hinder explainability and trustworthiness and complicate safety assurance. Motivated by these challenges, we propose a hybrid planning architecture that combines the advantages of machine learning with the verifiability and the determinism of classical approaches. Specifically, we developed a deep neural network to interpret complex traffic scenes and propose driving behavior, while an optimization-based supervision layer validates this proposal and enforces explicit drivability and safety constraints. We evaluate the learned planner's driving behavior in open-loop studies on real-world urban data, discuss system integration aspects for stable closed-loop operation, and report results from real-world deployment on our research vehicle karl..

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [202] [FarSky: Task-Aware Latent-Space Coupling for Generative Intra-Hour Solar Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11254)
*Yann Fabel,Bijan Nouri,Milon Miah,Niklas Blum,Luis F. Zarzalejo,Julia Kowalski,Robert Pitz-Paal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FarSky is a generative forecasting framework that jointly learns image reconstruction and irradiance estimation via a multi-task autoencoder and uses a latent diffusion model to forecast future latent states conditioned on recent observations, yielding probabilistic irradiance forecasts from all-sky images with improved ramp-event detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deterministic solar forecasts from deep learning underperform in capturing ramp events and uncertainty, limiting grid integration of PV. All-sky imagers offer rich cloud information; a task-aware, probabilistic forecasting framework leveraging generative modeling can improve accuracy and uncertainty quantification.

Method: A multi-task autoencoder learns a shared latent space for reconstructing sky images and estimating irradiance. A latent diffusion model then generates future latent states conditioned on recent observations. Forecasts are decoded from these latent states, with stochastic sampling providing probabilistic predictions. Evaluations use a multi-year ASI dataset from Plataforma Solar de Almería and two independent test sets, compared against persistence, end-to-end state-of-the-art, and other generative approaches.

Result: FarSky achieves the best overall deterministic and probabilistic forecasting performance, with up to an 11 percentage-point improvement in forecast skill and F1-scores for ramp event detection above 60% across tests.

Conclusion: The integration of task-aware latent-space coupling with latent-diffusion-based generative forecasting enhances solar irradiance forecasting from all-sky imagery, delivering sharp probabilistic forecasts and improved ramp-event detection, supporting more reliable PV grid integration.

Abstract: Accurate solar irradiance forecasting is essential for the reliable integration of photovoltaic power into modern electricity grids. All-sky imagers (ASI) provide high-resolution observations of clouds, making them well suited for intra-hour forecasting. Recent deep learning approaches have substantially improved forecast accuracy but are often limited by deterministic predictions and a reduced capability to anticipate ramp events. This work proposes FarSky, a generative forecasting framework that leverages latent-space coupling to learn task-aware representations of sky images. A multi-task autoencoder first learns a shared latent representation for image reconstruction and irradiance estimation. A latent diffusion model then generates future latent states conditioned on recent observations, from which irradiance forecasts are directly decoded. Probabilistic forecasts are inherently obtained through stochastic sampling. The framework is developed using a multi-year ASI dataset acquired at the Plataforma Solar de Almería, Spain, and evaluated on two independent test datasets against persistence, state-of-the-art end-to-end, and generative forecasting approaches. FarSky achieves the best overall deterministic and probabilistic forecasting performance, improving forecast skill by up to 11 percentage points. Furthermore, it substantially improves ramp event detection over existing methods, achieving F1-scores above 60%. These results demonstrate the potential of combining generative models with task-aware latent-space coupling for solar forecasting.

</details>


### [203] [Why AI Detection Fails for Academic Integrity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11256)
*Jonathan A. Karr,Grigorii Khvatskii,Ting Hua,Nitesh V. Chawla*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Detectors misclassify AI-assisted editing as misconduct; policy implications show detector scores are unreliable alone for accusing authors of wrongdoing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether commercial AI detectors can reliably distinguish AI-assisted editing from full AI-generated drafts and whether policy outcomes based on those detectors are valid.

Method: Controlled study of published English abstracts across four domains, comparing 2013–2015 vs 2023–2025; using proxy human/AI labels at tau=0.50; evaluate flag rates for light 'refine abstract only' edits vs unmodified originals; test effect of 'undetectable AI humanization' on evasion; compare legitimate AI-editing vs evasion.

Result: Light edits flagged 64–80% (Pangram/GPTZero); unmodified 2023–2025 originals flagged 9–15%; non-STEM rates higher (p<0.001); longer token density and Academic Word List density predict flags; after humanization, evasion ~99% (<4% flagged; FNR >96%); honest AI editing yields higher sanction risk than evasion via humanization.

Conclusion: Detector scores are insufficient as standalone evidence of misconduct; policy should incorporate additional signals and human review; detector-based sanctions risk mislabeling non-intent or non-STEM content.

Abstract: Institutions use commercial AI detectors for academic integrity, yet detectors cannot distinguish AI editing from full LLM drafts and may treat both as misconduct. In a controlled study of published English abstracts (four domains; 2013 to 2015 vs. 2023 to 2025), we quantify this policy failure under proxy human/AI labels at tau=0.50. Light "refine abstract only" edits, a proxy for guideline-compliant AI assistance, are flagged at 64 to 80% (Pangram/GPTZero). Unmodified 2023 to 2025 originals are flagged at 9 to 15%, with non-STEM rates far above STEM (p<0.001); elevated scores track long-token and Academic Word List density, not authorship intent alone. After Undetectable AI humanization, evasion is near-total: fewer than 4% of AI-labeled rewrites remain flagged (post-humanization detection rate <4%; FNR >96%). Honest AI-editing results in a higher sanction risk than humanizer-assisted evasion. Therefore, detector scores should not serve as standalone misconduct evidence.

</details>


### [204] [Basin: Efficient and Extensible Numerical Optimization in Rust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11279)
*Johan Larsson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Basin is a Rust-based numerical optimization library offering a unified API to formulate and solve optimization problems with a wide range of solvers and built-in constraint support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optimization is central to science and engineering (model fitting, calibration, training, parameter tuning). A single, consistent interface reduces boilerplate and increases accessibility, while robust constraint handling broadens applicability.

Method: Design and presentation of Basin as a Rust library that provides a uniform way to define objectives and constraints and to apply a broad catalog of solvers, enabling first-class constraint support within the optimization workflow.

Result: A unified, multi-solver optimization framework with constraint capabilities in Rust, simplifying formulation and solving of optimization problems across domains.

Conclusion: Basin offers a cohesive, extensible platform for numerical optimization in Rust, enabling researchers and practitioners to pose and solve diverse optimization problems with consistency and constraints integration.

Abstract: Basin is a numerical optimization library for the Rust programming language. Numerical optimization is the task of finding the inputs that minimize a function, and it is a fundamental element across the sciences: fitting a model to data, calibrating a simulation, training a machine learning model, or choosing engineering parameters that minimize cost. Basin gives users a single, consistent way to both state and solve such problems, with a broad catalog of solvers and first-class support for constraints.

</details>


### [205] [Federated Learning for Distributed CNC Tool Wear Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11281)
*Afsana Khan,Morris Stallmann,Marcin Pietrasik,Charis Kouzinopoulos,Anna Wilbik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Federated learning enables tool wear prediction in distributed CNC data; performance approaches centralized training and surpasses local models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-sharing restrictions in CNC environments hinder centralized modeling; FL offers collaborative learning without raw data transfer, improving predictive maintenance across sites.

Method: Simulated distributed clients hold tool trajectory data; train FL models and compare against centralized and local baselines.

Result: Federated learning achieves performance close to centralized learning and significantly better than local client models.

Conclusion: Federated learning is a viable approach for collaborative CNC tool wear prediction in distributed manufacturing; further work needed on robustness to non-IID data and communication efficiency.

Abstract: Tool wear prediction is an important task in CNC machining, where accurate monitoring of tool condition supports product quality and process reliability. Machine learning methods have shown potential for this task, but their use in industrial environments is limited by the distributed nature of machining data and by restrictions on data sharing between machines, sites, or organizations. Federated learning offers a suitable framework for this setting by enabling collaborative model training without transferring raw operational data. This paper investigates federated learning for CNC tool wear prediction. Tool trajectories are distributed across simulated clients to represent a federated learning scenario. The federated models are compared against centralized references and local client baselines. Results show that federated learning achieves performance close to centralized learning and improves significantly over local client models. These findings indicate that federated learning can support collaborative tool wear prediction in distributed CNC manufacturing environments.

</details>


### [206] [Terminal Symmetry as a Decision Resource: Statewise Refinement for Anytime Verified Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11318)
*Yi Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A decision-resource framework (transport-refine-certify) for achieving terminal symmetry in directed sequential construction; introduces method \method with an episode-fixed transported structure, state-restricted rank, residual rank, and an ordinal meet; shows theoretical guarantees and large empirical gains across CAD assembly, Mini-Programs, exact-fill packing, and GRN OOD tasks; demonstrates cross-domain applicability and reuse of the terminal-symmetry signal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sequential construction tasks often reach symmetric terminal states despite history-dependent and directed execution. The goal is to harness terminal symmetry as a reusable decision resource by decomposing decision-making into transport, refinement, and verification, thereby improving accuracy, efficiency, and guarantees across domains.

Method: Proposes a decision-resource view of terminal symmetry: process evidence provides directionality, terminal correspondence transfers structure across equivalent outcomes, realized-state evidence refines decision relevance after transitions, and a fixed verifier certifies execution. This yields a transport--refine--certify decomposition. The instantiation, \method, uses an episode-fixed transported process structure, a state-restricted process rank, a state-dependent residual rank refreshed after accepted transitions, and an ordinal rank meet whose top-k set equals the union of two prefixes. The meet offers a completion guarantee under prefix coverage and attains the tight worst-case verifier-query bound under the corresponding prefix information model; a two-state construction shows a strict post-transition dynamic--static separation.

Result: Empirically, statewise refresh improves anytime AUC by up to 6.77, 21.75, and 8.68 points on CAD assembly, Mini-Programs, and exact-fill packing, respectively. On 1,135 GRN OOD target-removal episodes, the method achieves the lowest mean capped verifier cost at all three scales among GRN and CDGS-style planners. The statewise signal transfers across aggregation and scheduler organizations, indicating the approach’ s generalizability.

Conclusion: Terminal symmetry can be exploited as a reusable decision resource for directed construction. The transport-refine-certify framework and the \method instantiation provide theoretical guarantees, cross-domain performance gains, and transferability of the terminal-symmetry signal across architectures and tasks.

Abstract: Many sequential construction tasks exhibit exact symmetry at completion while their execution remains directed and history-dependent. We develop a decision-resource view of terminal symmetry: process evidence supplies directionality, terminal correspondence transports that structure across equivalent outcomes, realized-state evidence refines its current decision relevance after transitions, and a fixed verifier certifies execution. This decomposition yields transport--refine--certify. \method{} instantiates the principle with an episode-fixed transported process structure, its state-restricted process rank, a state-dependent residual rank refreshed after accepted transitions, and an ordinal rank meet whose top-$k$ set is exactly the union of the two proposal prefixes. The meet provides a completion guarantee under prefix coverage and attains the tight worst-case verifier-query bound under the corresponding prefix information model; a two-state construction predicts a strict post-transition dynamic--static separation. Across CAD assembly, Mini-Programs, and exact-fill packing, statewise refresh improves anytime AUC by up to $6.77$, $21.75$, and $8.68$ points, respectively. On 1,135 target-removal episodes from the official GRN OOD scenes, \method{} attains the lowest mean capped verifier cost at all three scales among the compared GRN and CDGS-style planners. The statewise signal also transfers across aggregation and scheduler organizations. Terminal symmetry thereby becomes a reusable decision resource for directed construction.

</details>


### [207] [Contextual Quality-Diversity Evolutionary Reinforcement Learning for HVAC Control in Tropical Commercial Buildings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11324)
*Tran Le Vu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A contextual quality-diversity RL controller (CQD-ERL) for supervisory HVAC control of a tropical water-cooled chiller plant; maintains a product archive of specialized policies indexed by operating context and a behavior descriptor, updated by an evolutionary operator and SAC gradient updates, with a safety shield; trained on a reduced-order model of Singapore building and evaluated against ASHRAE Guideline 36.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limitations of single-solution policies in dynamic building HVAC contexts; leverage context-conditioned policy diversity and safety constraints to improve robustness and performance across weather/load regimes.

Method: CQD-ERL framework: product archive of policies indexed by data-driven operating context, daily weather/load regime clusters, and a context-invariant behavior descriptor. Updates via gradient-free evolutionary operator and Soft Actor-Critic policy gradient; shared replay buffer; actions pass through a deterministic safety shield; training on a two-tier reduced-order environment simulating latent load, cooling-tower approach, humidity constraints; evaluation with full-year backtest vs ASHRAE 36 baseline.

Result: Evaluated on full annual backtest; demonstrates feasibility and potential performance gains relative to ASHRAE 36 baseline (details not provided in abstract).

Conclusion: Proposes CQD-ERL as a scalable, context-aware supervisory control framework enabling diverse, safety-filtered policies for building HVAC, with benefits in energy-efficiency and adaptability across regimes; combines evolutionary and gradient-based learning with a shared experience buffer.

Abstract: This paper proposes a contextual quality-diversity evolutionary reinforcement-learning controller, CQD-ERL, for the supervisory control of a tropical, water-cooled chiller plant and its associated air side. Rather than converging to a single scalarised policy, the controller maintains a product archive of specialised policies indexed jointly by a data- driven operating context, a cluster of daily weather and load regime, and a context-invariant behaviour descriptor, filled by a gradient-free evolutionary operator and a soft-actor-critic policy-gradient operator that share one replay buffer. Every action is filtered through a deterministic safety shield before execution. The controller is trained on a two-tier reduced-order environment representing the latent load, cooling-tower approach and humidity constraints of a Singapore commercial building, and is evaluated over a full annual backtest against an ASHRAE Guideline 36 baseline.

</details>


### [208] [Long-Horizon Forecasting of Complete Financial Statements with Forma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11327)
*Travis L. Johnson,Jiannan Jiang,Soumyabrata Chaudhuri,Yihao Chen,Lauren Falvey,Donal O'Cofaigh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A transformer-based forecasting framework (Forma) plus a reproducible benchmark (ProForma-20Q) for 78 financial statement items across 1–20 quarters. Forma outperforms baselines, yields coherent forecasts with Gaussian predictive intervals, and enables scenario analysis without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill the gap in long-horizon, complete financial-statement forecasting for valuation (beyond one year) and provide a public, reproducible benchmark.

Method: Forma treats statements as sets of (account, quarter, value) tuples and optimizes a masked-tuple Gaussian likelihood over them. Evaluation uses change-space R^2. The dataset uses anonymized firms with industry codes. Comparisons include classical ML, gradient boosting, a zero-shot time-series foundation model, and frontier LLMs. Outputs include Gaussian predictive intervals and near-identity coherence; exact coherence is recoverable without accuracy loss. The interface supports scenario analysis without retraining, and future revenue pinning improves other items.

Result: Forma beats all competitors; the lead grows with forecasting horizon. Predictive intervals are well-calibrated (do not under-cover). Forecasts nearly satisfy accounting identities; exact coherence can be recovered without significant accuracy cost. The tuple interface enables scenario analysis, and pinning future revenue paths sharpens other line items.

Conclusion: Specialist, tuple-based forecasting can surpass generalist models for long-horizon financial statements; the work provides a reproducible benchmark and a scalable forecasting approach with practical coherence guarantees and scenario-analysis capabilities.

Abstract: Specialist training beats generalist scale when forecasting financial statements. To our knowledge, no prior work jointly forecasts complete financial statements beyond one year, yet in a discounted-cash-flow valuation most firm value sits past that window. We release ProForma-20Q, a reproducible benchmark for forecasting 78 statement line items 1-20 quarters ahead, for anonymized firms, from past statements and an industry code, scored by change-space $R^2$. On it, Forma, a transformer that reads statements as sets of (account, quarter, value) tuples and maximizes a masked-tuple Gaussian likelihood, beats every competitor we field: classical machine learning, chained gradient boosting, a zero-shot time-series foundation model, and frontier large language models. Its lead widens with horizon, where valuation needs accuracy most, and its Gaussian predictive intervals never under-cover. Forma's forecasts nearly satisfy accounting identities; exact coherence is recoverable at no statistically significant accuracy cost. Its tuple interface supports scenario analysis without retraining, and we show that pinning future revenue paths sharpens the rest of the statement.

</details>


### [209] [Weightless Fine-Tuning: Personalizing LLMs via Logit-Space Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11342)
*Bohan Zhang,Anqi Ni,Yixin Wang,Paramveer S. Dhillon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Weightless Fine-Tuning (WFT) is a training-free decoding-time method that approximates the distributional impact of supervised fine-tuning by transporting supervised residuals from an author's training sequence to the current prompt via a cross-prefix transport operator derived from dropout-induced cross-covariance, replacing gradient updates with logit-space corrections; it achieves SFT-like performance on LaMP personalization benchmarks with under 7% of the effective computation and high alignment of logit shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce the cost of personalization and similar tasks that require per-author weight updates, by eliminating weight updates while preserving the distributional effects of supervised fine-tuning.

Method: 1) Compute supervised residuals from the author’s training sequence. 2) Transport these residuals to the current prompt using a cross-prefix transport operator, estimated from dropout-induced cross-covariance. 3) Apply corrections in logit space to influence next-token predictions without updating model weights.

Result: WFT achieves the best average performance across three LaMP personalization benchmarks, matching or surpassing SFT on individual tasks, and outperforming other lightweight baselines. In budget-controlled experiments, WFT approaches SFT performance with under 7% of the effective computation. Logit-level analysis shows cosine similarity of 0.875 between WFT- and SFT-induced logit shifts over 95% of the next-token probability mass, indicating that WFT captures the distributional effect of supervised adaptation without weight updates.

Conclusion: WFT provides an effective, training-free alternative to SFT for personalization, delivering comparable performance with substantially lower compute by applying logit-space, cross-context residual corrections derived from dropout-based transport rather than gradient updates.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is a standard approach for adapting LLMs to a target distribution, but in settings such as personalization, where each author requires separate weight access, optimization, storage, and retraining, its costs become prohibitive. We propose Weightless Fine-Tuning (WFT), a training-free decoding-time method that approximates the distributional effect of SFT without weight updates. WFT computes supervised residuals on an author's training sequence and transports them to the current prompt through a cross-prefix transport operator estimated from dropout-induced cross-covariance. The operator captures how a perturbation at one context propagates to predictions at another, replacing gradient-based parameter updates with logit-space corrections. On three LaMP personalization benchmarks, WFT achieves the best average performance across datasets, matches or exceeds SFT on individual tasks, and outperforms other lightweight baselines on average. In a budget-controlled comparison, WFT approaches SFT performance using less than 7% of the effective computation. Logit-level analysis shows a cosine similarity of 0.875 between the logit shifts induced by WFT and SFT over 95% of the next-token probability mass, suggesting that WFT captures the distributional effect of supervised adaptation without modifying model weights.

</details>


### [210] [Dynamics Models for Offline Hyperparameter Selection in Real-World RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11349)
*Jordan Coblin,Han Wang,Martha White,Adam White*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Offline calibration models can support RL hyperparameter tuning in real-world settings, demonstrated on a municipal water-treatment plant using high-dimensional, non-stationary sensor data to produce realistic long-horizon rollouts and reveal hyperparameter trends; scalability to year-long data and robustness under distribution shift are examined, establishing a proof-of-concept with practical challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address hyperparameter selection for RL in real-world systems where simulators are unavailable and online experimentation is costly; offline dynamics models aim to approximate environment dynamics to enable offline evaluation and tuning.

Method: Evaluate several calibration model approaches (including a k-nearest neighbors model with a Laplacian distance metric) on high-dimensional, non-stationary sensor data for nexting prediction tasks; assess long-horizon rollouts, hyperparameter sensitivity recovery, scalability to year-long datasets, utility for selecting fine-tuning learning rates for pre-trained agents, and robustness under distribution shift.

Result: Calibration models can generate realistic long-horizon rollouts; they recover meaningful hyperparameter sensitivity trends; scalable to year-long datasets; assist in selecting fine-tuning learning rates; exhibit robustness under distribution shift.

Conclusion: Provides a proof of concept that offline dynamics models can support RL deployment in real-world environments, while highlighting practical challenges and avenues for future work.

Abstract: A key obstacle to deploying reinforcement learning in real-world systems is hyperparameter selection, particularly when simulators are unavailable and online experimentation is costly. Prior work has proposed calibration models trained on offline data to approximate environment dynamics and enable offline hyperparameter selection, but these methods have so far been evaluated only in simple simulated settings. In this paper, we present the first application of calibration models in a real-world industrial setting: a municipal water treatment plant. We evaluate several calibration model approaches, including a k-nearest neighbors model with a Laplacian distance metric, on high-dimensional, non-stationary sensor data for nexting prediction tasks. Our results show that these models can generate realistic long-horizon rollouts and recover meaningful hyperparameter sensitivity trends. We further examine how calibration models scale to year-long datasets, how they support the selection of fine-tuning learning rates for pre-trained agents, and how robust they are under distribution shift. Overall, our findings provide a proof of concept for using offline dynamics models to support RL deployment in real-world environments, while highlighting important practical challenges for future work.

</details>


### [211] [Market-Information-Aware Gated-LoRA of Foundation Models for Transferable Day-Ahead Electricity Price Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11359)
*Hang Fan,Wei Wei,Shengwei Mei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A market-information-aware transfer framework for day-ahead electricity price forecasting that adapts Chronos-2 to data-scarce markets via a multi-source market information interface and a source-domain gated low-rank adapter (LoRA), evaluated with leave-one-market-out in four Chinese provinces, achieving notable MAE/RMSE improvements over zero-shot Chronos-2 and vanilla Source-LoRA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electricity price forecasting is hindered by market-specific, volatile dynamics and scarce data in new markets; existing supervised methods rely on market-specific historical data, limiting transferability.

Method: Construct MSMI interface with 7-day price context and variables (pre-clearing supply-demand, reserve, maintenance, generator-capacity, interties). Train a source-domain gated LoRA updating ~1% of parameters without target-market labels; gate scales the frozen source adapter using reserve-tightness and operating-state signals; evaluate with leave-one-market-out; four Chinese provincial markets.

Result: MAE/RMSE improvements: 6.24%/7.99% vs market-info-aware zero-shot Chronos-2; 3.05%/3.52% vs vanilla Source-LoRA. Gains not reproduced by a learned global scalar or random gate initialization; additional improvement over Source-LoRA is limited.

Conclusion: Market-structured inputs and state-dependent gated LoRA offer a practical transfer path for data-scarce electricity markets; the approach leverages cross-market information with minimal target-market labeling.

Abstract: Electricity price forecasting is crucial for market participants but remains difficult because prices are volatile, market-specific, and closely tied to anticipated system conditions. Existing supervised methods depend largely on market-specific historical data, limiting their use in newly established or data-scarce markets. This paper proposes a market-information-aware adaptation framework that transfers the Chronos-2 time-series foundation model to day-ahead electricity price forecasting. It first constructs a multi-source market information (MSMI) interface aligning 7-day price context with pre-clearing supply--demand, reserve, maintenance, generator-capacity, and intertie variables, and then trains a source-domain gated low-rank adapter (LoRA), updating about $1\%$ of model parameters without target-market labels. The gate scales the frozen source adapter according to reserve-tightness and operating-state signals. A leave-one-market-out protocol is adopted for evaluating cross-market transferability. Experiments on four Chinese provincial day-ahead spot markets show that the proposed framework reduces the average MAE/RMSE by $6.24\%/7.99\%$ relative to market-information-aware zero-shot Chronos-2 and by $3.05\%/3.52\%$ relative to vanilla Source-LoRA. Experiments show that the gain is not reproduced by a learned global scalar or by random gate initialization, while the additional improvement over Source-LoRA is limited. These results suggest that market-structured inputs and state-dependent gated LoRA can provide a practical transfer path for data-scarce electricity markets.

</details>


### [212] [Lifecycle-Optimal Tokenization: Vocabulary Size as a Deployment-Regime-Dependent Infrastructure Parameter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11361)
*Rima Mittal,Ankit Gubrani,Satyanarayana Kakollu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Vocabulary size for tokenizers should be treated as a deployment parameter rather than a fixed training-time choice; optimal V depends on serving regime and can differ by up to ~16x between on-device and datacenter deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce total lifecycle cost by aligning vocabulary size with serving workload and hardware characteristics, rather than relying on conventional training-time choices.

Method: Controlled experiments across two GPU families (A10G and A100) covering memory-bound to compute-bound regimes. Measured lifecycle cost C_lifecycle(V) = C_train(V) + λ·C_infer(V,B); evaluated BPB (bits per byte) and unembedding costs across varying V, B, and λ.

Result: Inference-optimal vocabulary V increases with serving batch B (32k at B=1 to 524k at B≥64); at model scales of 1.3–2.3B, quality peaks at V≈65k BPB; lifecycle-optimal V can diverge from training-optimal by up to 16x. Quality remains ~constant across the optimal V range (<2% BPB spread). Practical guidance: on-device (B=1) -> V≈32k; datacenter serving (B≥64, λ≥10) -> V≈131k–262k.

Conclusion: Vocabulary choice is a pure systems optimization with negligible quality penalties within the measured range; deployment-aware vocabulary design yields substantial cost savings and capacity-planning guidance.

Abstract: Tokenizer vocabulary size is a foundational design choice in large language model (LLM) infrastructure, yet it is typically fixed at training time based on convention rather than deployment analysis. We show that the cost-optimal vocabulary is not a constant but a function of the serving regime. We formalize total deployment cost as $C_{lifecycle}(V) = C_{train}(V) + λ\cdot C_{infer}(V, B)$, where $λ$ is inference volume and $B$ is the serving batch size. Through controlled experiments on two GPU families spanning the memory-bound to compute-bound regimes (A10G, ridge $\approx$ 117 FLOP/byte; A100, ridge $\approx$ 183 FLOP/byte), we demonstrate: (1) the inference-optimal vocabulary shifts 16x with serving batch, from 32k at $B=1$ to 524k at $B=64+$, driven by amortization of the $V \times d$ unembedding matrix read; (2) at 1.3-2.3B model scale, quality (bits per byte, BPB) is optimized at $V=65$k, confirming scale-dependent vocabulary preference; (3) the lifecycle-optimal vocabulary diverges from training-optimal by up to 16x for production deployments. Quality is approximately invariant across the optimal range ($<$2% BPB spread), making vocabulary a pure systems optimization with no quality penalty in the measured range. Our results provide actionable capacity planning guidance: on-device deployments ($B=1$) should use $V \approx 32$k; datacenter serving ($B \geq 64$, $λ\geq 10$) should use $V \approx 131$-262k.

</details>


### [213] [PAIR: Pairwise-Aware Inclusion Reweighting for Adaptive Rollout Allocation in RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11368)
*Pixel Nomand,Elena Voss,Marcus Hale,Sofia Reyes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PAIR introduces a pairwise, graph-based reweighting scheme to estimate the complete candidate-pair gradient in RLVR, achieving an unbiased estimator, better accuracy, and substantial token savings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The unclipped leave-one-out gradient is not a sum of independent point contributions but a second-order U-statistic over rollout pairs; adaptive endpoints make pair terms observable differently, causing bias with standard pointwise allocators.

Method: Define a contrast graph with rollout prefixes as vertices and pair-gradient terms as edges. Use a prefix-only predictor for correctness and remaining cost; solve a convex design to set positive continuation probabilities under an expected suffix-token budget; weight each edge by the inverse of its logged joint inclusion probability. Assumes conditionally independent on-policy rollouts and an unclipped objective, yielding a design-unbiased estimator for the complete candidate-pair gradient.

Result: In compute-matched RLVR runs on Qwen3-1.7B/4B, PAIR improves average accuracy by +1.2 and +1.4 over the strongest pointwise allocator and uses about 51–52% fewer generated tokens than full-group GRPO.

Conclusion: Pairwise inclusion reweighting provides an unbiased, complete-pair gradient estimator in RLVR, with empirical accuracy gains and token-efficiency backed by a bias audit showing unweighted adaptive selection is biased but pair-inclusion correction recovers the target at matched cost.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) spends most of its compute generating groups of long reasoning trajectories. Recent allocators reduce this cost by assigning budgets to prompts, rollouts, or tokens according to a pointwise notion of difficulty or utility. We identify a statistical mismatch: the unclipped leave-one-out group-relative score gradient is not a sum of independent point contributions, but a second-order U-statistic over pairs of rollouts. Completing one rollout therefore reveals contrast with every other completed rollout, and adaptive endpoint selection changes which pair terms are observable. We introduce PAIR (Pairwise-Aware Inclusion Reweighting), which treats short rollout prefixes as vertices and pair-gradient terms as edges of a contrast graph. A prefix-only predictor estimates correctness and remaining token cost; a convex design chooses positive continuation probabilities under an expected suffix-token budget; and each edge induced by completed vertices is inverse-weighted by its logged joint inclusion probability. Under conditionally independent on-policy rollouts and an unclipped, unstandardized objective, the resulting estimator is design-unbiased for the complete candidate-pair gradient. Across compute-matched RLVR runs on Qwen3-1.7B/4B, PAIR improves average accuracy by +1.2 and +1.4 over the strongest pointwise allocator while using 51% and 52% fewer generated tokens than full-group GRPO. A frozen-population estimator audit confirms that unweighted adaptive selection is biased, whereas pair-inclusion correction recovers the complete-pair target at matched suffix cost.

</details>


### [214] [Towards an approach to multivariate outlier detection for District Heating System data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11375)
*Rajko Turudija,Dušan Stojiljković,Milan Zdravković,Marko Ignjatović*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multivariate outlier detection for district heating energy data using PCA, Isolation Forest, and Hotelling's T-squared; final ensemble uses concordance among these methods; Z-score baseline; aims to reveal irregular operation and reduce gas usage/CO2 emissions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve detection of irregular plant operation to reduce gas consumption and CO2 emissions in district heating systems. Incorporate ambient temperature as external variable and account for domain-specific issue that zero transmitted energy does not imply off-grid operation.

Method: Compared five methods: Z-score (univariate benchmark), Mahalanobis distances, PCA, Isolation Forest, and Hotelling's T-squared, with ambient temperature as a covariate. Domain constraint: zero energy points are not treated as off-grid. Results from all methods discussed with domain experts; final approach adopts an ensemble method defined by agreement among PCA, Isolation Forest, and Hotelling's T-squared to label outliers.

Result: PCA, Isolation Forest, and Hotelling's T-squared produced relevant, actionable outlier indications; the ensemble derived from concordance among these three methods was adopted as the final detection strategy.

Conclusion: An ensemble approach based on agreement among PCA, Isolation Forest, and Hotelling's T-squared improves multivariate outlier detection in district heating energy data and aligns with operational objectives to reduce energy waste and emissions; further validation and broader application are recommended.

Abstract: In this paper, we test different methods for multivariate detection of outliers in the data of transmitted heat energy in the selected substation of local District Heating System, by also considering outside ambient temperature, namely Z-score (univariate, as a benchmark), Mahalanobis distances, Principal Component Analysis (PCA), Isolation Forest and Hotelling's T-squared test. The overall research aims at uncovering irregular plant operation, with a wider objective of identifying the opportunities for reducing the consumption of gas in central heating plants as well as the CO2 emission. The proposed approach considers specific domain circumstances, such as irrelevance of zero transmit-ted energy timepoints as indication of off-grid plant. The outcomes of the different methods are discussed with domain experts. It was concluded that PCA, Isolation Forest and Hotelling method provide relevant results. Finally, we adopt the ensemble method (selection based on the agreement of all three methods on the detected outliers) as the final approach.

</details>


### [215] [Reoptimization Algorithms for Contextual Bandits with Knapsack Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11383)
*Zhen Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UCB-based algorithm for Contextual Bandits with Knapsack achieving O((ln T)^3 / T) regret via re-optimization, improving over prior O(1/√T).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address online assignment under resource constraints to maximize revenue while learning reward structure.

Method: Extend UCB with re-optimization; treat rewards as linear in features with noise; model capacities; use online decision making to balance exploration and resource usage.

Result: Regret bound of O((ln T)^3 / T).

Conclusion: Re-optimization yields significantly improved regret bounds for this setting; potential for practical deployment with finite types; suggests further improvements and extensions.

Abstract: We study new algorithms for Contextual Bandits with Knapsack. In these problems, there are finitely many types of customers, products, and resources. Each product is made from a fixed combination of resources, and resources have finite capacity. A decision maker must assign each arriving customer one out of a set of multiple possible products. Every assignment of a customer to a product will generate a random reward, which equals an unknown linear function of customer and product features, plus a noise term. The objective is to jointly learn the mean reward function, and to make online assignments to minimize the expected revenue loss relative to an optimal policy that knows the reward function. We propose a natural and simple extension of the Upper-Confidence-Bound (UCB) family of algorithms and apply re-optimization techniques. We show that by taking advantage of re-optimization, our algorithm achieves an average regret of $O(\frac{(\ln T)^3}{T})$ where $T$ is the horizon length. Our bound significantly reduces the $O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}})$ bound in the literature for closely related dynamic-pricing problems that are based on re-optimization.

</details>


### [216] [Mechanism Design for Generative Engines: From Exploitation toward Win-Win Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11390)
*Chen Xu,Zitian Guo,Chenyan Xiong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes VCR, a verifiable-content rewards mechanism, to counter GEO-driven citation wars by aligning creator incentives with trustworthy attribution; models supplier-platform interaction as a repeated Stackelberg game with partial monitoring; shows significant net defense-utility gains and win–win outcomes in experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rising GEO-driven citation optimization creates citation wars that threaten answer quality and trust. Need mechanisms that reward verifiable, checkable content rather than solely punishing rewrites.

Method: Formulates supplier--platform interaction as a repeated Stackelberg game with partial monitoring. Conducts local best-response analysis to identify inert stationary outcomes. Proposes VCR (verifiable-content rewards) that credits checkable factual substance in rewrites, not just penalizing suspicious ones. Evaluates on three benchmarks.

Result: VCR achieves the largest Net defense-utility score, outperforming the strongest baseline by about 12.1 percentage points on average. Demonstrates a win–win outcome under empirical equivalence criteria.

Conclusion: Aligning creator incentives with verifiable content via rewards can mitigate citation wars, preserve answer trustworthiness, and yield improved platform defense outcomes.

Abstract: Generative engines are reshaping the web ecosystem by making citations a key mechanism for allocating attention, attribution, and downstream value. This creates a strategic tension: content providers are incentivized to optimize for model citation, while platforms must preserve answer quality and trustworthy attribution. We show that this tension can escalate into citation wars. In repeated simulations, state-of-the-art generative engine optimization (GEO) attacks adapt to conventional defenses by producing citation-seeking rewrites that degrade document quality and introduce unsupported claims. To study this problem, we formulate the supplier--platform interaction as a repeated Stackelberg game with partial monitoring. A local best-response analysis identifies when citation competition approaches an inert stationary outcome. Motivated by this finding, we propose a platform--creator mechanism called VCR based on verifiable-content rewards. Rather than only penalizing suspicious rewrites, the platform also credits rewrites that surface checkable factual substance, aligning creator incentives with answer trustworthiness. Experiments on three benchmarks show that VCR consistently achieves the largest Net defense-utility score, outperforming the strongest baseline by an average of 12.1 percentage points, and produces a win--win outcome under our empirical equivalence criterion.

</details>


### [217] [Unmasking Toxic Mimicry in Medical Offline Reinforcement Learning for ICU Sepsis Management via Counterfactual Clinical Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11410)
*Hangqi Ren,Junyi Liao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Counterfactual clinical audits reveal that offline RL can exhibit Toxic Mimicry in ICU decision-making; using MIMIC-III data and SSC-guided perturbations, MedDT reduces vasopressors as lactate rises (misaligned with guidelines) while HCT-RL maintains physiologically consistent responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional evaluation metrics (MSE, FQE) measure imitation accuracy but miss safety failures like Toxic Mimicry; there is a need for evaluation frameworks that test clinical safety and robustness of offline RL in critical care.

Method: Introduce Counterfactual Clinical Audit (CCA) framework. Stress-test RL agents on MIMIC-III data with physiological perturbations anchored in Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines. Audit a Medical Decision Transformer (MedDT) and a Historical Causal Transformer (HCT-RL) that uses Causal Action Shielding, propensity-based weighting, and Conservative Q-Learning.

Result: CCA uncovers that MedDT paradoxically reduces vasopressor dosage as lactate rises, contradicting resuscitation guidelines, whereas HCT-RL yields physiologically consistent responses. Indicates a systemic mismatch between statistical fit and clinical safety.

Conclusion: Counterfactual audits are a necessary evaluation standard for medical RL to detect safety-relevant failures not captured by imitation metrics and to guide safer deployment.

Abstract: Offline reinforcement learning (RL) offers considerable promise for optimizing ICU treatment decisions, yet standard evaluation metrics Mean Squared Error (MSE) and Fitted Q-Evaluation (FQE) assess only behavioral imitation and cannot detect Toxic Mimicry, a failure mode in which agents replicate harmful patterns such as treatment withdrawal during comfort-care transitions. Using the MIMIC-III database, we propose the Counterfactual Clinical Audit (CCA) framework, which stress-tests RL agents through physiological perturbations anchored in Surviving Sepsis Campaign (SSC) guidelines. We audit a Medical Decision Transformer (MedDT) and a Historical Causal Transformer (HCT-RL), the latter employing Causal Action Shielding, propensity-based importance weighting, and Conservative Q-Learning. CCA reveals that MedDT paradoxically reduces vasopressor dosage as lactate escalates, contradicting resuscitation guidelines, while HCT-RL maintains physiologically consistent responses. These findings expose a systemic misalignment between statistical fit and clinical safety, supporting counterfactual audits as a necessary evaluation standard for medical RL.

</details>


### [218] [Diffusion-Based Data-Driven Assortment Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11419)
*Junyi Liao,Xiaohui Jiang,Zhengwei Tong,Ethan X. Fang,Vahid Tarokh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A model-agnostic, diffusion-based framework for assortment optimization that generatively searches the space of binary assortment vectors with a reward-guided reverse diffusion process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the sensitivity of parametric choice models (like MNL) to misspecification and their limited ability to capture complex customer behavior; seek a scalable, robust, and flexible approach for combinatorial optimization in revenue management.

Method: Represent assortments as binary vectors and perform stochastic search via a learned reverse diffusion process. Use a reward-guided mechanism to bias local transitions using estimated revenue, balancing exploration and exploitation. Avoid explicit combinatorial enumeration and enable diverse, high-quality solutions.

Result: Empirically, the method consistently identifies high-quality assortments, remains robust under model misspecification, and scales to high-dimensional settings. It tends to recover near-optimal solutions and produces diverse high-performing assortments.

Conclusion: Generative diffusion provides a scalable, robust paradigm for combinatorial optimization in data-driven decision-making, offering flexibility beyond a single deterministic solution.

Abstract: Assortment optimization is a fundamental problem in revenue management, typically addressed using parametric choice models such as the multinomial logit (MNL) and its variants. While these models enable tractable formulations, their performance is sensitive to model misspecification and often struggles to capture complex customer behavior. In this paper, we propose a model-agnostic framework for assortment optimization based on guided discrete diffusion. We represent assortments as binary vectors and perform stochastic search via a learned reverse diffusion process, avoiding explicit combinatorial enumeration. To incorporate decision objectives, we introduce a reward-guided mechanism that biases local transitions using estimates of expected revenue. This allows the method to effectively balance exploration and exploitation during generation. Empirically, we show that the proposed approach consistently identifies high-quality assortments and remains robust under model misspecification, often recovering near-optimal solutions in high-dimensional settings. Moreover, the generative nature of diffusion enables the production of diverse high-performing assortments, offering flexibility beyond a single deterministic solution. These results highlight the potential of generative modeling as a scalable and robust paradigm for combinatorial optimization in data-driven decision-making.

</details>


### [219] [Analysis of Federated Aggregation under Model Poisoning and Backdoor Attacks: A Reconstructed Cross-Dataset and Cross-Architecture Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11423)
*Soumya Mazumdar,Vineet Kumar Rakesh,Tapas Samanta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Descriptive analysis of federated aggregation robustness using a seeded evaluation matrix; highlights performance rankings, metric audit findings, and provenance limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable robust, reproducible comparisons of federated aggregation methods across threat models, datasets, and architectures, and audit metric implementations and data provenance to avoid misleading conclusions.

Method: Reconstructed a 500-cell seed-1 evaluation matrix across five aggregation methods, five datasets, five architectures, and four conditions (clean, sign-flipping, Gaussian, BadNets); identified successful and repaired runs; performed cross-method comparisons (Trimmed Mean, Krum); restricted analyses to 21 task pairs with complete logs; audited BadNets metric implementation (TTL R interpretation) and FedPARETO scaffold for potential discrepancies between predictive summaries and aggregation updates; noted that the canonical matrix has a single seed per cell and incomplete lineage.

Result: Trimmed Mean yields highest clean macro-mean accuracy (76.02%) and lowest mean within-task rank (1.70); Krum achieves highest recorded accuracy under sign-flipping and Gaussian; results persist when analyses are limited to the subset with complete logs; audit reveals BadNets metric effectively measures Triggered Target-Label Rate rather than a conventional attack success rate; FedPARETO audit identifies a pathway where predictive summaries may misrepresent uncorrupted local models when corrupted updates are applied; single seed per cell and incomplete attack lineage; thus findings are descriptive within the recorded configurations and not universal claims or statistical estimates.

Conclusion: Interpret results as descriptive comparisons confined to the recorded configurations and seeds, not universal robustness estimates or statistical generalizations.

Abstract: Robust comparisons of federated aggregation methods require joint consideration of predictive performance, threat definitions, metric semantics, and execution provenance. A 500-cell seed-1 evaluation matrix was reconstructed across five aggregation methods, five datasets, five architectures, and four recorded conditions: clean, sign-flipping, Gaussian, and BadNets. Successful execution logs were identified for 454 original runs and 36 repaired or rerun executions, whereas 10 clean SVHN cells were supported by summary-only provenance. Trimmed Mean achieved the highest clean macro-mean accuracy (76.02%) and the lowest mean within-task rank (1.70). Krum attained the highest recorded accuracy under both sign-flipping and Gaussian configurations. These relative rankings remained unchanged when analysis was restricted to 21 task pairs for which original successful logs were available for every method-condition combination. Audit of the supplied BadNets metric implementation established that every test input is triggered prior to target-label counting; consequently, the retained metric represents Triggered Target-Label Rate (TTLR) rather than a conventional target-excluding attack success rate. An audit of the supplied FedPARETO scaffold further identified a pathway in which predictive summaries may characterize an uncorrupted local model while the aggregation weight is applied to a separately corrupted update, introducing a potential discrepancy between reported predictive outcomes and the updates used for aggregation. The canonical matrix contains a single identified seed for each cell, and exact attack and configuration lineage is incomplete. Accordingly, the findings should be interpreted as descriptive comparisons within the recorded configurations and not as statistical estimates or universal claims regarding robustness.

</details>


### [220] [Click2Poly: A VLM for vector mapping buildings and walls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11424)
*Nicolas Girard,Jawher Ben Abdallah,Arno Gobbin,Liuyun Duan,Sacha Lepretre*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Click2Poly extends the Florence-2 Vision Language Model to support interactive editing of building and wall vectors via user clicks, integrated as a QGIS plugin to accelerate manual quality control in a production setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cartographic vector extraction remains labor-intensive; automatic methods still require QA and complex fixes. A human-in-the-loop editor can dramatically speed up vector corrections.

Method: Extends the Florence-2 Vision Language Model to respond to user clicks by editing the target vector layers directly; implemented as a QGIS plugin for real-world production environments.

Result: In practice, speeds up manual editing of building and wall vector layers, enabling rapid, iterative corrections within GIS workflows.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that human-in-the-loop AI, leveraging VLM-based interactive editing, can substantially accelerate geospatial vector QC in production pipelines.

Abstract: Accurate vector mapping of buildings and walls is critical for geospatial applications but remains a labor-intensive process. While recent deep learning methods have improved automatic extraction, in order to meet cartographic standards they always require a human to perform quality control and fix complex cases in the extraction. We present Click2Poly, a human-in-the-loop AI assistant designed to speed up this manual step. Extending the Florence-2 Vision Language Model (VLM), Click2Poly responds to user clicks by editing the building or wall vector layer directly. Implemented as a QGIS plugin, Click2Poly speeds up the manual editing of building and wall vector layers in a real-world production environment.

</details>


### [221] [Three Tokens Force Exponential Feature Rank in Nonnegative Kernel Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11427)
*Vicente Opazo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Exponential expressivity gap between full attention and kernel (sketch) attention in sequence processing; lower bounds show kernel attention requires exponential features for 3-token sequences; dense softmax avoids this with m-dimensional scores; results extend to position-dependent and causal settings; transcript lower bounds for finite-alphabet sketch models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess the limits of kernel-based attention and sketch models in sequence processing, and establish lower bounds contrasting them with full attention.

Method: Analysis of Min-IP over Boolean inputs; demonstration that rank-one normalized kernel attention solves length-2 sequences exactly; proving that any single normalized nonnegative kernel-attention head solving all 3-token sequences with error < 1/2 requires 2^{Ω(m)} features; comparison with dense softmax using m-dimensional scores; extension to position-dependent maps and causal final query; asymptotic behavior as context grows; transcript lower bound for deterministic multihead, multilayer sketch models with finite alphabets.

Result: Proves an exponential separation: kernel attention requires exponential feature counts in m to handle even modest context; full dense softmax with fixed dimensional scores suffices; lower bounds persist under various model extensions; establishes transcript-based lower bounds for finite-alphabet sketch networks.

Conclusion: Kernel attention has provable inefficiency relative to full attention for growing context, with tight-ish lower bounds; dense softmax remains more expressive in these regimes; for finite-alphabet sketch models, there are linear-in-number-of-answers and log-alphabet-size lower bounds, illustrating fundamental limits of such architectures.

Abstract: Full attention exposes every token pair, whereas kernel attention compresses a sequence into a fixed-dimensional sketch. We show that this distinction becomes exponential at the first context length containing two competing candidates. On Min-IP over Boolean inputs, rank-one normalized kernel attention solves every sequence of length at most two exactly. In contrast, any single normalized nonnegative kernel-attention head that succeeds on all three-token sequences with error strictly below $1/2$ requires $2^{Ω(m)}$ features, even with arbitrary finite-dimensional tokenwise values and an arbitrary query-dependent affine readout. Dense softmax solves the same task with $m$-dimensional scores and constant temperature. The conclusion survives position-dependent token maps and a causal final query. As context length grows, the lower bound approaches the exact $2^m$-feature realization. Separately, for deterministic multihead, multilayer sketch models whose cross-token channels have finite alphabets, we prove a transcript lower bound linear in the number of independent answers and logarithmic in their alphabet size.

</details>


### [222] [AutoGrable: What Is a Good Graph for a Table?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11431)
*Tamara Cucumides,Floris Geerts*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AutoGrable provides a training-free, greedy method to construct graphs from tabular data for GNNs, using 1-WL color-refinement partitions of rows to evaluate candidate graphs and selecting column subsets (or declining) to maximize label-alignment with occupancy regularization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph neural networks require an explicit graph, which is absent in tables; current approaches rely on manual graph design or costly training on many candidate graphs. A training-free, scalable criterion is needed to automatically construct useful graphs from relational data.

Method: Represent each row as a node; for minimal table-to-graph abstractions, a 1-WL (color-refinement) partition of rows captures the graph structure. For incidence constructions, edges are determined by a subset of columns; auto-grable scores each candidate subset by a label-alignment risk (held-out risk of a predictor constant on graph blocks) plus an occupancy penalty to discourage sparse blocks. The score requires no graph training or GNN; a greedy search over column subsets yields a grable for single tables and for foreign-key schemas.

Result: Empirical results show the scoring rule efficiently prunes a large fraction of candidate graphs without losing the best option; AutoGrable recovers the true label-generating columns on controlled tasks; it outperforms fixed, random, and task-aware constructors under a fixed predictor; and it is the only method among those compared that can abstain from constructing a graph when it does not help.

Conclusion: AutoGrable is a scalable, model-free approach that enables effective graph construction for tabular data, compatible with GNNs, and can identify when a graph is unnecessary; it applies to both standalone tables and foreign-key schemas.

Abstract: Graph learning presupposes a graph, and tables and relational databases do not come with one. Applying a GNN to them requires deciding which entities become nodes, which of them to connect, and through which relations---a decision made by hand, by schema heuristics, or by training a model on every candidate graph and keeping the best. We give a criterion that requires no trained graph model. In the minimal table-to-graph abstraction each row is a node, so a message-passing GNN, bounded by 1-WL, sees a construction only as a partition of the rows into colour-refinement classes: a construction is good for a task when that partition separates rows with different labels and does not split rows that share one. AutoGrable turns this criterion into a construction procedure. For incidence constructions the partition is fixed by the selected columns, so building a graph reduces to choosing them, and we score a candidate subset by a label-alignment risk: the held-out risk of the best predictor constant on its blocks, penalised by an occupancy term measuring how thinly the blocks are populated. The score materialises no graph and trains no GNN, so AutoGrable can search the space of subsets greedily and cheaply, and returns the resulting grable for single tables and for foreign-key schemas alike. Our experiments show that over a space of candidate graphs the score discards a large fraction while retaining the best; that AutoGrable recovers the columns that generate the label on controlled tasks and outperforms fixed, random, and task-aware constructors on real tasks under a fixed predictor; and that it is the only method compared that can decline to build a graph when none helps.

</details>


### [223] [Variational Parameter Calibration with Physics-Aware Latent-Space Surrogates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11435)
*Qiyao Zhou,Xujia Zhu,Pierre Joli,Yu Cong,Sibo Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physics-aware autoencoder surrogate enables end-to-end differentiable forward and variational parameter estimation for parametric dynamical systems, improving calibration accuracy and robustness under noisy/partial observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current surrogates struggle to support end-to-end differentiable coupling with variational parameter estimation; there is a need for models that preserve parameter-relevant information in latent space and are differentiable to enable joint optimization.

Method: Propose a physics-aware neural-network latent-space framework with an autoencoder-based surrogate. Train offline with observable supervision to ensure latent variables encode parameter-related information. The forward map goes from physical parameters to flow fields via the latent representation. The online inverse problem is solved in the parameter space using the surrogate-induced observation operator. Evaluate on two CFD benchmarks under realistic measurement conditions (noisy, low-resolution, masked, block-wise data).

Result: Reconstruction accuracy alone is insufficient for robust inverse modeling; end-to-end differentiability and physics-awareness are essential for effective variational calibration. Latent-space analysis shows observable supervision improves case-level separability and temporal organization. Under noisy/partial observations, the framework reduces calibration error and variability compared with standard surrogates, demonstrating robustness and practical utility.

Conclusion: A differentiable, physics-informed latent-space surrogate with observable supervision enhances parameter calibration for parametric dynamical systems, particularly CFD, and is robust to realistic measurement imperfections; end-to-end differentiable training aligns forward prediction with variational estimation.
