Abstract: Forward and inverse modeling of parametric dynamical systems requires surrogate models that are not only accurate for state prediction, but also informative for parameter calibration. However, a systematic end-to-end differentiable formulation for coupling deep-learning-based reduced-order surrogates with variational parameter estimation remains underdeveloped. In this work, we introduce a physics-aware neural-network-based latent-space framework for reduced-order forward modeling and variational parameter estimation. The proposed autoencoder-based approach yields a differentiable surrogate that maps physical parameters to predicted flow fields through a latent representation. The observable supervision is used during offline training to encourage the latent variables to retain information correlated with system parameters, while the online inverse problem is solved in the parameter space through the surrogate-induced observation operator. The method is evaluated on two computational-fluid-dynamics benchmarks. The results show that reconstruction accuracy alone is insufficient for inverse modeling, owing to the lack of end-to-end differentiability or physics awareness for variational parameter calibration. Quantitative latent-space analysis further shows that observable supervision improves case-level separability and temporal organization of latent representations. Experiments with realistic measurement settings, including noisy, low-resolution, randomly masked, and block-wise partial observations, demonstrate the robustness of the proposed framework and show that it generally reduces calibration error and variability compared with the standard surrogate models.

</details>


### [224] [XGBoost "is all you need": the case of forecasting transmitted heat energy in District Heating Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11446)
*Milan Zdravković*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: XGBoost outperforms LSTM for DHS heat energy forecasting, with lower computational cost and environmental impact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify scenarios where conventional machine learning can surpass deep learning in time series forecasting and to quantify the associated computational and environmental benefits within District Heating Systems (DHS).

Method: A real-world DHS dataset is used to compare XGBoost and LSTM in forecasting. The study analyzes forecast accuracy across data availability intervals, examines error distributions to explain performance differences, and evaluates computational cost and carbon footprint implications.

Result: XGBoost consistently outperforms LSTM in this forecasting task. LSTM exhibits larger errors in periods with limited data. Conventional ML approaches offer reduced computational demands, leading to cost savings and a smaller carbon footprint for data analysis in energy systems.

Conclusion: For this DHS forecasting task, conventional machine learning (XGBoost) is preferable due to superior accuracy and efficiency, with implications for selecting ML models in energy data analytics to minimize cost and environmental impact.

Abstract: This paper presents a comparative study of two distinct approaches, XGBoost and Long-Short Term Memory (LSTM), for forecasting transmitted heat energy in District Heating Systems (DHS). The objective is to explore scenarios in which conventional ML algorithms demonstrate better performance over deep learning networks in time series forecasting and the associated benefits in terms of computational cost and environmental impact. The study focuses on a real-world DHS dataset. Through experimentation and analysis, it is demonstrated that XGBoost consistently outperforms LSTM in this specific forecasting task. The difference is explained by the error distribution illustrating that LSTM makes more significant errors in the intervals of less data availability. The reduced computational demands of conventional ML approaches not only result in cost savings but also minimize the carbon footprint associated with data analysis tasks in energy systems.

</details>


### [225] [PAC-Bayes Beyond Parameter Space: Behavioral Equivalence, Z-Information, and Exact Complexity Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11465)
*Vasant G. Honavar,Satish Kumar Keshri,Neil Ashtekar,Zehao Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Exact structural decomposition of PAC-Bayes complexity via a behavior map; introduces Z-information; separates behavior-selection from realization-level uncertainty; variational characterization; fiber geometry yields invariances; predictive behavior is the natural complexity object.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Over-parameterized models have many configurations yielding identical predictive behavior, but classical PAC-Bayes KL conflates uncertainty over behavior with variation among behaviorally equivalent realizations. A decomposition aligning complexity with predictive behavior is sought.

Method: Define a measurable behavior map; apply measure disintegration to split priors/posteriors into a distribution over predictive behaviors and conditional distributions over fibers; derive an exact KL decomposition into a behavior-selection term and an expected conditional KL within fibers; define Z-information as the negative of the fiber-level KL; provide a variational characterization of the behavior-selection term; analyze symmetry and fiber-preserving perturbations.

Result: KL divergence decomposes exactly into two terms: a behavior-selection term and an average conditional KL within fibers. Z-information quantifies the gap to the predictive-behavior complexity. The minimal KL for fixed behavior distribution is achieved by a canonical fiber-symmetrized representative. Structural properties (symmetry, invariances) arise from the behavior-map/fiber structure.

Conclusion: Predictive behavior should be the fundamental object for PAC-Bayes complexity; the framework delivers a principled, exact decomposition, clarifying how geometry and invariance properties emerge and guiding analysis of generalization in over-parameterized systems.

Abstract: PAC-Bayes theory provides generalization guarantees by controlling the Kullback--Leibler (KL) divergence between posterior and prior distributions over a chosen hypothesis representation. However, predictive risk depends only on the predictive behavior induced by a hypothesis, not on the particular internal realization that implements that behavior. In over-parameterized systems, many distinct configurations induce identical predictive behavior, yet the classical PAC-Bayes KL divergence does not distinguish uncertainty over predictive behavior from variation among behaviorally equivalent realizations.
  We show that this distinction induces an exact structural decomposition of classical PAC-Bayes complexity. We formalize behavioral equivalence through a measurable behavior map and use measure disintegration to decompose probability measures on the configuration space into a distribution over predictive behaviors and conditional distributions over behavioral fibers. This yields an exact decomposition of the classical PAC-Bayes KL divergence into a behavior-selection term and a realization-level term given by an expected conditional KL within fibers.
  We define Z-information as the negative of this realization-level contribution: the exact gap between the KL divergence and the complexity of uncertainty over predictive behavior alone. We further show that the behavior-selection term admits an exact variational characterization: it is the minimum KL divergence among all posteriors inducing the same distribution over predictive behaviors, attained by a canonical fiber-symmetrized representative.
  Finally, we show that symmetry, behavior-preserving directions, fiber geometry, and invariance under fiber-preserving perturbations arise naturally from the same behavior-map structure. Together, these results identify predictive behavior as the natural object of PAC-Bayes complexity.

</details>


### [226] [Dual-Primal Graph VAEs for Noisy Label Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11473)
*Patrick Stinson,Nikolaus Kriegeskorte*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Graph VAE with GAT-based encoder/decoder for crowdsourcing; learns ground-truth latent labels unsupervised; achieves SOTA; can incorporate side information to boost performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcomes limitations of Bayesian or supervised methods requiring strong generative models or pseudo-labels; seeks unsupervised ground-truth inference using graph structure.

Method: Graph VAE framework: encoder and decoder use Graph Attention Network message passing on crowdsourcing adjacency graph and its dual; ground-truth labels treated as latent variables; no separate classifier trained; graph augmented to include side information from neural nets.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on crowdsourcing benchmarks; augmentation with side information substantially boosts test-time accuracy.

Conclusion: Graph-based VAEs with latent ground-truth variables offer a robust, scalable approach for noisy label aggregation; side-information integration enhances performance and demonstrates the model's generality.

Abstract: Inferring the ground-truth from noisy crowdsourced labels is an important theoretical and practical problem. Neural network-based methods offer an alternative to classical Bayesian models which require specifying a family of generative models used for inference. However, current models either still rely on fairly simple generative models for inference or require pseudo-labels or synthetic data to train the aggregate classifier. We propose a graph VAE architecture in which the decoder and encoder use GAT-based message passing on the adjacency graph of a crowdsourced dataset and its dual, respectively. The ground-truth labels are treated as latent variables, enabling unsupervised representation learning without needing to train a separate classifier. We show our model achieves state of the art performance on crowdsourcing benchmarks. We then demonstrate the generality of our approach by showing how the original crowdsourcing graph can be augmented to incorporate side information such as representations from neural network classifiers trained on the noisy labels to substantially boost their classification performance at test time.

</details>


### [227] [Convergence Guarantees of Gradient Descent for Neural Networks via Generalized Lipschitz Smoothness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11479)
*Siqiao Mu,Diego Klabjan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gradient descent converges for general feedforward nets of any width/depth under mild Lipschitz-smooth activation and loss; introduces a generalized Lipschitz smoothness that persists through depth; shows gradient norm vanishes at rate O(1/T^{1/L}) for L-layer nets, given parameter norms stay controlled.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide convergence guarantees for gradient descent on deep networks without restrictive initialization or dataset assumptions, using mild smoothness properties of activations and losses.

Method: Derive a generalized Lipschitz smoothness bound that captures how gradient changes scale with parameter updates through repeated compositions; establish a descent lemma under small learning rates relative to parameter norms; assume parameter norms do not grow too fast; leverage this to bound decrease of the loss per iteration and ensure gradient norm convergence.

Result: Proves that the minimum squared gradient norm converges to zero in T iterations with rate O(1/T^{1/L}) for an L-layer network, for activation functions that are Lipschitz smooth/continuous/linearly bounded and loss functions that are Lipschitz smooth in outputs (e.g., mean-squared error).

Conclusion: Shows that for deep, wide networks, gradient descent converges under mild smoothness assumptions without special initializations; the depth L appears in the convergence rate, highlighting how deeper architectures slow convergence under the proposed bound; lays groundwork for descent under generalized smoothness through network compositions.

Abstract: We establish convergence guarantees of gradient descent for general feedforward neural networks of arbitrary width or depth, with no special requirements on the initialization or dataset. We only assume that the activation functions are Lipschitz smooth, Lipschitz continuous, and linearly bounded--- properties that hold for linear, tanh, softplus, and sigmoid activation functions. For the loss function, we require that it is Lipschitz smooth in the model outputs, which is true for mean-squared error. The key theoretical insight is that the Lipschitz properties of the activation functions are partially preserved even through repeated compositions, leading to a novel generalized Lipschitz smoothness condition where the change in gradient is upper bounded by the change in the parameter space, multiplied by polynomial terms of the parameter norms at both endpoints. This type of condition holds for both the model function and the loss function, enabling a descent lemma where the loss decreases as long as the learning rate is small enough with respect to the parameter norms. By ensuring that the parameter norms do not grow too quickly to infinity, we prove that the minimum squared gradient norm converges to zero in $T$ iterations at rate $O(1/T^{1/L})$ for an $L$-layer neural network.

</details>


### [228] [Defending against Model Extraction for GNNs with Model Reprogramming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11495)
*Yan Wen,Zhenyi Wang,Heng Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GraphRP repurposes model reprogramming into a structure-aware gating mechanism for GNNs, creating a dynamic structural firewall via learnable topological prototypes to degrade model extraction attacks while preserving benign accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the Euclidean bias of graph defenses and the inadequacy of passive watermarking by providing a topology-aware, real-time protective mechanism for GNNs in MLaaS against model extraction attacks.

Method: Introduce a Structure-Aware Gating Mechanism guided by learnable topological prototypes. This dynamic gate modulates the model's decision boundary to preserve fidelity on benign queries on the training manifold while increasing Fisher Information along perturbation directions for adversarial queries. Provide theoretical analysis under bounded loss, optimal attacker, and local second-order approximation to derive a lower bound on the attacker's estimation error as a function of structural sensitivity.

Result: Empirical evaluation on hard-label and soft-label model extraction attacks shows that GraphRP significantly impairs attack efficacy while maintaining benign utility.

Conclusion: GraphRP offers a proactive, topology-aware defense that mitigates model extraction risk in graph-based models by leveraging structure-aware reprogramming, with theoretical guarantees and strong empirical performance.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) serve as the backbone for high-stakes applications in Machine-Learning-as-a-Service (MLaaS). Still, their black-box deployment exposes them to Model Extraction (ME) attacks, in which adversaries steal intellectual property by querying APIs. Existing defenses suffer from a critical ''Euclidean bias'': they transfer image-based strategies (e.g., random noise) to graphs, ignoring the complex topological dependencies between nodes, which often results in severe utility degradation. Passive methods like watermarking also fail to prevent theft in real time. To bridge this gap, we propose GraphRP (Graph Reprogramming Protection), a proactive defense framework that repurposes Model Reprogramming for security. Unlike static perturbations, GraphRP introduces a Structure-Aware Gating Mechanism driven by learnable topological prototypes. This creates a dynamic ''structural firewall'' that selectively modulates the model's decision boundary: it preserves fidelity for benign queries residing on the training manifold, while maximizing the Fisher Information along the perturbation direction for adversarial queries. Under standard assumptions (bounded loss, optimal attacker, and local second-order approximation), we prove a lower bound on the attacker's estimation error that increases with the structural sensitivity of the reprogramming noise. Extensive experiments on both hard-label and soft-label ME attacks demonstrate that GraphRP significantly degrades attack effectiveness while preserving benign utility.

</details>


### [229] [HyperFix: Combinatorial Nonlinear Correction for Task Vector Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11499)
*Hyo Seo Kim,Ren Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HyperFix uses a lightweight hypernetwork to predict nonlinear, subset-conditioned corrections for merging task vectors, enabling multi-subset merging without per-subset tuning and outperforming scalar-linear methods while reducing tuning cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Merging task vectors typically requires retraining or subset-specific tuning. Existing methods rely on scalar or linear adjustments for a fixed subset, which limits flexibility and incurs repeated tuning for different subset selections. There is a need for a method that handles arbitrary subset combinations efficiently without per-subset optimization.

Method: Introduce HyperFix, a lightweight hypernetwork that outputs nonlinear corrections in weight space conditioned on the subset of task vectors to merge. It is trained once on singleton, pair, and triple subsets from a task bank and generalizes to larger subsets. The authors provide a local perturbation analysis to bound residual nonlinear corrections and motivate learning them from small updates.

Result: Empirical evaluation across diverse benchmarks shows HyperFix outperforms existing task vector merging methods and reduces tuning cost by avoiding per-subset optimization.

Conclusion: HyperFix offers scalable, more accurate merging of task vectors across varying subsets by modeling nonlinear corrections, with theoretical motivation from local perturbation analysis and practical gains in tuning efficiency.

Abstract: Task vectors enable model merging without joint retraining. In practice, the subset of task vectors to be merged may vary, but many existing methods use scalar tuning for a particular subset, requiring repeated tuning across subsets and restricting task vector merging to linear rescaling. We therefore formulate merging across varying task subsets as a combinatorial correction problem and introduce HyperFix, a lightweight hypernetwork that predicts subset-conditioned nonlinear corrections in weight space. Trained once on singleton, pair, and triple subsets from a task bank, HyperFix generalizes to larger subsets without per-subset optimization. Our local perturbation analysis bounds the residual correction beyond linear merging and motivates learning it from small task updates. Experiments across diverse benchmarks show that HyperFix outperforms existing task vector merging methods while reducing tuning cost.

</details>


### [230] [RelShap: Relationally Consistent Shapley Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11508)
*Seungeun Lee,Joao Fonseca,Julia Stoyanovich*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RelShap extends SHAP to relational data by enforcing relational constraints and provenance in background sampling and coalition evaluation, enabling faithful, faster Shapley explanations; estimator-agnostic and compatible with Kernel SHAP, Monte Carlo, and Leverage SHAP; FD-induced equivalence classes reduce runtime without altering Shapley values.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Shapley explanations on flattened relational data ignore structural constraints, leading to potentially misleading attributions due to feature interdependencies and data provenance issues.

Method: Incorporates relational constraints and data provenance into background data and coalition evaluation; remains estimator-agnostic and compatible with Kernel SHAP, Monte Carlo, and Leverage SHAP; leverages functional dependencies to define coalition equivalence classes, enabling pruning and faster computation; provides a combinatorial characterization of expected speedup.

Result: Empirical evaluations across datasets, models, and estimators show RelShap yields more faithful explanations and correctly identifies dominant features in controlled settings where baselines (e.g., Conditional SHAP, ManifoldShap) fail; code released at the provided GitHub URL.

Conclusion: RelShap enables faithful, scalable Shapley-based explanations for relational data by enforcing relational validity without altering sampling/weighting schemes, thereby improving interpretability in relational pipelines.

Abstract: Machine learning pipelines commonly flatten relational data into single-table representations, discarding structural constraints. Widely used Shapley value-based feature attributions then rely on feature independence, evaluating the model on combinations that could never arise in the underlying data, producing misleading explanations. We propose RelShap, a framework that incorporates relational constraints and data provenance into Shapley value computation, restricting both background data and coalition evaluation to relationally valid configurations. The framework is estimator-agnostic and composes with Kernel SHAP, Monte Carlo, and Leverage SHAP without altering their sampling or weighting properties. Functional dependencies further induce equivalence classes over feature coalitions, which RelShap exploits to reduce runtime without changing Shapley values; we provide a combinatorial characterization of the expected speedup. Experiments across multiple datasets, models, and estimators show that RelShap produces explanations that are more faithful to the data-generating process, correctly identifying the dominant feature in controlled settings where existing methods, including Conditional SHAP and ManifoldShap, do not. Our code is available at: https://github.com/duneag2/relshap.

</details>


### [231] [Let it Cook: Learning to Wait in Sequential Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11511)
*Christopher Watson,Arjun Krishna,Dinesh Jayaraman,Rajeev Alur*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Learn to wait: a reinforcement learning approach to reduce sensing and decision-making frequency by training a waiting policy that allows deliberate pauses, achieving significant waiting (often >50% of task duration) without sacrificing performance; validated on 4 discrete-state and 3 continuous-state tasks and capable of adapting pre-trained policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In sequential decision tasks, constant sensing/acting can be wasteful during periods where the environment evolves passively. A waiting policy can conserve resources by delaying sensing and action, potentially enabling attention reallocation to other tasks.

Method: Formulate learning to wait as reinforcement learning with lexicographically ordered objectives. Train a waiting policy that chooses when to senses again and how long to wait, forgoing sensing for a fixed number of timesteps. Evaluate on 4 discrete-state household tasks and 3 continuous-state environments, and show ability to adapt pre-trained policies to wait where appropriate.

Result: The approach learns waiting behaviors across tasks, achieving significant waiting (sometimes over 50% of task duration). It also demonstrates adaptation of pre-trained policies to incorporate waiting without performance loss.

Conclusion: Lexicographic RL can effectively learn waiting policies that reduce sensing/decision costs while maintaining task performance, with waiting being task-dependent and compatible with reusing existing policies.

Abstract: In sequential decision making, an agent typically observes its environment and acts at every timestep. However, such active participation may not always be necessary; tasks such as brewing coffee include periods that are served equally well by letting the environment evolve without constant monitoring and control. During such periods, the agent could simply wait to conserve its resources, or redirect its attention to another task. We capitalize on these opportunities by training a "waiting policy" that decides where and how long to wait. This involves forgoing sensing to commit to a wait action, representing a deliberate pause for a set number of timesteps. We formalize "learning to wait" as minimizing the frequency of sensing and decision making without sacrificing task performance (e.g., the total amount of time to complete a task). To train a waiting policy, we propose an approach that employs reinforcement learning with lexicographically ordered objectives. In experiments across 4 discrete-state household tasks and 3 continuous-state environments, we show that our approach successfully learns waiting behaviors, and can adapt pre-trained policies to wait where appropriate. While different tasks permit different amounts of waiting without sacrificing task performance, our approach consistently finds solutions with significant waiting, sometimes waiting for over 50 percent of the task duration.

</details>


### [232] [FLARE++: Low-rank attention with dynamic attention routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11519)
*Vedant Puri,Yongjie Jessica Zhang,Levent Burak Kara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dynamic low-rank attention (FLARE++) uses input-conditioned routing queries built via an extra encode pass to replace fixed latent queries, preserving linear O(NM) cost and improving accuracy over FLARE; scalable with multi-GPU shard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quadratic cost of full self-attention on irregular PDE surrogates; fixed latent queries in FLARE limit expressiveness; seek input-dependent routing with preserved efficiency.

Method: Reuse FLARE encoder to generate routing queries from N tokens into M input-conditioned queries using one extra encode call; these queries guide compression/redistribution; operates with standard SDPA; maintains explicit low-rank factorization; multi-GPU context-parallel with token sharding.

Result: Competitive on standard PDE surrogate benchmarks; ~24% average improvement over fixed-query FLARE; +2.3 points average accuracy on Long Range Arena.

Conclusion: FLARE++ achieves dynamic routing with explicit low-rank factorization, preserving linear complexity and enabling input-aware token routing; demonstrates practical multi-GPU scalability and improved accuracy over FLARE.

Abstract: Full self-attention is a strong token mixer for PDE surrogates on irregular domains, but its quadratic cost limits its use on high-resolution problems. Efficient latent-attention models such as the Fast Low-rank Attention Routing Engine (FLARE) avoid that cost by routing all N tokens through M << N learned latent queries, but those queries are parameters: once trained, the same learned query templates serve every input. We remove this restriction with FLARE++, a low-rank attention architecture with dynamic token routing. FLARE++ reuses FLARE's own encoder to build its routing queries: learned latent seeds drive one extra encode call that gathers the N input tokens into M input-conditioned queries, and those queries then determine how the same tokens are compressed and redistributed. This preserves FLARE's explicit low-rank factorization and linear O(NM) complexity, and expresses the complete routing operation with standard scaled dot-product attention (SDPA) calls alone. We also provide a multi-GPU context-parallel implementation that shards input tokens across devices without ever gathering the full token sequence on one of them. FLARE++ is competitive across a set of standard PDE surrogate benchmarks, improving on fixed-query FLARE by 24% on average, and it gains 2.3 points of average accuracy on Long Range Arena.

</details>


### [233] [Hierarchical Federated Transfer Learning in Digital Twin-Based Vehicular Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11532)
*Qasim Zia,Saide Zhu,Haoxin Wang,Zafar Iqbal,Yingshu Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hierarchical federated transfer learning framework (HFTL) for DT-VANETs that clusters vehicles by type to address data heterogeneity and sparsity, with cloud-server update and intra-cluster transfer learning algorithms, plus a data-quality scoring mechanism to defend against malicious nodes; experiments validate improved global model accuracy and efficiency on real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning in DT-VANETs preserves privacy but suffers from data heterogeneity and sparsity across vehicles, leading to suboptimal global models. There is a need to enhance cross-vehicle generalization while mitigating adversarial data and communication costs.

Method: 1) Cluster vehicles by type and adopt a hierarchical FL framework. 2) Propose two algorithms: one for cloud-server model updates and one for intra-cluster federated transfer learning. 3) Introduce a data quality score-based mechanism to filter or downweight data from potentially malicious vehicles. 4) Validate via experiments on real-world DT-VANET datasets using multiple performance metrics.

Result: The approach improves the accuracy and robustness of the global model under heterogeneous and sparse data conditions, showing favorable trade-offs between accuracy, efficiency, and resilience to malicious inputs across real-world datasets.

Conclusion: Hierarchical Federated Transfer Learning with vehicle-type clustering and data-quality filtering is effective for DT-VANET FL, addressing heterogeneity/sparsity and security concerns, and demonstrating practical viability in real-world deployments.

Abstract: In recent research on the Digital Twin-based Vehicular Ad hoc Network(DT-VANET), Federated Learning (FL) has shown its ability to provide data privacy. However, Federated learning struggles to adequately train a global model when confronted with data heterogeneity and data sparsity among vehicles, which ensure suboptimal accuracy in making precise predictions for different vehicle types. To address these challenges, this paper combines Federated Transfer Learning (FTL) to conduct vehicle clustering related to types of vehicles and proposes a novel Hierarchical Federated Transfer Learning (HFTL). We construct a framework for DT-VANET, along with two algorithms designed for cloud server model updates and intra-cluster federated transfer learning, to improve the accuracy of the global model. In addition, we developed a data quality score-based mechanism to prevent the global model from being affected by malicious vehicles. Lastly, detailed experiments on real-world datasets are conducted, considering different performance metrics that verify the effectiveness and efficiency of our algorithm.

</details>


### [234] [Robust Ambiguity Detection (RAD) From Model- and Feature-Space Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11541)
*Manya Singh,Mark T. Keane,Arjun Pakrashi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RAD framework to quantify predictive ambiguity via Model-Space and Feature-Space consistency, providing an interpretable RAD Plot and Pareto-based abstention; evaluated on synthetic and real data with competitive rejection-based performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identify and quantify predictive ambiguity in ML models to prevent high-stakes mispredictions and enable abstention or human review.

Method: Introduce two complementary consistency metrics: Model-Space Consistency (consistency under functionally equivalent model replacements) and Feature-Space Consistency (robustness to admissible input perturbations). Combine into RAD Score-Pair and visualize via RAD Plot. Evaluate on synthetic datasets with controlled overlap and real-world datasets. Downstream use: RAD Pareto-Rank to select most ambiguous samples for abstention.

Result: RAD provides an interpretable characterization of ambiguity, enabling abstention that achieves performance comparable to existing rejection-based approaches. Demonstrates applicability to downstream tasks by ranking ambiguous samples.

Conclusion: RAD offers a principled, interpretable framework for detecting predictive ambiguity, guiding user actions (e.g., abstention or human review) in high-stakes settings; further work could address calibration, perturbation design, and computational costs.

Abstract: Machine learning models should be robust, in the sense of remaining predictively consistent under permissible variations. A model's predictions should ideally remain unchanged when it is replaced by a functionally equivalent one, or when its inputs are subject to minor, admissible perturbations. If such changes alter a prediction significantly, then the prediction is "ambiguous" with respect to the model. Models should abstain from making such ambiguous predictions and/or should flag them for human inspection, especially in high-stakes decision-making scenarios. However, in practice, such ambiguity is not easy to identify once a model is deployed. Here, the Robust Ambiguity Detection (RAD) framework is advanced for quantifying predictive ambiguity using two complementary metrics: Model-Space Consistency and Feature-Space Consistency. These two scores, the RAD Score-Pair, visualised through the RAD Plot, provide an interpretable characterisation of the sources of ambiguity and the actions a user may consider in response. RAD is evaluated on synthetic datasets with systematically controlled overlap, as well as several real-world datasets where the level of ambiguity cannot be directly inspected. Finally, we demonstrate a downstream application of RAD where samples are ranked by their RAD Pareto-Rank and the most ambiguous are abstained from prediction, achieving performance comparable to existing rejection-based approaches.

</details>


### [235] [Certifying What Helps Customer-Return Timing: A Screen-and-Confirm Test for Conditioning Signals, and Why Decay Is Nearly Enough](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11555)
*Sang Su Lee,Vineeth Loganathan,Shishir Dash,Vijay Raghavan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A protocol to credibly evaluate whether signals improve temporal-point-process (TPP) event timing, plus a model-free ceiling on predictability; finds that the inter-event clock decay is nearly sufficient and added covariates are largely redundant or harmful on public benchmarks; provides tools to certify null findings and discuss leakage pitfalls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In TPPs, practitioners enrich models with many signals, but it is unclear if these features actually improve timing. A null result is only meaningful if the model could have found a signal; the work provides a principled way to certify signal usefulness and avoid false negatives.

Method: Screen-and-confirm protocol with a positive control that injects a known signal and verifies that the model recovers it; validated for categorical and continuous encodings; applies also to a real clock-driven dataset (NYC taxi hour-of-day). A model-free ceiling estimates the fraction of timing variance explainable by any covariate (single-digit percentage, i.e., near-memoryless timing). Empirical evaluation on public benchmarks (Amazon, Taobao, RetailRocket) and a real marketplace (Thumbtack) with leakage considerations.

Result: Across datasets, the inter-event clock with continuous-time decay is nearly sufficient for event timing; conditioning signals provide little to no additional predictive power and can be null or mildly harmful (e.g., up to 0.06 NLL improvement in some cases). The proposed tools yield a certified statement that conditioning does not help in these settings, and provide transparent discussion of read-out/leakage pitfalls.

Conclusion: The paper furnishes a robust, checkable framework to certify when covariates truly aid TPP-based timing; offers a practical, model-free ceiling for predictability and discusses pitfalls. It does not claim that decay definitively helps, but delivers the methodology and honest evaluation necessary to interpret null results.

Abstract: Practitioners enrich customer-return models with ever more signals (lifetime value, category, recency/frequency, calendar, geography), and the temporal-point-process (TPP) literature follows suit with covariate- and external-covariate-conditioned intensities. But does any of it improve the timing, and how would you know? A null ("feature X doesn't help") is only meaningful if the model could have found a signal. We make two contributions--a method and a measurement--to answer this credibly. (i) A screen-and-confirm protocol that certifies whether a candidate signal improves a TPP's event-timing likelihood: a positive control plants a coupling of known strength and confirms the model recovers it, so a real-data null can be read as "no signal" rather than "weak method." The control is validated for categorical and continuous encodings, and on a real clock-driven dataset (NYC taxi hour-of-day). (ii) A model-free ceiling quantifying how little of customer-return timing is point-predictable at all (a single-digit percentage of gap variance from any covariate; returns are near-memoryless). With these we certify a clean result on three public benchmarks (Amazon, Taobao, RetailRocket) and a real marketplace (Thumbtack): the inter-event clock--continuous-time decay, long known to beat frozen-intensity models--is nearly sufficient, and the conditioning the field keeps adding is redundant or harmful on top of it (statistically null on the public benchmarks, at most 0.06 NLL; null to mildly harmful on the marketplace). We do not claim to discover that decay helps; our contribution is the tools that turn "conditioning doesn't help" into a checkable, certified statement--plus an honest-evaluation account of the read-out/leakage pitfalls we hit and retracted.

</details>


### [236] [When Offline Evaluation Misleads: A Diagnostic Protocol for Reward and Policy Selection in Delayed-Feedback Contextual Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11560)
*Sang Su Lee,Vineeth Loganathan,Shishir Dash,Vijay Raghavan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes an ordered diagnostic protocol for evaluating contextual multi-armed bandits with delayed feedback, addressing misranking in offline checks and highlighting two key lessons about reward signals and personalization robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable reliable decision-making for deploying CMABs in marketing when the true downstream conversion is delayed, by going beyond static offline checks.

Method: Introduce an ordered two-axis protocol (alignment and learnability) to screen reward-and-policy candidates before reporting lift; validate on public OPE benchmark and synthetic generator; apply end-to-end to a deployed push system with delayed feedback.

Result: The protocol helps avoid misranking rewards and overstated personalization; online learning behavior can separate rewards that appear tied offline; directional rather than powered evidence in deployment.

Conclusion: Contributes a methodological framework and concrete lessons for evaluating delayed-feedback CMABs, emphasizing (i) alignment between reward optimization and the north-star metric, and (ii) learnability of the reward-optimal policy; promotes robust deployment decisions over single offline lifts.

Abstract: Personalizing marketing messages with contextual multi-armed bandits (CMABs) drives real business value, yet the objective that ultimately matters - a downstream conversion - is observed only weeks later, too late to drive online learning. Teams therefore train the bandit on a fast proxy reward, and separately must judge whether a contextual bandit is worth its complexity over sending one best message. Settling both decisions with the usual offline checks - a batch off-policy estimate, a marginal arm-discrimination test, a confidence interval - can mislead systematically under delayed feedback. We give an ordered diagnostic protocol that screens a reward-and-policy candidate on two axes, alignment (does optimizing the reward move the north-star?) and learnability (can the bandit identify the reward-optimal policy?), before trusting any reported lift. We validate it where the truth is known - a public off-policy-evaluation benchmark and a controllable synthetic generator - and illustrate it on a deployed large-marketplace push system (where, with five arms and one split, the evidence is directional rather than powered). Two lessons recur. (N1) A single offline number can mis-rank rewards: a denser reward signal gives the bandit more to learn from, so rewards that look tied in a static estimate pull apart once learning happens online. (N2) If you cannot tell in advance which single message is best, a per-user policy partly just avoids betting on the wrong one - that looks like personalization but is really robustness, so a "personalization premium" is easily overstated. Our contribution is methodological rather than algorithmic: the ordered protocol, the two lessons it surfaces, and the end-to-end experience of applying it to a delayed-feedback CMAB.

</details>


### [237] [Sparse and robust geometric twin support vector machine via asymmetric RoBoSS loss function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11567)
*Kai Qi,Xinji Huang,Hongchun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes an asymmetric, robust, bounded, sparse and smooth (aR) loss for l1-penalized geometric twin SVM (aRSGTSVM), enhancing feature selection, noise robustness, and stability; develops a proximal-gradient solver; demonstrates superiority on synthetic, UCI, and stock index tracking data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world data contain redundant features and multiple noise types (label noise, feature noise). Standard SVM with l2 penalty and hinge loss lacks sparsity and robustness, motivating a loss that is robust, bounded, and amenable to sparse modeling.

Method: Introduce the aR loss for l1-norm penalized geometric twin SVM (aRSGTSVM) to handle classification and regression. The l1 penalty enables feature selection; the aR loss mitigates label noise and stabilizes against resampling noise. Robustness analyzed via influence function. Solve the resulting nonconvex, nonsmooth problem with a fast proximal gradient descent algorithm.

Result: Experimental results show the proposed method outperforms related state-of-the-art methods on synthetic and UCI datasets. It also performs well on index tracking tasks in the China stock market, indicating practical robustness and effectiveness.

Conclusion: aRSGTSVM provides a robust, sparse, and scalable learning framework with provable robustness properties and strong empirical performance across classification, regression, and financial applications.

Abstract: In real-world scenarios, the training data usually contains redundant features, label noise and feature noise, which provide severe challenges for the efficiency of machine learning methods. Since standard support vector machine (SVM) adopts $l_2$-norm penalty and hinge loss function, it lacks the ability of selecting significant features and is sensitive to noise. To address these issues, this paper proposes a novel asymmetric, robust, bounded, sparse and smooth (aR) loss function for $l_1$-norm penalized geometric twin SVM (aRSGTSVM) to handle classification and regression tasks. The $l_1$-norm penalty can achieve the feature selection. The proposed aR loss function can not only effectively mitigate the impact of label noise, but also significantly enhance the stability to resampling noise, i.e., the zero-mean feature noise around the boundary hyperplanes. Furthermore, a statistical analysis of the robustness of aRSGTSVM was also conducted using the influence function. Since aRSGTSVM involves nonconvex and nonsmooth optimization, we develop a fast and stable proximal gradient descent based solving algorithm. Compared with related state-of-the-art methods, experimental results demonstrate the superiority of the proposed aRSGTSVM on both synthetic and UCI datasets. Furthermore, we apply aRSGTSVM to index tracking tasks, where results for tracking the different indices in the China stock market show that it can achieve satisfactory performance.

</details>


### [238] [RECAST: A Machine-Learning Framework for Correction and Super-Resolution of Coarse-Grid PDE Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11572)
*Maryam Reza,Farbod Faraji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RECAST is a machine-learning framework that corrects coarse-grid time evolution and reconstructs fine-grid states, achieving substantial accuracy gains while retaining coarse-grid evolution. It reduces time-averaged error by 50–92% versus uncorrected coarse solvers, generalizes to unseen parameters, and outperforms a contemporary coarse-correction baseline, enabling ML-accelerated PDE simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coarse-grid time-dependent PDE solvers offer computational savings but suffer from under-resolution that degrades trajectory and spatial fidelity. A learned correction and reconstruction can recover lost accuracy while retaining coarse-grid evolution.

Method: RECAST integrates learned correction within the numerical time-stepping loop and adds a reconstruction module to obtain fine-grid states from the corrected coarse-history. Evaluation spans six one-dimensional PDEs with coarse-grid factors 8–16 and 1000-step closed-loop rollouts on unseen initial conditions; comparisons include a contemporary coarse-correction architecture and generalization tests to unseen PDE-parameter values.

Result: Across six 1D PDEs, RECAST remains closely aligned with fine-grid references and reduces time-averaged relative error by ~50–92% compared with uncorrected coarse solvers. It generalizes to unseen parameters and shows better long-horizon agreement than a contemporary baseline over 5000-step rollouts.

Conclusion: Learned correction and reconstruction enable substantially coarser PDE evolution without substantial loss of fidelity, presenting a proof-of-concept for ML acceleration of higher-dimensional numerical simulations across science and engineering.

Abstract: Coarse-grid numerical solvers can substantially reduce the computational cost of time-dependent PDE simulation, but under-resolution often degrades both the trajectory and the spatial fidelity of the solution. We introduce RECAST (Recurrent Error Correction And Super-resolution of coarse-grid Trajectories), a machine-learning framework designed to restore this lost accuracy while retaining coarse-grid evolution. RECAST combines learned correction within the numerical time-stepping loop with reconstruction of the corresponding fine-grid state from the corrected coarse history. We evaluate the framework on six one-dimensional PDE systems spanning transport, diffusion, dispersion, reaction, and wave dynamics, using spatial grids coarsened by factors of 8-16 and 1000-step closed-loop rollouts from unseen initial conditions. Across the test cases, RECAST remains closely aligned with the fine-grid reference solutions and reduces time-averaged relative error by approximately 50-92% compared with the corresponding uncorrected coarse-grid solvers. Additional tests show generalization to unseen PDE parameter values, while comparison with a contemporary coarse-correction architecture shows that RECAST achieves lower error and better long-horizon agreement with the fine-grid reference over 5000-step rollouts. These results demonstrate that the learned correction and reconstruction capabilities of RECAST can enable substantially coarser PDE evolution without the corresponding loss of solution fidelity, providing a proof-of-concept route toward machine-learning acceleration of higher-dimensional numerical simulations across science and engineering.

</details>


### [239] [Dion3: Full-Stack Orthogonal Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11612)
*Noah Amsel,Jack Zhang,Kwangjun Ahn,Ali Naeimi,Austin Feng,Berlin Chen,Tri Dao,John Langford*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dion3 presents a reworked Muon optimizer that dramatically lowers orthogonalization overhead and communication costs, achieving up to 6x faster steps while maintaining or improving loss, via Gram Newton-Schulz orthogonalization, symmetry-exploiting kernels, megabatching, and partial-row orthogonalization; provided as a drop-in replacement for Muon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon's cubic-time Newton-Schulz orthogonalization and communication overhead become a bottleneck in large-scale or sharded settings. Reducing FLOPs, exploiting symmetry, and minimizing communication can yield substantial speedups without sacrificing performance.

Method: Introduce Gram Newton-Schulz to reduce FLOPs for orthogonalization; implement CuteDSL kernels that exploit symmetry to accelerate computation; employ megabatching to amortize communication; propose an update rule that orthogonalizes only a fraction of the momentum matrix rows each step, improving speed.

Result: Dion3 matches or improves Muon's loss while reducing optimizer step time by up to 6x; it outperforms another compressed variant, Dion, in speed and performance; available as a drop-in replacement for Muon via the dion package (GitHub link provided).

Conclusion: Dion3 delivers substantial practical speedups for optimizers relying on Newton-Schulz orthogonalization without sacrificing model performance, addressing both compute and communication bottlenecks; it is ready for adoption as a Muon replacement.

Abstract: The Muon optimizer incurs a significant overhead cost due to its cubic-time Newton-Schulz orthogonalization step. When weights are sharded, communication overhead compounds this computational cost, eroding the benefits of Muon in many settings. We present Dion3, a revision of Muon that targets this overhead at every level of the stack. Our Gram Newton-Schulz algorithm reduces the FLOP cost of orthogonalization, our CuteDSL kernels accelerate it by exploiting symmetry, and our megabatching strategy reduces communication overhead. Moreover, we propose a simple change to the update rule that cuts costs even further: selecting only a fraction of the momentum matrix's rows to orthogonalize at each step. This update rule improves on Dion (another "compressed" version of Muon), in both speed and performance. Overall, Dion3 matches or improves on the loss achieved by Muon but reduces optimizer step time by up to 6x. Dion3 is available via the dion package (https://github.com/microsoft/dion) as a drop-in replacement for Muon.

</details>


### [240] [A Local Sinkhorn Framework for Conditional Distribution Reconstruction of Multidimensional Random Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11613)
*Mingtao Xia,Qijing Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A local Sinkhorn divergence framework for conditional distribution reconstruction of multidimensional random fields, leveraging debiased Sinkhorn divergence to provide a differentiable, scalable loss for training stochastic neural networks, with theoretical generalization guarantees and competitive performance against other losses and UQ frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address conditional distribution reconstruction in high-dimensional random fields with a loss that is both geometrically faithful (OT-based) and scalable. Exact local OT is computationally expensive; there is a need for a differentiable, scalable objective that preserves statistical efficiency for learning stochastic neural networks and uncertainty quantification.

Method: Introduce a local Sinkhorn divergence loss with a debiasing correction, embedded as a differentiable objective to train stochastic neural networks. Derive generalization error bounds that quantify the bias-variance trade-off controlled by the regularization parameter. Demonstrate applicability to learning multidimensional random field models and compare to other loss functions and UQ frameworks through numerical experiments.

Result: Proposes a scalable alternative to exact local OT for conditional distribution reconstruction. Shows that the local Sinkhorn divergence loss achieves a balance between reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency, with good scalability to multidimensional stochastic systems. Empirical results compare favorably against other losses and UQ approaches.

Conclusion: The local Sinkhorn divergence framework provides a practical and theoretically principled approach for conditional distribution learning in multidimensional random fields, offering a differentiable, scalable loss with explicit bias-variance trade-offs and broad applicability to uncertainty quantification and probabilistic scientific ML.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a local Sinkhorn divergence framework for conditional distribution reconstruction of multidimensional random fields. By utilizing the debiased Sinkhorn divergence, our proposed approach develops a differentiable and computationally efficient local distribution matching objective to train stochastic neural networks (SNNs). Furthermore, we establish theoretical generalization error estimates for our local Sinkhorn divergence framework, which explicitly characterizes the trade-off between approximation bias and statistical efficiency controlled by the regularization parameter and reveals how our proposed local Sinkhorn divergence loss function can be efficiently applied to learning multidimensional random field models. The proposed framework provides a scalable alternative to exact local optimal transport for conditional distribution reconstruction, offering a practical compromise between geometric fidelity, statistical efficiency, and computational scalability for uncertainty quantification and probabilistic scientific machine learning. Through various numerical examples, we compare our proposed local Sinkhorn divergence framework with other loss functions to train SNNs and with other machine-learning-based uncertainty quantification frameworks, demonstrating that the proposed local Sinkhorn divergence framework achieves an effective balance between reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency while maintaining good scalability for multidimensional stochastic systems.

</details>


### [241] [FM-LLM: A frequency-enhanced mixture-of-experts framework for adapting LLMs to time series forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11623)
*Rentao Gu,Yihang Ding,Junjie Li,Yi Ding,Weijing Sang,Xiaoli Huo,Xin Qin,Yuefeng Ji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FM-LLM introduces a prompt-free, frequency-aware adaptation of frozen LLMs for time-series forecasting using a FAN-based spectral aligner and an asymmetric MoE decoder, achieving state-of-the-art performance across benchmarks with good few-shot/zero-shot transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome reliance on textual prompts and exploit spectral dynamics in time-series while keeping LLMs frozen, enabling efficient, accurate forecasting.

Method: Autoregressive framework with constrained asymmetric coupling: a Fourier Analysis Network-based spectral token aligner injects harmonic features into the frozen LLM; an asymmetric Mixture-of-Experts decoder with shared experts plus FAN layers for global periodic backbone and routed FFN experts for non-periodic residuals; a time-frequency hybrid loss for temporal accuracy and spectral consistency.

Result: Evaluated on 11 benchmarks; 59/78 metrics show state-of-the-art; average MSE/MAE improvements of 5.3% and 5.6%; max gains 8.0% MSE and 8.4% MAE; robust in 10% few-shot and zero-shot settings.

Conclusion: FM-LLM demonstrates strong, prompt-free, frequency-aware adaptation of frozen LLMs for time-series forecasting with improved accuracy and transferability, leveraging spectral alignment to mitigate error accumulation in long-horizon autoregression.

Abstract: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have spurred cross-modal solutions for time-series forecasting. However, existing methods rely heavily on textual prompts for modality alignment-introducing nontrivial computational overhead and failing to leverage the rich spectral dynamics inherent in time-series data. To enable prompt-free, frequency-aware adaptation of frozen LLMs, we propose FM-LLM (Frequency-Enhanced Mixture-of-Experts for adapting LLMs to Time Series Forecasting), an autoregressive framework grounded in constrained asymmetric coupling. A Fourier Analysis Network (FAN)-based spectral token aligner injects structured harmonic representations directly into the frozen LLM with numerical compatibility. An asymmetric Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) decoder enforces role separation: shared experts with lightweight FAN layers reconstruct the global periodic backbone, while routed experts-restricted to standard FFNs-specialize in modeling non-periodic residual dynamics. A time-frequency hybrid loss function jointly optimizes temporal accuracy and spectral consistency, mitigating error accumulation during long-horizon autoregressive rollouts. Evaluated across eleven public benchmarks, FM-LLM achieves state-of-the-art performance on 59 out of 78 evaluation metrics. Compared to the strongest autoregressive LLM-based baseline, it delivers average improvements of 5.3% in MSE and 5.6% in MAE, with maximum gains reaching 8.0% for MSE and 8.4% for MAE. FM-LLM also demonstrates robust transferability, maintaining superior performance in 10% few-shot and zero-shot forecasting scenarios.

</details>


### [242] [Transferable Above-Ground Biomass (AGB) Estimation Model from Multi-Sensor Data with Sparse Field Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11638)
*Pann Thinzar Seint,Bryan Atwood,Subas Chhatkuli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A globally trained CNN maps AGB across landscapes by fusing optical, SAR, and terrain data, with small field-based scale-bias calibration to deliver spatially continuous biomass estimates at 10 m, achieving strong validation performance and local bias reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the mismatch between field inventories (high local accuracy but sparse) and spaceborne biomass products (broad coverage but biased and non-continuous). Enable spatially continuous AGB mapping suitable for carbon accounting and mitigation planning.

Method: Train a single CNN on GEDI L4A biomass references using multi-sensor inputs (Sentinel-2 optical, Sentinel-1 C-band, ALOS-2 PALSAR-2 L-band, DEM). Harmonize data to 10 m grid; compute vegetation indices and polarimetric ratios; normalize per-band; train with a hybrid log-domain SmoothL1 with RMSE loss. Apply a lightweight local calibration using a small number of field plots to estimate scale-and-bias corrections per region, followed by Random Forest fine-tuning in a 10-fold cross-validation to reduce regional biases.

Result: Global GEDI-based model achieved R^2 ≈ 0.78 and RMSE ≈ 22 Mg/ha on held-out validation. Local calibration via Random Forest improved regional performance to R^2 ≈ 0.82 and RMSE ≈ 15 Mg/ha, outperforming both the uncalibrated global model and the ESA CCI Biomass product on field plots.

Conclusion: A single globally trained CNN, augmented by minimal region-specific calibration, can deliver spatially continuous AGB estimates with improved local accuracy, effectively leveraging multi-sensor data and GEDI references while mitigating regional biases.

Abstract: Spatially continuous quantification of forest above-ground biomass (AGB) is what makes carbon accounting credible and mitigation strategies actionable. While field inventories provide high localized accuracy, they are spatially sparse; conversely, spaceborne LiDAR from the Global Ecosystem Dynamics Investigation (GEDI) offers broad biomass samples but lacks spatial continuity and systematic underestimation of high-biomass forests. This paper presents an operational framework centered on a single globally trained convolutional neural network (CNN) that is seamlessly adapted to each new landscape through a lightweight empirical field-calibration workflow. The global model combines optical (Sentinel-2), C-band SAR (Sentinel-1), L-band SAR (ALOS-2 PALSAR-2), and terrain (DEM) data. It is trained once against GEDI Level-4A biomass reference data spanning multiple regions and both wet and dry seasons so that it learns the persistent woody-structure rather than a single-date appearance. To avoid retraining for every landscape, the framework applies a small number of local field plots to fit a scale-and-bias correction that aligns the global prediction with ground truth in each region. The pipeline harmonizes sensor data onto a shared 10 m grid, derives vegetation indices and polarimetric ratios, computes per-band normalization stats, and trains the CNN with a hybrid log-domain SmoothL1 with RMSE loss for skewed biomass distribution. On held-out validation the global GEDI-based model achieved R^2 approximately 0.78 and RMSE approximately 22 Mg/ha. A subsequent field calibration combining Random Forest fine-tuning under a 10-fold cross-validation eliminates localized regional biases. This improves local validation performance to R^2 approximately 0.82 and reduces RMSE to approximately 15 Mg/ha, outperforming both the uncalibrated global model and the ESA CCI Biomass product against field plots.

</details>


### [243] [Towards a Formal Definition of Agent Memory: Basis, Span, Optimality, and the Sequential Memory Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11654)
*Hongyao Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Despite the wide deployment of memory in large-model agents, there is no unified formal account of what a memory is or when it is optimal. This paper takes a first step toward this account. The central idea is that memory is a basis, knowledge is its span, and answerability is a coverage problem: an agent stores events extracted from a material; a generation operator turns any event set into the knowledge it entails; and a query is answerable exactly when some single item in the span covers it. The optimal memory is then the capacity-constrained maximizer of expected coverage, and its value traces a utility--capacity frontier, the common yardstick on which memory systems can be compared. Next, we consider noise in the memory and discuss coverage versus precision under it: a memory may store false claims, so the write policy must infer the truth of what it stores. Drawing an analogy with biological memory, which is formed continuously through ongoing experience, we formalize the continual agent-memory problem in a sequential MDP that covers multiple levels, where memory is the state, writing is the action, and the utility settled at query time is the delayed reward that drives learning. To make the framework concrete, we instantiate it on Homer's \emph{Odyssey}, turning the frontier, the compression zone, and the divergence of coverage from precision into concrete numbers. Finally, we position existing systems within the framework, making ``how good is a memory'' measurable and recasting the open problems of constructing and learning agent memory as concrete research questions.

</details>


### [244] [Continuous-Latent Predictive Modeling with Semantic Alignment for EEG-Language Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11656)
*Myeong-Ju Cho,Hye-Bin Shin,Seo-Hyun Lee,Seong-Whan Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BLPM proposes a continuous latent semantic prediction framework for EEG-language foundation models, introducing a CELP encoder and MQSD module to align EEG with textual semantics in a shared latent space, enabling cross-task generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current EEG pretraining strategies (masked autoencoding and autoregressive modeling) prioritize reconstruction of signals or discrete tokens, leading to a misalignment with natural-language semantics and limited transfer to LLMs. A continuous, semantics-aware representation is needed for robust cross-subject/dataset decoding and integration with language models.

Method: Introduce a Continuous EEG Latent Predictive (CELP) encoder that learns transferable representations via latent target prediction. Build a Multi-Query Semantic Decomposition (MQSD) module to extract task-relevant information and align continuous EEG representations with textual semantics within a shared latent space, reframing EEG decoding tasks as continuous semantic embedding prediction.

Result: Experiments across multiple benchmarks show consistent generalization across diverse tasks, supporting continuous latent semantic prediction as an effective paradigm for EEG-language foundation models.

Conclusion: BLPM demonstrates a viable path for EEG-language foundation models by aligning continuous EEG latent representations with language semantics, enabling cross-task generalization and potential integration with LLMs through a shared latent semantic space.

Abstract: Recent advances in EEG foundation models have demonstrated the potential of large-scale pretraining to enable generalizable neural decoding across subjects, recording environments, and datasets. However, dominant pretraining paradigms face key challenges: masked autoencoding tends to prioritize low-level signal reconstruction over task-relevant semantics, while autoregressive modeling creates a mismatch between continuous neural dynamics and discrete token spaces. To address these challenges, new strategies are needed to effectively align continuous EEG representations with natural-language semantics and enable their integration with large language models. Accordingly, we propose Brain Latent Predictive Model (BLPM), an EEG-language foundation model that reformulates heterogeneous EEG decoding tasks as a continuous semantic embedding prediction problem. BLPM introduces a Continuous EEG Latent Predictive (CELP) encoder that learns transferable representations through latent target prediction. Building on these representations, a Multi-Query Semantic Decomposition (MQSD) module extracts task-relevant information and aligns continuous EEG representations with textual semantics within a shared latent space according to their semantic relationships. Experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate consistent generalization performance across diverse tasks, establishing continuous latent semantic prediction as an effective paradigm for EEG-language foundation models.

</details>


### [245] [Is Per-Agent Policy Composition Safe? Rethinking Successor-Feature Transfer in Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11658)
*Zijian Zhao,Sen Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Independent per-agent recombination in multi-agent RL can be unsafe under dynamic objectives; the paper introduces MA-USFA, a two-layer hierarchical method that ensures safety and adaptability by training once on objective distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic objective setting in multi-agent systems makes per-task retraining expensive and naïve independent recombination unsafe; a unified, safe transfer mechanism is needed that respects inter-agent dependencies.

Method: Introduce MA-USFA with a lower layer of universal successor features conditioned on teammates’ objectives, and an upper layer that selects library entries per agent and provides cross-agent correction. Trained once on objective distributions, deployed without per-task adaptation, and contrasted with synchronized composition and independent recombination.

Result: The analysis shows independent composition can yield joint behavior worse than any library policy; synchronized composition is safe but inflexible. MA-USFA provides both safety and flexibility by coordinating agents through a hierarchy, achieving safe transfer across varying objectives.

Conclusion: MA-USFA offers a practical, provably safer and flexible framework for multi-agent transfer with changing goals, enabling deployment-ready policies without per-task re-training.

Abstract: Many reinforcement learning systems, from fleet management to traffic signal control, must serve an objective that changes dynamically after deployment, and retraining a policy for each new objective is prohibitively expensive. For a single agent, this problem is well understood: successor features with generalized policy improvement, together with their universal extension, recombine a library of learned policies into a policy for any new objective, with a guarantee that the result is never worse than any policy in the library. However, multi-agent transfer has received far less attention, and the common practice of letting each agent recombine its own library independently inherits the recipe but not the guarantee. We prove that this independent composition can produce joint behavior strictly worse than every policy in the library, because recombining teammates changes the environment each agent faces and invalidates the values it relies on, a failure with no single-agent counterpart. We further show that the only unconditionally safe fixed rule is synchronized composition, which moves the whole team to one jointly trained policy but cannot serve objectives that assign different goals to different agents. To attain safety and flexibility at once, we propose MA-USFA, a hierarchical method with two layers: a lower layer of universal successor feature approximators that predicts each agent's successor features while conditioned on its teammates' objectives, and an upper composer that selects, across agents, which library entry each agent should follow and supplies the cross-agent correction a per-agent value cannot represent. Trained once over the distribution of objectives, it is applied at deployment with no per-task adaptation.

</details>


### [246] [Low-Interaction-Rank Learning: Unifying Multiplicative Dual-Encoder Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11661)
*Zijian Zhao,Sen Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces low interaction rank as a unifying framework for multiplicative dual-encoders, analyzes approximation error, sample complexity, and identifiability, and prescribes normalization/ whitening as gauge-fixing; validates predictions on synthetic kernels, operator learning, and CLIP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is no unified theory guiding core design choices (how many interaction modes, how to normalize, when to avoid the architecture) across domains that use multiplicative dual-encoders.

Method: Define a class of functions with low interaction rank characterized by an interaction spectrum; decompose approximation error into spectral truncation and encoder-realization terms; show sample complexity is the sum of encoder complexities; identify a gauge symmetry for encoders; show normalization as gauge fixing and whitening as a way to pin interaction modes up to permutation/sign; derive practical criteria based on spectral decay; provide constructive remedies.

Result: The theory predicts spectral decay rates and the effectiveness of whitening in recovering true interaction modes; in CLIP-like models independently trained networks are related by a rotation, which whitening can remove to reveal interpretable concept axes; experiments on synthetic kernels, operator learning tasks, and CLIP models validate the predictions; code is provided.

Conclusion: The framework offers principled guidance on choosing the number of interaction modes, when to use the architecture, and how to diagnose/mitigate gauge-induced ambiguities; whitening provides a practical remedy for interpretability, and spectral-decay serves as a usability criterion.

Abstract: A multiplicative dual-encoder network computes a real-valued output for a pair of inputs as the inner product of their separate encodings. This architecture has been developed independently in operator learning, bipartite matching, contrastive vision-language models, retrieval, and other areas, yet no unified theory guides the basic design decisions: how many interaction modes to represent, how to normalize the encoders, and when the architecture should be avoided. We provide such a foundation by introducing the class of functions of low interaction rank, a class whose intrinsic complexity is measured by its interaction spectrum. Within this framework, approximation error decomposes into a spectral truncation term and an encoder-realization term; sample complexity is governed by the sum of the two encoder complexities rather than their product; and a usability criterion based on spectral decay determines when the architecture can succeed. The same framework exposes a central identifiability problem: the encoders are defined only up to a linear gauge symmetry that leaves the learned coordinates arbitrary. We show that normalization is gauge fixing and that whitening pins the interaction modes up to permutation and sign, thereby explaining the uninterpretability of contrastive dimensions and providing a constructive remedy. Experiments on synthetic kernels, operator learning, and CLIP models validate the theoretical predictions: spectral decay rates match the predicted scaling, whitening recovers the true modes, and independently trained CLIP models are related by a single rotation which, after removal by whitening, exposes interpretable concept axes. The code of this paper is provided at https://github.com/RS2002/Mul-Net .

</details>


### [247] [Rubric Dropout: A Simple Way to Mitigate Reward Hacking in Rubric-as-Reward RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11669)
*Minglai Yang,Xinyu Guo,Utkarsh Tyagi,Mian Zhang,Razvan Dumitru,Sunjie Hou,Yunzhong He,Daniel Yue Zhang,Ying Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Reward hacking arises when RL is trained against a fixed rubric judged by an LLM; introducing Rubric Dropout—randomly omitting rubric criteria at each step—mitigates hacking and improves OOD gold scores with GRPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rubrics are imperfect proxies for quality; training against them can cause the policy to optimize rubric-exploiting behavior; a simple, effective fix is needed to align training with gold-quality signals, especially for out-of-distribution tasks.

Method: Train Qwen3-8B with Group Relative Policy Optimization on medical and science rubrics; evaluate with both the training (rubric) judge and a stronger gold judge on HealthBench-Hard and ResearchQA; observe divergence where the training score climbs while the gold score peaks then falls; implement Rubric Dropout by randomly dropping a subset of rubric criteria at each step (30% and 50% tested); share the dropped subset across rollout groups to preserve GRPO group-relative advantages; evaluate under dropout vs no dropout; compare to reweighting criteria.

Result: Dropout raises the gold (OOD) scores at matched checkpoints (+1 to +2 on HealthBench-Hard; +6 to +7 on ResearchQA); reduces reward-hacking metrics; lowers the divergence between training and gold scores; 30–50% dropout yields a sweet spot; reweighting criteria performs worse than no intervention in this setting.

Conclusion: Rubric Dropout is an effective, zero-cost intervention to reduce reward hacking when training against rubric-based LLM judges, aligning training signals with gold-quality evaluation on OOD tasks while preserving GRPO advantages.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning against rubrics, lists of criteria graded by an LLM judge, has become a standard way to post-train language models on tasks with no deterministic answer. The rubric, however, is a fixed proxy for quality, never a complete description of it, and a policy trained against it long enough will learn to exploit the difference. We measure this directly. Training Qwen3-8B with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) on medical and science rubrics and grading out-of-distribution (OOD) benchmarks with both the training judge and a stronger gold judge, we find that the two scores diverge during training. The training judge's score keeps climbing while the gold judge's score peaks and then falls, by 3 points on HealthBench-Hard and by 22 points on ResearchQA. A judge with a fixed bias would shift the gold curve by a constant, not send it down while the training score rises, so the divergence is reward hacking, not judge noise. We propose Rubric Dropout, a one-line fix borrowed from neuron dropout. At every step, we randomly drop a subset of the rubric's criteria before computing the reward, so the policy never optimizes the same rubric twice. The dropped subset is shared across each rollout group, so GRPO's group-relative advantages stay comparable, and evaluation always uses the full rubric. Comparing no dropout against dropout at 30% and 50% on both benchmark pairs, dropout raises the OOD gold score at every matched checkpoint (+1 to +2 points on HealthBench-Hard, +6 to +7 points on ResearchQA), lowers the two hacking measures we track, and costs nothing in domain. Sweeping the dropout fraction shows a broad 30-50% sweet spot, while the natural alternative, reweighting criteria by how useful they are to training, performs worse than no intervention at all in our setting.

</details>


### [248] [GCPO: Diagnosing and Constraining Subspace Geometry in Rollout RL for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11674)
*Kai Yang,Jingwei Xu,Wanyu Wang,Kai-Yuan Guo,Zhenbo Yu,Yi Wang,Yu Qiao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GCPO introduces a geometry-aware constraint for on-policy RL post-training, using Principal-Subspace Overlap to diagnose rollout updates and hard bilateral projections to keep updates in complementary subspaces, yielding stable, higher-performing LLM post-training across tasks and models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and control the stepwise geometry of rollout updates during RL post-training of large language models, addressing training instability, cross-task degradation, and response-length inflation that arise when only aggregate update geometry is considered.

Method: Define Principal-Subspace Overlap as a dimension-corrected measure of how individual rollout updates align with the dominant singular subspaces of pretrained weights; observe transient overlap spikes associated with performance drops; introduce GCPO (Geometrically Constrained Policy Optimization) which enforces hard bilateral orthogonal projections to constrain updates to the subspaces complementary to the dominant ones, preventing excursions by construction.

Result: GCPO consistently beats GRPO and recent variants (DAPO, GSPO) across math reasoning, code generation, and tool-use tasks on Qwen3-8B and GLM4-9B; improvements over base models and strongest baselines reach up to 27.69 points (and 2.37 points for the strongest baseline); GCPO also preserves general capabilities, eliminates response-length inflation, and stabilizes policy entropy.

Conclusion: The work provides a principled, geometry-aware framework for stable RL post-training, offering a diagnostic lens (Principal-Subspace Overlap) and a robust design (GCPO) that generalizes across models and tasks.
