Abstract: On-policy rollout methods such as GRPO are central to post-training of large language models, yet they frequently suffer from training instabilities, cross-task capability degradation, and response-length inflation. Although prior work has characterized the subspace geometry of aggregate updates, the stepwise variation of this geometry and its relationship to model performance remain unclear. We introduce Principal-Subspace Overlap, a dimension-corrected measure of individual rollout updates relative to the dominant singular subspaces of pretrained weights. Despite low average overlap, transient spikes often precede performance degradation. To address this, we propose GCPO (Geometrically Constrained Policy Optimization), which applies hard bilateral orthogonal projections to constrain updates to the complementary subspaces, preventing such excursions by construction. Across mathematical reasoning, code generation, and tool-use tasks on Qwen3-8B and GLM4-9B, GCPO consistently outperforms GRPO and recent variants, including DAPO and GSPO, improving over the base models and the strongest baseline by up to 27.69 and 2.37 points, respectively. Furthermore, GCPO preserves general capabilities, eliminates response-length inflation, and stabilizes policy entropy. Our findings provide a new diagnostic lens and a principled design perspective for stable reinforcement learning post-training.

</details>


### [249] [FunnelCausalNet: Funnel-aware Joint Conversion-Revenue Uplift for Multi-tier Coupon Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11675)
*Yu Zhang,Zhihan Wang,Guanlin Chen,Min Jiang,Shuai Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FunnelCausalNet is a two-headed uplift model for coupon-driven GMV, combining a binary conversion head with a nonnegative value head via a multiplicative GMV law, evaluated with RCT-based assumptions and conformal CATE summaries; it shows competitive GMV uplift and ROI performance across semi-synthetic and real data, with ablations indicating variance reduction but results are heuristic and regime-dependent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GMV in coupon campaigns is zero-inflated and heavy-tailed, and uplift estimators must account for the conversion funnel and conditional-value dynamics. The work seeks to improve uplift accuracy for GMV and ROI under RCT conditions, while providing uncertainty summaries and budget-aware allocation.

Method: Proposes FunnelCausalNet: a neural uplift model with a binary conversion head and a nonnegative conditional-value head, enforcing μ_gmv = μ_conv μ_val. Relies on explicit RCT, support, rate-gap, and cross-head covariance-control assumptions. Compares to idealized leading-order MSE, uses marginal split-conformal CATE summaries, Bonferroni audit bands, and a Lagrangian budgeted allocator for subsidy-aware ROI. Evaluates on semi-synthetic Criteo-MT7 and de-identified Hotel-Coupon RCT logs; includes ablations and regime-boundary analysis.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows FunnelCausalNet achieves mean AUUC_GMV within one seed SD of the top baseline among eleven on Criteo-MT7; ablations reduce GMV error by 18–48% across zero-inflation regimes. On Hotel-Coupon data (~4.9M hold-out per seed), it attains best seed-averaged DeltaROI across seven anchors (10–60%), with results described as frontier-consistent rather than statistically independent. On sparse binary-spend benchmarks, revenue-focused rankers can outperform uplift-curve proxies, indicating explicit regime boundaries.

Conclusion: FunnelCausalNet demonstrates competitive GMV uplift and ROI performance in coupon campaigns and advances by modeling the funnel via a multiplicative GMV structure and providing uncertainty-aware summaries and budget-aware allocation. The results are promising but framed as heuristic and frontier-descriptive, with regime-dependent performance and reliance on strong RCT-based assumptions.

Abstract: Coupon campaigns seek to lift both conversion and revenue, but gross merchandise value (GMV) follows a deterministic funnel from conversion to conditional order value and is zero-inflated and heavy-tailed. We propose FunnelCausalNet, an uplift estimator coupling a binary conversion head with a nonnegative conditional-value head through $μ_{\mathrm{gmv}}=μ_{\mathrm{conv}}μ_{\mathrm{val}}$. Under explicit RCT, support, rate-gap, and cross-head covariance-control assumptions, an idealized leading-order MSE comparison identifies a regime in which funnel composition can reduce pointwise variance; this is a heuristic, not a guarantee for the shared-representation neural model. The estimator is paired with marginal split-conformal CATE summaries, combined through a Bonferroni union as audit bands, and a Lagrangian budgeted allocator using RCT-anchored estimates for subsidy-aware ROI accounting. On semi-synthetic multi-tier Criteo-MT7, FunnelCausalNet's mean AUUC_GMV is within one seed standard deviation of the leading feature-interaction baseline among eleven baselines, while a controlled ablation reduces GMV effect error versus direct GMV regression by 18--48% across tested zero-inflation regimes. On de-identified industrial Hotel-Coupon RCT logs with about 4.9 million hold-out exposure records per seed, expected-outcome evaluation sweeps full LP frontiers; FunnelCausalNet has the best seed-averaged mean DeltaROI at all seven correlated anchors from 10% to 60%, which we treat as descriptive frontier consistency rather than independent significance. On sparse binary-spend public benchmarks, revenue-focused rankers can dominate uplift-curve proxies, defining an explicit regime boundary.

</details>


### [250] [Drift and Dependence: Layer-wise Information-Theoretic Bounds for Replay-Based Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11690)
*Tieliang Gong,Zhongbo Zhang,Wen Wen,Yong-Jin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Layer-wise information-theoretic decomposition of replay in continual learning; separates drift from optimization effects, yielding a depth-dependent trade-off and a trajectory-based forgetting diagnostic, with empirical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how finite-memory replay and repeated-use optimization jointly affect forgetting; disentangle representation drift from optimization dynamics across layers in continual learning.

Method: Propose a layer-wise information-theoretic framework that decomposes the generalization gap into a replay-induced representation drift and an optimization-dependence term, with the latter split further into stability, plasticity, interaction, and residual-coupling components. Introduce a Wasserstein relaxation of the drift under support mismatch to yield a depth-dependent drift-sensitivity trade-off and identify which interior layer to stabilize. Instantiate the optimization term via SGLD to obtain a trajectory-level log-determinant budget, yielding a curvature-aware gradient-alignment statistic as an online forgetting diagnostic.

Result: The decomposition reveals a depth-dependent drift-sensitivity trade-off; it points to which interior layer to stabilize; provides an online diagnostic via gradient alignment; experiments corroborate the predicted memory scaling, interior funnel, and the alignment signal’s connection to forgetting.

Conclusion: The framework operationalizes replay analysis in continual learning, guiding buffer design and layer stabilization. The drift–optimization decomposition and the gradient-alignment diagnostic enable principled prediction and mitigation of forgetting across tasks.

Abstract: Continual learning must absorb new tasks without erasing old ones, and replay---mixing a small buffer of past examples into current training---is among the most effective remedies for catastrophic forgetting. Yet its generalization behavior is shaped by two coupled effects that existing analyses fold into a single hypothesis-level quantity: finite memory replaces each past distribution with an empirical proxy, and repeated reuse couples the buffer, the current data, and the final hypothesis through a shared optimization trajectory. We develop a layer-wise information-theoretic framework that separates these effects at every depth. Our main result decomposes the expected generalization gap into a replay-induced representation drift and an optimization-dependence term, the latter further resolved into stability, plasticity, interaction, and residual-coupling components. Two refinements make the framework operational. A Wasserstein relaxation of the drift term, valid under support mismatch, yields a depth-dependent drift--sensitivity trade-off whose minimizer identifies which interior layer to stabilize. An SGLD instantiation of the optimization term reduces it to a trajectory-level log-determinant budget, exposing a curvature-aware gradient-alignment statistic that serves as an online diagnostic of task-wise forgetting. Controlled and benchmark experiments confirm the predicted memory scaling, the interior funnel, and the alignment signal's link to forgetting.

</details>


### [251] [LEMUR: Latent Entropy-aware Multimodal Unlearning via Visual-anchored Reasoning Redirection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11691)
*Xinhao Zhong,Yuxia Qiao,Junhao Li,Hao Fang,Yi Sun,Bin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RL-trained multimodal language models reveal sensitive content in their reasoning traces even after unlearning, posing privacy risks. LEMUR is a training-free, inference-time unlearning framework that uses entropy dynamics to identify sensitive reasoning, then sanitizes the reasoning path via entropy-modulated visual-anchor latent injection, replacing committed tokens with sanitized, probability-weighted embeddings grounded in the input image; it suppresses leakage while preserving utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Privacy leakage arises when RL-induced exploration leaves distinctive trace signatures in reasoning paths of multimodal models. Existing unlearning methods focus on final outputs and fail to remove leaked traces, especially in natively RL-trained MLRMs. A training-free, inference-time solution is needed to mitigate leakage without retraining.

Method: Uses entropy dynamics as a control signal to detect the onset of sensitive reasoning. During the leakage window, it redirects the reasoning trajectory through entropy-modulated visual-anchor latent injection, replacing committed tokens with sanitized, probability-weighted embeddings re-grounded in the input image. No additional training required; operates at inference time.

Result: Empirically, LEMUR outperforms existing unlearning methods across diverse MLRMs in suppressing both reasoning-trace and answer leakage, while better preserving non-sensitive utility and output fluency.

Conclusion: RL-induced entropy signatures reveal a viable signal for privacy leakage; exploiting this signal enables effective training-free unlearning for reasoning-capable multimodal models.

Abstract: Reinforcement-learning (RL) post-training equips multimodal large reasoning models (MLRMs) with exploratory chains of thought (CoT), substantially improving visual reasoning. However, we find that this capability introduces a distinct privacy vulnerability: even when a sensitive fact is successfully unlearned from the final answer, the model may still reproduce it in its reasoning trace. This leakage is substantially more pronounced in natively RL-trained MLRMs than in their non -reasoning base models, revealing a privacy risk that existing unlearning methods are not designed to address. We show that RL-induced exploration leaves sensitive content with a distinctive token-level entropy signature that is largely absent from base models. Based on this observation, we propose LEMUR, a fully training-free, inference-time unlearning framework for natively RL-trained multimodal models. LEMUR uses entropy dynamics as a control signal to identify when sensitive reasoning begins and when sanitization should stop. During this interval, it redirects the reasoning trajectory through entropy-modulated visual-anchor latent injection, replacing committed tokens with sanitized, probability-weighted embeddings re-grounded in the input image. Across diverse MLRMs, LEMUR consistently outperforms existing unlearning met hods in suppressing both reasoning-trace and answer leakage, while better preserving non-sensitive utility and output fluency. These results demonstrate that RL-induced entropy dynamics provide a distinctive signal for privacy leakage and that exploiting this signal enables effective training-free unlearning for reasoning-capable multimodal models.

</details>


### [252] [REOPD: Reliability-Adaptive Reward Extrapolation for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11698)
*Yang Sun,Lichao Ma,Houyuan Qin,Yuxin Liu,Hanyang Lu,Yao Zhu,Pinlong Cai,Guohang Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: REOPD introduces reliability-adaptive reward extrapolation for on-policy distillation by using a token-level compatibility weight and batch-level adaptive budget to produce a token-wise lambda_b,t = 1 + γ_b q_t. This avoids a single global coefficient, reducing reward hacking and instability while preserving teacher alignment. It requires no extra verifiers or rollout. Empirically, REOPD outperforms G-OPD in single-teacher math and multi-teacher scenarios, and matches G-OPD on single-teacher code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy distillation with ExOPD uses a single global extrapolation coefficient λ for all tokens, which can cause extreme reward exaggeration (reward hacking) and unstable training; optimal λ and its domain-dependence require costly sweeps. A per-token, batch-adaptive extrapolation mechanism could maintain alignment while selectively extrapolating in reliable directions.

Method: REOPD combines a token-level compatibility weight q_t with a batch-level budget γ_b to form a token-wise coefficient λ_{b,t} = 1 + γ_b q_t. This adaptor preserves teacher alignment while extrapolating along reliable token-directions. It operates with standard OPD without needing verifiers, reward models, value models, or extra rollouts.

Result: In experiments, REOPD outperforms G-OPD on single-teacher mathematics and on both domains in the multi-teacher setting, while matching G-OPD on single-teacher code.

Conclusion: REOPD provides effective, fine-grained reliability adaptation across domains and teacher configurations, without extra components, improving stability and performance over global-coefficient ExOPD-like methods.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own trajectories under dense token-level supervision from a teacher. Reward-extrapolation methods such as ExOPD amplify the teacher-reference log-likelihood ratio to move beyond direct imitation, but apply a single global coefficient $λ$ to every token. This can drive the student to fit extreme peaks in the implicit reward, causing reward hacking and unstable training, and the optimal $λ$ varies across domains, requiring costly sweeps. We propose REOPD, a reliability-adaptive reward extrapolation framework for OPD. REOPD combines a token-level compatibility weight with a batch-level adaptive budget, yielding a token-wise coefficient $λ_{b,t}=1+γ_b q_t$ that preserves teacher alignment while selectively extrapolating along reliable teacher-reference directions. It requires no verifier, reward model, value model, or extra rollout beyond standard OPD. REOPD outperforms G-OPD on single-teacher mathematics and on both domains in the multi-teacher setting, while matching G-OPD on single-teacher code, demonstrating effective fine-grained reliability adaptation across domains and teacher configurations.

</details>


### [253] [Consolidator: Learning Persistent Routed Memory Across Context Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11701)
*Sungwoo Goo,Hwi-yeol Yun,Sangkeun Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Learned consolidation with PMNet's Consolidator lets LTM be read as content and used as an access-state to guide memory routing, with a tiny trainable footprint yielding large gains in updated-mapping recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dissociate persistence from influence on retrieval: can a slow storage (LTM) also shape future memory access via routing decisions, rather than only storing data?

Method: Use Phasor Memory Network (PMNet) with a shared slot-local Consolidator operator; freeze backbone/memory interface; train only ~12.35k Consolidator parameters (0.041% of a 29.95M model); evaluate on a two-segment modulo-10 mapping task where the second segment updates the same memory address; after consolidation and clear, a held-out query must retrieve the updated mapping from LTM; compare learned consolidation vs forced identity accumulation; evaluate with and without LTM routing (hierarchical router); five paired runs from the same STM-pretraining checkpoint.

Result: Direct LTM routing raised updated-mapping recall from 44.38±1.94% to 87.02±1.76% (+42.64±1.10 pp); immediate STM recall remained 89.90% in both conditions; learned consolidation outperformed forced identity accumulation by 21.40±1.91 pp without routing and 68.70±1.76 pp with routing; 0.041% of parameters trained; consolidation enables LTM to be retrieved and to condition subsequent slot access.

Conclusion: Consolidated LTM functions as both retrievable content and an access state that shapes which explicit-memory slots are accessed next; even with minimal trainable consolidation, the LTM can actively influence retrieval behavior, supporting memory-system designs where non-volatile stores guide access patterns.

Abstract: Copying short-term memory (STM) into a slower store can preserve state across a context boundary, but persistence alone does not ensure that the retained state influences subsequent memory access. We test this distinction in a Phasor Memory Network (PMNet) using Consolidator, a shared slot-local operator that transforms routed STM before accumulating it into long-term memory (LTM), without replaying the source tokens. After each consolidation, the KV cache and STM are cleared. The retained LTM can still be read and is also fed into the hierarchical router, thereby conditioning which explicit-memory slots subsequent inputs access. We evaluate this mechanism on a two-segment modulo-10 mapping task in which the second segment updates the mapping at the same memory address. Following a second consolidation and reset, a held-out query must recover the updated mapping from LTM. The backbone and memory interface are frozen, leaving only 12.35K Consolidator parameters trainable (0.041\% of a 29.95M model). Across five paired runs from the same STM-pretraining checkpoint, direct LTM routing raises updated-mapping recall from $44.38\pm1.94\%$ to $87.02\pm1.76\%$ ($+42.64\pm1.10$ percentage points), while immediate STM recall remains 89.90\% in both conditions; both train separate Consolidators and retain the same LTM read paths. Learned consolidation outperforms forced identity accumulation by $21.40\pm1.91$ percentage points without routing and $68.70\pm1.76$ with routing. Thus, on this task, consolidated LTM serves as both retrievable content and an access state that shapes subsequent slot selection.

</details>


### [254] [Robust and Efficient Noisy-Label Time-Series Classification via Dynamic Time Warping Based Granular Ball Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11704)
*Ziqiang Li,Yun Liu,Gouhei Tanaka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DTW-GBC organizes training samples into granular balls for DTW-based classification, improving robustness to label noise and reducing inference comparisons; two granular-ball constructions perform well under symmetric label noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DTW-based nearest-neighbor classifiers are vulnerable to mislabeled training samples and require many DTW computations at inference time; a robust and efficient alternative is needed for time-series classification.

Method: Build granular balls by clustering temporally similar training samples and perform classification at the granule level; propose two granular-ball construction strategies for DTW-GBC; evaluate performance under label noise.

Result: On four benchmark datasets with symmetric label noise, the two DTW-GBC variants mitigate performance degradation and require substantially fewer DTW comparisons than DTW-based 1-NN during inference.

Conclusion: DTW-GBC offers a favorable balance between classification robustness to label noise and inference efficiency in time-series classification.

Abstract: Dynamic Time Warping (DTW)-based Nearest-Neighbor (NN) classifiers are effective for time-series classification but are vulnerable to mislabeled training samples and require numerous DTW computations during inference. We propose DTW-based Granular Ball Computing (DTW-GBC), which organizes temporally similar training samples into granular balls and performs classification at the granule level. We further develop two granular-ball construction strategies for DTW-GBC. Experiments on four benchmark datasets with symmetric label noise show that the two DTW-GBC variants generally mitigate the performance degradation caused by label noise while requiring substantially fewer comparisons than DTW-based 1-NN during inference. These findings suggest that DTW-GBC provides a favorable balance between classification robustness and inference efficiency.

</details>


### [255] [High-dimensional Multi-objective Bayesian Optimization with Learned Variable Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11713)
*Hongyan Wang,Jiayu Huang,Haotian Zheng,Xin Gao,Chi Ding,Ying Liu,Xia Wang,Qing Xu,Keqiang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ViaMOBO introduces a variable interaction analysis-based decomposition for high-dimensional multi-objective Bayesian optimization, enabling local BO in subspaces and handling separable, partially separable, or non-separable objectives. It shows improved Pareto-front approximation over baselines on synthetic and real-world benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MOBO methods scale poorly with high-dimensional decision spaces due to exponential sampling; there is a need to exploit variable interactions to enable scalable optimization.

Method: Construct a variable interaction analysis model to decide whether the decision variables can be partitioned into subspaces (completely/partially) or must be treated jointly. Perform local Bayesian optimization within each subspace/subspace combination, effectively decomposing the problem and addressing separability structure without strong a priori assumptions.

Result: Empirical evaluation on synthetic and real-world benchmarks shows ViaMOBO outperforms state-of-the-art MOBO baselines in approximating the Pareto front for high-dimensional expensive multi-objective problems.

Conclusion: ViaMOBO provides a generic, scalable MOBO framework for high-dimensional decision spaces by leveraging variable interaction analysis to enable decomposition and local optimization, achieving improved Pareto front approximation without strong presuppositions about objective separability.

Abstract: Multi-objective Bayesian optimization (MOBO) is effective in identifying the Pareto fronts for expensive black-box problems. However, most current MOBO approaches are limited to low-dimensional decision space due to its exponential sampling complexity. This paper presents decision variable interaction analysis-based MOBO, ViaMOBO, a generic framework for expensive multi-objective problems with high-dimensional decision space. The key idea of ViaMOBO is that it utilizes a variable interaction analysis model to determine whether the decision space can be completely or partially divided, and then performs local Bayesian optimization in the divided decision subspaces. Through the variable analysis model, it can be derived whether the objectives in black-box problems are separable, partially separable, or non-separable based on the potential independent or interdependent relationships among decision variables without any strong assumptions. We compare ViaMOBO with the state-of-the-art MOBO methods on both synthetic and real-world benchmarks. The experimental results demonstrate that ViaMOBO outperforms other related MOBO baselines in approximating the Pareto front of high-dimensional expensive multi-objective problems.

</details>


### [256] [Chain-of-Thought Shows the Path to a Tree: Realizing Branching Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11716)
*Debanjan Dutta,Anish Chakrabarty,Swagatam Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Provides explicit depth-bounded CoT realizations of DFS and Dijkstra using two-layer hard-attention decoders; shows linear-step computation of Strahler numbers and tree width, via Dyck-path bijection; handles arbitrary n-ary trees without layer normalization or positional encodings, illustrating a concrete NC^1-hardness witness for the CoT hierarchy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between high-level complexity-theoretic characterizations of chain-of-thought and explicit, depth-bounded constructions; demonstrate that CoT can realize classical branching algorithms within tractable step budgets.

Method: Construct unique hard-attention decoders implementing DFS (two layers) and Dijkstra (two layers) as a shared computational substrate; reuse the DFS decoder to compute the Strahler number of an n-vertex tree in 2n−1 steps (four layers) and reuse the Dijkstra decoder to compute the tree’s width in n−1 steps (three layers); realize the bijection between ordered trees and Dyck paths via the DFS construction and emit the path during traversal; provide independent path-representation constructions for both measures, without layer normalization or positional encodings, for arbitrary n-ary trees.

Result: Concrete depth-bounded CoT constructions realizing DFS and Dijkstra, enabling linear-step computation of branching measures: Strahler number and width; path-representation constructions via Dyck-path bijection; establishes a non-trivial witness for the linear-step regime of the CoT hierarchy and extends to arbitrary trees.

Conclusion: The work offers a tangible, depth-bounded CoT instantiation for fundamental algorithms, delivering evidence that chain-of-thought can reach meaningful branching-complexity tasks within strict step budgets; it also links CoT with classic combinatorial representations and invites further exploration of additional algorithms and architectures.

Abstract: Chain of Thought (CoT) lifts the expressive ceiling of bounded-depth Transformers, with characterizations tying the number of CoT steps to circuit complexity classes. What remains largely missing are concrete instantiations with explicit, depth-bounded constructions, and the traversal procedures such characterizations presuppose. We close this gap for branching complexity. We give CoT realizations of depth-first search (DFS) and of Dijkstra algorithm, the latter subsuming breadth-first search, by unique hard-attention decoders of at most two layers, and use them as a shared computational substrate: reusing the DFS decoder yields the Strahler number of an $n$-vertex tree in $2n-1$ steps with four layers, and reusing the Dijkstra decoder yields its width in $n-1$ steps with three. Since computing the Strahler number of a binary tree given as a term is \textsf{NC\textsuperscript{1}}-complete, and our constructions handle arbitrary $n$-ary trees without layer normalization or positional encodings, this is a non-trivial witness for the linear-step regime of the CoT hierarchy. Exploiting the classical bijection between ordered trees and Dyck paths, itself realized by our DFS construction, which emits the path as it traverses, we give independent constructions for both measures on the path representation.

</details>


### [257] [Epiplexity Guided Data Selection and Generation for Out-of-Distribution Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11746)
*Ellen Su,Andres Potapczynski,Shikai Qiu,Edward Hughes,Andrew Gordon Wilson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Operationalizes epiplexity as online signals for data selection and synthetic data generation, showing that data with higher structural information improves zero-shot and fine-tuning transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what data fosters generalization across unseen tasks and whether a measure of structural information (epiplexity) can predict and enhance transfer.

Method: Data selection: fit scaling laws to training loss across natural domains to predict epiplexity gain as a function of tokens and adjust domain sampling weights during training. Synthetic data: define generator reward as the change in learner epiplexity over a data buffer and optimize with REINFORCE toward an epiplexity-maximizing distribution.

Result: Higher epiplexity predicts improved downstream performance on zero-shot and fine-tuning tasks, supporting the idea that structure-rich data yields transferable representations.

Conclusion: Epiplexity-guided data selection and synthetic data generation can enhance generalization, aligning data richness in structural information with better transfer across domains.

Abstract: Modern systems are increasingly expected to transfer across tasks not specified during training. What data facilitates generalization in these new, unanticipated settings? One hypothesis is that data with more structural information could contain shared circuits and subprograms that could be recycled in a wider array of downstream settings. Epiplexity, a recently proposed measure of the structural information a compute-bounded learner can extract from data, provides a mechanism to reason about this relationship. In this paper, we show how to operationalize epiplexity as an online training signal for data selection and synthetic data generation. For selection, we fit scaling laws to the training loss curves of natural data domains to predict the expected epiplexity gain as a function of training tokens, and use this signal to adaptively determine the sampling weights over domains during training. For synthetic data generation, we define a generator's reward as the change in learner epiplexity over a buffer of previously generated data and use REINFORCE policy gradients to guide the generator toward an epiplexity-maximizing distribution. In both cases, higher epiplexity predicts improved downstream performance on zero-shot and fine-tuning based tasks, supporting the hypothesis that data rich in structural information yield representations that transfer across domains.

</details>


### [258] [MOON: Multi-Objective OrthoNormalized Updates for Multitask Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11749)
*Shiji Zhou,Kunlin Lyu,Lei Zhang,Ruodong Wang,Yifan Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MOON performs gradient manipulation for multi-task learning using matrix-geometry (spectral-nuclear norm) and orthonormalized gradients, improving optimization efficiency and final performance with convergence guarantees in smooth non-convex settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Euclidean gradient manipulation treats parameters as vectors, ignoring the matrix structure of modern models (e.g., Transformers), which may prevent using true steepest-descent directions under matrix geometry. Adopting matrix geometry aims to yield better Pareto-optimal updates.

Method: Introduce MOON (Multi-Objective OrthoNormalized Updates): gradient manipulation under spectral-nuclear norm geometry for matrix-valued parameters, using an orthonormalized manipulated gradient for updates. Provides convergence theory for smooth non-convex objectives: O(T^{-1/2}) Pareto-stationarity in deterministic setting and O(T^{-1/4}) under stochastic gradients.

Result: Empirical experiments show MOON consistently improves optimization efficiency and final multi-task performance across benchmarks. Code is released at the provided URL.

Conclusion: Leveraging matrix-geometry in MOOO updates yields better optimization trajectories and performance, with theoretical convergence guarantees and practical effectiveness in multi-task learning.

Abstract: Multi-objective optimization (MOO) has demonstrated significant success in multi-task learning by mitigating task conflicts through gradient manipulation. However, most existing methods flatten model parameters into vectors and perform gradient manipulation under Euclidean geometry, thereby overlooking the matrix structure prevalent in modern architectures such as Transformers. In this paper, we show that gradient manipulation in Euclidean space does not generally yield the steepest descent direction under matrix geometry, potentially limiting optimization efficiency. Drawing from the theory of steepest descent for matrix-valued parameters, we propose MOON (Multi-Objective OrthoNormalized Updates), which performs gradient manipulation under spectral--nuclear norm geometry and uses the orthonormalized manipulated gradient for parameter updates. Theoretically, for smooth non-convex objectives, we establish convergence of the averaged Pareto-stationarity measure at rates of $\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/2})$ in the deterministic setting and $\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/4})$ under stochastic gradients. Empirical results across various benchmarks show that MOON consistently improves both optimization efficiency and final multi-task performance. Our code is available at https://github.com/KunlinLyu/MOON.

</details>


### [259] [TradingMoE: Routing the Right Experts in Evolving Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11785)
*Chang Zhou,Xingtong Yu,Minbin Huang,Zhennan Wu,Yuan Fang,Hong Cheng,Xinming Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TradingMoE introduces a trading-oriented sparse MoE that augments a frozen dense LLM with lightweight residual experts, using a Query-Key router and a dynamic inactive-expert replacement mechanism to adapt to market conditions and improve returns, outperforming 22 baselines on stock and crypto data with persistent gains in forward deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MoE routers poorly reflect each expert's contribution to trading decisions and fail to signal when inactive experts become relevant as market regimes change; there is a compact, low-dimensional structure in token-level expert usefulness that can be exploited for efficient routing.

Method: Freeze a dense LLM and attach lightweight residual experts. Implement a Query-Key router that encodes required expert context as a low-dimensional query and matches it to learnable expert keys. Use a sparse Top-k routing. During training, sample inactive experts and estimate whether they should replace the weakest expert in the current Top-k, enabling dynamic adaptation while maintaining sparsity.

Result: Empirical evaluation against 22 baselines on stock and cryptocurrency markets shows substantial improvements in cumulative return: +30.89% (stock) and +30.7% (crypto) over the best-performing baselines. Forward-only deployment (rolling paper-trading) confirms the advantage persists.

Conclusion: TradingMoE demonstrates that a trading-focused sparse MoE with a dynamic, signal-aware routing mechanism can improve decision quality under varying market conditions while preserving sparse computation and generalizing to forward deployment.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong potential for financial analysis and trading, but direct trading remains challenging because the predictive capabilities required can vary across assets, decision fields, and market conditions. Existing LLM-based trading systems either coordinate human-defined external experts or adopt conventional internal Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routers that do not directly evaluate how individual experts contribute to trading decisions. Moreover, these routers receive no direct signal indicating when an inactive expert has become more suitable as market conditions change. We find that native router scores poorly reflect how much individual experts improve trading decisions, frequently leaving better alternatives unselected. We further reveal that token-specific expert usefulness exhibits a compact low-dimensional structure. Based on these findings, we propose TradingMoE, a trading-oriented sparse MoE that augments a frozen dense LLM with lightweight residual experts. We introduce a Query-Key router that represents the expertise required by each token under the current market context as a low-dimensional query and matches it with learnable expert keys. We further propose a sparse expert selection update mechanism that samples a few inactive experts during training and estimates whether they should replace the weakest expert in the current Top-k route. This mechanism enables the router to update expert selection as market conditions change while preserving sparse computation. Experiments against 22 baselines on stock and cryptocurrency markets show that TradingMoE improves cumulative return over the best-performing baselines by 30.89% and 30.7%, respectively. Rolling paper-trading experiments further demonstrate that its advantage persists under forward-only deployment.

</details>


### [260] [High-Order Liquid Evidence Encoding for Gradual GNSS Spoofing Detection in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11790)
*Muhammad Ayub Sabir,Junbiao Pang,Fatima Ashraf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A causal high-order liquid evidence framework for GNSS spoofing detection in autonomous driving, using multi-stream evidence (residual, first- and second-order) with adaptive liquid encoders to capture evolving inconsistencies between GNSS and onboard motion; achieves state-of-the-art F1-scores on AV-GPS subsets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Subtle GNSS spoofing gradually corrupts localization by increasing GNSS-motion inconsistency; existing methods rely on static features or a single residual and fail to model temporal evolution.

Method: Compute physics-guided GNSS–motion inconsistency residual by comparing GNSS-implied displacement with onboard-motion displacement; create three evidence streams for residual level, and its first- and second-order discrete variations; select contextual cues per stream; process each with an adaptive liquid encoder; hierarchically couple temporal states to predict spoofing at the window endpoint using current and past observations.

Result: Evaluated on three AV-GPS subsets: Dataset 1 F1 = 0.9535; Dataset 3 F1 = 0.9777; capable of detecting normal-to-attack transitions within four sampling steps on Dataset 3.

Conclusion: The approach effectively models high-order temporal dynamics of GNSS inconsistency for timely spoofing detection, outperforming other temporal models; code and data are publicly available for reproducibility.

Abstract: Accurate Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS)-based localization is essential for safe and reliable autonomous driving. However, spoofing attacks can manipulate vehicle position estimates. Continuous and subtle attacks are particularly difficult to detect because individual GNSS observations may remain plausible while the inconsistency between GNSS-implied displacement and onboard vehicle motion gradually increases. Existing methods often rely on static vehicle-behavior features or a single residual signal and do not explicitly model this evolution. To address this problem, we propose a causal high-order liquid evidence framework for GNSS spoofing detection. The method first constructs a physics-guided GNSS--motion inconsistency residual by comparing GNSS-implied displacement with onboard-motion-derived displacement. It then forms separate evidence streams for the residual level and its first- and second-order discrete variations, with relevant contextual cues selected according to the evidence order. Each stream is processed by a separate adaptive liquid encoder, and the resulting temporal states are hierarchically coupled to predict spoofing at the window endpoint using only current and past observations. Experiments on three subsets of the real-world AV-GPS dataset show that the proposed method achieves the highest F1-scores among the evaluated temporal models on Dataset~1 and Dataset~3, reaching 0.9535 and 0.9777, respectively. On Dataset~3, it detects both labeled normal-to-attack transitions within four sampling steps. Code and datasets are publicly available at: https://github.com/pangjunbiao/GNSS_Spoofing.git.

</details>


### [261] [Orientation, not magnitude: the causal structure of task-vector interference in merged language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11797)
*Chencheng Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cross-term dynamics, not magnitude, govern task-arithmetic model merging. Layerwise cross-term transport dominates merges; erasure travels via propagation and is nullified unless applied near the output. Instruction wrappers modulate the interference by amplifying templates, while magnitude (e.g., bfloat16 roughness) is a coarse, imperfect correlate. Six-displacement basin tests reveal an attractor direction for forward pass, with causally load-bearing erasure that saturates; 46 preregistered predictions confirmed or falsified as reported.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diagnose why task-arithmetic based model merging fails across LLMs and identify the true causal axis—beyond naïve magnitude—and establish robust, generalizable diagnostics or interventions.

Method: Exact layerwise decomposition of flux across merged models; factorial ledger of cross-terms; six-point basin test with displacement starting points; causal erasure experiments; comparison with instruction wrappers vs length-matched controls; cross-family model analysis; preregistration of 46 predictions.

Result: The cross-term transport dominates the layerwise flux (about 65–70% in both model families) with amplification per late block; erasing the term is undone by forward propagation unless near the output; the forward-pass direction is an attractor and is causally load-bearing for interference; instruction wrappers can drastically reduce or amplify interference depending on their effect on the cross-term; magnitude is a coarse proxy and often reflects quantization roughness (±15% universality of bfloat16 generation); cross-term generation differences of up to 1.9x correspond to 14x–337x differences in removable interference; all 46 predictions preregistered and reported with falsifications where applicable.

Conclusion: Magnitude alone is insufficient as a diagnostic; the layerwise cross-term direction and its transport are the primary causal axis in these merges. Interventions near the cross-term (or altering templates via wrappers) can materially change interference, while typical magnitude-based indicators fail to generalize across families. The results emphasize path-dependent, propagation-sensitive interference governed by an attractor direction and robust falsification reporting.

Abstract: Model merging by task arithmetic works until it doesn't, and the field diagnoses why with magnitudes: layerwise representation bias, deviations from cross-task linearity, parameter overlap. Tracking the exact layerwise cross-term of merged LLMs through a factorial ledger and intervening on it directly, we find magnitude insufficient - and inconsistent across model families - as a diagnostic axis. An exact decomposition of the layerwise flux shows it is dominated by amplifying transport of the existing cross-term (~65-70% in both families, gain >1 per late block), and erasing the term is undone by propagation - rebuilt to 99% of its norm at cosine 0.99 - unless applied near the output; a basin test with six starting displacements establishes the carried direction as an attractor of the forward pass. That direction is causally load-bearing: erasure along it removes expressed interference dose-dependently and saturates at exact erasure, while norm-matched wrong-direction controls fail or backfire. Instruction wrappers gate the effect: the same erasure finds 13x less relative interference to remove under a wrapper that internally amplifies the cross-term, because the wrapper drowns the interaction in a template-pinned main effect rather than shrinking it - a structure that replicates across further instruction templates but not under a length-matched control. Magnitude, by contrast, is at best a coarse correlate, and the striking +-15% "universality" of naive bfloat16 generation turns out to be quantization roughness. Task pairs whose local cross-term generation differs by at most 1.9x differ by 14x-337x in causally removable interference. All 46 predictions were preregistered and frozen before their data; falsifications, including of our own headline expectations and of behavioral recovery under a validated continuous endpoint, are reported as such.

</details>


### [262] [JAPE: Joint Anomaly Prediction and Intrinsic Explanation in Multivariate Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11801)
*Yian Wei,Yuanyuan Yao,Lu Chen,Xiangmin Zhou,Tianyi Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: JAPE introduces a joint anomaly prediction and explanation framework that models evolving dependency structures rather than only numerical deviations, achieving notable performance gains and native variable-level explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing anomaly prediction methods focus on future numerical deviations and lack native explanations; subtle dependency changes (precursors) are not captured.

Method: Proposes DSTR backbone to decouple temporal/spatial modeling with learnable lag aggregation; dual-view alerting combining numerical forecasts with evolving dependency graphs; Native Predictive Explanation (NPE) reuses predicted graphs to rank variables by structural deviations.

Result: Experimental results on five real-world benchmarks across three horizons show gains: ~19.7% F1 and ~41.3% AUC-PR; 26.6% improvement in MRR for explainability.

Conclusion: JAPE offers a principled approach to joint prediction and explanation by dependency-structure modeling, enabling early detection via structural precursors and native variable-level explanations; demonstrates strong predictive performance and interpretability.

Abstract: Multivariate time-series anomaly prediction aims to identify whether and when anomalies will occur over a future horizon from historical observations. Existing methods primarily characterize anomalies as deviations in future numerical values, which may overlook subtle dependency changes induced by weak anomaly precursors and provide no native variable-level explanation together with the alert. To bridge these gaps, we propose JAPE, a Joint Anomaly Prediction and Explanation framework that lifts anomaly prediction from numerical-deviation modeling to dependency-structure modeling. JAPE is the first anomaly prediction framework to explicitly model evolving dependency structures for both point-wise alerting and native variable-level explanation. Specifically, JAPE (i) proposes a Decoupled Spatio-Temporal Representation (DSTR) backbone that decouples temporal and spatial modeling and captures lag-aware dependencies via learnable lag aggregation, thereby perceiving structural precursors before numerical deviations emerge; (ii) designs a dual-view alerting mechanism that fuses numerical forecasts with evolving dependency graphs for point-wise anomaly prediction, capturing structural evidence even under subtle numerical deviations; and (iii) presents Native Predictive Explanation (NPE), which directly reuses the predicted dependency graphs to rank variables by structural deviations without additional models or training. Extensive experiments on five real-world benchmarks across three prediction horizons demonstrate that JAPE improves average F1 and AUC-PR by 19.7% and 41.3%, respectively, while improving explainability with 26.6% gain in MRR.

</details>


### [263] [Learning with Bilevel-Minimax Optimization for Efficient and Reliable Transfer Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11815)
*Yaohua Liu,Yifan Guo,Jiaxin Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BMAT proposes a bilevel-minimax framework to unify initialization, surrogate adaptation, and perturbation for transfer-based adversarial attacks; introduces a bottom-up solver with a Soft Weight Modulator and an Implicit Gradient Approximator; achieves strong cross-architecture transfer and segmentation attack effectiveness, with code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transfer-based adversarial attacks are limited by the intertwined roles of initialization, surrogate adaptation, and gradient dynamics. Previous work typically handles these components in isolation; a joint framework could better capture their coupling and improve cross-model generalization.

Method: Formulate the problem as a bilevel-minimax optimization. The outer level models initialization; the inner minimax optimizes adversarial perturbations while adapting the surrogate model to be robust across architectures. A bottom-up solver combines a Soft Weight Modulator (adjusts component weighting) and an Implicit Gradient Approximator (efficiently estimates gradients under the bilevel setting) to realize ternary coupling among initialization, surrogate adaptation, and perturbation optimization. The paper also offers theoretical analysis of the optimization dynamics.

Result: Empirical evaluation on classification and segmentation benchmarks shows BMAT outperforms more than 10 strong baselines across over 30 victim models. It improves both intra- and cross-architecture transfer and yields up to a 2x reduction in mIoU for segmentation, indicating substantially stronger attack performance. Code is provided at the stated repository.

Conclusion: BMAT advances transfer-based attack research by providing a principled bilevel-minimax formulation and a practical solver that jointly optimizes initialization, surrogate adaptation, and perturbation. It delivers consistent gains across diverse victim models and tasks, backed by theoretical insights. Potential future work includes refining computational efficiency, extending to other modalities, and exploring defense implications.

Abstract: Transfer-based adversarial attacks craft adversarial examples using surrogate models to mislead black-box victim models. Beyond perturbation generation, transferability is fundamentally governed by the coupling of initialization, surrogate adaptation, and gradient dynamics. We revisit this challenge from a bilevel-minimax perspective and propose BMAT (Bilevel-Minimax Adversarial Transfer). The bilevel formulation captures the dependency between initialization and perturbation, while the inner minimax problem promotes surrogate robustness for cross-architecture generalization. Algorithmically, we develop an integrated bottom-up solver that combines a Soft Weight Modulator and an Implicit Gradient Approximator to enable ternary coupling among initialization, surrogate adaptation, and perturbation optimization. We further provide theoretical insights into the optimization dynamics of the proposed bilevel-minimax framework. Extensive experiments on classification and segmentation benchmarks show that BMAT outperforms more than 10 strong baselines across more than 30 victim models, improving both intra- and cross-architecture transfer and yielding up to a 2x reduction in mIoU. Code is available at https://github.com/callous-youth/BMAT.

</details>


### [264] [Towards Understanding On-Policy Distillation through the Lens of Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11829)
*Xinmu Ge,Zizhuo Zhang,Yu Huang,Jianing Zhu,Lin Yuan,Wanli Gu,Weichang Wu,Weiran Huang,Xiaolu Zhang,Bo Han,Jun Zhou,Jiangchao Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OPD improves sampling efficiency, maintaining better avg@K across budgets, but its pass@K advantage shifts to the base model as K grows. The apparent gains do not reflect expanded reasoning boundaries; OPD also causes asymmetrical solvability (more problems become unsolvable than solvable), supporting the view of “illusory distillation.”


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess whether On-policy distillation (OPD) truly expands a student model’s reasoning capabilities or mainly improves sampling efficiency, by examining performance as a function of test-time sampling budget and solvability.

Method: Evaluate multiple OPD variants across a range of sampling budgets K using pass@K and avg@K metrics; analyze pass@1024 for problem-level solvability; compare OPD-trained models to pre-OPD base students.

Result: OPD-trained models exhibit consistently higher avg@K across budgets, but the advantage in pass@K diminishes for larger K, with pre-OPD base models outperforming OPD models in pass@K as K grows. The pass@K dynamics reveal stronger small-K performance at the cost of reduced large-K capability. Solvability analysis shows OPD causes more problems to become unsolvable than vice versa, indicating no genuine expansion of problem-solving capability.

Conclusion: OPD behaves like an illusory distillation: gains come from improved sampling efficiency rather than acquiring new reasoning capabilities from the teacher. This has implications for evaluating whether distillation methods truly expand capabilities versus merely enabling more efficient sampling.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) has emerged as a promising post-training technique for enhancing LLM reasoning. It is commonly believed to enable the student model to distill knowledge from a stronger teacher model, thereby expanding capabilities beyond the pre-OPD base model. In this study, we examine this view through the lens of test-time scaling by varying the sampling budget K and evaluating performance with pass@K and avg@K. Specifically, across several OPD variants, we observe that OPD-trained models maintain superior avg@K performance across sampling budgets, while the advantage in pass@K gradually shifts to the pre-OPD base models as K increases. These results suggest that OPD primarily improves sampling efficiency rather than consistently expanding the student's reasoning capability boundary. The pass@K dynamics throughout OPD training further reveal a progressive shift toward stronger small-K performance at the expense of the large-K capability boundary. Furthermore, a problem-level solvability analysis using pass@1024 as the criterion reveals an asymmetry: OPD causes more previously solvable problems to become unsolvable than previously unsolvable problems to become solvable. Together, these findings suggest that, from the perspective of capability expansion, OPD behaves more like an "illusory distillation": its apparent gains arise primarily from improved sampling efficiency rather than from acquiring genuinely new reasoning capabilities from the teacher.

</details>


### [265] [Kernel Methods for Learning Operators with Multiple Inputs and Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11831)
*Adrien Weihs,Chunyang Liao,Jingmin Sun,Hayden Schaeffer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Kernel-based encoder–decoder framework for operator learning that handles multi-input, multi-output operators using operator-valued kernels; convergence determined by the hardest subproblem; introduces KernelMO; achieves competitive to state-of-the-art performance on five parametric PDE families with lower training/inference cost compared to neural operators; offers a lightweight, tractable alternative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning mappings between infinite-dimensional objects (operators) is central in scientific ML. Neural operators can be powerful but may entail high computational costs and opaque training dynamics. A tractable, closed-form kernel approach could provide theoretical guarantees, efficient training/inference, and scalability for multi-input/multi-output operators.

Method: A general kernel-based encoder–decoder framework that separates observation, representation, learning, and reconstruction for operator learning. Extends to multi-input, multi-output operators mapping products of function spaces. Develops theory showing convergence rate is governed by the most challenging constituent approximation problem. Proposes KernelMO, a family of kernel methods with operator-valued and product-space formulations for multiple operator learning. Applies to five parametric PDE families with competitive/state-of-the-art accuracy and reduced training/inference costs.

Result: Theoretical: convergence rates are dictated by the hardest subproblem, not the overall dimensionality. Practical/kernel methods with closed-form training/inference. Empirical: competitive or state-of-the-art predictive accuracy across five PDE families; reduced training and inference costs relative to neural operator architectures.

Conclusion: KernelMO and the broader kernel-based encoder–decoder framework offer an efficient, lightweight alternative to neural operators for multi-input/multi-output operator learning, balancing mathematical tractability with practical performance, and validating applicability across multiple PDEs.

Abstract: Learning mappings between infinite-dimensional objects is a central challenge in scientific machine learning. We introduce a general kernel-based encoder-decoder framework for operator learning that separates observation, representation, learning, and reconstruction. We develop this framework for multi-input, multi-output operator learning, where operators map between products of potentially distinct function spaces. Our approximation theory shows that, although the number of inputs and outputs can increase, the convergence rate is governed by the most challenging constituent approximation problem rather than the overall problem dimension. The framework leads to practical kernel methods with closed-form training and inference, combining mathematical tractability with computational efficiency. We further specialize the approach to multiple operator learning by introducing KernelMO, a family of kernel methods with complementary operator-valued and product-space formulations. Across five families of parametric partial differential equations, the proposed methods achieve competitive or state-of-the-art predictive accuracy while reducing training and inference costs relative to neural operator architectures and deep learning based models, offering an efficient and lightweight alternative.

</details>


### [266] [Air Quality Station Simulation via LSTM and Attention-Based Modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11839)
*Alexander Kostadinov,Petar O. Hristov,Dessislava Petrova-Antonova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A deep-learning model SATADL forecasts multi-hour air-quality and simulates data for a failed monitoring station to serve as a virtual proxy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address data gaps and interruptions in urban air-quality networks caused by hardware faults, by creating a model that can infer missing data and maintain analyses.

Method: Develop SATADL with multi-aspect data extraction to forecast PM10 hours ahead; simulate an unresponsive station; evaluate on four global station datasets; compare against baselines and published DL models.

Result: SATADL achieves better R^2 and RMSE across prediction windows, outperforming baselines; demonstrates viability as a virtual proxy station.

Conclusion: SATADL provides reliable multi-hour forecasts and robust data imputation for station outages, supporting continuous air-quality monitoring.

Abstract: Poor air quality in urban areas is driven by a complex chain of processes and presents a significant public health concern. To better understand and control the mechanisms that determine air quality, cities deploy networks of measurement stations, and launch initiatives for collecting denser data about the concentration of pollutants in the atmosphere. Extracting insights from the stations relies on their reliable and uninterrupted operation. However, hardware is susceptible to faults and black- outs that may result in data unavailability, which affects the overall quality of analyses. In this paper, we present a deep-learning model, called SATADL, which can infer complex relations and output multiple-hour-ahead air-quality forecasts. The goal of the model is to simulate the mea- surements of an unresponsive station until its operation is restored. The architecture of the model, which allows it to extract information from different aspects of the data, is described in detail and a careful examination of all of its components is provided. We demonstrate the performance of SATADL on four sets of air quality stations from around the world, by using it to simulate the concentration of PM10 for periods of hypothetical failures of one of the measurement stations, lasting for as long as 48 hours. A selection of baseline and published deep learning models were trained and used as a benchmark. The results show that SATADL per- forms better across different prediction windows, for both coefficient of determination and root mean squared error, demonstrating its suitability as a virtual proxy station.

</details>


### [267] [Small-Scale Experiments: Are We There Yet?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11859)
*Nicholas Lourie,Kyunghyun Cho,Karen Ullrich,Sanae Lotfi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hyperparameter tuning drives scaling effects; small-scale sensitivity misleads; scaling laws emerge with larger scales under systematic hyperparameter search; a model-centric, holistic methodology is needed; applied to transformer normalization placement showing pre-norm scales better.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain why scaling laws appear unreliable at small scales and propose a framework to recover their predictive power by isolating hyperparameter effects and adopting a scalable, model-centric experimental methodology; demonstrate on normalization placement in transformers.

Method: Ablate the standard scaling recipe, quantify hyperparameter sensitivity across scales, analyze loss-surface dimensionality, synthesize the literature into a new methodology, and test on normalization placement in transformer architectures.

Result: Small models exhibit strong hyperparameter sensitivity; as scale increases, sensitivity decreases and well-tuned hyperparameters account for major performance; scaling laws persist but require extensive search; pre-normalization performs better at larger scales; small-scale experiments can reveal large-scale trends with proper tooling.

Conclusion: Hyperparameters are central to scaling behavior; scaling laws exist but are contingent on proper hyperparameter search; a holistic, model-centric methodology allows reliable extrapolation from small to large scales.

Abstract: Scaling laws promised cost-effective experiments; six years later, they have yet to fully deliver. Instead, researchers have found them unreliable at small scales (starting at 4M parameters) and concluded that sizable models cannot be avoided. We show this is not the case: the confounding factor is hyperparameters. Small models are highly sensitive, but hyperparameter sensitivity fades with scale. This small-scale sensitivity makes scaling laws easy to miss because they only emerge on the fully tuned frontier, and reaching that frontier requires an extensive search far beyond what most ever run. By ablating the basic scaling law recipe, we show well-tuned hyperparameters matter more than any other ingredient. Further, we reveal why those hyperparameters become easier to find: as scale increases, the hyperparameter loss surface becomes lower dimensional. Nevertheless while scaling laws exist in small models, extrapolation hits statistical limitations. A holistic approach is required. Synthesizing our insights with the recent literature, we develop a new methodology for model-centric research and demonstrate it on a question that once took the field years to settle: where to place normalization layers in the transformer architecture. From small-scale experiments, we recover the large scale result: pre-normalization works better as models grow in size. With the right tools and a better understanding, small-scale experiments can deliver on scaling laws' long-awaited promise.

</details>


### [268] [Forward and Inverse Virtual Metrology for Phototransistor Gain: A Hierarchical, Uncertainty-Aware Approach for Small Production Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11868)
*Mahshid Amirabgir,Lorenza Ferrario,Paolo Conci,Mahdieh Amirabgir,Giancarlo Orengo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hierarchical, data-driven metrology for silicon bipolar phototransistors: ~50% of gain variance between runs; develop uncertainty-aware forward predictor, inverse recipe search for target gain, and multi-level quality assessment; dataset and code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predict device gain from process parameters before a run to save cleanroom time and cost. In a small-sample, nested (batch–wafer–die) setting, recipe-only models are inherently limited due to cross-run variance.

Method: Decompose gain variance across process runs to quantify cross-run effects; develop a forward gain predictor with uncertainty estimates; implement an inverse search to generate process recipes achieving a target gain; create a multi-level data-quality assessment tailored to batch/wafer/die, with a cross-level linkage score; release a normalized dataset and analysis code for reproducibility.

Result: Found that roughly half of device gain variance lies between process runs; recipe-only prediction is inherently bounded; provided a forward predictor with uncertainty, an inverse design search for target gain, and a hierarchical data-quality framework with an explicit linkage score; dataset and code are released.

Conclusion: Cross-run variability is a major bottleneck; hierarchical, uncertainty-aware models plus explicit data-quality assessment enable pre-run gain prediction and inverse design with reproducible resources; this approach is well-suited to small-sample, nested fabrication histories.

Abstract: The customization, optimization and stabilization of the process flow of a silicon bipolar phototransistor commits months of cleanroom time before a finished device can be measured, so a model that predicts device gain from process parameters before a run has value out of proportion to its accuracy. We study this problem on a real fabrication history, thirteen to fourteen process runs of a single device: a small-sample, hierarchically structured setting unlike the large-corpus regime of conventional virtual metrology. Decomposing the variance of device gain, we find that roughly half of it lies between process runs rather than within them, so recipe-only prediction is bounded by construction. Building on these findings we provide a forward gain predictor with a relative, uncertainty-aware signal, an inverse search that returns recipes for a target gain, and, as the foundation for all of it, a multi-level data-quality assessment tailored to the nested physical entities of fabrication (batch, wafer, die) with an explicit cross-level linkage score. The normalized dataset and analysis code are released for full reproducibility.

</details>


### [269] [DCM Bandits: Multiplayer Information Asymmetric Cascading Bandits for Multiple Clicks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11873)
*Andy Wang,Charlton Shih,William Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Extends Dependent Click Model (DCM) Bandits to multiplayer, information-asymmetric settings with shared ranked lists and multi-click feedback; provides sublinear regret guarantees in three asymmetric settings; leaves information-theoretic lower bounds open; shows that for small termination probabilities, termination ranking need not be known; experiments underscore the impact of feedback structure (full vs first-click) on exploration and regret.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address learning and optimization in multi-agent ranking with information asymmetry, where multiple agents interact with a common ranked list and may observe multiple clicks. Understand how asymmetry in actions and rewards affects regret and algorithm design, and extend theoretical guarantees beyond single-agent DCM Bandits.

Method: Develop algorithms for three settings that include at least one asymmetry in actions or rewards; prove sublinear regret guarantees; discuss open lower bounds; analyze the special case of small termination probabilities where termination ranking is not required; conduct experiments comparing full feedback vs first-click feedback to study exploration-exploitation coordination.

Result: Sublinear regret bounds are established for the three asymmetric settings; information-theoretic lower bounds for these settings remain open; for small termination probabilities, the termination ranking is not required, improving on prior single-agent results; experiments validate performance across asymmetric environments and reveal the critical role of feedback structure in coordinating exploration and minimizing regret.

Conclusion: The work broadens DCM Bandits to multiplayer, information-asymmetric contexts, providing theoretical regret guarantees and practical guidance on feedback structures, while identifying key open questions on lower bounds and the role of termination ranking in certain regimes.

Abstract: In this work, we extend the Dependent Click Model (DCM) Bandits to a multiplayer information-asymmetric setting, where multiple agents interact with a shared ranked list and may observe multiple clicks per session, introducing new challenges for selection strategies. We study asymmetry in (1) actions and (2) rewards, providing sublinear regret guarantees for three settings where at least one asymmetry is present. Establishing matching information-theoretic lower bounds for these settings is left as an open problem. We further show that for small termination probabilities, the termination ranking need not be known, improving on prior single-agent results. Experiments confirm that our algorithms perform well across asymmetric environments and highlight the critical role of feedback structure, specifically the distinction between full versus first-click feedback, in coordinating exploration and minimizing regret.

</details>


### [270] [Disentangling the Expressivity of RoPE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11909)
*Selim Jerad,Anej Svete,Jiaoda Li,Ryan Cotterell*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Periodic RoPE components, if truly periodic, make RoPE-transformers exactly recognize languages definable in past temporal logic with modular predicates; conventional RoPE rotations do not repeat, yielding a bounded, fixed-offset look-back behavior. Constructed periodic schedules generalize modular languages, while conventional RoPE acts as a locality bias that can hinder long-range, position-invariant access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconcile two popular explanations for RoPE's success (expressivity via modular predicates vs. locality/anchor-based mechanisms) by providing a formal expressivity analysis for fully uniform, finite-precision soft-attention transformers.

Method: Formalize RoPE under fully uniform, finite-precision soft-attention. Prove that if every rotary component is periodic, RoPE transformers recognize exactly the languages definable in past temporal logic with modular predicates. Compare with conventional RoPE, whose rotations never repeat, yielding a precision-dependent bounded simulation of fixed-offset look-back operators. Perform constructed periodic schedules and controlled experiments to test length-generalization on modular languages and assess position-invariant long-context access in conventional RoPE.

Result: Two regimes emerge: periodic RoPE implements modular-language expressivity; conventional RoPE induces a bounded locality bias. Experiments show periodic schedules generalize modular languages across lengths, while conventional RoPE behaves like a bounded-access mechanism that can hurt tasks requiring distant, position-invariant context.

Conclusion: The work unifies prior views of RoPE by showing that expressivity hinges on rotation periodicity. It clarifies when RoPE can support modular, position-aware reasoning and when it acts more like a locality bias, aligning theoretical characterizations with practical usage.

Abstract: Two accounts recur in explanations of the success of rotary position embeddings (RoPE). Expressivity studies associate periodic position information with modular predicates, whereas mechanistic and long-context studies emphasize positional anchors and local offsets. We formalize both accounts for fully uniform, finite-precision soft-attention transformers. We find that, if every rotary component is periodic, RoPE transformers recognize exactly the languages definable in past temporal logic with modular predicates. Conventional RoPE is different: The rotations it computes never repeat. This yields a precision-dependent bounded simulation of fixed-offset look-back operators, rather than an all-length modular characterization. Controlled experiments match this separation: Constructed periodic schedules length-generalize on modular languages, while conventional RoPE behaves more like a bounded locality bias and can impair tasks requiring position-invariant access to distant context. Altogether, our findings shed light on RoPE transformers, bringing theoretical expressivity characterizations closer to models used in practice.

</details>


### [271] [A Factor Graph Approach to Scalable Multi-Output Gaussian Process Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11917)
*Wouter W. L. Nuijten,Esther G. van Pelt,Albert Podusenko,İsmail Şenöz,Wouter M. Kouw*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Forney-style factor-graph formulation for multi-output Gaussian process regression using a chain over a fixed candidate input set, enabling exact Gaussian message passing with complexity O(C(DL^2+L^3)); natural handling of missing observations; scalable for low input dimension and large data in time-series forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the cubic scaling and handling of irregular/missing inputs in multi-output GP regression by exploiting a chain-structured factor graph and linear-Gaussian dynamics.

Method: Construct a Forney-style factor graph where a chain orders C candidate inputs; latent Matérn processes evolve through linear-Gaussian transitions along the chain; L latent processes mix into D outputs via a deterministic mixing factor and per-output scalar observation factors (linear model of coregionalization). Perform exact Gaussian message passing on the chain; omit local factors for missing observations. Complexity: O(C(DL^2+L^3)). Compare against exact kernel-matrix baseline, sparse-variational inducing-point baseline, and nearest-neighbor baseline on synthetic and electricity time-series data.

Result: At low input dimension, the factor-graph posterior closely tracks the exact kernel-matrix posterior; gaps grow with input dimension but remain competitive with approximate baselines. On electricity time-series, the approach matches all baselines in forecast accuracy while scaling linearly with data points; the exact kernel-matrix is infeasible and the inducing-point baseline is slower.

Conclusion: The factor-graph formulation provides accurate and scalable inference for multi-output GP regression with missing observations, excelling in low-dimensional input regimes and large data contexts like time-series forecasting; it offers a practical alternative to fully dense kernel methods and variational approximations.

Abstract: Multi-output Gaussian process regression scales cubically in the number of observations times outputs, and dense kernel-matrix methods need bespoke handling whenever different outputs are observed at different inputs. We express multi-output Gaussian process regression as a Forney-style factor graph in which a nearest-neighbor chain orders a fixed candidate set of $C$ inputs into a one-dimensional sequence. Along this chain, latent Matérn processes evolve through linear-Gaussian transition factors, while the linear model of coregionalization mixes $L$ latent processes into $D$ outputs through a deterministic mixing factor and per-output scalar observation factors. Posterior computation reduces to exact Gaussian message passing on the chain at cost $\mathcal{O}(C(DL^2 + L^3))$ after chain construction, and missing observations omit their local factor without any covariance-matrix restructuring. The formulation therefore scales in the number of data samples and in the rate of missing observations, while remaining best suited to candidate sets in low input dimension.We compare the factor-graph formulation against an exact kernel-matrix baseline, a sparse-variational inducing-point baseline, and a nearest-neighbor baseline on a synthetic input-dimension sweep and on electricity time series forecasting. At low input dimension the factor-graph posterior tracks the exact kernel-matrix posterior closely, and the gap grows gradually as input dimension increases while staying competitive with both approximate baselines. On the electricity time series our factor-graph formulation matches all three baselines in forecast accuracy while scaling linearly in the number of data points, where the exact kernel-matrix method becomes infeasible and the inducing-point baseline remains substantially slower.

</details>


### [272] [Distillation of Foundation Models for Time-dependent PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11937)
*Daniel Musekamp,Boshra Ariguib,Andrei Manolache,Mathias Niepert*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TREX distills a pretrained PDE foundation model into a compact student by augmenting limited downstream data with long synthetic rollouts from the teacher (with optional noise). The student learns to imitate the teacher over long horizons and can incorporate inductive biases, achieving comparable or better accuracy with far fewer parameters and large speedups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome the computational burden of large foundation models for time-dependent PDEs while maintaining accuracy and generalization, enabling fast surrogates in low-data regimes.

Method: Start from a fine-tuned teacher. Generate long synthetic trajectories via teacher rollouts (optionally with periodic noise) to form an expanded training distribution without requiring explicit initial-condition sampling. Train a smaller student to imitate the teacher on these rollouts, enabling long-horizon forecasting and local recovery behavior. Allow the student to incorporate task-specific inductive biases (e.g., equivariance) not enforced by the teacher.

Result: On multiple PDE benchmarks, the compact student matches or surpasses the teacher’s accuracy while using orders of magnitude fewer parameters and achieving over an order-of-magnitude speedup in inference.

Conclusion: TREX provides an effective knowledge-distillation pathway for time-dependent PDEs, delivering fast, accurate surrogate models that retain teacher performance in data-scarce settings.

Abstract: Foundation models for time-dependent partial differential equations (PDEs) are trained on large and diverse collections of physical systems and can generalize effectively to new downstream tasks. After fine-tuning on only a few trajectories from a target domain, they can achieve strong accuracy in low-data regimes.
