However, these models are typically large and computationally intensive, limiting their usefulness as fast surrogates for numerical solvers. We propose Teacher Rollout Extension (TREX), a knowledge distillation framework that transfers the predictive capability of a pretrained foundation model into a compact and efficient student. Starting from a fine-tuned teacher, TREX augments limited downstream data by generating long synthetic trajectories through teacher rollouts, optionally with periodic noise injection. This procedure samples from the teacher-induced rollout distribution without requiring explicit knowledge of the initial-condition distribution, while exposing the student to long-horizon states and local recovery behavior around states encountered during autoregressive prediction. The student can further incorporate task-specific inductive biases, such as equivariance, that the teacher does not necessarily enforce. We evaluate TREX on multiple PDE benchmarks. The resulting students can match or surpass the teacher's accuracy while reducing the number of parameters by several orders of magnitude and achieving more than an order-of-magnitude speedup in inference.

</details>


### [273] [TailBooster: A Dual-Layer Generative Framework for Extreme Value Augmentation with Operational Validity Enforcement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11951)
*Karim Aly,Alexei Sharpanskykh,Jacco Hoekstra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TailBooster combines tail-focused generative modeling with autoencoder-based data cleaning to better model extreme events in mixed-type tabular flight data, achieving large MAE reductions for extreme-air-time and extreme-arrival-delay prediction while ensuring operational validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extreme events in air transport cause cascading disruptions and are rare, leaving limited training signal. Conventional generative models underrepresent distributional tails and may produce operationally infeasible samples. There is a lack of approaches addressing both tail fidelity and feasibility for mixed-type tabular data.

Method: A dual-layer framework: (1) a statistical layer uses the interquartile range to extract extremes and provide tail-focused training data to a Tabular Variational Autoencoder; (2) a deep learning layer uses autoencoder-based cleaning to discard synthetic records violating historical operational envelopes. The approach is data-driven and model-agnostic.

Result: Evaluated on US flight records across five metrics—diversity, statistical similarity, fidelity, operational validity, and utility—with data-driven cleaning improving operational validity and augmented tails improving extreme-event prediction. Across six regression models, training on TailBooster data reduced MAE by 47–49% for extreme air time and 29–57% for extreme arrival delay versus conventional synthetic data (and similar gains when real data is enriched with synthetic extremes).

Conclusion: TailBooster is a fully data-driven, model-agnostic framework that addresses both tail representation and operational feasibility in synthetic data; it is transferable to domains with critical extreme-event prediction and no predefined domain rules.

Abstract: Extreme events in air transport, such as severe arrival delays and abnormal air times, cause cascading network disruptions with substantial operational, economic, and safety costs. Such events are rare in historical records, leaving insufficient training signal for machine learning models. Synthetic data augmentation offers a principled solution, but conventional generative models under-represent distributional tails and give no guarantee against operationally infeasible instances, such as a short air time paired with a long flight distance. No existing approach addresses both limitations for mixed-type tabular records. We propose TailBooster, a dual-layer generative framework combining generative modelling with two anomaly detection layers. A statistical layer extracts extremes via the interquartile range, supplying tail-concentrated training signal to dedicated generative models, here a Tabular Variational Autoencoder. A deep learning layer then applies autoencoder-based cleaning, discarding synthetic records that violate the operational envelope learned from historical data. The framework was evaluated on US flight records across five dimensions: diversity, statistical similarity, fidelity, operational validity, and utility, the latter two being the primary improvement targets. Data-driven cleaning markedly improved operational validity, while targeted augmentation enhanced utility for extreme-event prediction. Across six regression algorithms, training on the framework's records reduced Mean Absolute Error by 47-49% on extreme air time and 29-57% on extreme arrival delay prediction relative to conventional synthetic data, with comparable gains when real records were enriched with synthetic extremes. Being fully data-driven and model-agnostic, TailBooster extends to domains where extreme-event prediction is critical and domain-specific rules are unavailable.

</details>


### [274] [LoongReflect: Boosting Long-Horizon Reflection in Search Agents via Global Perspective Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11967)
*Zhixin Zhang,Xinke Jiang,Zhibang Yang,Weixuan Xu,Guohong Qiu,Xu Chu,Junfeng Zhao,Yasha Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoongReflect introduces a memory-control policy for reflection in LLM agents, using a reversible trajectory tree with reflect and backtrack actions; it uses a fast teacher channel and a slow outcome-based objective to align local reflection decisions with global task outcomes, yielding improvements over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reflection in long-horizon planning is local and its value depends on the final trajectory outcome, making purely outcome-based RL sparse and delayed. A memory-control approach that integrates reflection decisions with trajectory-level success is needed.

Method: Propose LoongReflect: a memory-control policy operating on a reversible trajectory tree with reflect and backtrack actions. Reflection consolidates verified facts, missing evidence, and risks into working memory; backtracking removes unreliable branches and preserves corrective lessons. A fast channel distills globally informed reflective behavior from a privileged teacher using supervision limited to reflection and backtracking tokens; a slow channel optimizes complete trajectories with an outcome-based GRPO objective to align local controls with final success.

Result: Empirical evaluations on multi-hop retrieval-augmented generation and mathematical reasoning benchmarks show consistent improvements over outcome-only RL and self-distillation baselines.

Conclusion: Reflection-guided memory-control with dual-channel training effectively coordinates local reflective decisions with global trajectory outcomes, improving performance across long-horizon tasks and suggesting good generalization to related domains.

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly rely on long-horizon reasoning to solve complex tasks involving planning, tool use, and memory. A critical capability in such settings is reflection: assessing trajectory progress, identifying missing evidence and unreliable intermediate states, and deciding whether to continue, revise, or abandon the current branch. Learning effective reflection, however, is challenging because reflection is performed locally within the current branch, whereas its utility can only be determined by its contribution to the final trajectory outcome. This local-global mismatch makes outcome-based reinforcement learning provide only local, sparse and delayed supervision for reflective decisions. To solve these, we propose LoongReflect, a training framework that formulates reflection as a memory-control policy. The agent operates over a reversible trajectory tree using explicit reflect and backtrack actions. Reflection consolidates verified facts, missing evidence, and branch-specific risks into working memory, while backtracking removes an unreliable branch from the active context and preserves a concise corrective lesson. To learn this policy, LoongReflect combines two complementary signals through a look-ahead, extragradient-style coordination mechanism. A fast channel distills globally informed reflective behavior from a privileged teacher, with supervision restricted to reflection and backtracking tokens. A slow channel optimizes complete trajectories using outcome-based GRPO, aligning local control decisions with final task success. Experiments on multi-hop retrieval-augmented generation and mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over outcome-only reinforcement learning and self-distillation baselines.

</details>


### [275] [TESLA: Taylor Expansion of Sinusoidal Learnable Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11970)
*Daehwa Ko,Jaehyeon Kim,Seunghyun Ham,Jay Hoon Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learnable sine-cosine activation (TESLA) enables explicit control over polynomial degree and prioritization of high-frequency components, addressing parity-like global-structure learning; it yields strong parity generalization with limited data and transfers to vision tasks, with competitive results on ImageNet-100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parity and related global-structure problems are hard for standard neural nets due to linear inseparability and the need for high-order interactions; activate-level control of frequency content can provide theoretical and practical benefits.

Method: Introduce TESLA as a learnable combination of sine and cosine terms with coefficients constrained to shape complexity. Analyze Lipschitz/Rademacher bounds and training dynamics under coefficient constraints; compare against SIREN, SNAKE, and Fourier features on parity and Forrelation; evaluate transfer to ImageNet-100; release code.

Result: TESLA generalizes well from ~100K samples on parity with n=32 (≈0.002% of the input space), remains robust under up to 30% label noise, and outperforms periodic baselines on parity/Forrelation; demonstrates transferable performance to ImageNet-100.

Conclusion: Activation-level degree control via TESLA yields theoretical and empirical benefits for learning high-order structure, with potential applicability beyond synthetic tasks to real vision workloads; code is provided for reproducibility.

Abstract: The parity problem--deciding whether the number of ones in a binary vector is odd or even--remains challenging for standard neural networks due to linear inseparability and the need for global interactions. We propose TESLA, an activation defined as a learnable combination of sine and cosine terms, enabling explicit control over polynomial degree and selective amplification of high-order components. Theoretically, we show that constraining TESLA's coefficients yields Lipschitz/Rademacher complexity bounds and shapes the training dynamics to emphasize higher-frequency structure. Empirically, on parity with input length n = 32, TESLA attains strong generalization with 100K training samples (approximately 0.002% of the 2^32 input space) and remains robust under heavy corruption, retaining high accuracy with up to 30% label noise. We also compare against periodic and frequency-based baselines (SIREN, SNAKE, and Fourier feature embeddings) on parity and Forrelation. Beyond synthetic structure, TESLA delivers comparable performance on ImageNet-100, indicating that activation-level degree control transfers to more general vision workloads. Code: https://github.com/KAU-QuantumAILab/TESLA

</details>


### [276] [Remote Sensing and Machine Learning-Based Analysis of Land Use and Vegetation Change in Dhaka District, Bangladesh](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12001)
*Muhammad Masud Tarek,Md. Alamgir Hossain,Md. Samiul Islam,Muntasir Hasan Kanchan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Remote sensing and ML assess urban land-use change in Dhaka (2019–2024); built-up areas rise sharply while vegetation and water decline; Random Forest most accurate; findings inform sustainable urban planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address rapid urban expansion and environmental degradation in Dhaka by providing timely spatial insights to support planning, regulation, and ecosystem conservation.

Method: Multisensor data fusion using Sentinel-2 MSI and Landsat 8; land-cover classification; spectral indices NDVI, NDBI, NDWI; supervised ML with Decision Tree, KNN, and Random Forest in Google Earth Engine; training points; accuracy assessed with confusion matrices and kappa.

Result: Built-up area increased by 59.5%; vegetation declined by 8.46%; water bodies declined by 7.77% from 2019 to 2024; land conversion from vegetated/ aquatic areas to built-up infrastructure; Random Forest achieved the highest classification accuracy.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the utility of remote sensing and ML for delivering timely, actionable data to guide sustainable urban development, land-use regulation, and ecosystem conservation policies in rapidly urbanizing settings.

Abstract: Rapid urbanization in Dhaka District, Bangladesh has triggered substantial alterations in land use and environmental conditions, necessitating systematic monitoring for informed urban planning and ecological sustainability. This study employs remote sensing data and machine learning techniques to analyze spatiotemporal changes in land cover and vegetation dynamics between 2019 and 2024. High-resolution satellite imagery from Sentinel-2 MSI and Landsat 8 was utilized to classify land cover types and compute spectral indices including the Normalized Difference Vegetation Index (NDVI), Normalized Difference Built-up Index (NDBI), and Normalized Difference Water Index (NDWI). A supervised machine learning approach incorporating Decision Tree, K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN), and Random Forest classifiers was applied using labeled geospatial training points within Google Earth Engine. Accuracy assessments were conducted using confusion matrices and kappa statistics. Results indicate a 59.5% increase in urban built-up areas and a significant decline in vegetation (-8.46%) and water bodies (-7.77%) over the five-year period. Land conversion from vegetated and aquatic areas to urban infrastructure was identified as a dominant trend. Among the models, Random Forest demonstrated the highest classification accuracy. These findings underscore the growing environmental pressures driven by unregulated urban expansion in Dhaka. The study highlights the potential of remote sensing and machine learning tools in providing timely, actionable data to support sustainable urban development, land-use regulation, and ecosystem conservation policies.

</details>


### [277] [Dual-Model Sentiment Analysis of Consumer Reviews in the Retail Coffee Sector Using Machine Learning and Deep Learning Approaches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12007)
*Muntasir Hasan Kanchan,Md. Alamgir Hossain,Md. Samiul Islam,Muhammad Masud Tarek*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comparative study evaluating classical ML and deep learning for binary sentiment analysis on Starbucks reviews; SVM (ML) achieves 91% accuracy; BiLSTM best among DL; small, imbalanced dataset; binarization of ratings; implications for retail CX analytics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess the relative effectiveness of traditional ML and DL approaches for real-world sentiment analysis in a service industry, and understand how preprocessing and class imbalance impact performance and generalization.

Method: Dataset: 700+ reviews from ConsumerAffairs; preprocessing and EDA; label creation by binarizing star ratings (4–5 positive, 1–3 negative); address imbalance. Models: 5 ML classifiers (LR, SVM, DT, RF, NB); 5 DL models (LSTM, RNN, BiLSTM, GRU, CNN). Evaluation: accuracy, precision, recall, F1; analysis of temporal/geographic patterns; reporting on training/testing and generalization (unseen data).

Result: ML: SVM highest among ML with 91.0% accuracy; DL: Bidirectional LSTM strongest among DL and shows good generalization on unseen data; class imbalance reduced positive recall across several models; overall, a comparative performance landscape between ML and DL approaches.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that both classical ML and DL can be effective for real-world sentiment analytics in retail coffee; emphasizes careful model selection and preprocessing, particularly handling class imbalance; provides guidance for CX analytics applications and future work on improving generalization and data utilization.

Abstract: Consumer reviews play an important role in shaping brand perception and business strategies, particularly in service-driven industries such as retail coffee. This study presents a comparative sentiment analysis framework for Starbucks customer reviews using classical machine learning and deep learning approaches. The dataset, collected from ConsumerAffairs, contains more than 700 reviews and was analyzed through preprocessing and exploratory data analysis to identify temporal and geographic patterns. Sentiment labels were generated by binarizing star ratings, with ratings of 4 and 5 classified as positive and ratings of 1 to 3 as negative. The resulting dataset was substantially imbalanced toward negative sentiment. Five machine learning classifiers, including Logistic Regression, Support Vector Machine (SVM), Decision Tree, Random Forest, and Naive Bayes, were evaluated alongside five deep learning models: LSTM, RNN, Bidirectional LSTM, GRU, and CNN. Model performance was assessed using accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score. SVM achieved the highest accuracy among the machine learning models at 91.0 percent, while Bidirectional LSTM showed the strongest performance among the deep learning models and demonstrated good generalization on unseen data. The findings also show that class imbalance negatively affected positive sentiment recall across several models. Overall, this study provides a comparative evaluation of machine learning and deep learning approaches for real-world consumer sentiment analysis and highlights the importance of appropriate model selection and preprocessing for customer experience analytics in the retail coffee sector.

</details>


### [278] [Reducing Symmetry Increase in Equivariant Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12010)
*Ning Lin,Jiacheng Cen,Anyi Li,Wenbing Huang,Hao Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Characterizes symmetry increase in Equivariant Neural Networks (ENNs) and provides a principled framework to mitigate it, including a computable infimum for the increased symmetry, an algorithm to derive it, design guidelines, and empirical validation on synthetic data and the QM9 dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ENNs sacrifice expressivity due to symmetry increases when processing symmetric inputs; understanding the causes and reducing harmful symmetry increases can improve representation power and performance.

Method: Theoretically characterize the increased symmetry, prove the existence of an infimum determined by the feature space and input symmetry group; develop a computable algorithm to derive this infimum; propose practical feature-design guidelines; validate under standard regularity assumptions that guidelines reduce symmetry increase for most equivariant maps; provide visualizations and experiments on synthetic data and QM9.

Result: A rigorous framework for symmetry increase in ENNs is established, including (i) an infimum characterization of the increased symmetry; (ii) a computable algorithm to obtain the infimum; (iii) design guidelines to prevent adverse symmetry increases; (iv) theoretical justification under regularity assumptions that these guidelines are effective for most maps; and (v) empirical validation on synthetic datasets and the QM9 real-world dataset.

Conclusion: The paper delivers a principled approach to understand and mitigate symmetry increases in ENNs. By linking the limit of increased symmetry to feature-space structure and providing a practical algorithm, it offers actionable guidance for feature design and demonstrates effectiveness on synthetic and real data, with broad implications for improving ENN expressivity.

Abstract: Equivariant Neural Networks (ENNs) have empowered numerous applications in scientific fields. Despite their remarkable capacity for representing geometric structures, ENNs suffer from degraded expressivity when processing symmetric inputs: the output representations are invariant to transformations that extend beyond the input's symmetries. The mathematical essence of this phenomenon is that a symmetric input, after being processed by an equivariant map, experiences an increase in symmetry. While prior research has documented symmetry increase in specific cases, a rigorous understanding of its underlying causes and general reduction strategies remains lacking. In this paper, we provide a detailed and in-depth characterization of symmetry increase together with a principled framework for its reduction: (i) For any given feature space and input symmetry group, we prove that the increased symmetry admits an infimum determined by the structure of the feature space; (ii) Building on this foundation, we develop a computable algorithm to derive this infimum, and propose practical guidelines for feature design to prevent harmful symmetry increases. (iii) Under standard regularity assumptions, we demonstrate that for most equivariant maps, our guidelines effectively reduce symmetry increase. To complement our theoretical findings, we provide visualizations and experiments on both synthetic datasets and the real-world QM9 dataset. The results validate our theoretical predictions.

</details>


### [279] [SoftWater: Class-Aware Rate Allocation for Softmax Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12026)
*Joao V. Cavalcanti,Ashia C. Wilson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SoftWater is a KL-driven, class-aware softmax head quantizer that uses a separable per-class factorization and lattice decoding to compress the softmax head with 2–4 bits, achieving large KL reduction and minimal perplexity impact across large LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In small LLMs with large vocabularies, the softmax head contains a substantial fraction of parameters; quantizing it under standard metrics degrades output distributions. Need a rate-distortion framework that targets KL divergence and accounts for class- and feature-structure.

Method: Model quantization as rate-distortion under KL between original and quantized distributions; second-order analysis shows class-aware geometry; propose separability approximation to replace full Cholesky with per-class n×n factorization; enable lattice decoding via successive interference cancellation using statistics from a single forward pass; calibrate with domain-specific data.

Result: SoftWater outperforms WaterSIC at matched head rates on 59/60 test points across 5 models (1B–32B); cuts head-induced KL by 6.5×–8.3× at 2 bits; for Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct with quantized bodies, 2-bit head saves 45–60% of head bytes with 2.9–3.7% perplexity increase; on a tied model, 4-bit head is near-lossless and 2-bit head incurs <4% perplexity.

Conclusion: Class-aware rate-distortion softmax head quantization with SIC-based encoding enables practical and substantial compression of the softmax head with minimal impact on perplexity, especially when calibration aligns with deployment domain; a 2–4 bit head is viable for real-world models.

Abstract: Post-training quantization pipelines routinely leave the softmax output layer in high precision. Yet in small LLMs with modern vocabularies, the head holds 15--30\% of all parameters, so a nominal ``2-bit'' model with an fp16 head can store several times as many bits per weight. We pose softmax-layer quantization as a rate-distortion problem under the KL divergence between the original and quantized output distributions. A second-order analysis reveals a class-aware geometry: quantization error is weighted jointly by feature covariance and class-specific softmax curvature. A separability approximation replaces the $Kn\times Kn$ Cholesky with one $n\times n$ factorization rescaled per class, making the lattice encodable by successive interference cancellation, with both statistics from a single forward pass. The resulting method, SoftWater, gives fine grids to frequent, low-variance classes and coarse grids to rare ones, a large gap under Zipfian token distributions. Across five models from 1B to 32B, SoftWater outperforms the released WaterSIC quantizer (near-optimal under linear-layer WMSE but not output KL) at matched head rates on 59 of 60 test points, using none of that pipeline's refinements and cutting head-induced KL by $6.5\times$--$8.3\times$ at 2 bits. On Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct with quantized bodies, a 2-bit head removes 45--60\% of stored bytes for a $2.9$--$3.7\%$ perplexity increase. Because the class-side statistic comes from calibration data, matching calibration to the deployment domain gives the lowest KL on that domain throughout. On a tied model, a 4-bit head is near-lossless and a 2-bit head costs under 4\% perplexity, making head quantization of such models practical.

</details>


### [280] [Uncertainty-Aware Probabilistic Constrained Clustering from Entangled Pairwise Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12027)
*Shaojie Zhang,Ke Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UPCC framework for probabilistic pairwise supervision in clustering, featuring ProbPair angular objective and ECI-PP estimator--corrector--integrator pipeline. Demonstrates superior performance and robustness to probabilistic supervision noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-valued pairwise supervision introduces uncertainty due to ambiguity, expert judgment, and stochastic noise; existing deep constrained clustering focuses on hard, expert-agnostic constraints and underutilizes semantically informative soft labels.

Method: Formalize an aleatoric target via a heterogeneous observation process and analyze conditional identifiability. Introduce ProbPair, an angular pairwise objective for probabilistic relations. Propose ECI-PP, an estimator--corrector--integrator framework that refines imperfect supervision through belief estimation, correction, and reliability-aware integration.

Result: ECI-PP outperforms state-of-the-art deep constrained clustering methods across diverse benchmarks under probabilistic supervision and remains robust with a shared default configuration.

Conclusion: The work advances probabilistic constrained clustering by combining a principled target, a novel angular relation objective, and an end-to-end refinement framework, achieving improved accuracy and robustness in realistic supervision settings.

Abstract: Pairwise constrained clustering typically relies on hard must-link/cannot-link labels, whereas realistic pairwise supervision may be real-valued and entangle intrinsic ambiguity, expert judgment, and stochastic corruption. Existing deep constrained clustering (DCC) methods mainly target hard, expert-agnostic constraints, treating soft labels mostly numerically rather than semantically. We formalize this setting as uncertainty-aware probabilistic constrained clustering (UPCC), defining a canonical aleatoric target through a heterogeneous observation process and analyzing its conditional identifiability. We introduce ProbPair, an angular pairwise objective for probabilistic relations, and build ECI-PP, an estimator--corrector--integrator framework that refines imperfect supervision via belief estimation, correction, and reliability-aware integration. Across challenging probabilistic supervision settings, experiments on diverse benchmarks show that ECI-PP outperforms state-of-the-art DCC methods and remains robust with a shared default configuration.

</details>


### [281] [Clustered Randomized Smoothing for Stochastic Prediction Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12037)
*Eduardo Figueiredo,Frederik Mathiesen,Julian Schumann,Jens Kober,Arkady Zgonnikov,Luca Laurenti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Clustered α-smoothing partitions noisy samples with clustering, applies α-smoothing within each cluster, and forms a mixture distribution. It derives a lower bound on the probability that the smoothed prediction lies in the union of compact regions corresponding to distinct modes. Empirically, it improves over state-of-the-art: driving trajectory prediction shows 27% lower Wasserstein distance to ground-truth; quadrotor control shows 81% reduction in collision rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve robustness in stochastic, multi-modal regression while preserving multiple modes. Randomized smoothing often collapses modes (mode averaging); need a method that maintains multi-modality in safety-critical domains.

Method: Cluster the noisy samples (using an arbitrary clustering algorithm); perform α-smoothing locally within each cluster; aggregate the locally smoothed predictions into a mixture distribution (interpreting the overall smoothing as a mixture of α-smoothers); derive a lower bound on the probability that the smoothed prediction lies within the union of regions corresponding to distinct modes.

Result: Evaluated on two benchmarks. In stochastic trajectory prediction for driving simulations, the method achieves an average 27% lower Wasserstein distance to the ground-truth distribution compared with α-smoothing alone. In quadrotor control, where modes map to distinct feasible paths to a target, the method reduces collision rate by 81% relative to state-of-the-art randomized smoothing.

Conclusion: Clustered α-smoothing effectively preserves multi-modality while providing robustness gains. The approach mitigates mode collapse inherent in standard randomized smoothing, yielding improved distributional alignment (lower Wasserstein distance) and safer behaviors (fewer collisions). The framework is compatible with any clustering method and α-smoothing variant, offering a versatile robustness tool for stochastic predictors.

Abstract: Modern stochastic predictors can model rich, multi-modal outcome distributions. However, this expressive power comes with challenges in ensuring robust predictions $-$ a critical requirement in safety-critical domains. Randomized smoothing is a leading technique for improving robustness, particularly against adversarial perturbations. Yet, in stochastic multi-modal regression settings, randomized smoothing often fails due to mode collapse, yielding averaged predictions that do not reflect the underlying distribution. To address this limitation, we propose clustered $α$-smoothing, a framework that (1) partitions noisy samples using an arbitrary clustering algorithm, (2) applies $α$-smoothing locally within each cluster, and (3) combines the resulting predictions into a mixture distribution. By interpreting the smoothing distribution as a mixture of $α$-smoothers, we derive a lower bound on the probability that the smoothed prediction lies within a union of compact regions corresponding to distinct modes. We empirically evaluate our framework on two benchmarks, demonstrating substantial improvements over state-of-the-art methods. In stochastic trajectory prediction on a driving simulator dataset, our approach achieves, on average, a $27\%$ lower Wasserstein distance to the ground-truth distribution compared to $α$-smoothing. In quadrotor control, where modes correspond to distinct feasible paths to a target, our method reduces the collision rate by $81\%$ relative to the state-of-the-art randomized smoothing.

</details>


### [282] [Towards Truly Unsupervised Evaluation of Feature Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12057)
*Hafiz Saud Arshad,Muhammad Rajabinasab,Arthur Zimek*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper critiques existing unsupervised evaluations for feature selection and proposes a truly unsupervised framework using PCA and optimal transport.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unsupervised evaluation methods for feature selection often rely on labels or supervised downstream tasks, undermining their unsupervised nature. A genuinely unsupervised metric is needed.

Method: Critical discussion of current techniques; introduction of a novel framework that uses unsupervised PCA and optimal transport to assess feature selection without labeled information.

Result: Conceptual framework with theoretical justification; the abstract does not report empirical results.

Conclusion: A truly unsupervised evaluation framework is proposed, leveraging PCA structure and optimal transport to quantify feature-selection quality without label information.

Abstract: Feature selection is one of the most important and fundamental tasks in data mining, tackled by a family of methods with an established set of evaluation techniques to measure the quality of a specific method. Most of the methods commonly used for the unsupervised evaluation of feature selection algorithms suffer from critical design flaws which question their unsupervised nature. In this paper, we provide a critical discussion on the established allegedly unsupervised evaluation techniques, and shed light on the reasons why they are not truly unsupervised but, at best, supervised evaluation under an unsupervised downstream task. We also propose a novel, truly unsupervised evaluation framework to measure the quality of the feature selection algorithms without any form of information about the labels. The proposed framework utilizes unsupervised Principal Component Analysis, and optimal transport to measure the quality of the feature selection methods in a truly unsupervised manner.

</details>


### [283] [Faithful, Sufficient and Understandable: Rethinking Graph Counterfactual Explanations via Discrete Diffusion Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12083)
*David Bechtoldt,Sidney Bender*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GDCE-I introduces a discrete denoising diffusion model with a novel discrete inversion scheme to produce distribution-aware, domain-consistent counterfactual explanations for graphs. It respects data-manifold constraints and domain rules (e.g., valency) and evaluates explanations with a unified protocol, outperforming prior methods across four benchmarks and yielding interpretable molecular solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNNs lack intrinsic explanations, which is critical for safety-critical domains. Counterfactual explanations aim to reveal minimal, valid edits to change predictions, but prior methods either stray off the data manifold or miss parts of the edit space. A method that jointly respects discreteness, domain rules, and full edit space is needed, alongside a standardized evaluation framework.

Method: Proposes Graph Diffusion Counterfactual Explanation via Inversion (GDCE-I): a discrete denoising diffusion model with a novel discrete inversion scheme that enables distribution-aware edits within the full domain edit space while enforcing data-manifold validity. Additionally, introduces a framework of explanation desiderata and a unified evaluation protocol applicable to all methods.

Result: GDCE-I outperforms related work by a large margin on the defined framework across four benchmarks. In the molecular domain, it qualitatively yields interpretable in-distribution counterfactuals, suggesting practical interpretability alongside predictive explanations.

Conclusion: GDCE-I provides effective, manifold-consistent counterfactual explanations for graphs and demonstrates strong performance under a standardized evaluation framework, with tangible interpretability in molecular graphs. The work also contributes a reusable evaluation protocol to benchmark graph counterfactuals.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) achieve strong predictive performance on graph-structured data across domains such as chemistry, biology, and network analysis, yet they provide no intrinsic explanation of their predictions. This limits their adoption in high-stakes and safety-critical settings. Counterfactual explanations address this by revealing the minimal structural modifications that would change a model's prediction. On graphs, however, such a modification is hard to produce. The search space is discrete and combinatorial, and a valid answer must respect categorical node and edge types together with domain rules such as chemical valency in the case of molecular graphs. Existing explainers give up one of two things. Either edits are not held on the data manifold, or the search does not span the full edit space. We propose Graph Diffusion Counterfactual Explanation via Inversion (GDCE-I), which gives up neither. A discrete denoising diffusion model with a novel discrete inversion scheme enables distribution-aware edits leveraging the whole domain edit space. We further address the incomplete and inconsistent evaluation of graph counterfactuals by deriving a framework of explanation desiderata and applying it to every method under one shared protocol. Across four benchmarks, GDCE-I outperforms related work by a large margin on the defined framework. For the molecular domain, we further qualitatively show that GDCE-I attains interpretable in-distribution solutions.

</details>


### [284] [NAE: Normalizing AutoEncoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12084)
*Muhammad Abdur Rafae,Niels Landwehr*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Suboptimality of existing loss in flow autoencoders; introduces Normalizing Autoencoder (NAE) with a conditional loss that aligns surrogate gradients with reconstruction loss, achieving state-of-the-art across multiple modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate training dynamics of normalizing flows with approximate inverses (full-dim and bottleneck settings) and the misalignment between surrogate losses and reconstruction objectives in flow autoencoders.

Method: The authors theoretically show that current surrogate losses are misaligned with reconstruction loss, and propose a novel conditional loss for NAE that aligns the surrogate gradient with the reconstruction loss. Empirically, they validate on molecule generation, tabular data, and image benchmarks.

Result: NAE yields state-of-the-art performance across diverse tasks, demonstrating improved generative quality due to loss alignment.

Conclusion: Loss alignment between encoder/decoder surrogates and reconstruction loss is crucial in flow autoencoders; NAE provides a principled, effective generative framework and sets a new standard for training dynamics in this class.

Abstract: We consider the setting of Normalizing flows with approximate inverses, an established paradigm spanning both full-dimensional ($d=D$) and bottleneck ($d<D$) settings, and group these models under the term flow autoencoders. We present a theoretical investigation into their training dynamics and prove that the proposed loss used by existing approaches is suboptimal; specifically, both encoder and decoder surrogates must be optimized in alignment with reconstruction loss. Guided by these insights, we propose Normalizing Autoencoder (NAE), which employs a novel conditional loss that aligns the surrogate loss gradient with that of reconstruction loss, directly improving upon the current standard. Extensive experiments across molecule generation, tabular data, and image benchmarks demonstrate that NAE achieves state of the art performance. Our work highlights the importance of loss alignment in flow autoencoders and establishes NAE as a powerful generative framework.

</details>


### [285] [Task- and dataset-specific information in protein language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12090)
*Roman Joeres,Ilya Senatorov,Olga V. Kalinina*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Last-layer embeddings are not always the most informative for protein downstream tasks; information about tasks is distributed across PLM layers, with shallow layers favoring certain data (DMS) and deeper layers favoring natural protein datasets; task alignment with pre-training objectives increases information transfer; artificial protein tasks reveal performance drops.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand which PLM layers carry the most task-relevant information and how that distribution depends on the task and dataset, challenging the common practice of using only last-layer embeddings.

Method: Evaluate embeddings from every layer of 13 PLMs on 15 downstream tasks across 11 datasets by training layer-specific probes. Analyze latent-space characteristics to estimate information content and examine how task type and dataset shape layer usefulness.

Result: Last layers rarely provide the best performance for downstream tasks. The layer-wise information distribution correlates with task characteristics: tasks aligned with pretraining objectives show increasing information across layers; whole-protein tasks are more dataset-dependent; DMS data favor shallow layers, diverse natural proteins favor deeper layers; performance drops when tasks involve artificial proteins.

Conclusion: Layer choice matters: select embeddings from layers that align with the task and data, or design models that expose informative representations across layers. These findings challenge the default use of last-layer embeddings for diverse protein-downstream applications.

Abstract: Protein language models (PLMs) have transferred the latest advances from natural language processing to computational biology. These models, trained on large corpora of protein sequence data, are widely used to translate amino acid sequences into latent-space embeddings, ready for use in diverse downstream tasks (DTs). By a common consensus, embeddings from the model's last layer are used, and the model's internal behavior remains poorly understood. We analyzed 13 PLMs across 15 DTs from 11 datasets to investigate the informativeness of embeddings created in intermediate PLM layers. We trained probe models on embeddings from each layer, compared their performance, and computed characteristics of the latent spaces they span to estimate the information they contain, and found that the last layers of PLMs rarely contained embeddings that led to the best results on downstream tasks. Furthermore, we identified a connection between DTs and the distribution across PLMs' layers of the relevant information to predict that task. For example, similarity between the pre-training objective and the objective of predicting properties of individual residues leads to a steady increase in understanding of such tasks across the layers of PLMs. On the other hand, for whole-protein tasks, we observe that the dataset, rather than the task itself, defines PLMs' ability to perform well on a DT. Embeddings from shallow layers of PLMs perform better for datasets that contain deep mutational scan (DMS) data, while datasets containing diverse natural proteins find most useful embeddings in the models' deeper layers. Additionally, we discover that the performance of PLMs drops significantly when tasks are introduced for artificial proteins.

</details>


### [286] [Confidence Calibration of Deep Learning Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12100)
*Coby Penso*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This thesis develops noise-robust calibration methods across label noise, unsupervised domain adaptation, and privacy-preserving conformal prediction to enable reliable uncertainty estimates under practical data challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable calibration under label noise, domain shifts, and privacy constraints is essential for safe deployment of deep learning, yet standard calibration assumes clean validation data.

Method: Propose a noise-model-driven framework to reconstruct clean confidence estimates from noisy labels; extend to Conformal Prediction with noise-aware conformity scores; study calibration in unsupervised domain adaptation by estimating target-domain accuracy from source performance and domain discrepancy; propose a locally differentially private conformal prediction framework.

Result: Enables reliable uncertainty quantification under label noise, domain shifts, and privacy constraints; links calibration theory with practical deployment in safety-critical contexts.

Conclusion: Contributes to reliable, privacy-preserving, and noise-resilient neural network predictions suitable for high-stakes applications.

Abstract: In high-stakes applications, reliable confidence estimates are as important as the predictions themselves. Confidence calibration ensures that predicted probabilities reflect the likelihood of correctness, making it essential for safe deployment of deep learning models. However, existing methods typically assume access to clean validation data, which is often unrealistic due to label noise and domain shifts. This thesis develops methods for improving calibration under these conditions.
  First, we address calibration under label noise. Standard methods can produce misleading confidence estimates when labels are unreliable. We propose a framework that uses an estimated noise model to reconstruct noise-free confidence estimates by modeling the relationship between noisy and clean label distributions. We extend this approach to Conformal Prediction (CP), which provides set-valued predictions with guaranteed coverage. Our noise-aware CP method estimates clean conformity scores despite label noise, enabling reliable uncertainty quantification. Next, we study calibration in unsupervised domain adaptation, where a model trained on a labeled source domain is adapted to an unlabeled target domain. Since labeled target data are unavailable, we estimate target-domain accuracy from source performance and domain discrepancies, enabling calibration without target labels. We also consider privacy-preserving settings in which user labels and model outputs must remain protected. We propose a locally differentially private conformal prediction framework that provides valid uncertainty quantification while maintaining privacy guarantees and balancing privacy, computational feasibility, and prediction reliability.
  Our results bridge calibration theory and practical deployment in safety-critical applications, contributing to reliable, privacy-preserving, and noise-resilient neural network predictions.

</details>


### [287] [Beyond Parameter Space: NTK-Guided Personalized Aggregation for Robust Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12108)
*Mirko Konstantin,Stefan Zachow,Anirban Mukhopadhyay*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LIGHTYEAR is a federated learning method that selects updates in function space using NTK-based agreement scores in a peer-to-peer setting, improving personalized aggregation under heterogeneous (non-IID) data and outperforming centralized FL baselines and existing P2P methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parameter-space similarity often fails to predict actual predictive usefulness under data heterogeneity; misaligned updates can hurt local performance. Function-space criteria can better capture predictive behavior aligned with a client's target domain.

Method: LightYEAR constructs a P2P federation where clients exchange updates and evaluate incoming models on private validation data. It uses Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) to compute an agreement score that reflects predictive behavior, selecting only updates beneficial to each client's target domain and aggregating them with a regularized rule to improve stability under heterogeneity.

Result: Empirically, LIGHTYEAR outperforms centralized FL baselines and existing P2P approaches across five datasets and nine baselines.

Conclusion: Function-space-based update selection via NTK-based agreement enables personalized, robust aggregation in heterogeneous FL, yielding consistent gains over traditional parameter-space or centralized approaches.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables collaborative model training across distributed clients while keeping data local. A central challenge is determining which client updates are beneficial for aggregation with respect to each client's target domain. Existing methods typically address this problem in parameter space by comparing model parameters or gradients. However, parameter-space similarity can be a poor proxy for predictive behavior, especially under heterogeneous, non-IID data. Consequently, updates that are misaligned with a client's target domain, including those caused by heterogeneous data or malfunctioning clients, may degrade local model performance.
  We propose Local Inference Guided Aggregation for Heterogeneous Training Environments to Yield Enhancement Through Agreement and Regularization (LIGHTYEAR), a federated learning framework that performs update selection in function space. LIGHTYEAR uses an NTK-based agreement score to characterize predictive behavior and determine a personalized aggregation set for each client. By relating model parameters to local predictive responses, the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) provides a more expressive criterion for update selection than parameter-space similarity alone.
  Because function-space information is not available before aggregation in conventional centralized FL, LIGHTYEAR uses a peer-to-peer (P2P) topology in which clients exchange updates directly and evaluate incoming models on private validation data. Each client selects only updates that are beneficial for its own target domain and aggregates them using a regularized rule that improves stability under heterogeneity.
  Across five datasets and nine baseline methods, LIGHTYEAR consistently outperforms centralized FL baselines and existing P2P approaches.

</details>


### [288] [Attractor Image-Based Deep Learning of Arterial Pulse Waves for Age Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12117)
*Sara Vardanega,Patrick Segers,Philip Aston,Ernst Rietzschel,Jordi Alastruey,Manasi Nandi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SPAR-based image representations of pulse waves from PPG and arterial tonometry enable CNN-based discrimination between closely spaced healthy age groups (35-40 vs 50-55), with F1 scores above 0.70 on internal and external tests, indicating discriminative morphological cues for early vascular aging; supports wearable-based risk detection in principle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vascular aging alters pulse waveform morphology, providing a noninvasive surrogate for cardiovascular health. Transforming time-series into images via SPAR allows CNNs to learn complex waveform shape features and assess feasibility for risk stratification in asymptomatic adults, potentially enabling early intervention through wearables.

Method: Acquire pulse-wave time-series from photoplethysmography (PPG) and arterial tonometry. Convert sequences into SPAR images. Train a convolutional neural network to classify healthy subjects into two close age bands (35–40 and 50–55). Evaluate performance on internal and external test sets, reporting F1 scores for both signal types.

Result: The CNN achieved F1 scores above 0.70 for both PPG and tonometry SPAR images, with consistent performance across internal and external test sets. SPAR-derived images appear to capture discriminative morphological features in pulse waves even among healthy adults with small age differences.

Conclusion: SPAR-based pulse-wave imaging shows promise as a noninvasive approach for early vascular aging detection and risk stratification, including potential integration with wearables. Further validation in larger, more diverse cohorts and robustness analyses are warranted.

Abstract: Arterial pulse waveform morphology evolves with age, reflecting structural and functional changes in the cardiovascular system. Thus, vascular age is a valuable surrogate marker of cardiovascular health, and premature vascular ageing can indicate increased disease risk. Pulse wave analysis could support risk stratification in otherwise asymptomatic adults. We transformed pulse wave time-series data from photoplethysmography (PPG) and arterial tonometry into images, using the Symmetric Projection Attractor Reconstruction (SPAR) method. These SPAR images were used to train a convolutional neural network to classify healthy subjects into two closely spaced age groups (35-40 and 50-55 years). The model demonstrated consistent classification performance across internal and external test sets, achieving F1 scores above 70% for both PPG and tonometry signals. These results suggest that SPAR-derived pulse wave images contain discriminative morphological features even among healthy adults close in age. This proof-of-concept lays the groundwork for future research into the use of SPAR for early risk detection using smart wearables.

</details>


### [289] [Adversarial Resilience of Poisson-Process Submodular Maximization over Matroids: From Robust Offline Optimization to Full-Bandit Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12134)
*Vaneet Aggarwal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adversarially robust submodular maximization under a general matroid via SGS-Poisson; preserves 1/e and 1-1/e approximations under a bounded-noise oracle, with near-optimal regret for bandit CMAB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robustness of submodular maximization when the objective oracle is noisy/adversarial; enable offline-to-online reductions for CMAB with matroid constraints.

Method: Introduce SGS-Poisson algorithm with Poisson process, single-element exchange, and spiteful drop; show adversarial resilience under controlled oracle with |fhat(S)-f(S)| ≤ ξ; derive approximation guarantees and oracle complexity; translate to online setting via offline-to-online reduction.

Result: For non-monotone: E[f(S)] ≥ (1/e - ε) OPT - O(k ξ); for monotone: E[f(S)] ≥ (1 - 1/e - ε) OPT - O(k ξ); oracle calls ~ Õ(n k^2 ε^-2). Offline-to-online yields full-bandit CMAB with exact factors 1/e and 1-1/e and regret Õ(n^{1/5} k^{4/5} T^{4/5}).

Conclusion: Shows resilience of SGS-Poisson to controlled perturbations, preserving classic submodular maximization factors; enables practical bandit algorithms with predictable regret bounds under noise; broad applicability to CMAB with matroid constraints.

Abstract: We study nonnegative submodular maximization subject to a general matroid when the offline algorithm is given an arbitrary controlled value oracle. Our main result is an adversarial resilience theorem for the Spiteful Greedy Swap Poisson Process (SGS-Poisson): without modifying its Poisson intensity, single-element exchange rule, or spiteful drop step, the algorithm retains limiting approximation factors $1/e$ for non-monotone objectives and $1-1/e$ for monotone objectives. More precisely, under every controlled oracle $\widehat f$ satisfying $|\widehat f(S)-f(S)|\le ξ$ for every set $S$, our implementation returns a feasible set with expected value at least $(1/e-\varepsilon)\OPT-O(kξ)$ and $(1-1/e-\varepsilon)\OPT-O(kξ)$, respectively, using $\widetilde O(nk^2\varepsilon^{-2})$ oracle calls. As a consequence, the offline-to-online reduction yields full-bandit CMAB algorithms for general matroid-constrained submodular rewards with exact limiting approximation-regret factors $1/e$ and $1-1/e$ and $\widetilde O(n^{1/5}k^{4/5}T^{4/5})$ regret.

</details>


### [290] [HYDRA: Hyperbolic Dynamic Representation Architecture for Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12194)
*Zhao Su,Yuxin Xia,Haoran Li,Jun Shen,Qi Zhu,Qingguo Zhou,Binbin Yong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HYDRA is a parameter-efficient hyperbolic extension of Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks that uses spline-based functional learning in the Poincaré ball with a low-rank prototype block, yielding competitive accuracy with fewer parameters and interpretable hyperbolic representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KANs suffer substantial parameter redundancy when assigning an independent function to every connection. There is a need for scalable, efficient nonlinear function approximation with better interpretability.

Method: Map vector-valued inputs into a bounded hyperbolic latent space (Poincaré ball), perform KAN-style updates in tangent space, employ spline-based functional learning, and incorporate a low-rank prototype block to share transformations across hidden dimensions; use radius control to stabilize training and provide structured radial coordinates for interpretation.

Result: Across eight benchmark datasets, HYDRA achieves competitive or superior predictive performance while reducing parameter count and enhancing representation interpretability.

Conclusion: HYDRA offers a scalable, parameter-efficient hyperbolic KAN modification that combines geometric priors with shared functional transformations, improving both performance and interpretability relative to baseline KANs.

Abstract: Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) enhance nonlinear function approximation by replacing scalar weights with learnable univariate functions. However, assigning an independent function to every connection results in substantial parameter redundancy, limiting their scalability and efficiency. To reduce this redundancy, we introduce \textbf{HY}perbolic \textbf{D}ynamic \textbf{R}epresentation \textbf{A}rchitecture (HYDRA), a parameter-efficient hyperbolic extension of KAN that combines spline-based functional learning with representations in the Poincaré ball. HYDRA maps vector-valued inputs into a bounded hyperbolic latent space, performs KAN-style updates in tangent space, and employs a low-rank prototype block to share functional transformations across hidden dimensions. The resulting hyperbolic representations provide a structured radial coordinate for interpretation, while radius control improves training stability by preventing boundary saturation. Extensive experiments across eight benchmark datasets demonstrate that HYDRA consistently achieves competitive or superior predictive performance while improving parameter efficiency and representation interpretability.

</details>


### [291] [ScreenShot: A Foundation Model for Few-Shot Combination Drug Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12219)
*Antoine de Mathelin,Christopher Tosh,Wesley Tansey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ScreenShot is a hierarchical transformer pretrained on 40 drug screening datasets that predicts combination-therapy responses from few observed functional measurements, without fine-tuning or molecular profiling, and guides efficient experimental design via active learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Combinatorial drug screening offers rich therapeutic options but is extremely costly and often infeasible at scale; existing models require molecular profiling and cohort-specific training, limiting use when samples are scarce.

Method: A hierarchical transformer architecture mirrors the nested structure of screening data and is pretrained on 40 datasets (3,700 drugs, 6,000 samples). For a new patient, it performs few-shot in-context learning to predict responses to combination therapies directly from functional measurements, without fine-tuning or molecular profiling. The model’s internal representations are then used to drive a weighted k-means++ active-learning strategy to select experiments.

Result: On four held-out datasets, ScreenShot outperforms all baselines in prediction accuracy and in identifying selectively effective treatments. The active-learning strategy achieves the same hit detection as uniform screening with about one-third the experimental budget.

Conclusion: ScreenShot enables accurate, data-efficient prediction of combination-therapy responses without molecular profiling and provides actionable guidance for experimental design via learned representations; source code and dashboard are available.

Abstract: Treating patients with combinations of drugs reduces the risk of resistance to any individual drug. Finding effective combinations is difficult because the large search space makes combinatorial screens prohibitively expensive, time consuming, and often technically infeasible. Predictive models can fill this gap, yet existing methods typically require molecular profiling of each sample and per-cohort training, limiting their applicability when time and tissue are scarce. To address this challenge, we introduce ScreenShot, a hierarchical transformer pretrained on 40 drug screening datasets covering 3,700 drugs and 6,000 biological samples, whose architecture mirrors the nested structure of screening data. Given a few-shot context of observations from a new patient, ScreenShot predicts the response of the sample to combination therapies through in-context learning, operating directly on functional measurements with no fine-tuning and no molecular profiling. On four held-out datasets, ScreenShot outperforms all baselines in both prediction accuracy and identification of selectively effective treatments. ScreenShot's internal representations are directly useful for experimental design: we use them to drive a weighted k-means++ active learning strategy that selects which experiments to run, achieving the same hit detection as uniform screening with a third of the budget. Source code and interactive dashboard: https://github.com/tansey-lab/screenshot.

</details>


### [292] [An Efficient Near-Optimal Algorithm for Adversarial $m$-Set Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12231)
*Francesco Bacchiocchi,Tommaso Cesari,Roberto Colomboni*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Efficient polynomial-time algorithm for adversarial combinatorial bandits with m-set actions that achieves near-optimal high-probability regret without enumerating the exponentially large action set.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome the exponential action space in combinatorial bandits by exploiting the shared d-dimensional vector of item losses; resolve an open problem regarding efficient algorithms with favorable regret.

Method: Introduce a sampling distribution represented by only d parameters instead of enumerating K=binom(d,m) actions, enabling polynomial-time computations while maintaining adversarial regret guarantees against adaptive non-anticipating adversaries.

Result: With probability at least 1-δ, the algorithm achieves regret R_T = O(sqrt(d T log(K/δ))) against the best fixed action, matching the high-probability bound of the finite-action EXP3-KW algorithm.

Conclusion: The proposed algorithm is computationally efficient (polynomial-time) and does not require enumeration of the exponential action set, thus resolving the open problem posed by Maiti et al. and aligning with EXP3-KW in high-probability regret bounds.

Abstract: We study adversarial combinatorial bandits with $m$-set actions, where at each round the learner selects $m$ out of $d$ items and observes only the aggregate loss of the selected items. The resulting action set contains $K=\binom{d}{m}$ elements and can therefore be exponentially large. Nevertheless, the loss of every action is determined by the same $d$-dimensional vector of item losses. We propose a computationally efficient algorithm that exploits this structure without explicitly enumerating the action set. Against adaptive non-anticipating adversaries, it guarantees, with probability at least $1-δ$, regret against the best fixed action of \[
  R_T =
  O\left(\sqrt{dT\log(K/δ)}\right). \] This matches the high-probability regret bound of the finite-action EXP3-KW algorithm of Zimmert and Lattimore, whose direct implementation may require exponential space. Our algorithm instead represents each sampling distribution with $d$ parameters and runs in polynomial time without enumerating the action set. Thus, it resolves the open problem posed by Maiti et al.

</details>


### [293] [Calibration Bets on the Past: Post-Training Quantization for Financial Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12259)
*Junyi Ye,Ivy Gateri Wanjiku*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Activation calibration critically governs 4-bit PTQ performance in financial forecasting; abs-max calibration severely degrades information, while percentile calibration recovers most of the loss; 8-bit activations or weight-only 4-bit quantization offer more robust deployment options, and calibration decisions should adapt to market regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how activation calibration strategies affect post-training quantization performance in cross-sectional volatility forecasting, addressing a gap in deployment guidance for financial forecasting models.

Method: Systematic empirical study across seven neural architectures, eight walk-forward test years (2018–2025), and 560 trained models; compare 4-bit quantization with abs-max vs percentile activation calibration; assess impact on mean information coefficient (MIC); evaluate 8-bit activations and weight-only 4-bit quantization as alternatives; analyze across different market periods.

Result: Activation calibration is a first-class deployment decision at 4-bit PTQ in finance: abs-max calibration degrades MIC by 11–62% in affected architectures; percentile calibration recovers 53–94% of this degradation in the four most affected architectures. At 8 bits, calibration has little effect; activation range choice varies with market regime, with narrow ranges helping under normal conditions but harming when dispersion exceeds history. If degradation remains, 8-bit activations or weight-only 4-bit quantization are more robust.

Conclusion: For reliable 4-bit PTQ in financial forecasting, activation calibration must be carefully chosen and often augmented by using 8-bit activations or weight-only 4-bit quantization; percentile-based calibration is particularly beneficial and calibration ranges should adapt to market regime changes.

Abstract: Financial forecasting models are typically developed in full precision, yet production deployment often requires low-precision inference to reduce memory and computational cost. Post-training quantization (PTQ) enables such deployment without retraining. However, reliable activation quantization requires calibration: activation ranges are estimated from historical data before deployment and then remain fixed during future inference. The importance of this deployment choice for financial forecasting remains poorly understood. We present a systematic study of activation calibration for PTQ in cross-sectional volatility forecasting on the S&P 500. Our evaluation covers seven representative neural architectures, eight walk-forward test years (2018-2025), and 560 trained models. We find that activation calibration has little effect at 8 bits but becomes the primary determinant of predictive performance at 4 bits. Under default absolute-maximum (abs-max) calibration, static 4-bit quantization of both weights and activations removes 11-62% of the full-precision mean information coefficient in affected architectures. Replacing abs-max with percentile calibration recovers 53-94% of this degradation in the four most affected architectures. The preferred activation range also varies across market periods. Narrow ranges improve resolution under typical market conditions but lose part of their advantage when test-period market dispersion exceeds the calibration history. These findings show that activation calibration is a first-class deployment decision for reliable 4-bit PTQ in financial forecasting. When substantial degradation remains, 8-bit activations or weight-only 4-bit quantization provide more robust deployment choices.

</details>


### [294] [Earth observation embeddings are effective sub-grid descriptors for probabilistic weather downscaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12271)
*Pedro Sousa,Will Tebbutt,Sadiq Jaffer,Robin Young,Anil Madhavapeddy,Richard E. Turner*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Earth-observation foundation-model embeddings (TESSERA, 10 m) used as a learned local surface descriptor in a convolutional conditional neural process to downscale coarse ERA5 fields (~25 km). Out-of-sample tests show improved probabilistic skill (CRPS) for 2 m temperature and 10 m wind speed across five regions, and robustness to forecast inputs and new stations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sub-grid surface variability and unresolved terrain/land-surface properties limit site-specific downscaling. Traditional downscalers rely on hand-crafted descriptors; this work probes whether transferable Earth-observation embeddings can provide generalized sub-grid surface representations for probabilistic weather downscaling.

Method: Augment a convolutional conditional neural process (CCNP) that downscales coarse ERA5 fields with a learned local surface descriptor derived by compressing a patch of TESSERA embeddings at 10 m. Evaluate across five climatically diverse regions with stations held out in space and/or time. Analyze CRPS for 2 m temperature and 10 m wind speed. Test robustness by replacing input with Aurora AI forecasts and by predicting at new stations with no regional history.

Result: Embedding improves probabilistic and point skill: CRPS improves by 11.5% for 2 m temperature and 6.2% for 10 m wind speed. Surface information better captures sub-grid departures; topography explains more for temperature, while TESSERA adds extra surface cues for wind speed. Improvements persist when switching coarse input from ERA5 to Aurora forecasts and for newly deployed stations with no regional history.

Conclusion: Long-timescale Earth-observation embeddings can support short-timescale weather downscaling by encoding persistent surface properties that structure sub-grid departures. This represents the first evidence of transferability of foundation-model surface representations to probabilistic sub-grid downscaling.

Abstract: Global weather reanalyses and forecasts resolve the evolving atmospheric state on coarse grids, but site-specific applications require predictions at arbitrary locations where near-surface conditions also depend on unresolved terrain and land-surface properties. Existing probabilistic downscalers address this gap using hand-crafted topographic descriptors. We ask instead whether Earth observation foundation models can provide transferable sub-grid surface representations for probabilistic weather downscaling.
  We augment a convolutional conditional neural process that downscales coarse ERA5 reanalysis fields at ~25 km resolution with a learned local surface descriptor, obtained by compressing a patch of TESSERA embeddings at 10 m resolution. Although these embeddings summarise surface conditions over annual timescales, they improve downscaling of instantaneous 2 m temperature and 10 m wind speed by encoding persistent surface properties that capture a location's departure from the coarse-grid atmospheric state. Across five climatically diverse regions, the embedding improves point and probabilistic skill at stations held out in both space and time, overall improving CRPS skill by 11.5% for 2 m temperature and 6.2% for 10 m wind speed. We further analyse how its contribution differs by variable, finding that topography explains more of temperature's sub-grid structure, while TESSERA provides additional surface information for wind speed.
  These improvements persist when the coarse input is changed from ERA5 to forecasts from the Aurora AI forecasting model, and when predicting at newly deployed stations with no regional history. To our knowledge, this is the first evidence that long-timescale Earth-observation embeddings can support short-timescale weather downscaling where sub-grid departures are systematically structured by persistent surface properties.

</details>


### [295] [A Framework for Designing Reward Functions: From Objectives to Features to Human-Aligned Reward Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12302)
*Di Yang Shi,W. Bradley Knox*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A formal workflow for non-experts to design linear, human-aligned reward functions across three steps: distill task objectives into measurable outcome variables, select a causally representative subset of outcomes via a minimum-cost partial cover on a causal DAG (solved by max-flow in polynomial time), and fit weights through convex feasibility using preference elicitation. It claims a deterministically conflict-free feasible weight region narrowed by a separation oracle with O(n log κ) preference queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable non-experts to safely and efficiently specify reward functions that reflect a given preference ordering over trajectories, improving alignment guarantees while reducing manual trial-and-error and accounting for causal structure between outcomes.

Method: Three-step process: (1) distill task objectives into fundamental objectives and measurable outcome variables; (2) select a causally representative subset of outcome variables as reward terms by reducing to minimum-cost partial cover on a causal DAG, solvable in polynomial time via max-flow; (3) fit weights by posing preference queries, reframing weight fitting as a convex feasibility problem iteratively narrowed by a separation oracle, achieving a deterministically conflict-free weight region; analyzed under a tolerance κ with O(n log κ) preference queries.

Result: Theoretical framework with formal guarantees: polynomial-time solutions for the term-selection step; a separation-oracle-based process to converge to a feasible weight region; claims of deterministic conflict-free region and efficient preference querying; no empirical validation reported in the abstract.

Conclusion: The work presents a cohesive, provably efficient reward-design methodology that enables non-experts to instantiate and iterate human-aligned reward functions with formal guarantees, laying groundwork for practical tools and future empirical validation.

Abstract: We present a formal process to enable non-experts to instantiate and iterate on human-aligned reward functions, i.e. reward functions that adhere to a given preference ordering over trajectories. Given a task described in natural language, our process produces a linear reward function in three steps: distill the task's objectives into a set of fundamental objectives and derive measurable outcome variables that capture those fundamental objectives, select a causally representative subset of outcome variables as the reward terms, and fit weights to those reward terms via preference elicitation. Our contributions describe the first step and formalize the latter two steps. The first is a guided workflow for deriving outcome variables. The second is a reduction of reward term selection to minimum-cost partial cover on a causal DAG, solved in polynomial time via max-flow. The third is a geometric framing of weight fitting as a convex feasibility problem iteratively narrowed by preference queries, solved by existing separation oracle methods. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first reward-design method that maintains a deterministically conflict-free feasible weight region, narrowed to a desired tolerance via a separation oracle with O(n log κ) preference queries.

</details>


### [296] [Redistribution-based Cost Inference Improves Sparse Safe Offline RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12306)
*Ebenezer Gelo,Geraud Nangue Tasse,Steven James,Benjamin Rosman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RCI converts sparse trajectory stop-feedback into dense per-step costs via return redistribution; enables lossless CMDP reformulation and improved offline policy learning with lower policy violations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the credit-assignment problem in offline RL when supervisors provide only binary stop-feedback at the first unsafe transition; aims to enable dense cost signals without per-step annotations.

Method: Redistribution-based Cost Inference (RCI) that performs return decomposition to assign per-step costs from sparse stop signals, then trains a constrained offline policy on the augmented data. Theoretical results show return-equivalent redistribution preserves the CMDP feasible policy set and optimal Lagrangian; empirically, improves cost-critic conditioning.

Result: Experiments on highway driving and robotic manipulation show substantially lower violation rates than sparse and classifier-based baselines, with robustness to heterogeneous dataset compositions and label noise.

Conclusion: RCI enables a lossless transformation from sparse stop-feedback to dense costs, improving offline RL safety and performance, and showing strong empirical gains across domains.

Abstract: Safe offline RL typically assumes access to dense per-step cost annotations, but in practice supervisors provide only trajectory-level stop-feedback: a binary signal at the first unsafe transition, with no per-step attribution. We frame this as a temporal credit assignment problem and propose the Redistribution-based Cost Inference (RCI) framework, which converts sparse stop-feedback into dense per-step costs via return decomposition, then trains a constrained offline policy on the augmented dataset. We show that return-equivalent redistribution preserves the feasible policy set and the optimal Lagrangian in a CMDP, establishing that the transformation is lossless in theory while yielding better-conditioned cost critic learning in practice. Experiments on highway driving and robotic manipulation demonstrate substantially lower violation rates than sparse and classifier-based baselines, with robustness to heterogeneous dataset compositions and label noise.

</details>


### [297] [AI4AI at Test-Time: Strong-to-Weak Capability Transfer via Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12307)
*Cheng Qian,Wenting Zhao,Liangwei Yang,Heng Wang,Jielin Qiu,Heng Ji,Silvio Savarese,Huan Wang,Shelby Heinecke*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Test-time inference-time harnesses enable strong models to transfer cognitive structure to weaker models without retraining, dramatically improving weak-model performance on Theory-of-M mind tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether transfer of capabilities can occur at inference time via harnesses, rather than via parameter updates, to aid weaker models.

Method: Four Theory-of-Mind benchmarks; a strong builder model creates an inference-time harness using 5% of data for validation; the harness is refined over multiple rounds and then evaluated on the full test set.

Result: Harness-enabled target-model performance nearly doubles (0.49 to 0.91); gains largely come from deterministic code offloading, task-specific routing, and strict answer formats; minimal gains from prompting/deeper reasoning or broader sampling.

Conclusion: Inference-time harness design is a valuable complement to training-time distillation, enabling strong models to transfer cognitive structure to weaker models without retraining.

Abstract: Recent work on distillation transfers the capabilities of large models to smaller ones often by updating the latter's parameters, through teacher forcing, on-policy distillation, and related training-time methods. In this paper, we ask whether such transfer can instead occur at test time. We study strong-to-weak scaffolding: whether a stronger builder model can construct inference-time harnesses that help a weaker target model solve tasks more reliably without any parameter updates. Using four representative Theory-of-Mind benchmarks, each builder model uses 5% of the data as a validation set to iteratively refine its harness over multiple rounds, after which the finalized harness is evaluated on the full test set. Empirically, this form of test-time capability transfer is highly effective, nearly doubling average target-model performance from 0.49 to 0.91. Our analysis shows that the gains come primarily from offloading unstable model reasoning into deterministic code, benchmark-specific routing, and strict answer-format enforcement, rather than from encouraging the target model to reason more extensively or sample more broadly. We further find that builder-model reasoning effort improves harness quality monotonically, platform effects are modest relative to the builder model's own capability, and weaker target models receive the largest gains. These results suggest that inference-time harness design is an important complement to conventional training-time distillation, enabling strong models to transfer cognitive structure to weaker models without retraining.

</details>
